# Necessary conditions for valid causal inference from observational data 

Louis Anthony Cox Jr.<br>Cox Associates, Entanglement, and University of Colorado, Denver, CO, USA


#### Abstract

Observational studies often guide policy, regulation, litigation, and public health decisions. However, causal claims from such studies can be misleading if key logical and methodological requirements are not met. We synthesized insights from foundational causal theory, contemporary methods, and critiques of common practices to develop a framework of necessary conditions for valid interventional causal inferences predicting the effects of changes in exposure on changes in risk. The framework organizes these conditions into five domains: (A) conceptual and definitional clarity, (B) study design prerequisites, (C) data analysis requirements for valid causal modeling and effect estimation, (D) interpretation and result integrity, and (E) robustness, generalizability, and external validity of results. Each domain includes criteria, rationale, and practical evaluation tools, integrating Potential Outcomes (PO), Structural Causal Models (SCM), Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAG), information-theoretic measures, and machine-learning (ML) approaches for heterogeneity. Summary tables and STROBE-style checklists translate the framework into actionable guidance. Producing trustworthy, decision-relevant causal claims from observational data requires meeting all necessary conditions across the five domains. Modern study designs, methods, and software make it increasingly practical to do so.

## ChatGPT 5.0-assisted executive summary

This experimental Executive Summary is an AI-assisted preview of the full paper that follows. It was developed collaboratively by the author and ChatGPT 5.0 to distill the main ideas while preserving technical precision. We believe that such humanmachine coauthoring can help improve and accelerate the communication of scientific work, and we hope it will soon become a common practice. Key technical terms are explained in the text and in the glossary at the end of the paper.

Observational studies often inform policy, regulation, litigation, and public health, but their causal claims are only valid when specific logical and methodological conditions are met. This paper identifies necessary conditions for interventional causal inference-claims about what would happen if a variable were deliberately changed-organizing them into five domains: (a) conceptual clarity, (b) study design, (c) data analysis, (d) interpretation of results, and I generalizability/external validity. The framework integrates modern causal tools (Potential Outcomes, Structural Causal Models, DAGs, systems dynamics, time series, and information-theoretic approaches) with practical checklists to support transparent, reproducible, and decision-relevant conclusions. The following outline of key contents summarizes these domains in turn, highlighting key conditions, useful methods, and common pitfalls to provide a concise preview of the full framework

## A. Conceptual \& definitional foundations

- Clarify the causal claim: Distinguish between association, attribution, prediction, intervention, counterfactual, and mechanistic causation; only interventional claims predict the consequences of deliberate changes. Interventional causation refers specifically to effects (changes in outcome probabilities) caused by changing the values of one or more variables.
- Define the estimand: Specify the contrast - e.g. Average Treatment Effect (ATE), Average Treatment Effect on the Treated (ATT), Conditional Average Treatment Effect (CATE) - and the population, the time frame, and the conditions under which it applies.
- Well-defined interventions: Ensure the treatment or exposure is specific, feasible (in principle), and anchored in time to avoid biases like immortal time bias.
- Formal frameworks:
- Potential Outcomes: Requires exchangeability, positivity, and consistency.
- Structural Causal Models: Represent mechanisms explicitly, support empirical testing, and define interventions via the do-operator.
- DAGs/PAGs (Directed Acyclic Graphs and Partial Ancestral Graphs): Identify confounders, colliders, and valid adjustment sets; make assumptions transparent.
- Dynamic systems: Information-theoretic measures (e.g. transfer entropy) and stability/invariance testing help diagnose causal direction in time-dependent processes.
- Bradford Hill revisited: Traditional heuristics are neither necessary nor sufficient but can be reframed in testable terms within modern frameworks.


## B. Study design prerequisites

- Internal validity first: Designs must approximate the counterfactual contrast as closely as possible, even without randomization.
- Foundational requirements:

1. Clear treatment/control definitions.
2. Comparability of groups (exchangeability).
3. Avoidance of bias from selection, measurement error, or misclassification.
4. Correct temporal ordering.
5. Sufficient variation in exposures (positivity).

- Fit-for-purpose: Align design with the causal question, data, and feasible assumptions.
- Design strategies: Target trial emulation, quasi-experiments, longitudinal/panel designs, instrumental variables.


## C. Necessary conditions for valid analysis

- Model specification: Select adjustment sets that block all backdoor paths without conditioning on colliders.
- Variable roles: Identify confounders, mediators, colliders explicitly.
- Identification: Confirm the causal effect is estimable from observed data under stated assumptions.
- Estimation methods: Regression, matching, inverse probability weighting, $g$-methods for time-varying data, and machine learning for heterogeneity.
- Practical challenges: Address measurement error, missing data, latent confounding, and model misspecification.
- Validation: Use falsification tests, triangulation, negative controls, and sensitivity analysis to challenge and refine conclusions.


## D. Interpretation \& result integrity

- Guardrails: Avoid causal claims from non-causal designs or unjustified assumptions.
- Pre-specification: Lock hypotheses, analysis plans, and outcome definitions in advance.
- Measurement: Use validated tools; verify exposure-outcome links persist after adjustment.
- Heterogeneity: Explore and interpret effect modification responsibly.
- Transparency: Present results clearly; share data and code where feasible.


## E. Robustness, generalizability \& external validity

- Challenge conclusions: Test against plausible alternative explanations, model variants, and adversarial scenarios.
- Transportability: Formally assess whether effects hold across populations, contexts, and times using reweighting or causal transport formulas.
- Heterogeneity analysis: Use causal tree/forest methods to identify subgroups with consistent and generalizable effects.
- Evidence base integrity: Evaluate publication bias, selective reporting, and the completeness of the evidence base.


## F. Discussion \& recommendations

- Sequential logic: Internal validity is necessary before external validity can be assessed.
- Framework utility: The proposed conditions, tables, and checklists provide a structured, operationalizable approach for researchers, reviewers, and decision-makers.
- Call to action: Adoption of these tools can improve the credibility, reproducibility, and policy relevance of causal claims from observational studies.

Valid interventional causal inference from observational data requires meeting all necessary conditions across conceptual, design, analytic, interpretive, and generalizability domains. The framework offered here seeks to combine modern causal analysis theory and methods with practical diagnostics to help enable more trustworthy and actionable conclusions in science, policy, and public health. Table 0 gives an overview of suggested necessary conditions, useful methods, and common pitfalls for each domain described above; these are developed and explained in the sections that follow.

## 1. Introduction

Much of current science, policy, and regulation hinges on causal claims drawn from non-experimental data. Yet the conditions under which such claims are valid remain poorly specified, often unstated, and frequently violated (loannidis 2005; Hernán 2016; Gerrits et al. 2019; Schwartz and Prins, 2024). As stated by Bulbulia (2024), "[M]any researchers collect data, apply complex regressions, and report model coefficients without understanding that the assumptions needed to support causal inferences differ from those needed to support predictions." In 1965, Sir Austin Bradford Hill famously

asserted that "What I do not believe - and this has been suggested - is that we can usefully lay down some hard-and-fast rules of evidence that must be obeyed before we accept cause and effect."

The point of departure for this paper is a conviction that the "causal revolution" of the past six decades (Schwartz and Prins 2024) now warrants a less pessimistic view: hard-andfast rules in the form of necessary conditions that must be obeyed before we accept cause and effect can and should be set forth and used in causal analysis and interpretation of observational data. For example, no matter what we intend " $X$ causes $Y$ " to mean - and several distinct meanings related to association, attribution, prediction, intervention, counterfactuals, and mechanistic views of causation are reviewed in Section 3 of this paper - a necessary condition for $X$ to be a direct cause of $Y$, denoted $X \rightarrow Y$, is that the value of $Y$ (in deterministic cases) or the probability distribution of $Y$ (more generally) must depend on the level of $X$ for at least some levels of the other variables. More formally, if $Y$ is conditionally independent of $X$ for all values of the other variables, then $X$ is not a direct cause of $Y$ (Pearl 2009). Another necessary condition is that information must flow from changes in $X$ to future changes in $Y$, rather than in the reverse direction (Granger 1969; Schreiber 2000). These and other necessary conditions about dependence, conditional independence, direction of information flow, and related concepts are explained further in Section 3. They are empirically testable conditions, meaning that they can be compared to observations, if the requisite data are collected, both to determine
whether the belief that $X$ causes $Y$ is consistent with observations; and to estimate the quantitative effect on the distribution of $Y$ of real or contemplated changes in $X$. Section 4 discusses study design requirements and Section 5 discusses data analysis methods for doing so.

Thus, this paper seeks to meet the challenge of Hill's skepticism by summarizing constructive insights and methods for articulating and empirically testing necessary conditions that must hold before we can be satisfied that available data provide evidence that $X$ causes $Y$. That is, we seek to apply perspectives from modern causal analysis frameworks to identify necessary conditions for valid interventional causal inference from observational data-and to show how these conditions can be tested, evaluated, and applied in practice. Interventional causation refers to causal effects of interventions that change the values of one or more variables; in this sense, it is about changes caused by doing something rather than about changes in knowledge caused by observing something (Pearl 2009). Although Hill modestly wrote that "I have no wish, nor the skill, to embark upon a philosophical discussion of the meaning of 'causation' on to define causation," the intervening decades have produced several frameworks for defining causation and for quantitatively estimating causal effects of interventions on outcomes or their probabilities; these are discussed in Section 3. The following sections propose a framework for evaluating whether causal conclusions especially, interventional causal conclusions, as discussed in Section 3 - are logically and empirically justified based on consistency with observational (i.e. non-experimental) data.

From shoe-leather epidemiology to anticipatory causal study designs, public health has long cycled among observation, causal inference, and action. John Snow's 1854 cholera map and the removal of the Broad Street pump handle are remembered as an early instance where a causal hypothesis, grounded in observed patterns, prompted a decisive and successful intervention (Brody et al. 2000). Through the twentieth century, influential observational findings-exemplified by the smoking-lung cancer evidence-catalyzed policy (Doll and Hill 1954), while later methodological developments
formalized the counterfactual logic needed to separate association from intervention effects (Greenland et al. 1999; Glass et al. 2013; Hernán and Robins 2020). In parallel, the riskassessment enterprise increasingly showed that hazard identification alone is insufficient for sound decisions: quantitative characterization of potency and exposure is needed to produce credible, decision-relevant risk estimates; otherwise precautionary bans can displace more effective, risk-reducing investments (McClellan 1999). Recent episodes-such as the 2025 measles resurgence in the United States-underscore

![](https://cdn.mathpix.com/cropped/9cf8a422-856b-41dc-8da7-b268614f2772-007.jpg?height=1720&width=1241&top_left_y=704&top_left_x=442)
Figure 1. A framework for more trustworthy causal inference.

that observations must be converted into explicit, testable intervention strategies and their effects verified to prevent needless human misery (Mathis et al. 2025; Hernán and Robins 2020. This paper responds to that need by setting out necessary conditions that help make interventional claims from observational data clearer, more testable, and more reliable for decision-makers.

Figure 1 summarizes the conceptual structure of the paper. The top highlights the ubiquity of causal claims in science, policy, and risk assessment, together with the hazards that arise when such claims are drawn from observational data without adequate support. The central portion of the figure identifies five domains of necessary conditions-conceptual clarity, study design prerequisites, analytic conditions, interpretation principles, and robustness/generalizability— that jointly underpin credible interventional causal claims. These domains structure the remainder of the paper.

The domains are not intended to represent a strict analytic sequence or workflow. Rather, they summarize distinct but interdependent necessary conditions that must all be addressed, explicitly or implicitly, for an interventional causal claim to be well defined, logically coherent, and decisionrelevant. Weakness or failure in any one domain can undermine the credibility of a causal claim, even if other domains are addressed carefully.

In this framework, as discussed further in Section 3, identifiability (also called causal identifiability) refers to whether the causal effect of interest is uniquely determined in principle by the joint distribution of the observed data and the assumed causal structure (e.g. exchangeability, positivity, and consistency)-and not merely to statistical estimability or the existence of a fitted model describing the observations. A parameter may be statistically estimable yet fail to correspond to a well-defined causal effect if identifiability conditions are not met. Conversely, if a causal effect of interest is not identifiable from observed variables, then collecting more data or applying more ingenious and powerful statistical estimation methods will not suffice to yield a unique estimate of that effect-much as specifying that the product of two integers is 12 fails to provide sufficient information to uniquely identify them, no matter how accurately their product is measured.

Section 3 discusses conditions under which causal effects of real or contemplated interventions can be clearly defined and identified, at least conceptually, from observed data. These conditions typically require several technical assumptions (e.g. conditional exchangeability, positivity, and consistency, as discussed in Section 3.3.2) that can often be stated and displayed clearly with the help of directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) (Section 3.3.1). DAG models clarify dependence relationships between variables-represented by arrows between them-and also conditional independence relationships between them, represented by the absence of arrows. These relationships can be compared to results of conditional independence tests in data, forging empirically testable links between conceptual causal models and observations (Pearl 2009). DAG models are therefore valuable for conceptual clarity and definition of causal effects, and they also inform
causal study design and data analysis by clarifying which variables must be measured and how they should be related.

Section 4 discusses study designs that do-or do notprovide sufficient information to identify causal effects of interventions such as changes in exposures. With respect to confounding control, our intent in placing this item under study design prerequisites is to emphasize that the possibility of controlling confounding is largely determined at the design stage through decisions about which variables are measured, the timing and level of measurement, inclusion and exclusion criteria, and data collection protocols. By contrast, the implementation of confounding control typically occurs during data analysis. (Briefly, unbiased estimation of causal effects in the data analysis phase typically requires conditioning on the values of the variables in an adjustment set (Section 3.3.1), and this, in turn, requires a design that collects information on those variables. There are often multiple valid adjustment sets and hence multiple possible designs that can fulfull this requirement; see Section 3.3.).

Section 5 discusses data analysis methods for practical estimation of effects when identifiability conditions are met, including methods for addressing realistic challenges and limitations such as measurement and misclassification errors in exposures, covariates, and responses; missing data; unmeasured ("latent" or "hidden") variables; residual confounding; and model uncertainty. Sections 6 and 7 discuss interpretation of results, assessment of their robustness to remaining uncertainties, and their applicability to the target population and conditions of interest.

Throughout these sections, we discuss conditions for both internal validity and external validity of causal inferences. Internal validity concerns whether the estimated effect corresponds to the well-defined causal effect of interest for the study population, intervention, outcome, and conditions under the stated assumptions, given the data collected and the analysis actually performed. In this sense, internal validity asks whether the causal conclusion is correct for the setting being studied, rather than whether it is precise, statistically significant, or broadly applicable. Threats to internal validity arise when alternative explanations for the observed association cannot be ruled out, including failures of causal identifiability, uncontrolled confounding, selection processes (including attrition), time-related changes or comparisongroup nonequivalence, measurement error, model misspecification, or inappropriate conditioning. Such failures undermine causal interpretation even when estimates are stable or statistically precise (Campbell and Stanley 1963).

External validity (Sections 5.5.2 and 7), by contrast, concerns whether a causally valid conclusion for the study population and conditions can be generalized as-is to a different target population, setting, intervention implementation, or time period of substantive interest. Threats to external validity arise when causal effects differ across populations or contexts due to effect modification, differences in background conditions, or interactions between the intervention and characteristics of units, settings, or measurement processes, even when internal validity has been achieved. Heterogeneous treatment effecIHTE) models (Sections 4.3.2.3 and 5) explicitly model how treatment effects depend on
such contextual variables and can help clarify the conditions under which causal conclusions may or may not generalize.

Related but distinct is the more recent concept of transportability (Section 7.2), which addresses whether and how causal conclusions derived in one population or setting may be adjusted-using additional assumptions and data-to yield valid causal conclusions for a different target population or context. Transportability does not assert that the same causal effect holds elsewhere; rather, it specifies conditions under which causal knowledge can be appropriately transformed to account for systematic differences between study and target settings.

In the framework summarized in Figure 1, internal validity is addressed primarily through the domains of conceptual clarity, study design prerequisites, and data analysis conditions, while external validity and transportability are addressed through the robustness and generalizability domain and through explicit interpretation principles. Both internal and external validity are necessary for causal claims to be decision-relevant: internally valid but non-generalizable conclusions may misinform policy, while broadly framed but internally invalid conclusions may mislead decision-makers. Accordingly, the framework emphasizes conditions for both internal and external validity explicit, assessing their plausibility, and clearly delimiting the populations, interventions, and conditions to which causal conclusions are intended to apply.

When the necessary conditions in each domain are satisfied, the resulting causal claims are logically valid, empirically supported, and appropriately qualified for informing-rather than obscuring-risk assessment, policy deliberation, and decisions about causally effective interventions.

There is much current practical need and opportunity for clearer thinking about causation and for sound, practical methods to define and estimate impacts caused by interventions using available (typically, observational, i.e. nonrandomized, non-experimental) data. Advances in causal concepts and methods are needed to better inform policy decisions and deliberations in a host of important applications, including public health, economics, and social science (North 2020; Schwartz and Prins 2024). Despite major advances in causal methodology, many published studies continue to make interventional causal claims that are inadequately defined and inadequately supported by the available evidence or the assumptions required for valid inference (loannidis 2005; Hernán 2016; Gerrits et al. 2019; Nakagawa et al. 2025). This problem is not merely academic. Misunderstanding or misstating the strength of causal evidence can lead to regulatory missteps, unjustified litigation, and misinformed decisions in public and occupational health contexts, as well as to wasted or misdirected research effort (Nakagawa et al. 2025).

At stake, too, is an even deeper scientific concern: Are we fooling ourselves about the likely consequences of interventions and policy changes that are intended to protect human health because we use insufficiently rigorous approaches to causal inference and to expression of causal conclusions (Hernán 2016; Munafò et al. 2017)? More constructively, can we avoid misleading ourselves and others by rigorously applying objective, operational, necessary conditions for
sound causal inference? The major goal of this paper is to help provide an affirmative answer to this question and to show how it can be made practical to apply the key principles of modern causal analysis without having to specialize in it.

Headlines frequently make or suggest causal claims. Both experts and non-specialists benefit from an objective basis for assessing the extent to which the claimed causal conclusions are supported by the data and analyses, rather than by modeling choices, "researcher degrees of freedom" (Wicherts et al. 2016), unverified assumptions and investigator judgments, or other non-empirical processes. In practice, deciding what to believe and why is often difficult because the evidential contribution of the data is entangled with modeling and interpretation decisions and design limitations. Lists of logically necessary conditions, developed a priori from causal theory and sound methodology and outside the context of any single case, provide a transparent, auditable basis for dispassionate judgment about which causal claims are credible.

To motivate why a systematic framework for evaluating causal claims is needed, consider the examples of causal claims in Table 1. These are real-world cases where observational causal inference either succeeded in guiding effective policy or failed with costly consequences. These cases-from the opioid epidemic to air pollution, climate change, alcohol, nutrition, and chemical exposures-show that meeting (or failing to meet) necessary conditions often determines whether causal claims are trustworthy, actionable, or dangerously misleading.

Analytic flexibility (researcher degrees of freedom) is a specific and widespread threat to valid causal inference, so trustworthy decision-relevant conclusions should remain stable across pre-specified, reasonable alternatives or be explicitly qualified. Causal conclusions are often drawn in good faith, but without careful testing of their underlying assumptions (Gerrits et al. 2019). Influential critiques by loannidis and others have shown how flexible research practices, unexamined assumptions, weak inferential logic, and overstatement of results and conclusions can undermine the credibility of published findings (loannidis 2005; Munafò et al. 2017; Boutron and Ravaud 2018). In causal inference from observational data, these risks are especially acute. Unlike randomized trials, observational studies require strong and often untestable or untested assumptions to support interventional claims-yet these assumptions are frequently left implicit, unexamined, or miscommunicated (Hernán 2016).

At the same time, recent methodological advancesincluding the directed acyclic graph (DAG), structural causal model (SCM), and potential outcomes (PO) frameworks and the formal identification tools discussed in Section 3 and sub-sequently-make it possible to do better (Lederer et al. 2019; North 2019; Huntington-Klein 2021; Igelström et al. 2022; Duong et al. 2024). These developments, sometimes called the causal revolution (Schwartz and Prins 2024), provide new ways to clarify causal questions, make assumptions explicit, and test whether causal claims are justified (Hernán and Robins 2016). But widespread adoption has lagged, and many practitioners lack accessible, actionable guidance on

Table 1. Examples of causal claims and causal inference successes and failures.
| Domain | Causal claim | Violated/Tested necessary condition(s) | Outcome | Possible lessons |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Alcohol use (Stockwell et al. 2016) | Moderate drinking protects against heart disease; alcohol increases cancer risk | Confounding ("healthy user bias"), misclassification of exposure; biological mechanism \& Mendelian randomization support cancer link | Heart protection claims are largely spurious; cancer risk causal | Triangulating across methods (mechanism, genetics, epidemiology) distinguishes true vs. spurious causal claims |
| Chemical exposures (BeaneFreeman et al. 2009) | Formaldehyde exposure causes leukemia | Weak temporal evidence; mechanistic plausibility contested; alternative explanations (co-exposures, chance) not excluded | IARC classified formaldehyde as carcinogenic to humans; regulatory debates remain unsettled | Failure to meet necessary conditions (temporal sequence, mechanistic plausibility, exclusion of confounding) undermines confidence in causal claims and leads to prolonged scientific and regulatory disputes |
| Climate change \& disaster attribution (KirchmeierYoung et al. 2019; Bellprat et al. 2019) | Anthropogenic climate change increases risks of wildfires, floods, tornados, hurricanes, etc. | Lack of successful validation and falsification tests of some attribution models; untested robustness and transportability of causal attributions | Strong causal language used in policy debates based on modeling assumptions without demonstrated validity | Attribution $\neq$ intervention. Without validated causal models and tested necessary conditions, claims risk overstatement and policy misuse |
| Environmental risks/Global burden of disease (PrüssUstün et al. 2017) | "Nearly a quarter of global disease burden could be prevented by reducing environmental risks." | No well-defined intervention; required identification conditions not established; joint/overlapping causes and dynamics/lags ignored. (Rockhill et al. 1998; von Cube et al. 2020; Cox, 2021) | Interventional conclusion not warranted by PAF-based burden estimates; risk of unjustified assertions about policy implications. | Distinguish attribution from intervention; specify the policy and test identification and robustness before making preventable-risk claims. |
| Opioid epidemic (Leung et al. 2017) | Prescription opioids for pain do not cause addiction | Temporal ordering, confounding control, reverse causation | Misleading reassurance → overprescribing → epidemic of misuse/ addiction | Failure to establish necessary conditions for causality contributed to a public health disaster |
| Particulate air pollution \& allcause mortality (Clancy et al. 2002) | In Ireland, reducing particulate air pollution reduced all-cause and cardiovascular mortality rates | Temporal precedence, falsification tests, and robustness across settings not demonstrated; historical trends not corrected for (Zigler and Dominici 2014) | Quasi-experiments confirmed reductions in all-cause and cardiovascular mortality rates if unverified modeling assumptions hold, but not in reality (Lyons et al. 2023) | Assumption-dependent causal conclusions imply health effects that are not observed in reality |
| Trans fats (Restrepo and Rieger 2016) | Trans fat consumption increases heart disease risk | Multiple necessary conditions tested: biological plausibility, consistency, dose-response, natural experiments | Policy bans led to measurable population health improvements | Rigorous causal inference from observational data guided life-saving regulations |
| Vitamin E supplementation (Vivekananthan et al. 2003) | Vitamin E reduces cardiovascular risk | Publication bias, unmeasured confounding, lack of falsification | Large RCTs disproved benefit; possible harms found | Illustrates dangers of publication bias and unchecked confounding when causal conditions are not tested |


how to apply these methods or evaluate the claims of others (Xu et al. 2022). Despite their power, these tools are not yet widely used to evaluate the trustworthiness of causal claims in practice, and many remain unfamiliar to applied researchers and reviewers.

This paper proposes a framework for systematically evaluating whether the various necessary conditions for valid interventional causal inference are satisfied in a given study or claim. Our aim is to clarify what must be true-or shown to be true-if a causal claim is to be scientifically credible, decision-relevant, and robust. We focus on logical and methodological necessities, not merely best practices. These conditions are framed to support rigorous evaluation of claims in risk research.

An important implication of this framework is that causal conclusions are inherently provisional and subject to revision as scientific understanding evolves. When causal assumptions
and models are stated transparently-through explicit estimands, causal diagrams, and identification conditions-subsequent discoveries of previously unrecognized variables, pathways, or effect modifiers can be used to reassess earlier conclusions in a principled way. In this sense, transparent causal modeling does not weaken scientific claims; it strengthens cumulative learning by making clear which conclusions depended on which assumptions, and how those conclusions should be updated when those assumptions are no longer tenable in light of new empirical evidence. This view treats causal inference from observational data as an iterative scientific process rather than a one-time adjudication.

This paper is intended to be used flexibly and not necessarily read linearly from beginning to end. Readers primarily interested in evaluating the credibility of a specific causal claim may begin with the summary tables and reporting
checklists at the end of each section, which concisely state the necessary conditions for valid interventional causal inference. Readers seeking deeper understanding can then consult the accompanying text, which explains the underlying concepts, assumptions, and methods, with references provided for formal development, proofs, and technical detail. The framework is designed to support both high-level assessment and more detailed inquiry, allowing practitioners, reviewers, and decision-makers to engage with the material at a level of depth appropriate to their needs. To support this use, some key concepts are briefly repeated across sections and tables for clarity and self-containment and a glossary is provided at the end.

To make the proposed framework usable, operationalizable, and transparent, we organize it into the following five thematic sections:
A. Conceptual clarity about causal effects and interventions;
B. Observational (i.e. non-experimental) study design prerequisites for valid identification and estimation of causal effects;
C. Data analysis conditions for drawing valid causal conclusions from observational data;
D. Interpretation principles for responsible and transparent communication of the results; and
E. Robustness and generalizability checks for external applicability (e.g. to a target population or to specific conditions, interventions, or subpopulations of interest).

Each section synthesizes insights from foundational literature and leading methodologies, reformulated as discussion and guidance tables for risk science practitioners. We hope it will be useful to researchers, analysts, regulators, and peer reviewers of scientific documents making causal claims about exposure-response relationships. Where possible and appropriate, we integrate multiple causal frameworks-including the potential outcomes, SCM, DAG, information-theoretic, and dynamic systems modeling frameworks explained in Section 3-to highlight complementary insights and diagnostics. We seek to pair each condition with diagnostic tools, testable implications, and concrete examples of how causal inferences can succeed-or fail-in practice.

Each of the following five sections culminates in summary tables and a concise reporting checklist inspired by STROBE'Strengthening the Reporting of Observational Studies in Epidemiology'-adapted here for interventional causal claims (Cuschieri 2019). We hope this framework will serve as a practical reference and springboard for improving the conduct, evaluation, and communication of causal claims in risk-related science. Its central aspiration is not just methodological precision, but greater alignment between scientific claims about causality and the real-world decisions they are meant to inm.

## 2. Note on literature search and evaluation methods

This is a narrative, methods-focused review intended to synthesize necessary conditions and practical tools for valid
interventional causal inference from observational data. We did not conduct a formal systematic review or meta-analysis but instead used purposive search to identify foundational frameworks, widely used methods, high-quality tutorials, consensus statements, methodology checklists, and illustrative applications (including counter-examples).

Searches were conducted in PubMed/MEDLINE, Web of Science Core Collection, Scopus, and Google Scholar, with no start date limits. Citation chaining (backward/forward) from seminal articles and books and targeted searches of methodological venues (e.g. Epidemiology, Biometrika, Journal of Causal Inference) supplemented database searches. We also considered authoritative monographs and consensus/standards documents where relevant. For this search strategy, a typical example query is as follows: (PubMed, Title/Abstract fields): ("causal inference" OR "structural causal model" OR "directed acyclic graph" OR DAG OR "potential outcomes" OR "g-formula" OR "targeted maximum likelihood" OR TMLE OR "inverse probability weighting" OR "difference-in-differences" OR DiD OR "instrumental variables" OR "interrupted time series" OR "target trial emulation") AND (observational OR nonrandomized OR "non-randomized") AND (assumption* OR identif* OR "necessary condition*" OR validity OR robustness OR "measurement error" OR "missing data" OR transportab*). For illustrative example, we included sources that (i) define an estimand and the identification conditions needed to estimate it from observational data; (ii) articulate assumptions transparently (often via DAG/SCM/PO formalisms); and (iii) provide either proofs, reproducible code, or clear empirical demonstrations. We excluded purely predictive modeling papers lacking causal identification, and application-only reports without methods relevance to necessary conditions. Candidate sources were qualitatively appraised for clarity of estimand, explicitness and plausibility of assumptions (e.g. exchangeability, positivity, SUTVA [Stable Unit Treatment Value Assumption]), treatment of common threats (confounding, measurement error, missing data, model misspecification), and robustness and transportability claims. Findings were integrated to produce checklists, methodology tables, and examples that make assumptions and uncertainties explicit. Where appropriate, we include counter-examples to prevent over-interpretation of associational results.

Because this is a narrative methods review, PRISMA flow diagrams and risk-of-bias tools for effect studies are not applicable; the emphasis is on conceptual validity and identification rather than quantitative synthesis. For transparency, the databases, final search date, and exemplar queries are reported above.

## 3. Conceptual and definitional foundations for causal inference

At the heart of every policy or regulatory question about causality—and of many scientific and legal ones-lies a decision: What should we do differently? Questions like "Does benzene cause Acute Myeloid Leukemia (AML)?" or "Would prescription monitoring programs reduce opioid deaths?" are not merely seeking descriptive information; instead, they

Table 2. Common types of causal claims in scientific and policy discourse.
| Type of causal question | BPA examples | Opioid examples | Nature of inquiry |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Descriptive/Associational | Do people exposed to BPA have higher cancer rates? | Are opioid overdose rates higher in counties with more lenient pharmacy dispensing practices? | Observational correlation; hypothesisgenerating, not necessarily causal |
| Attributive/Statistical | What proportion of childhood obesity cases can be attributed to BPA exposure? | What proportion of opioid overdose deaths can be attributed to pharmacy overdispensing? | Quantifies population-level burden using association; often interpreted causally, but causal interpretation is not necessarily justified |
| Predictive | Do current BPA exposure levels help predict future rates of obesity? | Can pharmacy dispensing data help forecast future opioid overdose clusters? | Time-based correlation useful for forecasting, not causal inference |
| Interventional | Would banning BPA reduce cancer risk? | Would restricting high-dose opioid prescriptions through pharmacy monitoring programs reduce overdose deaths? | Action-oriented, policy-relevant, counterfactual reasoning |
| Counterfactual (retrospective) | Would I have avoided a heart attack if I had been male? | Would this patient have avoided overdose if her prescription had been denied at the pharmacy? | Individual-level hypothetical; not always tied to a feasible or ethical intervention |
| Mechanistic/ Explanatory | How does BPA interact with estrogen receptors? | How do high-dose opioid regimens affect neurobiological pathways involved in addiction? | Seeks understanding of physiological or pharmacological mechanisms |


concern the expected consequences of real or imagined actions such as reducing benzene exposure or monitoring prescriptions. This decision-relevant view of causality-known as interventional or manipulable causation-asks whether and how deliberately changing one variable would change the distribution of another (Pearl 2009). While some scientific inquiries focus on mechanistic or explanatory causation (e.g. how or why changes propagate through a complex system of causally linked variables) or ask about how outcomes would have been different under different conditions (Potential Outcomes models applied to counterfactual worlds, as discussed later in this section; see also Table 2), this paper focuses on causal questions that are explicitly interventional, and hence relevant to practical decision-making: What would happen to risk if we were to intervene to change exposure or other conditions?

The interventional view of causation is foundational for much of modern causal inference and for many regulatory, legal, and social policy decisions (Glass et al. 2013), but it imposes stringent requirements (Schwartz and Prins 2024). Before one can estimate a causal effect from observational data, it is first necessary to define the effect being estimated and the conditions under which it is identifiable and interpretable. Many published studies blur these distinctions. They use causal language for associations, adjust for covariates without clarifying what depends on what, or select statistical models without specifying a precise causal question to be addressed (Hernán and Robins 2020). A valid causal analysis must begin with conceptual clarity. While many frameworks for causal inference exist-including narrative or philosophical ones-only those that offer testable, logically coherent criteria for distinguishing causal from non-causal claims can support valid causal inference. Without such formal structure, methodological pluralism risks degenerating into ambiguity and interpretive inconsistency (Hernán 2016). Although the specific tools discussed later in this paper-such as causal diagrams, instrumental variables, g-methods, or machine-learning-based estimators-differ in implementation and assumptions, they can be viewed as complementary rather
than competing approaches, each offering a different way of attempting to satisfy the same underlying necessary conditions for valid interventional causal inference.

### 3.1. What kind of causation?

The phrase " $X$ causes $Y$ " is deceptively simple. It can refer to multiple types of causal claims, each with distinct implications for actions-and with different evidentiary requirements. This section distinguishes between common types of causal statements found in scientific and policy discourse, and then relates them to three levels of causal reasoning often referred to as Pearl's hierarchy or Pearl's "ladder of causation" (Pearl 2018; Bareinboim et al. 2022).

Not all causal claims are created equal. Some are descriptive summaries of patterns in data; others are attempts to explain mechanisms, predict future outcomes, or quantify how much of a burden can be attributed to an exposure. Still others-like the ones this paper focuses on-ask what would happen if we changed policies or tried something new. The language of causation is often used interchangeably across these contexts, but the logic and assumptions behind them differ. To clarify what kind of claim is being made in any given instance, we distinguish the following six common types of causal claims found in scientific and policy settings:

- Descriptive/Associational causation: Claims such as " $X$ is associated with $Y$ " or " $Y$ is more common in populations exposed to $\mathrm{X}^{\prime \prime}$ summarize observed correlations, typically without asserting a causal mechanism or direction. Historically, such claims have formed the starting point for much of epidemiology and public health-but too often, they've also been treated as the end point, with observed statistically significant associations reported and interpreted as if they implied causation, a phenomenon that has been dubbed the observational interpretation fallacy (D'Amico et al. 2025).
- Attributive/statistical causation: Burden-of-disease claims such as " $X$ accounts for $30 \%$ of $Y$ cases" may summarize
statistical associations - for example, via the population attributable fraction (PAF) - but they do not necessarily, and often do not, reflect a manipulable pathway or interventional causation. The PAF can be interpreted as a summary of excess risk in a population, yet its causal interpretation rests on strong assumptions (e.g. no confounding, proper exposure definition, identifiability of an appropriate intervention), which are frequently not met in practice (Rockhill et al. 1998; Levine 2007).
- Predictive (e.g. Granger) causation: $X$ is said to Grangercause $Y$ if the future values of $Y$ are not conditionally independent of the past values of $X$, given the past values of $Y$. In other words, knowing the past of $X$ improves predictions of $Y$ 's future beyond what can be inferred from $Y$ 's own history. This type of causation supports forecasting but it does not imply that manipulating $X$ would change $Y$; it reflects temporal predictability, not necessarily an interventional causal mechanism or pathway.
- Interventional causation: The central focus of this paper, it asks: Would setting $X$ to a different value change the distribution of $Y$, all else equal?
- Counterfactual causation: Concerned with hypothetical scenarios (e.g. "Would I still have gotten sick if I had not gone to the concert?"), which may or may not involve feasible interventions.
- Mechanistic causation: $X$ leads to $Y$ through a known physical or biological process, such as inflammation → DNA damage → cancer. Mechanistic causal claims (e.g. "this protein triggers that response") supports explanation, but may not correspond to feasible interventions.

These distinctions matter. Nicotine-stained fingers may Granger-cause lung cancer (by predicting future diagnoses), yet have no mechanistic role in tumorigenesis. But if stained fingers are a reliable proxy for smoking, and smoking causes cancer, then stained fingers might even function as an interventional cause-if intervening to keep fingers unstained requires reducing smoking. The meaning and truth of any causal claim thus hinges on which concept of causation is invoked.

### 3.1.1. Example: Conflating attribution (burden of disease) and interventional causation

Unfortunately, the distinct causal concepts introduced above are often conflated in both scientific studies and media reports, leading to influential articles and news stories that assert far more about the health benefits of hypothetical interventions than the supporting analyses can justify. A representative example is the claim that "Nearly a quarter of global disease burden could be prevented by reducing environmental risks" (Prüss-Ustün et al. 2017.) That is an explicitly interventional conclusion about what would happen if we changed environmental risks. But the result adduced to support it is attributive: "Twenty-three percent ( $95 \% \mathrm{Cl}$ : 13$34 \%$ ) of global deaths and $22 \%(95 \% \mathrm{Cl}: 13-32 \%)$ of global DALYs were attributable to environmental risks in 2012" (emphasis added). The latter quantities are based on
population-attributable fractions (PAFs)-descriptive summaries of excess risk patterns in a given population/year based on observed relative risk (RR) ratios-not estimates of what an actual, well-defined intervention would achieve. Conflating the two turns a statistical partition of observed disease into a prediction about preventable disease, without the identification work required to justify that leap.

The interventional causal conclusion is not warranted by the population attributable fraction (PAF) calculations (and Burden of Disease calculations based on the PAFs) that are presented to support it for at least the following reasons (Greenland and Robins 1988; Rockhill et al. 1998; von Cube et al. 2020; Cox 2021).

1. A PAF is typically computed from associations (relative risk ratios) under an idealized contrast (often, set exposure to a "theoretical minimum risk" everywhere). It does not define a feasible policy (what to change, by how much, when, for whom), nor the conditions that must hold for the PAF to approximate that policy's effect. Without a well-defined intervention and the usual conditions (discussed later in this section) that connect a hypothetical policy to what we can learn from data (for example, that the policy is precisely stated and that the data contain comparable cases for the policy changes), "attributable" is not "preventable."
2. Confounding and model dependence. PAFs are functions of exposure prevalence and estimated relative risks (RRs). If those RRs are confounded, misspecified, estimated with error or do not generalize to the target setting, the PAF inherits the bias and may lack a valid causal interpretation. Even with a specified intervention, estimating what a policy would do requires (i) accounting for all relevant differences between exposed and unexposed groups, (ii) adjusting for them correctly, and (iii) handling delays between exposure and outcome-none of which is established by a PAF calculation.
3. Joint and overlapping causes. "Environmental risks" aggregate many correlated, interacting exposures. PAFs for multiple risks need not sum to $100 \%$ and can "double count" shared causal pathways. Setting all listed risks to their minima simultaneously is rarely feasible and may change other determinants (substitution, compensation), undermining an "all-else-equal" reading.
4. Static snapshot vs. dynamic policy path. The cited PAFs describe 2012 cross-sectional patterns. Interventional claims concern future trajectories under policy, which depend on lags, adaptation, compliance, and system feedbacks-none addressed by cross-sectional PAFs. When exposure status changes over time, time-updated versions of the PAF (for changing exposures) show that any attributable share is defined relative to a specific, time-indexed hypothetical intervention (e.g. preventing exposure at different times), not a generic "removal"and it still requires additional assumptions to turn that hypothetical into a credible prediction about outcomes (von Cube et al. 2020).
5. Different causal questions. Attributive analyses ask "How much of observed disease is statistically assigned to
exposure X?" Interventional analyses ask "What would change if we set $X$ differently?" These are different prob-lems-"causes of effects " versus "effects of causes" (explanation of observed cases vs. prediction under a change)-and require different identification strategies.

Concrete case studies have shown that association-based attributions have repeatedly failed to predict correctly the effects of real-world interventions, underscoring the need for interventional analysis (Cox 2021).

In short, PAF-based "burden of disease" summaries are not, by themselves, evidence that removing or reducing the attributed factors would prevent the same fraction of deaths or DALYs. Reading them that way substitutes associational for interventional causation. The framework of modern causal analysis is designed precisely to keep those notions separate and to require the additional conditions-well-defined interventions, causal identification, and robustness checks-before translating statistical attribution into policy-relevant effect estimates.

To illustrate how different types of causal questions arise across distinct public health contexts, Table 2 presents examples from two policy-relevant domains: (i) chemical expo-sures-specifically, bisphenol A (BPA), an endocrinedisrupting compound commonly found in plastics and consumer goods-and (ii) pharmacy practices and the opioid overdose epidemic. It highlights how similar forms of causal language recur across settings, while underscoring how their implications for evidentiary requirements, interpretation of risks, and decision relevance depend on the type of causal claim being made.

Causal inference for policy, law, and public health typically focuses on interventional contrasts: Would setting a variable to a different value (e.g. lowering exposure, changing policy) lead to a change in outcomes? Addressing such questions demands a well-defined intervention-at least in principlethat specifies what it would mean to set a variable to a different value. In the Potential Outcomes framework discussed later, this requirement is formalized as the need for a well-
defined exposure ensuring that each unit under study can, at least hypothetically, be assigned to a clearly specified treatment condition (Hernán and Robins 2016). Other causal frameworks, such as Structural Causal Model (SCM) framework introduced later in this section (Pearl 2009), also require interventions to be formally expressible within the model, although they may accommodate a broader range of hypothetical or structural manipulations that are not necessarily implementable in practice (Pearl 2018).

Pearl (2018) depicts interventional causation as the middle rung of a three-level causal hierarchy or "ladder" (Bareinboim et al. 2022). To clarify how different types of causal reasoning relate to the assumptions and methods required to support them, Table 3 summarizes the three levels of Pearl's ladder of causation alongside typical questions that each level addresses, the analytic methods commonly used, and the core assumptions those methods rely on. Understanding where a given claim sits on this ladder is essential for evaluating its evidentiary requirements and decision relevance. Causal reasoning at the top (Counterfactual) level includes questions like, "Would I have had a heart attack if I had been born the opposite sex?" Such retrospective counterfactuals may not correspond to any feasible intervention, but Pearl argues that they are still meaningful and computable within a structural causal model (Pearl 2018, 3).

The rest of this paper focuses on interventional causation because is most relevant for predicting what would happen under hypothetical or real-world interventions-and hence, for informing practical decisions. But other forms of causal reasoning may serve important roles in science and policy and should not be dismissed or conflated with interventional causal claims.

### 3.2. From variables to effects: causal contrasts and estimands

A key shift in moving from statistical to causal analysis is from correlating variables to identifying effects. Saying "Exposure to $X$ causes increased risk of health effect $Y$ " is

Table 3. Pearl's ladder of causation-levels, questions, methods, assumptions, and pitfalls.
| Level | Typical questions | Common methods (explained later) | Key assumptions (explained later) | Common pitfalls (explained later) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1. Association | Is exposure $X$ associated with outcome $Y$ ? <br> What patterns exist in the data? | Regression, correlation, machine learning, unsupervised clustering | Associations provide information about causation-rather than about confounding, modeling assumptions and choices, etc. | Misinterpreting strength of association or predictive accuracy as causal influence; not correcting for omitted variable bias, selection bias, overfitting, residual confounding, multiple testing bias, model specification error, measurement error, etc. |
| 2. Intervention | How would $Y$ change if we set $X$ to $x$ ? <br> Does treatment $X$ reduce risk of outcome $Y$ ? | Randomized controlled trials, propensity score methods, G-computation, IPTW, target trial emulation | Exchangeability, consistency, positivity, well-defined intervention | Poorly specified interventions; unmeasured confounding; time zero ambiguity |
| 3. Counterfactuals | What would have happened to $Y$ if $X$ had been different, contrary to fact? <br> Would this patient have recovered if we had used treatment $B$ instead of $A$ ? | Structural causal models (SCMs), potential outcomes framework, mediation analysis, g-formula, double machine learning | Structural assumptions; model completeness; cross-world independence (e.g. ignorability across potential outcomes) | Misinterpreting individuallevel effects; assuming identifiability from associational data |


incomplete without specifying: Compared to what? In whom? Under what conditions? Over what time frame? The key idea developed in this subsection and used throughout the rest of the paper is that a causal effect is a contrast: the difference in outcomes (or in their probability distributions) that would occur if an exposure (or other cause) were set to one level versus another, under otherwise identical conditions. Different causal frameworks formalize this contrast in different ways, but all agree that causal effects reflect the differences that the cause makes in the effect by being at one level rather than another.

In the Potential Outcomes (PO) framework, discussed in the next section, effects are defined in terms of counterfactual out-comes-that is, outcomes that would occur under different, hypothetical exposure scenarios for the same unit. The standard notation expresses these as $Y(1)$ and $Y(0)$, where:

- $\quad Y(1)$ is the outcome that would be observed if the unit were exposed (e.g. received treatment),
- $\quad Y(0)$ is the outcome that would be observed if the same unit were not exposed (e.g. received no treatment).

Since only one of these two outcomes can ever be observed for any given unit, causal inference in the PO framework requires reasoning about the unobserved counterfactual outcome-what would have happened under the alternative exposure condition. This limitation is commonly referred to as
the Fundamental Problem of Causal Inference (FPCI) (Rubin 1974; Holland 1986). (However, some authors argue that this so-called "fundamental problem" is not unique to causal inference. Fernández-Loría (2025) proposes that causal inference is best understood as a prediction problem in which observed outcomes are biased by treatment assignment-meaning we see only one potential outcome per person, and the assignment itself depends on factors that also influence the outcome. From this perspective, the missing counterfactual is not a fundamentally different challenge from other prediction problems, but rather a special case of a more general problem: making predictions in domains where labels are selectively observed and sampling is biased. This reframing connects causal inference directly to well-established techniques in predictive modeling, such as reweighting, domain adaptation, and covariate shift correction.)

The PO framework leads to formal definitions of causal estimands-the specific quantities we aim to estimate to answer causal questions. Each estimand answers a different causal question and rests on different assumptions. Table 4 lists several common estimands (VanderWeele 2016; Huntington-Klein 2021; Rijnhart et al. 2021). Two especially widely used ones are the Average Treatment Effect (ATE) in a population and the Conditional Average Treatment Effect (CATE) for units with a specified covariate profile (i.e. CATE at $Z=z$ captures effect heterogeneity for different values, z , of covariate vector $Z$.

Table 4. Definitions of some common causal estimands (causal contrasts to be estimated).
| Estimand (abbr.) | Formula | Meaning, notation, notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Average treatment effect (ATE) | ATE $=E[Y(1)-Y(0)]$ | Average outcome change if everyone were exposed ( $X=1$ ) vs. if no one were exposed $(X=0)$. |
| Average treatment effect on the treated (ATT) | $\mathrm{ATT}=\mathrm{E}[\mathrm{Y}(1)-\mathrm{Y}(0) \mid \mathrm{X}=1]$ | Average effect among those who actually received the exposure ( $\mathrm{X}=1$ ). |
| Conditional average treatment effect (CATE) | $\operatorname{CATE}(\mathrm{z})=\mathrm{E}[\mathrm{Y}(1)-\mathrm{Y}(0) \mid \mathrm{Z}=\mathrm{z}]$ | Effect for units with covariate profile $Z=z$ (captures effect heterogeneity). |
| Local average treatment effect (LATE) | $\mathrm{LATE}=\mathrm{E}[\mathrm{Y}(1)-\mathrm{Y}(0) \mid$ complier w.r.t. V] | Effect among "compliers" (units whose X is shifted by a valid instrument V); identified under IV assumptions (relevance, independence, exclusion; monotonicity sometimes). |
| Marginal treatment effect (MTE) | $\operatorname{MTE}(\mathrm{u})=\mathrm{E}[\mathrm{Y}(1)-\mathrm{Y}(0) \mid$ selection index u$]$ | Effect at the margin of selecting $X=1 \mathrm{vs} . X=0$; u indexes resistance/propensity. Useful for policy targeting and selection models. |
| Variance-weighted ATE | Weighted average of subgroup ATEs; weights sum to 1 . | Summary that gives more influence to more variable or more informative subgroups; weights are an analytic choice. |
| Controlled direct effect (CDE) | $\operatorname{CDE}\left(x\right.$ vs $\left.x^{*}, m\right)=E\left[Y(x, m)-Y\left(x^{*}, m\right)\right]$ | Effect of changing $X$ from $x^{*}$ to $x$ while holding mediator M fixed at m (isolates the nonmediated pathway). |
| Pure natural direct effect (PNDE) | PNDE $=E\left[Y\left(x, M\left(x^{*}\right)\right)-Y\left(x^{*}, M\left(x^{*}\right)\right)\right]$ | Direct effect of changing $X\left(x^{*} \rightarrow x\right)$ while keeping M at the level it would naturally take under $\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{x}^{*}$. |
| Total natural direct effect (TNDE) | TNDE $=E\left[Y(x, M(x))-Y\left(x^{*}, M(x)\right)\right]$ | Direct effect of changing $X\left(x^{*} \rightarrow x\right)$ while keeping M at the level it would naturally take under $\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{x}$. |
| Pure natural indirect effect (PNIE) | PNIE $=E\left[Y\left(x^{*}, M(x)\right)-Y\left(x^{*}, M\left(x^{*}\right)\right)\right]$ | Indirect (mediated) effect of changing $M$ from its $\mathrm{x}^{*}$ level to its x level while holding X fixed at $\mathrm{x}^{*}$. |
| Total natural indirect effect (TNIE) | TNIE $=E\left[Y(x, M(x))-Y\left(x, M\left(x^{*}\right)\right)\right]$ | Indirect (mediated) effect with exposure held at x (mirror of PNIE). |
| Total effect (TE) | $T E=E\left[Y(x)-Y\left(x^{*}\right)\right]$ | Overall effect of changing exposure from $x^{*}$ to $x$ with M free to vary as it naturally would under each exposure level. |


Notation: $\mathrm{X}=$ exposure (or "treatment"), $\mathrm{Y}=$ outcome, $\mathrm{Z}=$ covariate vector, $\mathrm{M}=$ mediator. Specific values of these variables are denoted by lower-case letters: $\mathrm{x}, \mathrm{y}, \mathrm{z}, \mathrm{m}$.

When exposure $X$ is not binary (e.g. continuous or multilevel), the natural population-level overall contrast is the Total Effect (TE) comparing two levels x and $\mathrm{x}^{*}$, written TE $= \mathrm{E}\left[Y(x)-Y\left(x^{*}\right)\right]$; for a binary exposure with $x=1$ and $x^{*}=0$, TE reduces to the ATE.

In mediation analysis (VanderWeele, 2016; Rijnhart et al. 2021), the total effect can be decomposed into direct effects (not transmitted through a specified mediator $M$ ) and indirect effects (transmitted through $M$ ). These "natural" effects quantify how much of the overall impact operates along a pathway such as $X \rightarrow M \rightarrow Y$ versus directly from $X \rightarrow Y$. The definitions compare counterfactual outcomes under different combinations of exposure and mediator values, some of which are not jointly observable. Interpretation of mediated effects requires additional care. Standard mediation estimands typically assume no exposure-mediator interaction in the effect of the mediator on the outcome; when such interaction is present, alternative estimands or stratified interpretations are required. Moreover, conditioning on a mediator can induce collider stratification bias when there are unmeasured confounders of the mediator-outcome relationship, particularly if those confounders are affected by the exposure. These issues underscore the need for explicit causal diagrams, transparent assumptions, and sensitivity analyses when interpreting mediation results from observational data.

Causal interpretation of these estimands requires assumptions that are often untestable from the observed data alone. Key conditions include (i) consistency (well-defined interventions and no interference-one unit's exposure does not affect another unit's outcome); (ii) exchangeability (no unmeasured confounding of $X-Y$ and $X-M$, and-conditional on $X$ and $Z$-no unmeasured confounding of $M-Y$; and (iii) positivity (adequate overlap in covariate distributions between exposed and unexposed groups-meaning that, for all covariate patterns of interest, there is a non-zero probability of observing each exposure level-so that causal contrasts are supported by observed data). For natural direct/indirect effects, one also needs that there are no mediator-outcome confounders created by the exposure (VanderWeele 2016; Rijnhart et al. 2021). Because these assumptions are strong and typically unverifiable, transparent reporting and sensitivity analyses are essential when interpreting mediated effects from observational studies.

More specifically, identification of natural direct and indirect effects from observational data relies on the cross-world assumption that, after conditioning on appropriate baseline covariates, the outcome we would see if we set the exposure to one level and set the mediator to a particular value does not depend on the value the mediator would have taken under a different exposure level. This assumption supple-ments-rather than replaces-the assumptions of no unmeasured confounding of the exposure-outcome $(X-Y)$, exposure-mediator, and (given $X$ and $Z$ ) mediator-outcome relations. It is called "cross-world" because it links two incompatible counterfactual scenarios-one fixing the exposure at one level while referencing the mediator value that would arise under a different exposure level-which can never be jointly observed. Practically, it rules out variables that are (a) caused by the exposure and (b) influence both the mediator
and the outcome (often called exposure-induced mediatoroutcome confounders). Interventional direct/indirect effects avoid the cross-world assumption by imagining we randomize the mediator to the distribution it would have under each exposure level-answering a practical, policy-relevant "what if we shift $M$ this way?" question rather than splitting effects along a natural pathway (Vansteelandt and Daniel 2017).

In many real-world settings, an exposure creates downstream conditions that in turn influence both the mediator and the outcome. Let $L$ denote such an exposure-induced mediator-outcome confounder. Consider day-level air pollution $(X)$, time spent indoors or use of filtration ( $M$ ), and respiratory emergency visits ( $Y$ ). High-pollution days may trigger public alerts or institutional responses ( $L$ ) that both increase staying indoors or use of air filtration ( $M$ ) and also change care-seeking or medication use, directly affecting $Y$. Because $L$ is caused by $X$ and affects both $M$ and $Y$, the medi-ator-outcome relation is confounded downstream of the exposure, and natural direct/indirect effects are not identified from the observed data without additional assumptions. Similarly, for opioid policy, tighter prescribing controls ( $X$ ) may shift use toward illicit opioids ( $M$ ), while simultaneously altering enforcement intensity or toxicity ( $L$ ), each of which can independently change overdose risk ( $Y$ ). Here, $L$ is again caused by $X$ and influences both $M$ and $Y$, violating the cross-world condition needed to identify natural effects.

When exposure-induced mediator-outcome confounders exist, natural effects are not uniquely determined by the observed data (i.e. identifiable) without additional, typically untestable, assumptions. For example, if the exposure changes an intermediate factor (such as clinical surveillance, resource allocation, or job status) that in turn affects both the mediator and the outcome, that factor confounds the mediator-outcome relation and violates the cross-world assumption. Common additional assumptions that are introduced to help restore identifiability (meaning that the causal effect of interest is uniquely determined by the joint distribution of the observed variables under stated assumptions) include the following. We simply list them here with very brief descriptions and references, leaving fuller discussion and explanation for the following sections and the their references. This list provides a quick preview-albeit admittedly a jargon-laden one-of many of the technical issues and methods discussed later.

- Parametric/functional-form assumptions. These assume correctly specified models for treatment, mediator, and outcome (for example, linear or logit links, additivity, no interactions, omoscedastic, independent errors). Even small misspecifications can substantially change naturaleffect estimates (Imai and Yamamoto 2010; Valeri and VanderWeele 2013).
- Rank preservation or constant-effects assumptions. These assume that each person's outcome under one mediator level is a fixed shift of the outcome under another level (that is, there is no heterogeneity in mediator effects). This links counterfactual worlds, but is rarely defensible
in practice (Robins and Tsiatis 1991; Hernán and Robins 2020).
- Monotonicity constraints. These assume that the exposure moves the mediator in only one direction for everyone (or that the mediator affects the outcome in only one direction), which can aid identification or tighten bounds but is typically unverified (Imbens and Angrist 1994; Angrist et al. 1996; Tchetgen Tchetgen and VanderWeele 2014).
- Instrumental-variable (IV) mediation. This approach uses a variable ("instrument") that affects the mediator ("relevance"), is not related to hidden factors that affect both mediator and outcome ("independence"), and does not affect the outcome except through the mediator ("exclusion"). Investigators sometimes also assume monotonicity (the instrument moves the mediator in the same direction for everyone-no "defiers"). Because good instruments are hard to find, many applications instead rely on-or complement with-methods that assume all important confounders have been measured ("sequential ignorability," i.e. no unmeasured confounding given observed covariates) and then estimate effects in a way that stays valid if at least one of several working models is correct ("multiply robust" estimation). These approaches also encourage explicit sensitivity analyses when the no-unmeasured-confounding assumption might be doubtful (Tchetgen and Shpitser 2012; Imai et al. 2013). Sections 4 and 5 explain these concepts and IV study designs and data analyses further.
- Exclusion restrictions assume that some pathway is absent (for example, that the exposure affects the outcome only through the mediator and not directly, or that an instrument affects the mediator but has no direct effect on the outcome). These assumptions are strong, and violations bias results (Angrist et al. 1996).
- Principal stratification assumptions. These make statements about outcomes within latent (i.e. unobserved) mediator "types" (what the mediator would be under each exposure level), such as monotonicity or no direct effect within strata. Because the strata are unobserved the assumptions are untestable (Frangakis and Rubin 2002; VanderWeele 2011).
- Transportability/stability assumptions. These assume that mediator or outcome models developed in another setting (for example, a trial or validation study) apply in the target setting (Cole and Stuart 2010; Pearl and Bareinboim 2014).
- Positivity/extrapolation. These assume sufficient overlap in mediator values across exposure levels given covariates; when overlap is limited, identification relies on modelbased extrapolation, which is another strong and typically untestable step (Petersen et al. 2012; Westreich and Cole 2010).
- Measurement assumptions. These assume that the mediator and confounders are measured without error, or that the error model is known and correctly specified; otherwise, identification can fail or hinge on unverifiable corrections (Carroll et al. 2006; VanderWeele et al. 2012; Keogh and White 2014).
- No interference and well-defined interventions. These assume that one unit's exposure or mediator does not affect another unit's outcome and that manipulations of the mediator are coherent and well defined; although often left implicit, they are critical (VanderWeele and Hernán 2013; Hernán and Robins 2020).

Clarity about the causal estimand is essential for causal statements to be meaningful. One cannot interpret " $X$ causes $Y$ " or "Reducing $X$ by $10 \%$ reduces $Y$ by $5 \%$ " without knowing what is being contrasted, for whom, and under what assumptions, since the same causal claim might be true for some causal estimands and false for others (Igelström et al. 2022; Huntington-Klein 2021). The different estimands in Table 4 are interventional causal contrasts, not merely statistical associations. Each is suited to a different policy or decision context: ATE informs broad policy impacts, ATT evaluates targeted programs, and CATE supports individualized treatment decisions and assessment of heterogeneity in individual risks.

To illustrate why specifying the estimand clearly is crucial for risk assessment and communication, consider quantifying "the effect" of opioid prescriptions on deaths from opioid overdoses. In the interventional perspective, the effect contrasts the observed mortality rate with the rate that would have occurred under different prescribing practices. But the sign and size of that difference depend on what else is held fixed, and at what levels. Under the total effect, all downstream responses-including shifts to illicit opioids-are allowed; under that contrast, tighter prescribing could increase overdose deaths if some patients move to fentanyl. Under a controlled direct effect that keeps fentanyl use (or substitution) fixed in the comparison scenario, the same change in prescribing could reduce overdose deaths. If an "effect" is estimated and reported but the specific type of effect is not identified, readers cannot tell which question the estimate answers. A parallel example is wildfire risk: a direct effect that holds human ignitions, suppression, and land use fixed might show that warming increases conditions conducive to burning, whereas the total effect that lets those factors change may show smaller increases-or even decreases-in burned area. Clearly defining the estimand prevents claims that talk past each other.

### 3.2.1. Example: Exposure-response curves and unclear estimands

In health risk analysis, unfortunately, it is still common to present exposure-response curves as if their meanings were self-evident (Cox 2023). But such curves answer different causal questions depending on what the modeler holds fixed. In an air pollution-mortality example where air pollution affects both Chronic Obstructive Lung Disease (COPD) and mortality rates, holding COPD prevalence fixed as exposure changes would yield a controlled direct effect curve. Allowing COPD risk or prevalence in the population to change as air pollution level changes would yield a totaleffect curve that combines direct effects with the indirect effect mediated through changes in COPD. These are
typically different curves, which of these two curves is more relevant for quantifying health effects of changes in air pollution depends in part on how quickly COPD risk responds to changes in air pollution. To make exposure-response curves decision-relevant and clearly interpretable, authors should explicitly identify the causal estimand (e.g. total effect vs controlled direct effect), the time horizon, what variables are held fixed (and at what levels) or allowed to vary, and the population or joint covariate distribution over which the curve is averaged. Without this information, users cannot adapt a curve to a target population or to post-intervention states. Clear estimand definitions turn exposure-response plots from ambiguous summaries into transparent answers to explicit causal questions.

In the Structural Causal Model (SCM) framework discussed in the next section, causal effects are defined as contrasts (i.e. differences) between interventional distributions-for example, comparing the distribution of Y under $\operatorname{do}(X=1)$ versus $d o(X=0)$, where the notation " $d o(X=x)$ " (often abbreviated as simply $d o(x)$ ), denotes an intervention that exogenously sets $X$ to the value $x$ (Pearl 2009). When identifiability conditions are satisfied, SCM and PO approaches can yield the same numerical estimands, even though they rest on distinct conceptual foundations. Across all frameworks, the essential insight is that causal effects reflect contrasts (i.e. differences) in outcomes or their distributions under specified interventions, not just patterns passively observed in data. Understanding what those contrasts mean and what is required to estimate them is the central task of causal inference, as developed in the following sections. The answers depend on the causal framework used.

### 3.3. Formal causal frameworks for estimand definition, estimation, and interpretation

Once the causal estimand is specified, the next step is to embed it within a formal causal framework that distinguishes mere associations from genuine causal paths and makes required assumptions explicit. This enables analysts to determine whether the estimand is identifiable from the available data (meaning that it can be estimated from data); to select appropriate estimation strategies for identifiable causal effects or contrasts; and to interpret results in light of the assumptions required for valid inference.

Several such frameworks are commonly used, as reviewed next (Pearl 2009; Huntington-Klein 2021; Schwartz and Prins 2024). All of them provide methods to clearly define the scope and content of causal statements by specifying: (i) the target population and environment(s); (ii) interventions and scenarios used for causal contrasts; (iii) time scales and horizons for responses; and (iv) implicated pathway(s) and mediating variables. A scenario describes what is held fixed and what is allowed to change in the counterfactual world used to define an estimand. A baseline scenario is the reference state or trajectory under "status-quo/no-policy" conditions against which contrasts are drawn.

To help orient readers new to formal causal frameworks, Table 5 summarizes how the main frameworks introduced in
the rest of this section differ in their assumptions, identification logic, empirical checks, and the kinds of questions they answer. The aim is to make it easier to see which tool fits which causal questions and what must be shown for deci-sion-relevant inference.
