The frameworks-directed acyclic graphs (DAGs), potential outcomes (PO), structural causal models (SCMs), system dynamics, time-indexed causal models, and information-theoretic diagnostics-are largely complementary tools rather than competing paradigms. They differ in scope and emphasis: DAGs foreground assumptions about causal structure and valid adjustment sets; PO models emphasize counterfactual contrasts and estimands; SCMs formalize causal mechanisms via structural assignments and support identification through do-calculus; and dynamic or information-theoretic approaches probe temporal structure, feedback, and invariance.

Importantly, these frameworks are often used together in practice. DAGs are widely employed to clarify assumptions within both PO and SCM analyses, even though SCMs need not be represented graphically. Likewise, front-door and back-door criteria arise from SCM identification theory but are commonly applied using DAGs as visual and computational aids. Table 5 organizes these frameworks by their primary inferential roles-assumption articulation, identification logic, empirical diagnostics, and estimation-rather than implying sharp boundaries or exclusivity among them.

### 3.3.1. Directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) and causal Bayesian networks

DAGs are graphical representations of causal structure (i.e. causal dependencies) in which nodes represent variables and arrows (directed edges) indicate direct causal influences (Pearl 2009). The graph is acyclic, meaning it contains no feedback loops. Figure 2 illustrates a simplified DAG model of opioid-associated mortality (Inoue et al. 2022). Chronic pain $(X)$ is drawn as a direct cause of opioid use ( $M$ ), which in turn affects mortality $(Y)$. The tolox $(C)$ denotes a vector of measured covariates-e.g. age, sex, race/ethnicity, education, poverty-income ratio, insurance, marital status, smoking, alcohol intake, and antidepressant use-that may affect $X, M$, and $Y$. The bottom box ( $U$ ) represents unmeasured factors (e.g. disease severity or psychosocial factors) that may also act on these variables. The arrows from $C$ and $U$ into multiple nodes depict how these factors may confound observed associations, creating "backdoor paths" (non-causal sources of association) that must be addressed in analysis (Pearl 2009; Huntington-Klein 2021). Interpreting the DAG in this way makes explicit which assumptions are being made about causal structure. It also shows why careful adjustment for confounders is required to estimate the causal effect of opioid use on mortality. In short, the DAG diagram provides a visual map of the assumed data-generating process, clarifying which associations reflect causal hypotheses and which may reflect biasing pathways.

Two points help interpret DAGs. First, the drawn arrows encode the causal pathways that the analyst believes or assumes are present (e.g. $X \rightarrow M \rightarrow Y$ ). Second, the missing arrows are just as informative. For example, the DAG in

Table 5. Quick map of formal frameworks for interventional causal inference.
| Framework | What it is/role | Identification hinges on | Empirical checks | Natural outputs | When it's especially useful |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| DAGs | Graphs that encode assumed causal structure; guide valid adjustment and identify paths | Correct graph (no omitted key parents), valid adjustment set, positivity. | d-separation ⇒ testable conditional independences; negative controls; DAG-guided falsification. | Identifiable effects; minimal adjustment sets; path/blocking logic. | Clarifying confounding, mediators/colliders; pre-analysis design choices; transparency of assumptions. |
| Potential outcomes (PO) | Defines effects as contrasts of potential outcomes; design/ estimation focus (RCTs, target-trial emulation, gmethods). | Exchangeability (no hidden confounders after conditioning on observed ones) consistency (welldefined interventions), positivity. | Balance diagnostics; falsification outcomes; sensitivity to unmeasured confounding; overlap checks. | Average treatment effect (ATE); Conditional ATE (CATE); direct, indirect, and total effects; dynamic treatment-regime effects | Emulating trials with observational data; policy evaluation where interventions are well specified. |
| Structural causal models (SCMs) | Structural equations + dooperator; DAG is the graph of the structure; supports counterfactuals. | Correct structural form; independence of exogenous terms; docalculus identifiability. | Conditional independence tests from the graph; invariance across environments (e.g. ICP); front-/back-door verifications. | $\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{Y} \mid \operatorname{do}(\mathrm{X}=\mathrm{x})$, pathspecific effects; counterfactual queries. | Mechanism-based reasoning; combining identification with testable invariance and transportability properties. |
| System Dynamics (SD) | Stock-and-flow, feedback, delays (often ODE/SDE); dynamic, mechanistic SCMs. | Structural adequacy for the horizon; calibrated parameters; c | Dimensional and flow conservation checks; behavior reproduction across series; out-of-sample and stress tests; sensitivity and uncertainty analyses. | Time-path contrasts (level, slope, peaks, cumulative outcomes) under explicit policies. | Transient dynamics, feedbacks, and unintended consequences dominate decisions (e.g. epidemic or drug markets). |
| Time-indexed causal models (panels/ITS/ DiD/synthetic control) | Uses intervention timing and trajectories to estimate level/slope/ cumulative effects. | Credible counterfactual path (pre-trend comparability), no anticipatory shifts, treatment timing exogeneity, limited spillovers. | Pretrend and placebo tests; cohort-by-startdate profiles; donorpool and window robustness; shock controls. | Immediate and dynamic effects; cumulative differences over chosen horizons. | Policy rollouts with staggered adoption; place- or person-level series where mechanisms are not fully known. |
| Information-theoretic/ Discovery (Granger, transfer entropy, invariance/ICP) | Diagnostics for directionality and stability across environments; structure learning. | Correct embedding/ conditioning; sufficient environments; no hidden drivers (or controlled via conditioning). | Tests of predictive asymmetry, TE/ Granger; invariance tests across contexts; discovery algorithms (PAG/FCI). | Candidate causal directions; stable predictor sets; bounds/constraints on models. | Screening, probing necessary conditions, guiding model specificationcomplements (not replaces) interventional frameworks. |


Figure 2 allows unmeasured confounding between $X$ and $Y$ (as indicated by the arrows $U \rightarrow X$ and $U \rightarrow Y$ but it also assumes there are no unmeasured common causes of $X$ and $M$ or of $M$ and $Y$ once $C$ is conditioned (as indicated by the absence of any unmeasured parent into $M$. This visually encodes the assumption that the $X-M$ and $M-Y$ relations are unconfounded once $C$ and $X$ are included.) Stating these omissions visibly reminds readers that estimation depends on what is assumed absent as well as what is assumed present. In short, the DAG makes the data-generating story and the required adjustment strategy explicit, clarifying which observed associations are plausibly causal and which may be biasing pathways (Pearl 2009; Huntington-Klein 2021).

A DAG implies a set of conditional independence (Cl) relationships among variables-statements about which variables are statistically independent of others, given a set of conditioning variables. For example, in the DAG $X \rightarrow M \rightarrow Y$, the output variable $Y$ (e.g. an indicator of an adverse health effect) is conditionally independent of the input variable $X$ (e.g. exposure) given the value of the mediator $M$ (e.g. internal dose). The Cl implications of a DAG are empirically
testable, in the sense that statistical tests for conditional independence can be applied to test whether they are consistent with observed data (e.g. are $Y$ values indeed independent of $X$ values within any given level of $M$ ?) These Cl implications are typically assessed (using $d$-separation a graphical criterion for determining whether conditioning on a set of variables renders two other variables independent). They can be computed automatically from a DAG using DAG software packages such as dagitty (Textor et al. 2016).
3.3.1.1. Example: Calculating conditional independence relationships using the dagitty package. The function impliedConditionallndependencies() in the dagitty package in R lists the Cl relations implied by a DAG. For the DAG in Figure 2, the following code constructs the graph and prints its implied CIs (treating U as observed for illustration):

```
library(dagitty);
$\mathrm{g}<-$ dagitty('dag \{ $\mathrm{X}->\mathrm{M} ; \mathrm{M}->\mathrm{Y} ; \mathrm{C}->\mathrm{X} ; \mathrm{C}->\mathrm{M} ; \mathrm{C}->\mathrm{Y} ; \mathrm{U}->\mathrm{X} ; \mathrm{U}->\mathrm{Y}$
\}') \# Creates the DAG model;
print(impliedConditionallndependencies(g)) \# Shows the Cl list;
```


## Mediation DAG: Chronic pain $(\mathrm{X}) \rightarrow$ Opioid Prescriptions $(\mathrm{M}) \rightarrow$ Mortality $(\mathrm{Y})$

![](https://cdn.mathpix.com/cropped/9cf8a422-856b-41dc-8da7-b268614f2772-020.jpg?height=647&width=1144&top_left_y=320&top_left_x=496)
Figure 2. A directed acyclic graph (DAG) for drivers of opioid-associated mortality. Caption: Boxes represent variables; arrows indicate hypothesized direct causal influences. C = measured covariates (e.g. age, sex, race/ethnicity, education, poverty-income ratio, insurance, marital status, smoking, alcohol intake, antidepressant use); $\mathrm{U}=$ unmeasured confounders. The structure highlights $\mathrm{X} \rightarrow \mathrm{M} \rightarrow \mathrm{Y}$ while allowing U to confound $\mathrm{X}-\mathrm{Y}$; by design, no uncontrolled confounding is assumed for $\mathrm{X} \rightarrow \mathrm{M}$ or $\mathrm{M} \rightarrow \mathrm{Y}$ after conditioning on C . This DAG is adapted from Inoue et al. 2022.

This returns the following CI relationships (using the symbol " ⟂ " to stand for "is conditionally independent of" and the symbol "|" stands for "given" or "conditioned on"):

```
$\mathrm{C} \perp \mathrm{U}$
$\mathrm{M} \perp \mathrm{U} \mid \mathrm{C}, \mathrm{X}$
$\mathrm{X} \perp \mathrm{Y} \mid \mathrm{C}, \mathrm{M}, \mathrm{U}$
```

In words, C is (unconditionally) independent of U (since no conditioning symbol " $\mid$ " appears in " $\mathrm{C} \perp \mathrm{U}$ "); M is conditionally independent of $U$ given $C$ and $X$; and $X$ is conditionally independent of $Y$ given $C, M$, and $U$ (and hence $Y$ is conditionally independent of $X$ given $C, M$, and $U$, since independence is symmetric). Empirically "testing" a statement like " $\mathrm{A} \perp \mathrm{B} \mid \mathrm{C}$ " means assessing whether the conditional association between A and B , or the ability of B to help predict A (and vice versa), vanishes given C (e.g. via partial-correlation tests for Gaussian data, conditional mutual information for discrete data, or kernel/model-based Cl tests).

DAGs allow researchers to identify confounders (common parents or ancestors of exposure and response), mediators, and colliders (common children or descendants of exposure and response, such as hospitalization), and to determine a minimal sufficient adjustment set-the smallest set of variables that, when conditioned on, blocks all non-causal paths between the exposure and outcome. Blocking a path means that, according to the structure of the graph and the rules of d-separation, conditioning on selected variables eliminates statistical associations transmitted along that path, preventing bias in causal effect estimates. DAGs also clarify the assumed data-generating structure and help identify confounding pathways and bias-inducing structures such as collider stratification and overadjustment (Textor et al. 2016; Lederer et al. 2019; Huntington-Klein 2021). Software tools
like dagitty (Textor et al. 2016) automate the computation of valid adjustment sets and help diagnose identification problems.

For readers interested in the details of computer-aided DAG analysis, once the Figure 2 DAG model g has been defined as in the preceding example, with all variables treated as observed for purposes of illustration, the dagitty command adjustmentSets(g, "X," "Y," effect="direct") returns the output \{ $\mathrm{C}, \mathrm{M}, \mathrm{U}\}$, meaning that estimating the controlled direct effect of $X$ on $Y$ requires conditioning on levels of the variables in $\mathrm{C}, \mathrm{M}$, and U . By contrast, the command adjustmentSets(g, "X," "Y," effect="total") returns \{C, U\}, indicating that estimating the total effect of $X$ on $Y$ requires conditioning on the levels of variables in C and U . If this is not possible, e.g. because variables in U are not observed, then those effects cannot be identified, or uniquely estimated, from the data, although they can still be estimated using assumed levels and sensitivity analyses for the unobserved variables. Alternatively, reports can focus on effects that can be identified from available data, such as the indirect effect of Chronic Pain ( X ) on mortality ( Y ) mediated by Opioid Prescriptions (M) in Figure 2.

Both the Potential Outcomes (PO) framework and Structural Causal Models (SCMs) introduced next make use of DAGs to clarify concepts such as mediation, direct vs. indirect effects, and bias sources. While DAGs are not logically required in every framework, they have become a standard in applied research for articulating and evaluating causal assumptions (Tennant et al. 2021; Bulbulia 2024). Their omission may obscure key structural features of the assumed causal model. As Igelström et al. (2022) note, "DAGs represent theories about causal mechanisms underlying a specific research question."
3.3.1.2. Example: Causal Bayesian network DAG models of adverse outcome pathways (AOPs). Causal Bayesian networks (CBNs) augment a DAG with quantitative information: each node has a conditional probability model-a conditional probability table (CPT) for discrete states, or a conditional density/regression for continuous variables-specifying how that variable depends on its parents. A CBN is a compact, testable model predicting how changes at upstream nodes would shift probability distributions of downstream variables. Interventions have a simple semantics: setting a node by $d o(\cdot)$ corresponds to cutting its incoming arrows (since its value is now set, not determined by its parents) and recomputing downstream probabilities under the modified graph. This makes them a natural fit for representing adverse outcome pathways (AOPs) in toxicology and health risk assessment, where a stressor triggers a molecular initiating event (MIE) that cascades through key events (KEs) to an adverse outcome (AO). With data on some KEs but not others, the CBN can perform forward inference (predict the AO given a stressor or MIE) and diagnostic/backward inference (update beliefs about upstream events given observed downstream changes).

Figure 3, from Moe et al. (2021), shows a simple ecotoxicology AOP CBN for a stressor (DCP) leading to decreased frond number in a test organism. The network encodes two main routes: a mitochondrial path where oxidative phosphorylation (OXPHOS ↓ ) drives electron transport rate (ETR ↓ ) and then the AO, and a chloroplast/oxidative-stress path where reactive oxygen species ( $\operatorname{ROS} \uparrow$ ) affect photosystem II efficiency (Fv/Fm ↓ ) and lipid peroxidation (LPO ↑ ) before converging on the same AO. Each arrow is parameterized by a regression or response-response function (e.g. logistic/ordinal for discrete states or Gaussian for continuous), estimated
from experiments across doses and conditions. The fitted BN then answers decision-relevant questions such as: At a given DCP dose, what is the probability that frond number falls below a threshold? Which KE pathway contributes most to that risk? How much does risk change if an upstream KE is constrained (a hypothetical mitigation)? Because the model is a DAG, it remains transparent about what is assumed present (arrows) and absent (missing arrows), and its conditional-independence implications can be checked against data.

To support transparency and reproducibility, Tennant et al. (2021) recommend that researchers should explicitly present their DAGs, clearly justify their inclusion or exclusion of variables, and describe how the DAG informed their analytic choices. They should clearly state the causal estimands of interest at the outset (e.g. total or direct effects), make the causal diagrams for each estimand available (in the manuscript, supplementary material, or via platforms such as dagitty), and include in these diagrams all relevant variables, including unmeasured confounders, to make assumptions explicit. Omitted arcs should be carefully justified, as they encode strong assumptions of no causal effect. The DAGimplied adjustment set for each estimand should be clearly stated, and estimates based on it should be reported-even if alternative adjustment strategies are also explored. While DAGs are not logically necessary for valid causal inferencethe required assumptions can also be expressed algebraically using structural equations or counterfactual independence statements-they are often practically necessary for clearly specifying, justifying, and communicating those assumptions in applied work.

When some confounders are unmeasured or unmeasurable, Partial Ancestral Graphs (PAGs) provide a generalization of DAGs that can encode possible causal relationships among

![](https://cdn.mathpix.com/cropped/9cf8a422-856b-41dc-8da7-b268614f2772-021.jpg?height=854&width=1429&top_left_y=1617&top_left_x=348)
Figure 3. Adverse outcome pathway represented as a causal Bayesian-network DAG. Panel A is a conceptual model of and AOP. Panel B is an example. Nodes are stressor (DCP), molecular/physiological key events (e.g. OXPHOS ↓ , ROS ↑ , ETR ↓ , Fv/Fm ↓ , LPO ↑ ), and the adverse outcome (frond number $\downarrow$ ); arrows encode hypothesized causal dependencies and are parameterized by response-response models. Source: Adapted from Moe et al. 2021, Integr Environ Assess Manag; PMCID: PMC7820971. Licensed CC BY 4.0.

observed variables even in the presence of hidden confounding (Spirtes et al. 2001). PAGs are produced by causal discovery algorithms such as Fast Causal Inference (FCI), which infer possible causal structures-allowing for the possibility that some associations between variables have no clear causal dir-ection-from observed conditional independence patterns. Although PAGs do not uniquely identify causal effects, they can rule out some causal interpretations, identify testable implications, guide robustness checks, and help bound causal effects.

### 3.3.2. Potential outcomes (PO) counterfactual models

Potential Outcomes (PO) models define causal effects as contrasts (i.e. differences) between potential outcomes-e.g. $Y(1)$ versus $Y(0)$-under assumptions such as the following (Huntington-Klein 2021; Igelström et al. 2022; Duong et al. 2024):
a. Conditional exchangeability: Potential outcomes are conditionally independent of treatment assignment, given observed covariates-that is, once we control for a sufficient set of observed covariates, there are no unmeasured confounders. Causal Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs) can help assess whether this assumption is plausible: they show whether a candidate set of observed covariates blocks all backdoor paths-paths between treatment and outcome whose first edge points into the treatment node-without conditioning on any collider or its descendants. If every backdoor path is blocked and no member of the set is a descendant of the treatment, the set is a valid adjustment set (and it is minimal if no proper subset also suffices). As discussed in the previous subsection, modern DAG software such as dagitty (Textor et al. 2016) can compute such adjustment sets for both total and direct effects in a specified DAG. Conditioning on the variables in a valid adjustment set permits unconfounded estimation of the causal effect of a treatment (or exposure) variable on an outcome variable. However, DAGs assess only the assumed causal structure of confounding-they do not guarantee correct effect estimates, which also depend on factors such as model specification, measurement error, and the positivity assumption.
b. Positivity: For all values of covariates, the probability of receiving each treatment level must be positive. This ensures that all levels of treatment occur with non-zero probability across all covariate strata, so no combinations of treatment and confounders are inherently unobservable. Random non-positivity refers to treatment levels not represented in the data, while deterministic non-positivity refers to theoretically or biologically impossible treatments (e.g. male hysterectomy) (Bulbulia 2024).
c. Consistency: The observed outcome for a unit equals the potential outcome under the treatment that unit actually received. The term "treatment" in this context often includes varying levels of exposure (e.g. to pollutants or workplace risks), not just binary interventions.

To illustrate the use of the PO framework, consider Figure 2, where chronic pain $(X)$ can increase the chance of receiving opioid prescriptions ( $M$ ), and prescriptions can in turn affect mortality within a few years ( $Y$ ). In potential-outcomes terms, the path-specific effect of chronic pain on mortality through (i.e. mediated by) opioid prescribing asks: how much would mortality change if, holding everything else the same, we moved each person's prescribing status from what it would have been without chronic pain to what it would have been with chronic pain, and then let mortality respond to that change in prescribing? Formally, this compares the outcome when $M$ is set to the value it would take under $X=1$ versus $X=0$ while holding $X$ fixed at a reference level, $x$ (either 0 or 1 , depending on the desired estimand). (Symbolically: $E[Y(x,-M(1))]-E[Y(x, M(0))]$. Setting $x=0$ yields the natural indirect effect at the 'no-pain' reference; setting $x=1$ yields its value at the 'pain' reference.) Identification of the effect (estimand) in this simple setting rests on familiar PO conditions applied to the two links of the $X \rightarrow M \rightarrow Y$ chain shown in Figure 2:

1. After adjusting for measured covariates $C$, there are no unmeasured common causes (parents or ancestors) of $X$ and $M$ (i.e. no unmeasured confounding of the $X \rightarrow M$ relation);
2. After adjusting for $X$ and $C$, there are no unmeasured common causes of $M$ and $Y$ (i.e. no unmeasured confounding of the $M \rightarrow Y$ relation given $X$ and $C$ );
3. No mediator-outcome confounder that is itself affected by $X$ (i.e. no exposure-induced mediator-outcome confounding);
4. Positivity (each level of $X$ and of $M$ occurs with non-zero probability within each covariate stratum);
5. Consistency (the outcome we observe under a given prescribing status is the corresponding potential outcome).

Notably, these conditions still allow unmeasured factors to confound $X$ and $Y$ overall; we are isolating only the effect transmitted through prescribing.

A transparent way to estimate this quantity is to use two ordinary prediction models-one for the probability of prescribing given chronic pain and covariates, $P(M \mid X, C)$, and one for mortality given prescribing, chronic pain, and covariates, $P(Y \mid M, X, C)$. We then use these fitted relationships to (i) predict how the distribution of $M$ would change when we switch $X$ from 0 to 1, and (ii) predict the corresponding change in $Y$ that would result from that induced change in $M$, averaging over the observed covariates.

A published analysis of US NHANES data follows this blueprint (Inoue et al. 2022). It first quantified how strongly chronic pain predicts opioid prescribing after adjusting for demographics and health factors, and then how prescribing predicts 3 - and 5 -year all-cause mortality after similar adjustment. Combining those two fitted pieces to isolate the $X \rightarrow M \rightarrow Y$ pathway, the authors reported small but positive path-specific odds-ratio effects of chronic pain on mortality via prescribing (about 1.06 at 3 years and 1.03 at 5 years), alongside an elevated association between prescribing itself
and mortality (about 1.5 at 3 years and 1.3 at 5 years), with multiple sensitivity checks described in the paper.

This example shows how PO language lets us state precisely which question we are answering (here, the indirect effect of $X$ on $Y$ mediated by $M$, i.e. "through prescribing only"), why the answer depends on specific link-level assumptions (for $X \rightarrow M$ and $M \rightarrow Y$ ), and how a simple twomodel workflow can estimate the estimand without requiring the overall $X \rightarrow Y$ relation to be unconfounded. The PO framework defines causal effects as explicit contrasts between potential outcomes and then states the conditions (exchangeability, consistency, positivity) under which those contrasts are identified from observational data. The Structural Causal Models (SCM) viewed discussed next, by contrast, starts from a transparent description of how variables are generated from their "parents" and uses diagram-guided rules (e.g. the frontdoor approach) to translate a policy question into components that can be estimated from observed data-such as how pain predicts prescribing and how prescribing predicts mortality within covariate strata. The two perspectives are compatible, but they emphasize different starting points: PO begins with counterfactual contrasts; SCM begins with a structural data-generating story and leverages that structure for identification.

### 3.3.3. Structural causal models (SCMs)

SCMs describe how variables depend on one another by specifying, for each endogenous variable (i.e. a variable whose value is determined by other variables in the model), a structural equation that defines it as a function of its direct causes (or "parents") and an exogenous (unobserved) disturbance term. These equations represent the underlying causal mechanisms-that is, how outcomes are generated-not merely statistical associations. Combined with a probability distribution over the exogenous (non-endogenous) variables, the system of equations induces a joint (and conditional) distribution over the observed variables. For example, in a simple mediation structure where $X$ affects $Y$ only through a mediator $M(X \rightarrow M \rightarrow Y)$, an SCM model might specify that (a) $X:=U_{x}$; (b) $M:=a \cdot X+U \square$; (c) $Y:=b \cdot M+U_{\gamma \text {. }}$ (Here, " $:=$ " denotes assignment by structural mechanism, not equality or statistical identity. It means "is determined by" or "is generated by" a causal process-not simply "equals." For partial mediation, a direct effect term, e.g. + c ⋅ X, would also appear on the right side; this simplified form is used here for illustration.) Structural equations (a)-(c) express each left-side variable as a function of its direct causes and an independent exogenous random variable ( $U_{x}, U \square, U_{\gamma}$ ) on the right side, with $a$ and $b$ representing causal coefficients. ("Exogenous" simply means that the value of a variable is determined from outside the model, rather endogenously, i.e. within the model.) This structure encodes that the effect of $X$ on $Y$ is entirely mediated by $M$, and allows for computation of both direct and indirect causal effects under intervention. SCMs support interventional and counterfactual reasoning via docalculus and counterfactual logic, and can be expressed graphically using DAGs or algebraically using functional notation (Pearl 2018; Bareinboim et al. 2022).

Despite their differences, the DAG, PO, and SCM frameworks converge on a central goal: determining whether the causal effect of interest is identified-that is, whether it can be estimated from observational data under credible, clearly stated assumptions. Identifiability refers to whether a causal quantity (e.g. ATE, CATE, total effect) can be expressed as a function of the observed data distribution, given assumptions in a particular causal framework. For this to be possible, those assumptions must be both transparent and open to critical scrutiny. In the PO framework, key assumptions such as exchangeability and consistency cannot be verified from the data alone but are often viewed as necessary conditions for valid causal inference (Schwartz and Prins 2024). Both exchangeability and positivity depend on how the treatment is defined. For example, positivity may appear to hold for a broad class of treatments (e.g. 'antidepressants') but fail once stratified by specific subtypes. Precise treatment definitions sharpen both conceptual clarity and empirical identifiability of causal contrasts (Hernán 2016).

Identifiability of causal effects or estimands from the observed data is a necessary condition for drawing valid causal conclusions from data (Pearl, 2009; Hernán and Robins 2020; Huntington-Klein 2021; Schwartz and Prins 2024). If an estimand is not identifiable, then no statistical estimator, no matter how sophisticated, can recover it from data. Any numerical estimate would be model-dependent, assumptiondriven, or arbitrary, and thus not a valid causal conclusion from the data. This principle holds across all major causal frameworks. In the PO framework, identifiability hinges on assumptions such as exchangeability, positivity, and consistency. In the SCM framework, identifiability is determined via do-calculus and graph structure (e.g. the front-door/backdoor criteria explained in Pearl 2009). In the DAG/PAG calculations, identification depends on path blocking and causal sufficiency conditions (Pearl 2009; Huntington-Klein 2021). In all frameworks, there can be no valid estimation of causal effects unless those effects are identifiable. Similarly, even if a causal estimand is identifiable in principle, it must be detectable within the the stated horizon given plausible data quality and variability in order to be estimated in practice. Detectability is a conceptual property: the implied signal must be large enough, relative to noise and internal variability, to be distinguishable from chance with the desired level of confidence at the proposed aggregation and time scale. This is the familiar statistical idea of statistical power/minimum detectable effect size; it can be refined to also provide statistical protections against estimates that get the sign of an effect wrong or that get the magnitude of the estimate wrong by more than a specified factor (Gelman and Carlin 2014.) If detectability is low, responsible claims should either be bounds (what can be ruled out) or contingent statements (what additional information would render the claim resolvable). Sections 5 and 7 discuss methods for analyzing realistically uncertain and variable data to support causal claims.

To illustrate these concepts, the opioid prescription-mortality risk model in Figure 2 can be formulated in SCM terms as follows. The assumed data-generating process is represented with structural assignments and exogenous disturbances that encode causal mechanisms and (the absence or
presence of) unmeasured confounding. Let $C$ again denote measured covariates. A mediation structure consistent with Figure 2 can be written as:

- $X:=f_{x}\left(C, U_{x}\right)$ (i.e. $X$ is generated by a function of $C$ and the exogenous random term $U_{x}$ )
- $M:=f_{m}\left(X, C, U_{m}\right)$
- $Y:=f_{\gamma}\left(M, C, U_{\gamma}\right)$

Here, $U_{x}, U_{y}$, and $U_{y}$ are exogenous terms. The absence of a direct $X \rightarrow Y$ arrow is encoded by leaving $X$ out of $f_{\gamma j}$; any effect of chronic pain on mortality is thereby assumed to be transmitted through the mediator $M=$ Opioid Prescriptions. Allowing $U_{x}$ to be statistically dependent on $U_{y}$ represents possible unmeasured confounding of $X$ and $Y$ overall, while assuming (i) $U_{x}$ is independent of $U_{m}$ (no unmeasured confounding of $X \rightarrow M$ given $C$ ) and (ii) $U_{\gamma}$ is independent of $U_{\gamma}$ given $X$ and $C$ (no unmeasured confounding of $M \rightarrow Y$ after adjusting for $X$ and $C$ ) captures the link-level conditions needed for identification. Just as in the PO framework, positivity and consistency are also required (each level of $X$ and $M$ occurs with non-zero probability within covariate strata, and observed outcomes equal their corresponding potential outcomes under the realized treatments).

Under these assumptions, the interventional quantity $d o(X=x)$ - that is, setting $X$ to $x$ by intervention - is identified from observational data by the "front-door identification" implied by the graph. In practice, this works as follows: we first model how pain ( $X$ ) predicts prescribing ( $M$ ) within covariate strata (i.e. combinations of ranges of values of variables in $C$, perhaps identified using a Classification and Regression Tree). In other words, step 1 is to estimate $P(M \mid X, C)$ ). Step 2 is to model how prescribing ( $M$ ) predicts mortality risk ( $Y$ ) within strata of $X$ and $C$ (estimate $P(Y \mid M, X, C)$ ). We then combine these two pieces by propagating the change in the distribution of $M$ induced by the intervention $d o(X=x)$ through the $Y$ model and averaging over $C$ to obtain the causal effect of $X$ on $Y$ that operates specifically through prescribing. Inoue et al. (2022) implement exactly this SCM logic with NHANES data: they fit the $P(M \mid X, C)$ and $P(Y \mid M, X, C)$ components and combine them to recover the $X \rightarrow Y$ effect of pain on mortality mediated by prescribing-an instance of the front-door identification strategy that remains valid even when $X$ and $Y$ are confounded overall, provided the two linklevel assumptions hold (together with positivity and consistency).

In this example, the SCM view makes the model's mechanistic assumptions explicit and transparent via its structural equations (what causes what, and what does not); cleanly separates allowed from disallowed unmeasured confounding via the exogenous terms; and rewrites the estimand of interest in terms of two estimable pieces, $P(M \mid X, C)$ and $P(Y \mid M$, $X, C$ ), that let us predict how pain changes prescribing and how prescribing changes mortality, and then combine those predictions and average over covariates to obtain the path-way-specific effect of interest. Some implications of the assumed causal structure (e.g. certain conditional independences, overlap patterns, or model fit diagnostics for the two link models) can be empirically tested, and sensitivity
analyses can target the specific violations that would undermine the estimated effect (Inoue et al. 2022). This clarity and empirical testability make SCMs highly useful in practice.

### 3.3.4. System Dynamics (SD) models: stocks, flows, and feedback

System Dynamics (SD) models provide a causal modeling framework for describing causal processes that unfold over time (Homer and Hirsch 2006). They track how stocks-accumulated states such as numbers of people misusing opioids, in treatment, or in remission-change in response to flowsrates that add to or drain those stocks-and in response to feedback loops that make tomorrow's flow rates depend on today's levels. Whereas DAGs and many presentations of SCMs summarize contemporaneous, acyclic relations among variables, SD makes timing part of the causal claim: causes can operate with delays, effects can reverberate, and the system can overshoot or oscillate before settling. In other words, SD is built for transients, not just equilibrium comparisons. SD methods are widely used in physiologically-based pharmacokinetic (Pharmacodynamicscodynamic (PD) models in toxicology, and also in simulating epidemics and other time-varying phenomena in public health (Homer and Hirsch 2006). SD models are mechanistic in structure and may be used for explanation, simulation, or prediction, depending on context.

At the heart of any SD model is a simple accounting identity: over a small time step $\Delta \mathrm{t}$, the next value of a stock equals its current value plus (inflow rate $\times \Delta t$ ) minus (outflow rate $\times \Delta \mathrm{t}$ ). In continuous time, the same idea is d(stock)/ $\mathrm{dt}=$ inf-ow rate-outflow rate. Flows are specified as functions of the current-and sometimes lagged-state of the system and of exogenous inputs or policies; because flows depend on stocks, they generate feedback. By keeping accumulation explicit (stocks have units of "quantity," flows of "quantity per time"), SD naturally represents lags, capacity constraints, saturation, and other nonlinear responses that often dominate real decision horizons.

Qualitative causal-loop diagrams (CLDs) are hypotheses about structure, expressed in terms of reinforcing and balancing loops and delays, not estimates of effect size. Policy-relevant claims arise only after a CLD is mapped to a quantitative stock-and-flow (S\&F) model with explicit intervention "handles" (decision rules, capacities, rates), an estimand is specified (e.g. the policy-induced change in a trajectory over a stated horizon), and the resulting contrast is supported by disciplined calibration and validation. This mirrors the identifiability discipline emphasized throughout all formal causal modeling frameworks: assumptions must be explicit and tied to quantities that data can inform.

An SD model is causal in the same interventional sense used earlier: a policy or do-type change is represented as an exogenous modification to a stock, flow, or decision rule, and the model computes the resulting time path of stocks and outcomes. Identification, however, is assessed differently than in PO or SCM analyses that rely on conditional-independence structure. SD emphasizes structural adequacy and dynamic fit: does one transparent stock-and-flow structure, with a single parameter set, reproduce multiple features of the

![](https://cdn.mathpix.com/cropped/9cf8a422-856b-41dc-8da7-b268614f2772-025.jpg?height=534&width=1713&top_left_y=182&top_left_x=206)
Figure 4. A systems dynamics model of opioid use disorder. Source: Reproduced from Lim et al. PNAS 2022;119:e2115714119. © National Academy of Sciences.

observed time series-levels, trends, turning points, responses to shocks-without retuning for each? Modelbuilding proceeds by specifying a causal structure grounded in subject-matter knowledge, checking dimensional consistency and conservation of flowing quantities, calibrating parameters to data, and testing whether the model explains out-of-sample periods or out-of-domain scenarios. These are empirical checks on the causal story represented by the SD model that can be useful even when formal statistical identification is weak.

Figure 4 illustrates a stylized SD representation for the opioid example. Stocks include misuse, opioid use disorder, treatment, remission, and stable remission; flows govern initiation, progression, treatment entry and exit, and overdose deaths. Several flows are endogenous: higher overdose deaths can raise perceived risk and dampen initiation; tighter prescribing can, for some groups, accelerate transitions to illicit markets; limited treatment capacity slows exit from disorder to remission. Because the model describes transient adjustments, it can show that a sharp restriction on new prescriptions may quickly reduce the "prescribed use" stock yet temporarily raise the "illicit use" stock and near-term overdose risk before longer-run adaptations take hold. Those transient pathways-and the cumulative harms and benefits they imply-are often decisive for policy, but they are invisible in static comparisons of equilibria. In SD terms, a do-style intervention corresponds to setting a policy handle (e.g. treatment capacity, prescribing rule, enforcement intensity, or substitution elasticity) and simulating the implied counterfactual time path while holding the other structural relations fixed as specified.

Figure 5 provides a dynamic validation check: simulated trajectories (blue) are overlaid on historical surveillance data (gray, with $95 \%$ intervals where available) for multiple series at once (e.g. initiation, OUD prevalence, treatment, overdose deaths). The goal is to reproduce observed system behavior: a single transparent stock-and-flow structure, fit once, should simultaneously capture levels, trends, turning points, and responses to shocks across several outputs without per-series retuning. Close agreement builds credibility that the modeled feedbacks and delays are the dominant drivers over the decision horizon; systematic mismatches point to missing loops, mis-specified lags, or structural breaks (e.g. rapid fentanyl
penetration). Good dynamic fit is not proof of truth, but it is a practical minimum for using the SD model as a policy laboratory.

SD and DAG/SCM approaches are complementary. Figure 2 (DAG) cleanly displays a mediation story and clarifies the assumptions required to identify a pathway-specific contrast from observational data. Figure 3 (SD) shows how that same story behaves dynamically when feedbacks and delays matter. In practice, the approaches can be integrated: link functions inside an SD model (for example, how prescribing depends on pain and covariates, or how overdose risk depends on recent exposure history) can be estimated using PO/SCM methods; conversely, outputs from an SD policy simulation can be summarized as causal estimands that are meaningful for decision makers (for example, the policyinduced difference in cumulative deaths over the next five years).

Good practice and minimum reporting for SD studies (Barlas, 1996; Monks et al. 2019) include clearly stating the problem framing and model boundary-outcomes, time horizon, decision levers, and what is treated as endogenous versus exogenous; documenting structure by providing both a causal-loop diagram and a stock-and-flow diagram, naming conserved stocks, inflows and outflows, and information links, and enforcing accounting identities and feasible domains; specifying equations for flows as functions of current or lagged stocks and inputs (for example, capacity constraints, saturation, and decision rules), with dimensional consistency and units; reporting initialization and simulation details, including the time step or integration scheme, initial and boundary conditions, and both baseline and counterfactual runs; describing calibration choices-what parameters are estimated from data versus assumed-and assessing identifiability to flag equifinality risks; presenting validation and robustness checks such as extreme-conditions tests, behavior reproduction and out-of-sample performance, sensitivity and uncertainty analyses for both parameters and structure (alternative loop forms, lags, functional shapes), and transport or invariance checks across periods or contexts; and, finally, define each estimand and intervention precisely, specify the policy lever(s) being manipulated, the magnitude and timing of the changes made, and the population to which it applies. Reporting of and SD model should make explicit what

![](https://cdn.mathpix.com/cropped/9cf8a422-856b-41dc-8da7-b268614f2772-026.jpg?height=1655&width=1714&top_left_y=178&top_left_x=214)
Figure 5. Comparison of simulated model output (blue) to historical data (gray, $95 \%$ Cls where available) for selected time-series variables (Lim et al. 2022). Source: Reproduced from Lim et al. PNAS 2022;119:e2115714119. © National Academy of Sciences.

aspects of the system are held fixed and what are allowed to adjust endogenously. It should discuss the resulting causal contrasts-for example, differences in levels, trends, peak timing, or cumulative outcomes-together with appropriate measures of uncertainty and sensitivity to key assumptions.

Like any framework, SD depends on assumptions and can be misspecified. Common pitfalls include equifinality (different parameter combinations fitting the same history), model boundaries that omit important stocks or feedbacks, and good in-sample fit without prospective predictive power. Good practice therefore emphasizes transparency about what is endogenous versus exogenous, dimensional and conservation checks, sensitivity and uncertainty analysis, and validation against multiple independent patterns, not just a single goodness-of-fit metric.

For risk analysts, the practical advantage of SD is that it turns clear causal stories about stocks, flows, and feedback into time-resolved predictions under alternative interventions. It complements the identification-oriented strengths of DAGs, PO, and SCMs with a dynamic lens that captures lags, overshoot, and unintended consequences-features that often determine real-world risk within the window when decisions must be made.

### 3.3.5. Time-indexed causal models for panels and time series

Many policy questions are about changes over time: policies start or stop, people and systems adapt, and effects may build, fade, or rebound. Time-indexed causal models-
statistical causal models using time series or panel data in which the same individuals, places, or systems are observed repeatedly over time-make the timing of effects part of the causal claim. The guiding counterfactual is straightforward: given a unit's prior trajectory and what was happening elsewhere at the same time, how would its outcomes have evolved if the policy had not started when it did? The answer is constructed by comparing the observed post-start path with a credible no-policy baseline projected from the unit's pre-policy history together with information from contemporaneous units that were not yet exposed (Bernal et al. 2017; Schaffer et al. 2021).

The preceding subsection used System Dynamics (SD) to express hypothesized causal mechanisms with stocks, flows, and feedback. In practice, however, we often lack enough knowledge to specify that structure confidently. In such settings, time-indexed models offer a complementary approach: they rely on observed trajectories and the timing of interventions to estimate immediate and longer-run changes without committing to a full mechanistic specification. The resulting empirical response profiles (e.g. level shifts, changes in slope, cumulative differences over a defined horizon, or shifts in peak timing) can suggest plausible mechanisms for later SD modeling and provide summaries that help calibrate or cross-check SD formulations. Because these models consider trajectories rather than responses at a single time, it is helpful to define effects in terms of the shape of the path. Decision-relevant estimands typically include the immediate change in level at the start date, the change in slope (trend) that follows, the cumulative difference over a specified horizon (for example, one or three years), and timing outcomes such as shifts in peak timing or time to recovery. For count outcomes (e.g. monthly hospitalizations or deaths), the same logic can be expressed on a multiplicative scale by describing how rates change over time relative to the baseline path.
3.3.5.1. Example: Causal time series analysis of United States West-Coast warming. To illustrate how these concepts can be used to understand the causes of observed effects, a recent application of causal time series analysis dealt with a puzzling increase in temperatures along the western coast of the United States. Between 2010 and 2020, the Northeast Pacific logged its warmest decade on record, punctuated by recurring "warm blob" marine heatwaves that disrupted ecosystems and weather along the U.S. West Coast. Although individual events could perhaps be attributed to year-to-year variability, the persistence of the warming was harder to explain. An international team of investigators (Wang et al. 2024) framed a causal question that could be addressed by causal analysis of time series data: by how much did China's rapid post-2013 aerosol cleanup change the subsequent warm-season sea-surface temperature (SST) path along the Northeast Pacific margin, relative to a no-policy baseline extrapolated from pre-2013 behavior and common seasonal/hemispheric patterns? Estimating that contrast over one- to three-year horizons and cumulatively through 2019-while checking pre-trends, placebo dates, and out-ofsample prediction-highlighted a policy-related warming
signal: declining aerosol loads over East Asia warmed the lower troposphere, setting up atmospheric linkages that reduced evaporative cooling and raised SST in the Northeast Pacific. In this application, the time-indexed causal lens helps separate the contribution of aerosol abatement from background greenhouse-gas forcing and internal variability, clarifying why warm-blob conditions became more frequent and intense over the decade.

### 3.3.5.2. Example: Evaluating opioid policy impacts using

panel data causal models. Different U.S. states adopted several opioid-related policies at different times, creating a natural panel-data laboratory. Using a time-indexed causal approach, investigators can address a crucial question: how does each policy change the trajectory of opioid prescribing over the first few years after adoption? For example, Antonelli et al. (2024) model each state's no-policy baseline path from its pre-policy history and from contemporaneous states not yet treated, then estimates deviations (level and slope shifts, and cumulative 1-3-year differences) after a policy starts. A practical way to do this-without committing to a full mechanistic model-is a debiased autoregressive panel model: predict the counterfactual "as-if-no-policy" next step using time series (autoregressive) models for panel data, then attribute systematic departures after the start date to the policy, rather than conditioning directly on raw lags that already embody past treatment. This keeps the baseline clear and the effect interpretable as a change in the path (i.e. trajectory or sequence) of prescriptions.

Applied to three widely discussed policies-must-access PDMPs, naloxone access laws, and medical-marijuana lawsthis framework yields actionable contrasts. Must-access PDMPs measurably reduce prescribing trajectories, whereas naloxone access and medical-marijuana laws show little effect on prescribing (as expected, since naloxone targets mortality). When PDMPs and medical-marijuana laws are enacted together, the combined path is not as low as PDMP alone, indicating a modest dampening interaction. These findings help prioritize portfolios of policy interventions: if reducing prescribing is the near-term goal, lead with PDMPs and do not expect medical-marijuana laws to amplify that effect.

Several assumptions are specific to time-indexed analyses and should be made explicit. A common one is that, in the absence of the policy, the treated unit's path would have continued to track its pre-policy trajectory and the contemporaneous movements of comparable, not-yet-treated units; establishing this comparability before the start date is essential. Second, outcomes should not shift before the policy because of foreknowledge or anticipatory behavior, unless such anticipation is part of the question being asked. Third, major co-occurring shocks (for example, national market changes or other policies) should be represented so that the target policy is not credited with unrelated movements. Fourth, measurement practices, case definitions, and population composition should be sufficiently stable that observed changes can be interpreted as real, after accounting for seasonality, calendar effects, and serial correlation. Finally, spillovers to nearby or connected units should be limited or
explicitly treated as part of the estimand, since indirect exposure can blur comparisons.

Because adoption often occurs at different times across places, it is helpful to show effects by start date before summarizing. Readers should see whether units that adopted earlier or later display systematically different immediate or longer-run responses. Heterogeneity of this kind is substantively important and often expected. Transparent summaries can then average these cohort-specific trajectories with clear weights, while sensitivity analyses check that conclusions do not hinge on a few influential units, on a particular donor pool for the baseline, or on a specific choice of pre-trend controls.

The opioid example illustrates these ideas. States enacted prescription-monitoring enhancements and "pill-mill" restrictions at different dates. A time-indexed analysis aligns each state by months since enactment and compares its observed overdose trajectory with the path expected from its own prior trend and from similar states that had not yet adopted the policy, while also representing national waves in illicit opioid markets and other contemporaneous policies. This design yields interpretable quantities-such as the change in overdoses during the first year and the net cumulative difference after three years-and can reveal transient dynamics, for example, a short-run substitution-related bump followed by a longer-run decline as prescribing and initiation rates adjust (Antonelli et al. 2024).

These time-indexed models fit naturally within the broader frameworks previously discussed. In potential-outcomes terms, they compare entire trajectories under different policy start times or intensities. In structural causal models, the intervention is a do-operation at a specified date that alters downstream assignments from that point forward, period by period. Time-indexed DAGs clarify which lags to adjust for and help avoid controlling for post-treatment variables. The system-dynamics perspective complements them by proposing mechanistic stock-and-flow explanations-such as rates of movement among prescription use, misuse, treatment, and remission-that can generate the dynamic patterns detected empirically. Estimation strategies and specific study designs that implement these ideas are developed in Section 4, and practical methods for longitudinal and dynamic settings, including additional cautions and diagnostics, appear in Section 5.4.3.

For transparent reporting, readers should be told the time unit and coverage; how the intervention start, intensity, and duration were defined and translated into an exposure path; which features of the trajectory were targeted as estimands and over what horizon; how the no-policy baseline was constructed; how seasonality, calendar time, and common shocks were represented; which assumptions justify identification and how they were probed with falsification checks; how uncertainty was quantified in the presence of serial correlation and clustering; and how conclusions changed under alternative windows, baseline constructions, and comparison sets. The overarching aim is to make the time-based datagenerating story-and the inferential step from observed paths to causal claims-explicit, testable where possible, and useful for decisions.

### 3.3.6. Information-theoretic frameworks for dynamic causality

Many risk-related phenomena-such as pollution exposure, medication adherence, regulatory compliance, or health behavior-unfold not as one-time events but as dynamic processes evolving over time. In such systems, causal relationships are shaped by feedback, adaptation, lagged effects, and sometimes cyclic dependencies. While traditional frameworks for causal inference, such as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs), structural causal models (SCMs), and potential outcomes (PO), offer powerful tools for static or acyclic relationships, they may fall short in defining, identifying, and estimating causal effects in dynamic settings unless explicitly extended.

Recent work in information theory, dynamical systems, and machine learning has developed tools that aim to detect, quantify, and test causal relationships in time-series data without requiring full knowledge of the system's structural form. These methods do not, by themselves, establish interventional causality, but they can help identify necessary con-ditions-such as temporal precedence, predictive asymmetry, and invariance over time-that must be satisfied for interventional claims to be credible.
3.3.6.1. Information flow and transfer entropy. A central concept in this literature is information flow, which refers to the directional transfer of predictive information between time-dependent variables. Transfer Entropy (TE) is an informa-tion-theoretic measure of directed dependence between time series. It quantifies by how much knowing the past of one variable ( $X$ ) reduces quantitative uncertainty (entropy) about the future of another variable ( $Y$ ), beyond the uncertainty reduction achieved by knowing only $Y$ 's own past (Granger 1969; Schreiber 2000). Although TE $>0$ does not imply that actively changing $X$ would change $Y$, it can be used to falsify causal claims: if TE from $X$ to $Y$ is zero but both $X$ and $Y$ vary substantially over time, then it is unlikely that $X$ is a cause of Y-at least not in a way that leaves any trace in their joint temporal dynamics - barring common confounding that obscures the predictive relationship between them. Thus, nonzero TE may be a necessary (but not sufficient) condition for $X$ to be a causal influence on $Y$ in a time-dependent system. Interpretively, if causes are informative about their future effects, then nonzero TE from $X$ to $Y$ is expected as a condition for $X$ to exert a causal effect on $Y$ in a dynamic system. Multivariate extensions, such as conditional TE, can help control for confounding by evaluating predictive influence while holding other variables constant. TE and its multivariate extensions are increasingly used in neuroscience, climate science, and economics to elucidate likely causal pathways in complex systems. In health and policy settings, they can be used to screen for directional influence and to detect timelagged dependencies that may otherwise be misattributed to contemporaneous confounding. For those already familiar with Granger causation, discussed next, TE generalizes Granger causality to nonlinear, nonparametric settings.

Granger causality is a more familiar version of this logic, originating in econometrics (Granger 1969). It asks whether
past values of $X$ help forecast $Y$, given $Y$ 's own past (but the reverse relationship, from the past of $Y$ to the future of $X$, does not hold). If so, $X$ is said to "Granger-cause" $Y$. However, this form of causality is purely predictive. It does not guarantee that interventions on $X$ would affect $Y$. For Granger causality to support interventional claims, strong assumptions are needed: no hidden confounders, correct model specification, and no feedback from $Y$ to $X$. These assumptions are rarely guaranteed in observational settings. Nonetheless, as with TE, a lack of Granger causality can sometimes serve as a falsification of dynamic influence. If no forecasting advantage is gained by including $X$ 's history when predicting $Y$, then $X$ is unlikely to be a time-lagged cause of $Y$, at least under the modeling assumptions used.
3.3.6.1.1. Example: Granger-type directional influence between COVID-19 incidence and human mobility. Kwon and Koylu (2023) asked whether county-level changes in human mobility helped forecast subsequent COVID-19 inci-dence-and vice versa-across three U.S. pandemic waves (Apr 1-May 31, 2020; Jun 1-Aug 31, 2020; Nov 1, 2020-Jan 31, 2021). Using daily cases and anonymized mobile-phone mobility flows, they constructed, for each county and wave, two simple forecasting models: one that regressed today's cases on past cases and past mobility; the other regressed today's mobility on past mobility and past cases. After filtering to 425 counties with usable (stationary) series, more than half showed no significant Granger relationship in either direction in any wave. Among those with signal, incidence → mobility (cases helping forecast later mobility drops) was more common than mobility → incidence, and the incidence ⟶ mobility signal weakened over time. This incidence → mobility link is predictive (Granger-type) causality, not an interventional claim. Nonzero Granger influence can be consistent with a causal pathway, but hidden confounding, policy co-movements, and feedback can create or obscure predictive links. Conversely, a lack of Granger causality can help falsify specific dynamic influence claims under the modeling assumptions used. Because the basic test does not give the sign or magnitude of association, Kwon and Koylu complemented it with additional analyses to classify whether local case and mobility trends moved together or in opposite directions. As with transfer entropy, these tools are best viewed as screening and hypothesis-generating devices: they highlight where directional, time-lagged dependencies are detectable and where more richly specified causal analyses (e.g. conditional/multivariate variants that adjust for confounders, or designs aimed at intervention effects) are warranted.
3.3.6.2. Stability and invariance as causal signatures. A fundamental principle of causal inference is invariance: valid causal relationships or causal laws should remain stable across environments, subpopulations, or time periods-provided that the underlying mechanisms do not change (Peters et al. 2016). This idea underlies Invariant Causal Prediction (ICP), which uses data from multiple contexts to identify variables whose predictive relationships with an outcome remain constant (Pfister et al. 2019). If the association between $X$
and $Y$ breaks down across environments, it casts doubt on the claim that $X$ is a direct interventional cause of $Y$. Invariance can be tested empirically and is particularly valuable for assessing whether causal conclusions drawn in one setting will generalize to another-an essential concern in policy and risk contexts. Instability over time may indicate omitted confounding, feedback, or shifts in underlying processes that invalidate simple causal interpretations.
3.3.6.3. Learning causal graphs from time series. Several methods aim to infer causal graph structures from time-series data using statistical or algorithmic criteria. These include PCMCI+ (Peter-Clark Momentary Conditional Independence) algorithm (Runge 2020), Dynamic Bayesian Networks, and Granger (or Granger-Geweke) Causal Graphs (Kinnear and Mazumdar 2023). Such methods combine conditional independence tests with time-lagged modeling to recover likely pathways of influence. They are especially useful when systems involve multiple interacting variables with feedback, delays, or unobserved drivers. While these methods do not establish causality in the interventional sense, they can help determine whether the observed data support key preconditions for valid causal inference, including temporal priority (do changes in $X$ precede corresponding changes in $Y$ ?); directional dependence (does knowing the past of $X$ improve prediction of $Y$ ?); and stability of putative causal relationships across time or environment (does the effect of $X$ on $Y$ remain invariant?) When these conditions are violated, stronger causal claims become difficult to justify.

While most causal frameworks assume acyclicity-that is, no feedback loops-many real-world systems involve reciprocal influence. For instance, regulatory decisions may affect behavior, which in turn prompts further regulation. Emissions of particulate pollution may affect temperature, which in turn affects energy use and emissions of particulate pollution. To model such systems, cyclic causal models (CCMs) and dynamic structural equation models (SEMs) extend SCMs to include feedback and lags, possibly with hidden (unobserved, latent) confounders (Mooij and Heskes 2013; Lorbeer and Mohsen 2023). These models replace DAGs with graphs containing cycles or use time-indexed equations to unfold cyclic effects over temporal sequences. Inference in such models is more complex and may require equilibrium assumptions or dynamic stability criteria. Still, they are essential for representing controlled systems, ecosystems, or multi-agent behaviors where causality does not simply flow in one direction.
3.3.6.4. Limits and integration of information theory methods with interventional frameworks. Despite their value, information-theoretic and graph-learning methods have limits. Most notably, they do not on their own answer interventional or counterfactual questions. As already noted, a high TE or Granger score means that $X$ helps predict $Y$, not necessarily that changing $X$ would change $Y$. As a result, these tools must be interpreted cautiously and used in tandem with SCMs or PO-based reasoning. Moreover, causal informativeness can sometimes increase under coarse-
graining (Chvykov and Hoel 2020). In noisy or underactuated systems, fine-grained parameters may not correspond to distinct interventions or measurable effects. In such cases, higher-level descriptions may paradoxically support stronger causal claims than detailed ones. This phenomenon, termed causal emergence, reflects a necessary condition for valid inference: the model must mediate reliably between interventions and outcomes, at the relevant scale (Chvykov and Hoel 2020).

Although these empirically testable conditions-such as temporal priority, directional dependence, and stability/invariance of causal laws-are only necessary, not sufficient, for valid identification of interventional causal effects, their role as diagnostics is crucial: they help determine whether causal claims are even potentially supportable by the data. When testing them reveals an absence of directional information flow, a breakdown of invariance, or instability in predictive relationships over time, this provides evidence against the identification conditions required for valid interventional inference. In this sense, they contribute to the broader goal of testing whether the necessary conditions for causal interpretation are even met before stronger claims about interventions are made. Moreover, evaluating whether a system supports interventional causal inference requires assessing whether feasible interventions can be reliably distinguished based on their effects (Chvykov and Hoel 2020). This evaluation must take into account the resolution, noise, and scope of both manipulations and measurements. A causal model that fails to reflect this mapping-regardless of its internal consistency-lacks the necessary informativeness to support interventional claims.

More broadly, these information-theoretic tools help analysts evaluate whether the temporal structure of the data is consistent with hypothesized causal influence, and whether that influence is robust to adjustment for other factors (Sun et al. 2015). In practice, they can be used to identify candidate predictors for further causal modeling, to detect violations of independence or invariance assumptions, and to flag variables whose apparent influence may be spurious or unstable over time. Chvykov and Hoel (2020) introduce a measure of geometric effective information as a diagnostic of whether interventions lead to reliably distinguishable effects, under realistic noise and control limitations. They show that a causal model best supports causally effective interventions when the intervention manifold (reflecting feasible manipulations) and the effect manifold (reflecting system responses) are locally congruent. If interventions do not lead to reliably distinguishable effects, then a system likely lacks sufficient structure for valid interventional inference at that modeling level. In this view, a model supports interventional causal inference only if feasible manipulations (interventions) produce reliably distinguishable effects - a condition that-can be quantified using the Fisher information metrics for effects and interventions, respectively.

Technical advantages of information-theoretic methods for identifying and quantifying causal information flows typically include their flexibility (they do not require a parametric model), their ability to detect nonlinear and time-lagged dependencies, and their compatibility with multivariate
settings through conditional extensions (Shi et al. 2022). However, they also have limitations. Their performance depends on sufficient data resolution and sampling frequency, careful selection of embedding parameters, and assumptions about the underlying system's dimensionality and stationarity. In systems dominated by random noise or static equilibria, embedding-based methods may fail to detect meaningful structure. Moreover, as with all observational diagnostics, nonzero directed information flows do not establish a causal effect; they only suggest that the statistical prerequisites for dynamic influence are present.

### 3.4. Well-defined interventions and timing in the potential outcomes (PO) framework

Before a causal effect can be identified or estimated, it must first be clearly defined. In the potential outcomes (PO) framework, this means specifying exactly what intervention is being considered-what it means for a unit to "receive treatment" versus "not receive treatment," or to receive a certain level of treatment such as exposure. If this distinction is vague or ambiguous, then the potential outcomes under each scenario (often written as $Y(1)$ and $Y(0)$ ) are themselves ill-defined-and so are any causal effects based on them.

A point of view popular with advocates of the PO framework is that perfectly specified intervention is not required for valid inference. What matters, in this view, is that the intervention is defined clearly enough so that experts agree that the remaining ambiguity does not materially affect its meaning or implementation. Interventions are considered "sufficiently well-defined" when their meaning is stable enough to support reproducible effect estimation, even if some contextual variation remains. This practical standard allows causal inference to proceed without demanding unattainable specificity (Hernán 2016).

Illustrative refinements can clarify the level of specificity required. Hernán (2016) uses the example "Does water kill?"-a question too vague to support unambiguous interpretation and independently reproducible causal inference from data-and transforms it into more specific, actionable causal contrasts, such as: "What is the effect of drinking a swig of Broad Street pump water between August 31 and September 10 compared with drinking all your water from other pumps?" Such refinements illustrate how vague exposures can be reformulated into interventions with betterdefined causal meaning, allowing identification of meaningful counterfactual contrasts. Greater specificity in intervention definitions is seen as not only aiding identifiability, but also reducing disagreement across studies and analysts. When the intervention is sufficiently well-defined, independent researchers are more likely to agree on the causal question being asked and on whether the assumptions needed for valid inference are satisfied. Precision in defining the intervention thus promotes reproducibility and interpretive convergence, both of which are essential for scientific credibility and policy relevance. From this perspective, what counts as a "well-defined" intervention may evolve over time. As scientific understanding deepens, previously vague or heterogeneous
interventions may become more clearly characterized and therefore more suitable for causal inference. For example, a generic category like "antidepressants" may come to be understood as distinct classes with different mechanisms and time profiles. The clarity of treatment definition is thus dynamic and context-dependent-what was once too vague may become actionable through methodological or pharmacological advances.

More generally, too define meaningful causal contrasts, the intervention must be sufficiently well-defined. Many researchers consider that this also implies that it should be implementable in principle, although this point remains debated (Schwartz and Prins 2024). Only interventions that are both well-defined and reflected in the available data can support transparent causal inference from observational studies. In contrast, reasoning about interventions that are not clearly defined and empirically instantiated may be conceptually interesting but lacks empirical grounding (Hernán, 2016). Vague or non-manipulable exposures-such as "low socioeconomic status" or "ethnicity"-may be associated with outcomes but they do not correspond to actionable or welldefined causes.

Valid causal inference also requires specifying feasible interventions with a clear "time zero"-the moment treatment is conceptualized as beginning (Hernán and Robins 2016; Hernán et al. 2025). Ambiguity in defining time zero can lead to structural biases, most notably immortal time bias, which arises when individuals are classified into treatment strategies based on post-eligibility or post-assignment information. In such cases, individuals must survive some period (the "immortal time") simply in order to be considered exposed, artificially inflating survival in the treated group and biasing effect estimates (Hernán et al. 2025). Anchoring time zero at the point of treatment assignment ensures proper temporal ordering of covariates, treatment, and outcomes. Target trial emulation provides a constructive framework for designing and analyzing observational studies to mimic the structure of a randomized controlled trial by explicitly defining assignment time, as well as eligibility criteria, treatment strategies, treatment-assignment mechanisms, time zero, fol-low-up, outcome measures, and causal contrasts (Hernán and Robins 2016; Matthews et al. 2023). As Hernán et al. emphasize, target trial emulation helps prevent immortal time bias by synchronizing eligibility, treatment assignment, and fol-low-up start, thus preserving the causal interpretation of contrasts. The Target Trial Framework (TTF) developed by Hernán and colleagues offers a useful conceptual scaffolding for clarifying an observational study by structure it as if designing a randomized trial. However, it is neither sufficient nor necessary for valid causal inference. As Hernán et al. (2025) acknowledge, "explicit target trial emulation alone cannot eliminate the bias that arises from lack of randomization." TTF is best seen as a heuristic tool, not a substitute for modeling assumptions and testing identification.

In longitudinal settings where exposures, outcomes, and confounders evolve over time, simple adjustment methodssuch as standard regression models that include baseline or time-varying covariates as predictors-may fail due to timevarying confounding, especially when past treatment affects
future confounders. These methods can introduce bias by conditioning on variables that lie on the causal path from earlier treatment to later outcome or that act as colliders, leading to incorrect (biased) estimates of causal effects. For example, in a study of how drug exposure affects disease progression over time, a biomarker that both influences future treatment decisions and is affected by earlier treatment can serve as a time-varying confounder; adjusting for it in standard regression may block part of the treatment effect or introduce collider bias if the biomarker shares unmeasured causes with the outcome.

To correctly estimate causal effects when treatments and confounders coevolve over time, the potential-outcomes framework relies on the family of g-methods, a set of related methods tailored to address different facets of the problem. (The " $g$ " stands for "generalized," reflecting the goal of extending standard statistical methods to more complex, time-varying, or longitudinal causal settings.) The parametric $g$-formula ("g-computation") evaluates well-defined static or dynamic interventions by modeling the joint data-generating process and simulating counterfactual outcome paths. Marginal structural models (MSMs) estimate population-average effects under specified treatment regimes by reweighting histories (typically with stabilized inverse-probability weights) to remove bias from time-varying confounding. Structural nested models (SNMs)-including structural nested mean and failure-time models-use g-estimation to parameterize the incremental ("blip") effect of treatment at each decision time given past history, enabling inference on dynamic treatment rules in the presence of treatment-confounder feedback (Robins 1986; Hernán and Robins 2020).

In practice, these identification strategies are often paired with semiparametric, doubly-robust estimators that allow flexible, data-adaptive nuisance models while preserving valid target-parameter inference. Examples include augmented Inverse Probability Weighting (AIPW) and targeted maximum likelihood estimation (TMLE)-and their longitudinal/sequential variants-which combine outcome-regression and treatment/ censoring models and remain consistent if at least one is correctly specified. Recent double/debiased machine-learning implementations use cross-fitting to reduce overfitting bias and deliver valid standard errors when high-dimensional or nonparametric learners are used for nuisance functions. (Closely related, policy-learning methods from reinforcement learning focus directly on estimating optimal dynamic treatment regimes, providing a complementary route to decisionfocused estimands.)

All of these approaches rest on the same core identification conditions-sequential exchangeability (no unmeasured confounding conditional on observed history), positivity (adequate overlap of treatment options across histories), and consistency (well-defined interventions with aligned measurement and timing)-plus correct temporal ordering. Practically, they demand design-stage clarity about the intervention(s) being emulated; diagnostics for weight stability/ overlap (truncation/smoothing if needed), covariate balance over time, model fit and residual checks, and sensitivity analyses for departures from exchangeability. While assumptionladen and computationally intensive, these methods are
foundational for longitudinal questions common in occupational and environmental health (e.g. exposure controls, adherence policies, staged regulatory compliance), where confounders are themselves influenced by prior treatment.

While many researchers treat intervention specificity as a matter of practical judgment, others raise concerns about this approach's subjectivity and lack of empirical grounding. A contrary view is that reducing "sufficiently well-defined intervention" to a matter of consensus among (presumably selected) experts risks replacing a scientific approach with a political one and objective, reproducible, data-driven considerations with subjective ones: "It is unclear who is creating the consensus or making these judgments: you'll know a sufficiently well-defined intervention when you see one" (Schwartz and Prins 2024, 85). Defining the intervention with sufficient specificity may also shift the causal question itself (Schwartz and Prins 2024, 85). For example, an initial interest in "the effect of obesity" may be transformed into a more precise and decision-relevant inquiry: "What is the effect of losing weight through caloric restriction between ages 40 and 50?"
