This refinement reflects a necessary tradeoffgreater causal interpretability at the cost of changing the estimand. Analysts must acknowledge that choosing a welldefined intervention may narrow or redirect the original question but also makes valid inference possible within the potential outcomes framework (Hernán 2016; Schwartz and Prins 2024, p. 85).

This need for both conceptual clarity and empirical grounding is captured formally in the consistency condition; as previously mentioned, this is a a core requirement of valid causal inference in the PO framework. Consistency rests on two interlocking requirements: (1) that the intervention is sufficiently well-defined to generate unambiguous potential outcomes, and (2) that it corresponds closely to the version of the treatment actually represented in the data. A hypothetical intervention-even one that is internally coherent and well-defined-cannot support valid inference if it has no empirical counterpart in the dataset. Conversely, an empirically represented exposure cannot support consistent inference unless its meaning as an intervention is clear. Consistency thus implies both definitional precision and data linkage. As Hernán (2016) emphasizes, interpreting causal estimates requires that the observed outcomes correspond to the potential outcomes under the version of treatment actually received-which in turn requires clearly defined interventions and their empirical representation in the data.

Compound exposures such as "obesity," "hypertension," or "stress" present additional challenges (Hernán 2016). These are not treatments or interventions per se, but syndromic states encompassing multiple potential causal mechanisms. Hernán (2016) argues that causal interpretation requires disaggregating such constructs into specific, manipulable inter-ventions-e.g. sustained caloric restriction, initiation of an exercise regimen, or administration of an antihypertensive drug-each with defined timing and implementation. Only then can potential outcomes be meaningfully compared. The interpretability of estimated effects depends on how clearly the exposure corresponds to a feasible intervention. Labels like "obesity" describe conditions, not actions or
interventions. If the causal claim is about reducing obesity, then the mode, timing, and mechanism of that reductiondiet, exercise, surgery, or pharmacotherapy-must be specified. Otherwise, causal estimates cannot distinguish among interventions or support decision-relevant conclusions. Vagueness in exposure definition leads to interpretational ambiguity and undermines the consistency assumption (Hernán 2016; Schwartz and Prins 2024).

Importantly, causal inference does not assume that all versions of an intervention have the same effect. Analysts must assess whether treatment heterogeneity-due to differences in dose, mode, or timing-might violate the assumption of a consistent treatment effect. For example, "exercise" as an intervention could mean walking 30 min a day, strength training three times a week, or running marathons; each version may have distinct effects on health outcomes. If different versions of a treatment yield different outcomes, the consistency assumption may be undermined. Identifying and modeling this heterogeneity is crucial to valid causal interpretation (Hernán 2016). Strategies for controlling confounding may also differ across intervention types: dietary interventions may require adjustment for lifestyle factors, while pharmaceutical ones may call for medical history covariates. Analysts should ensure that confounder sets are selected to match the specific version of treatment being evaluated (Hernán 2016). Failing to align adjustment strategies with treatment definition may result in overadjustment, residual confounding, or invalid estimates.

### 3.5. Modeling causal processes and sensitivities

Valid causal inference requires modeling how changes propagate through systems of causally linked variables, not just what predicts what (Pearl 2009). Predictive machine-learning models-such as random forests or neural networks-may identify correlates of risk but they cannot predict whether or how modifying a variable would change the outcome (Pearl 2018). A forecasting model that successfully predicts opioid overdose deaths does not necessarily tell us whether reducing prescription opioids would reduce deaths or increase them. Confusing prediction with causation can lead to misguided policies, wasted resources, or even harm (Nakagawa et al. 2025). Thus, causal aims must be stated clearly and addressed with appropriately causal methods (Pearl 2018).

Even when causal assumptions are made explicit, analysts must assess how sensitive their conclusions are to possible violations. Meaningful causal relationships useful for guiding decisions should be robust to small variations in modeling assumptions and choices and should apply more generally than to the precise historical conditions of a single study. Sensitivity analysis quantifies how estimates might change under varying degrees of unmeasured confounding, model misspecification, assumption violation, or selection bias (VanderWeele 2016; Cinelli and Hazlett 2020). As discussed later, approaches range from bias formulas (analytical expressions that quantify how much an unmeasured confounder would need to affect the outcome and treatment to explain away the observed effect), and E-values (summary measures
indicating the minimum strength of association an unmeasured confounder must have with both the treatment and the outcome to fully account for an observed effect), to full Bayesian models with priors over unobserved factors (which incorporate uncertainty about the existence or influence of hidden confounders by specifying prior distributions and updating them with observed data). Especially when identification relies on untestable assumptions, formal sensitivity analysis provides a disciplined way to report uncertainty and protect against overconfidence in causal claims. In SCMbased frameworks, recent tools allow simulation of causal graphs with uncertain edges or feedback loops to explore how misspecifications might affect conclusions. Modern causal research also increasingly evaluates interventions across multiple outcomes, not just one. Outcome-wide epidemiology (VanderWeele and Ding 2017) proposes pre-specifying structured outcome sets and estimating all effects systematically. This improves transparency, guards against selective reporting, and helps to assure that the causal model used for one outcome also applies to - and is informed by a wider set of observations.

More broadly, as discussed in greater detail in Section 7, generalizability—or external validity—is a crucial challenge in causal inference: Will a causal effect estimated in one setting apply to another population, time, or context? External validity concerns the conditions under which causal conclusions can be extrapolated beyond the original study (Campbell and Stanley 1963). In practice, differences in populations, treatment delivery, or background variables can all alter effect sizes. Addressing this requires both careful articulation of assumptions and formal methods for adjusting or transporting effects (Igelström et al. 2022). Techniques for evaluating and enhancing external validity include:

- Reweighting, which adjusts sample weights to resemble a target population;
- Effect modification analysis, which tests whether causal effects differ across subgroups (see Sections 6.4.4. and 6.4.5.);
- Causal transportability, which uses structural assumptions (often expressed via causal DAG diagrams) to formally identify when and how causal effects can be transported from one setting to another (see Section 7.2).


### 3.6. Structural causal models (SCMs) as an empirically testable framework for interventional inference

Although the potential outcomes (PO) framework has illuminated many essential distinctions-such as those between association and intervention, and between population-average and conditional effects-it also raises conceptual, technical, and practical challenges. These include the philosophical status of unobservable counterfactuals, the difficulty or impossibility of empirically validating assumptions like exchangeability, and uncertainty about what counts as a sufficiently well-defined intervention (Rubin 1974; Pearl 2009; Hernán et al. 2025).

Structural causal models (SCMs) offer a formal framework that directly addresses these concerns by embedding causal claims in explicit, testable representations of the underlying data-generating process (Pearl 2009; 2018; De Lara 2024). We have seen that SCMs specify causal relationships through a set of structural equations, each of which determines the value of a variable as a function of its causal parents, along with a probability distribution over exogenous (background) variables. These structural equations are typically depicted using a directed acyclic graph (DAG), which encodes the model's causal structure by showing the causal parents (if any) of each variable. Interventions are defined precisely using Pearl's do-operator, which replaces the structural equation for a target variable with a fixed value, while leaving all other equations unchanged. This operation simulates what would happen if the variable were actively set to a given level, independently of its usual causes. When the model is correct-and when the specified causal relationships remain invariant across different environments or policy settingsthe resulting predictions can support valid transportable causal inferences, i.e. inferences that can be applied reliably across different populations, interventions, settings, or studies beyond the one originally analyzed. Its proponents argue that the SCM formalism provides a natural and transparent way to answer interventional questions, such as: What would happen to the distribution of outcome $Y$ if we were to set exposure $X$ to a specific value?

Although SCMs and potential outcomes are logically compatible in many respects (Pearl 2009), their methodological implications diverge once empirical testability is made central. Tools such as Invariant Causal Prediction (ICP), discussed below, enable components of an SCM to be validated against data across varying environments (Peters et al. 2016; HeinzeDeml et al. 2018). No such empirical analog exists in the standard PO framework because of the unobservability of counterfactuals. As a result, SCMs augmented with tools like ICP support a more objective, data-driven approach to causal inference (Cox 2024; De Lara 2024).

Table 6 summarizes other differences between the PO and SCM frameworks. Crucially, SCMs do not have to treat the unobservability of counterfactuals as a fundamental problem requiring empirically untestable assumptions to overcome. A structural causal model can be empirically validated by confirming that its assumptions are consistent with observed conditional independencies and that its predictions remain invariant across diverse environments (ICP). Given such a validated SCM, it is straightforward to compute its implications for the predicted effects of interventions. In deterministic settings, this means evaluating $y=f(x, z)$ for a given treatment variable $x$ (e.g. exposure) and causally relevant covariate vector $z$, where $f$ is the structural equation and $y$ is the outcome variable. In probabilistic models, it means evaluating $P(y \mid d o(x), z$ ), where $d o(x)$ is Pearl's notation for setting the treatment variable to level $x$. If these conditional distributions are shown to be invariant under a range of background conditions or interventions-as tested, for example, using ICPthen the model supports decision-relevant causal predictions (Cox 2024). While ICP does not assume that a full SCM is specified, it offers a method for identifying causal predictors

Table 6. Potential outcomes vs. structural causal models.
| Dimension | Potential outcomes (PO) | Structural causal models (SCM) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Counterfactuals | Postulated primitives: Y_t (the potential outcome if treatment $t$ is assigned) is simply assumed to exist, not derived | Derived via do-interventions: $\mathrm{Y} \_\{\mathrm{T}=\mathrm{t}\}=$ output of modified structural equations |
| Interventions | Assumed to exist and yield well-defined potential outcomes | Explicitly defined as changes to structural equations (e.g. $\operatorname{do}(\mathrm{T}=\mathrm{t})$ ) |
| Causal semantics | Ceteris paribus (hold covariates fixed across worlds) | Mutatis mutandis (covariates may change with treatment through causal mechanisms) |
| Testability | Core assumptions (e.g. ignorability) are untestable from data | Many implications (e.g. conditional independencies, invariance) are testable |
| Equivalence to other models | Compatible with SCM only under strong assumptions (e.g. no effect of T on covariates) | Can simulate PO counterfactuals if SCM meets certain conditions |
| Graphical representation | No inherent DAG; requires external assumptions or mapping | DAG is integral: represents structural dependencies and supports d-separation |
| Main assumptions | Consistency, ignorability (also called conditional exchangeability), positivity | Functional form of equations, independence of exogenous variables, acyclicity |
| Level of model specification | Low to moderate (requires only outcome models and assumptions) | High (requires full or partial structural specification) |
| Counterfactual comparisons | Often only one potential outcome per world is estimable | Entire joint distribution of counterfactuals can be computed under model assumptions |
| Practical use cases | Widely used in epidemiology, especially when design-based reasoning dominates | Powerful in AI, policy analysis, and fairness contexts where model-based reasoning is key |


by searching for subsets of covariates whose conditional relationship to the outcome remains invariant across diverse environments.

This approach may also help resolve disputes over what constitutes a "well-defined intervention." In PO frameworks, an intervention must be defined clearly enough to produce stable potential outcomes and support the consistency assumption. But SCMs permit a more constructive and testable approach: interventions are formally represented by modifying the model to fix the value of a treatment variable via $d o(x)$, thereby severing its dependence on prior causes. This produces a submodel in which the outcome is computed under the intervention $d o(x)$, not merely observed under naturally occurring variations in $x$. To support accurate causal modeling, the requisite level of specificity in the intervention is determined not by expert consensus but by whether the resulting predictions hold stably across relevant contexts (ICP). If a coarsened or simplified intervention description suffices to support invariant predictions, then it may be considered to be sufficiently well-defined for causal purposes. Conversely, if finer-grained distinctions matter-for example, if different formulations or timings of treatment yield meaningfully different predicted outcomes-then the model must represent those distinctions. This supports a pragmatic, data-driven criterion for defining interventions: interventions are well-defined when they enable robust causal predictions that withstand changes in background conditions.

As mentioned earlier, DAGs also help to clarify the relationship between model structure (i.e. the DAG network for the variables in an SCM) and testable implications. When a DAG encodes assumptions about causal relationships, it implies specific conditional independencies among variables that can be tested empirically using tools such as d-separation (Textor et al. 2016). These tests do not prove the truth of the causal model, but they do enable falsification of incorrect models and strengthen confidence in models that pass multiple checks. Similarly, tools such as do-calculus can
determine whether a causal effect is identifiable from observed data, or whether additional data or assumptions are needed.

From the standpoint of necessary conditions for valid causal inference, SCMs make assumptions explicit, model the data-generating process, and permit clear articulation and testing of identification assumptions. They also facilitate reasoning about the consequences of specific interventions, whether those interventions are hypothetical or policy-relevant. SCMs can be used in conjunction with DAGs to identify appropriate adjustment sets for controlling confounding, and to avoid common pitfalls such as conditioning on colliders or posttreatment variables. More generally, SCMs offer a coherent strategy for reasoning about causation that is aligned with traditional scientific practice. Rather than attempting to infer unobservable individual counterfactuals, SCMs focus on discovering general causal mechanisms that can be applied to specific cases. This allows causal inference to proceed on the basis of model-based predictions, without requiring direct observation of counterfactuals. Causal inference becomes scientific when it connects data to reality through formal models that support testable and actionable conclusions (Pearl 2018; Bulbulia 2024).

### 3.6. Relation of modern concepts to the traditional Bradford Hill framework

The modern causal frameworks introduced in this section can be related to traditional concepts long used in epidemiology and risk assessment. In a landmark 1965 lecture, Sir Austin Bradford Hill offered nine "viewpoints" to help assess whether an observed association might be causal: strength, consistency, specificity, temporality, biological gradient (doseresponse), plausibility, coherence, experiment, and analogy (Hill 1965). These considerations have since become foundational in epidemiology and risk assessment. However, as mentioned in the Introduction, Hill himself warned against treating them as necessary requirements: "What I do not
believe-and this has been suggested-is that we can usefully lay down some hard-and-fast rules of evidence that must be obeyed before we accept cause and effect. None of my nine viewpoints can bring indisputable evidence for or against the cause-and-effect hypothesis and none can be required as a sine qua non." (Hill 1965, 299)

This disclaimer is critical. Hill's considerations were never intended to specify necessary conditions for valid interventional causal inference. Indeed, in retrospect, some commentators have concluded that not only are Hill's considerations neither necessary nor sufficient for drawing valid causal conclusions from observations, but some of them, such as strength, may often be misleading or not useful for this purpose: "[S]trength mostly does not work… when large effects are seen, they are mostly transient and almost always represent biases and errors. There is little evidence for specificity in causation in nature. Biological gradient is often unclear how it should it modeled and thus difficult to prove. Coherence remains usually unclear how to operationalize. Finally, plausibility as well as analogy do not work well in most fields of investigation ... ." (Ioannidis 2016). Likewise, Glass et al. (2013) note that "The classic [Hill-type] approach is vulnerable to subjectivity in the evaluation of evidence and to manipulation of the evidence, and stakeholders potentially affected by the finding that an association is or is not causal may take opposing positions on evidence interpretation. Additionally, as constructed and applied, the framework assumes a simplistic direct relationship between cause and putative effect without explicit consideration of the structure of the underlying causal processes." Our focus in this paper is, therefore, not on such heuristics or interpretive aids, but on conditions that must be satisfied for a causal claim to be scientifically credible and decision-relevant. That said, recent work has reinterpreted some of Hill's viewpoints using modern causal tools in ways that may complement, but not substitute for, testable identification criteria.

As causal methodology has advanced, scholars have explored how Hill's nine considerations map onto formal causal frameworks. Shimonovich et al. (2021) analyze how three tools-Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs) (Pearl, 2009), Sufficient-Component Cause (SCC) models (Rothman and Greenland 2005), and the GRADE framework (Guyatt et al. 2011)-reinterpret or replace Hill's heuristics with more useful analogs. For example,

- Specificity, rarely satisfied in multifactorial diseases, can be reconceived and related to the absence of residual confounding, tested through falsification outcomes or exposures (e.g. negative controls). Falsification testing, now widely encouraged in causal practice, can be framed using DAGs: for example, by specifying outcomes that should not be affected by the exposure if confounding has been adequately addressed. Observing an association where none should exist casts doubt on exchangeability or measurement validity (Shimonovich et al. 2021). These tests, while not always feasible, are valuable for probing residual bias.
- Consistency, rather than being judged by replication alone, can be reframed more generally and rigorously in
terms of transportability across populations with differing causal structures, testable via DAGs and effect modification analysis (Pearl and Bareinboim 2014; Levy et al. 2024). Transportability refines the Bradford-Hill notion of consistency by asking whether a causal effect estimated in one population is likely to hold in another, given differences in background risk, effect modification, or structural pathways. DAGs and SCMs support this by showing how causal paths may differ across contexts and whether key assumptions (e.g. invariance of conditional distributions across settings) hold (Levy et al. 2024).
- Dose-response trends, once seen as supporting causation, are recognized as potentially arising from confounding (including unmeasured confounders); modern tools examine whether they persist under proper adjustment using causally relevant adjustment sets. DAGs help assess when dose-response gradients are plausibly causal versus potentially spurious. GRADE upgrades certainty for doseresponse trends only when plausible confounding has been ruled out.
- Temporality remains necessary, but DAGs now offer explicit representations to detect reverse causation. These reinterpretations suggest that Hill's intuitions can still guide thinking, but their relevance depends on whether they can be embedded in a model that meets the formal criteria for valid inference.

Although we do not adopt Hill's heuristic viewpoints as methodological foundations, some of the modern reinterpretations do enrich practical diagnostics. The Hill heuristics are perhaps best viewed today not as evidence rules, but as motivating questions to be reframed within a modern causal modeling approach. When articulated using tools like DAGs or SCMs, they may inform the design, testing, or falsification of a proposed causal model. But none can substitute for the formal identification conditions described earlier. As Hill himself suggested, valid causal conclusions must rest not on checklists but on reasoned assessment grounded in explicit assumptions, testable implications, and an understanding of the data-generating process. DAGS and SCMs now provide concepts and algorithms that make it practicable to follow that advice.

### 3.8. Summary table and checklist for Section 3

To help bring clarity and structure to the foundational requirements for valid interventional causal inference, Table 7 proposes a set of necessary conceptual and definitional conditions (left column), based on the foregoing discussions, that any study seeking to support interventional claims from observational data should address. These conditions are not merely methodological preferences or best practices; rather, they are intended as logical and epistemological prerequisites for making meaningful, empirically supported claims about what would happen under a hypothetical interven-tion-for example, a change in exposure. Each condition is accompanied by its rationale, implications for inference, relevant techniques or diagnostics, and common pitfalls. The

Table 7. Necessary conditions for valid interventional causal inference from observational data.
| Necessary condition | Why this matters | Implications for practice | Supporting methods or tools | Purpose/Use case of methods | Common pitfalls | Example |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| A1. Be explicit about the type of causal claim being made. Do not conflate different types of causal claims. | Interventional, mechanistic, predictive, and associational claims differ fundamentally | Clarifies which types of causal inferences are justified and what assumptions are needed | Target trial emulation; interventional language; causal graphs (DAGs, PAGs) | Anchors the study's question in an interpretable framework | Vagueness about claim type; assuming association implies causation | "X increases mortality" is associational unless tied to a defined intervention |
| A2. Define and justify the causal estimand: What "effect" is being estimated? | Causal contrasts require clear specification of conditions being compared | Enables clear interpretation, identification, and potential relevance to policy | Potential Outcomes (PO); Structural Causal Models (SCMs); G-formula | Defines what is being compared across interventions | Using ill-defined or non-actionable contrasts | Comparing "high stress" vs. "low stress" without defining how to intervene |
| A3. Embed the causal estimand in a formal causal framework | Ensures that assumptions and pathways are transparent, interpretable, and analyzable | Supports adjustment sets, testing, and reproducibility | DAGs, SCMs, PO, decision-theoretic models | Makes assumptions explicit; guides estimation and critique | Implicit or informal causal claims without structure | Estimating an "effect" without showing a DAG or model to justify it |
| A4. Show that the estimand is identifiable from observed data and justified assumptions | Identifiability is a prerequisite for valid estimation | Shows whether desired causal effects can be estimated from data | Backdoor criterion; front-door; docalculus; selection diagrams | Determines whether causal inference is logically possible | Estimating unidentifiable effects; relying on unjustified assumptions | Assuming exchangeability without verifying the adjustment set |

Define the intervention, its timing, and the assignment mechanism: "effect" of what? | Causal inference requires a sufficiently welldefined intervention at a clear time point, with clear treatment assignment rules | Prevents timerelated bias and supports counterfactual interpretation | Risk set alignment; emulated trials; G-methods; assignment model specification | Anchors exposure, covariates, and outcomes | Immortal time bias; vague interventions; assuming random assignment | Studying "effect of opioid use" without defining when and how use is assigned |
| A6. | A7. Model causal processes, not just statistical prediction | Prediction is not explanation; causal inference requires assumptions about how interventions affect outcomes | Distinguishes predictive from interventional analytics | SCMs, mechanistic models, DAGinformed regressions | Ensures inferences reflect underlying causal processes | Equating model fit with causal validity | Using high-AUC models to claim effectiveness of an exposure |
| A8. The checklist is designed for use with scientific manuscripts, study protocols, or reports. It is intended not as a scoring tool, but as a diagnostic aid for reviewers, analysts, and (potentially Al-assisted) evaluators to assess whetherand how well-the foundational requirements for valid interventional causal inference are addressed.

This checklist supports practical use of Table 7. Each item corresponds to a necessary condition. It is intended as a diagnostic tool-not a scoring system-for use by reviewers, analysts, or automated evaluators assessing scientific studies that aim to estimate interventional causal effects from observational data.

## 4. Study design prerequisites for valid interventional causal inference

Valid interventional causal inference from observational data depends not only on having well-defined causal estimands and interventions, as discussed in Section 3, but also on a
study design that allows those estimands to be identified and meaningfully estimated. Without the right logic built into the data collection process, even the most sophisticated statistical methods cannot produce credible causal conclusions: no amount of ingenuity in analysis can compensate for a study design that does not logically support valid causal comparisons (Campbell and Stanley 1963).

In the absence of randomization, many studies adopt quasi-experimental (QE) study designs to strengthen internal validity-that is, the capacity to draw correct causal conclusions from the data in a study (Campbell and Stanley, 1963). This section discusses how QE designs use features such as comparison groups, pretests, temporal discontinuities, or naturally occurring assignment rules to approximate the desired counterfactual contrast (see Section 3) between treated and untreated conditions. Common QE approaches discussed in this section include difference-in-differences, interrupted time series, regression discontinuity, and nonequivalent control group designs. When implemented rigorously and when the necessary conditions described next are satisfied, such designs can support valid interventional causal inference.

Conversely, the practical importance of sound study design as a prerequisite for valid causal inference is underscored in cases where weak or non-existent designs have contributed to confident but mistaken conclusions. Consider three examples:

- A ban on smoky coal in Dublin was initially hailed as causing sharp mortality declines based on a "one-group pre/post comparison" design, i.e. a comparison of mortality rates in the Dublin population in the six years after the ban compared to the six years before it (Clancy et al. 2002). Methodologists objected (Wittmaack 2007) and later evaluations that introduced appropriate comparison populations and analyzed trends rather than just beforeafter comparisons showed that the apparent effect reflected broader secular improvements in cardiovascular health rather than the ban itself (Zigler and Dominici 2014). This illustrates what the opening pages of Campbell and Stanley (1963) termed the "history" threat to causal inference (i.e. coincident historical changes) that invalidates one-group pretest-posttest designs.
- A five-sentence 1980 letter in the New England Journal of Medicine about hospitalized patients (Porter and Jick 1980) was repeatedly cited to claim that addiction is rare with prescribed opioids; a systematic citation audit found that most secondary uses generalized the letter far beyond its design and context, illustrating how non-studies can be laundered into widely cited presumed causal "facts" that do not follow from the information collected (Leung et al. 2017).
- Several state-level studies reported fewer opioid-overdose deaths where medical-cannabis laws were in place (Bachhuber et al. 2014; Campbell et al. 2018), although the original authors warned that a direct causal link could not be established from the aggregate data collected (Bachhuber et al. 2014). Such associations were nonetheless sometimes read causally, conflating associations in aggregate data with interventional causation. This led to policy-relevant recommendations that implementing medical cannabis laws could reduce prescription opioid medication-associated mortality (e.g. Vyas et al. 2018). However, when additional data and policy changes were incorporated, the association not only vanished (Kim et al. 2022) but in some studies with individual-level data, it reversed sign (Rhew et al. 2023). This highlights the fragility of causal claims based on aggregate data (e.g. ecologic difference-in-differences) when needed conditions for valid causal inference are not secured by the study design.

These examples illustrate that unless the study design builds a credible counterfactual contrast-via randomized assignment or rigorously justified quasi-experimental designs-no sound and valid causal conclusion can be drawn from a design that does not logically support it.

### 4.1. Internal validity and the logic of comparative study design

More than six decades ago, Campbell and Stanley (1963) recognized that "Internal validity is the basic minimum without which any experiment is uninterpretable." Internal validity refers to the logical soundness of a causal inference-
whether a study truly supports the causal conclusion that a difference or change in exposure caused an observed difference or change in the observed outcome, rather than some alternative explanation. Study design is the primary tool for securing internal validity, because it determines whether threats to internal validity, such as confounding, selection bias, measurement error, and temporal ambiguity are adequately controlled or avoided. If these threats are not ruled out by design, then causal interpretations may remain speculative. Conversely, when study design anticipates and addresses these threats, it provides a foundation for credible causal inference. This section examines the design-level conditions that must be met for valid interventional causal conclusions to be drawn from observational data.

Although the field of study design is vast, Tables 8 and 9 seek to provide a quick overview by classifying some wellknown study designs according to their potential to support valid interventional causal inference. These tables distinguish between:

- Designs that can potentially support valid causal conclusions if their required assumptions or conditions are met (Tables 8 and 9), and
- Designs that typically lack internal validity and thus cannot support such conclusions, regardless of how the data are analyzed (Table 10).

Table 8 describes each study design in terms of key principles, its potential for internal validity, and the key assumptions or vulnerabilities that must be satisfied for it to yield credible causal inferences. These are intended not merely as desirable features, but as logically necessary ones if the goal is to estimate what would happen to a target group under alternative treatments (e.g. exposures) or conditions (e.g. for stated distributions of co-exposures and co-morbidities in the target population. Some of the conditions listed-such as exchangeability, positivity, and clearly defined time zeromay be unfamiliar to non-specialist readers. These concepts are introduced and explained in the subsections that follow (Sections 4.2-4.4), which unpack the logic and foundational requirements behind each design condition.

By contrast, Table 9 summarizes designs that fail to meet these necessary conditions. Such designs lack a valid comparison group, fail to establish temporal ordering, or do not address confounding. As a result, they cannot logically support interventional conclusions, even if supported by elaborate statistical analysis. Although not all study designs or analyses support valid causal inference, descriptive and correlational analyses remain valuable for characterizing patterns, generating hypotheses, and motivating subsequent causal investigations, provided their inferential limits are made explicit. Table 10 defines key terms and assumptions that appear throughout this section.

### 4.1.1. Design-based strategies to approximate counterfactuals

In addition to the major study designs summarized in Tables 8 and 9, many observational studies rely on design

Table 8. Study designs that can support valid interventional causal inference (if conditions are met).
| Design type | Basic idea | Potential for internal validity | Necessary design conditions for valid interventional causal inference |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Randomized controlled trial (RCT) | Units are randomly assigned to treatment or control | Highest-controls confounding and selection bias | Requires successful randomization to ensure conditional exchangeability; clearly defined time zero and outcome measurement; minimal attrition and adherence to assigned treatment; consistent application of the intervention; no interference across units (SUTVA, Stable Unit Treatment Value Assumption). |
| Pretest-posttest control group design (Campbell and Stanley 1963) | Randomized groups are both measured before and after treatment | High-controls for regression, maturation, and testing effects | Requires valid randomization, pretest and posttest consistency, and no pretest-induced behavior change; exchangeability must be preserved; outcome measurement must be stable across time and groups; temporal ordering must be preserved from exposure to outcome. |
| Regression discontinuity design (RDD) | Assignment to treatment is based on a cutoff or threshold value (e.g. income $<\$ 50 \mathrm{~K}$ ) | High near the thresholddeterministic rule mimics randomization | Requires (1) deterministic assignment at a known cutoff, (2) no manipulation of the assignment variable, (3) exchangeability of units just above and below the cutoff, and (4) correct model specification near the threshold; clearly defined time zero and smooth functional form assumptions. |
| Natural experiment | External event (e.g. policy change) assigns exposure "as if" randomly | High, if quasi-random assignment is credible | Requires credible justification of as-if randomization; structural invariance across groups or over time; well-defined exposure contrast and time zero; exchangeability must be plausible based on design features or external knowledge; must rule out confounding and spillover effects. |
| Instrumental variable (iv) design | A variable (instrument) influences exposure but is assumed to affect the outcome only through that exposure. | High, if the instrument is strong and valid. Estimates are for compliers (for whom the instrument affects exposure), not populationwide. | Requires (1) strong instrument relevance, (2) valid exclusion restriction (instrument affects outcome only via exposure), and (3) independence from unmeasured confounders; identifies causal effects only for compliers under LATE assumptions; requires clear assignment rules and timing alignment. |
| Nonequivalent control group design (NECG) (Krishnan 2019) | Comparison group lacks random assignment but is pretested alongside the treated group | Moderate-supports causal inference if baseline equivalence is credible | Requires that baseline equivalence be established through pretesting or matching; exposure must precede outcome; no unmeasured confounding between groups; exchangeability must be argued based on measured covariates and structural knowledge; requires attention to maturation and selection bias. |
| Interrupted time-series design (ITSA) | Multiple outcome measures before and after intervention in a single group | Moderate-can detect sudden or trend-level shifts | Requires (1) clearly timed intervention, (2) stable pre-intervention trend, (3) no concurrent events affecting the outcome, and (4) adequate pre/post data to detect changes; attribution depends on excluding alternative explanations (e.g. history effects); must define exposure start (time zero). |
| Multiple time-series design | ITSA with an added untreated control series | Higher than ITSA alonestrengthens causal attribution | Inherits all ITSA conditions, plus: requires valid untreated comparison group with parallel pre-intervention trends and no contamination; must justify comparability in trends, covariates, and timing; design must enable exchangeability in outcome trajectories across groups. |
| Difference-in-differences (DiD) (Wang et al. 2024; Baker et al. 2025) | Compares pre-post changes in a treated group vs. an untreated control group | Moderate-widely used in policy evaluations | Requires (1) parallel trends in absence of treatment, (2) exchangeability of treated and control groups with respect to outcome trends, (3) no anticipation or policy spillovers, and (4) clearly defined and consistent timing of intervention and outcome measurement; robustness depends on falsification tests. |
| Target trial emulation (TTE) | Observational study explicitly mimics a hypothetical RCT (eligibility, time zero, intervention) | Variable-depends on design rigor and assumption validity | Requires explicit protocol mimicking a randomized trial: clear eligibility criteria, time zero, exposure definition, follow-up period, and outcome metrics; must address confounding through design or covariate control; exchangeability and positivity must be justified by structure, not assumed. |


Table 9. Designs that typically lack internal validity for causal inference.
| Design type | What it is | Why it fails for causal inference | Common pitfalls |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| One-Group Pretest-Posttest Design (Campbell and Stanley 1963) | One group is measured before and after intervention, with no comparison group | Cannot distinguish effects of the intervention from time-related threats such as history, maturation, or regression to the mean | Appealing but invalid unless strong assumptions or analytic corrections are justified |
| Posttest-only design without control | Measures outcome in a treated group after intervention-no comparison, no baseline | Lacks counterfactual; all threats to validity are uncontrolled | Still common in pilot studies and case reports despite clear limitations |
| Static group comparison | Compares treated vs. untreated groups at one time point, with no pretesting | Vulnerable to confounding from group differences; selection bias cannot be ruled out | Resembles a cross-sectional study but often misinterpreted as causal |
| Case series/Descriptive studies | Documents outcomes in an exposed group without a comparison group | No information on what would have happened without the exposure | Descriptive only—not valid for causal conclusions. Can be used for hypothesis generation. |
| Cross-sectional designs with causal claims | Measures exposures and outcomes at the same time | Lacks temporal ordering; direction of causality cannot be determined | Common misuse in epidemiology and social sciences when causal language is used for results |
| Self-selected or convenience samples | Participants choose exposure or are not representatively sampled | High risk of selection bias and unmeasured confounding | Cannot estimate causal effects without strong assumptions |
| Exposure-outcome correlation without control | Reports simple associations between exposure and outcome with no covariate control | Confounding and reverse causation threaten validity of causal interpretations | Common in exploratory data alis, naive policy claims, and some burden-of-disease (BoD) models based on unadjusted or poorly adjusted observational data |
| Ecological studies with group-level exposure | Correlates aggregate (group-level) exposure (e.g. by city or county) with group-level outcome rates | Cannot identify individual-level causal effects from aggregate data; at risk of ecological fallacy | Misused to draw causal conclusions about individual risks (e.g. mortality or morbidity risks) from group-level associations |
| Adjustment for post-treatment variables | Includes variables affected by the exposure as covariates in outcome models | Distorts estimated effects by blocking causal pathways or inducing collider bias | Common error in mediation analysis and regression modeling |
| Unanchored longitudinal study | Tracks outcomes over time without clearly defining when follow-up begins ("time zero") | Exposure timing is ambiguous; risks immortal time bias and incorrect risk attribution | Frequent flaw in retrospective studies using real-world or administrative data |


Table 10. Glossary of key study design terms, concepts, and assumptions.
| Term | Explanation |
| :--- | :--- |
| As-if randomization | Assumes the outcome under an observed exposure reflects is the same as what would happen if that exposure were assigned. Violated if treatment versions differ in ways that affect outcomes. |
| Ecological fallacy | The mistaken inference that associations observed at the group or aggregate level also apply at the individual level. Occurs when group-level correlations (e.g. between air pollution and mortality by city) are interpreted as evidence of individual-level causal effects, despite potential differences in within-group relationships. |
| Exchangeability | The condition that, conditional on measured covariates, the distribution of potential outcomes is the same across treatment groups. Thus, treated and untreated groups are comparable after adjusting for covariates. This condition is required to identify causal effects from observational data. Some methods assume it; others attempt to test or adjust for violations (e.g. using instrumental variables, sensitivity analysis, or graphical algorithms that allow latent confounding). |
| Exclusion restriction for instrumental variables (IVs) | Instrument must influence the outcome only through its effect on the exposure. |
| Immortal time bias | A bias that arises when periods during which an outcome could not have occurred are misclassified or excluded, typically inflating survival time in exposed groups. |
| Instrumental variable (IV) | A variable that affects exposure but has no direct effect on the outcome and is not related to unmeasured confounders. Used to identify causal effects when randomization is not possible. Must satisfy exclusion and independence conditions (see text). |
| Internal validity | Whether the study design supports a valid causal interpretation by ruling out alternative explanations for the observed effect such as coincidental historical trends, confounding, bias, and reverse causation. |
| Parallel trends (DiD) | Assumption that, in absence of treatment, treated and control groups would follow similar trends. Required for traditional difference-in-differences (DiD) analysis. |
| Positivity (overlap) | The requirement that, for every combination of covariates, there is a non-zero probability of receiving each exposure level. Violations make causal effects undefined in some subgroups. |
| Pretest reactivity | Change in behavior or outcomes caused by taking the pretest, independent of the intervention. May bias results in pretest-posttest designs. |
| Quasi-experimental (QE) design | A study design used to estimate causal effects without using random assignment. |
| Regression to the mean | Tendency of extreme measurements to move closer to the average upon retesting. |
| Spillover effects | When treatment received by one unit affects outcomes for another unit-for example, via contagion, displacement, or herd effects. |
| SUTVA (stable unit treatment value assumption) | The assumption that (1) the potential outcome for any unit depends only on that unit's own treatment, not on the treatment of other units (no interference), and (2) each treatment condition is consistently defined across units (no hidden variations). Violations can arise from spillover effects, contagion, or treatment nonuniformity. |
| Time zero (index date) | The starting point for defining exposure and tracking outcomes. Must be clear and consistent to avoid biases like immortal time bias or misalignment of exposure and outcome. |


extensions and analytic strategies that, while not always stand-alone designs, can operationalize the necessary conditions or strengthen the support for valid causal inference in observational studies. These approaches include methods for constructing counterfactual contrasts, adjusting for confounding, modeling time dynamics, and handling unmeasured variables. Each depends on a distinct set of assumptions, and none can compensate for a fundamentally flawed design. When used appropriately, however, they can help make observational studies more fit for purpose and more closely approximate the logic of a randomized trial. For example, the following methods aim to reduce bias and strengthen identification by constructing better-matched groups, exploiting natural experiments, or using within-subject or longitudinal comparisons to isolate causal effects:

- Propensity Score Methods (e.g. matching, weighting, stratification) aim to create balance between exposed and unexposed groups on observed covariates, simulating some features of randomization. Their validity depends critically on correct model specification, sufficient covariate overlap (positivity), and absence of unmeasured confounding. They do not remove bias if the underlying design lacks a valid comparison or if treatment assignment is strongly confounded.
- Synthetic Control Methods construct a weighted combination of control units to approximate the counterfactual outcome trajectory of a treated unit. These methods are especially useful in comparative case studies or policy evaluations where traditional control groups are unavailable. Validity depends on a good pre-intervention fit and the assumption that unobserved confounding remains stable over time.
- Instrumental Variable (IV) Designs attempt to estimate causal effects by using an external variable-called an instrument-that induces variation in exposure but is assumed not to affect the outcome directly or share unmeasured causes with it. When valid, IV methods can overcome unmeasured confounding and support interventional causal inference. However, their validity depends on three key assumptions: (1) relevance (the instrument affects exposure), (2) exclusion restriction (the instrument influences the outcome only through the exposure), and (3) independence (the instrument is not associated with unmeasured confounders of the expos-ure-outcome relationship) (Keele and Small 2019; Lal et al. 2024). These assumptions are generally not testable, and violations-especially of exclusion or independ-ence-can produce substantial bias. Falsification tests, such as placebo outcomes, subgroup analyses, and covariate balance checks, are, therefore, important tools for probing plausibility (Keele and Small 2019). IV estimates typically identify a local average treatment effect (LATE) for the subgroup of units whose exposure is influenced by the instrument; this may differ from the population average effect (Angrist et al. 1996). Because IV designs often rely on specific, context-dependent instruments, researchers should clearly describe the population of compliers and consider whether the results generalize.

Like other quasi-experimental designs, IVs are most convincing when triangulated with alternative methods and when their assumptions are explicitly stated, justified, and tested where possible.

### 4.1.2. Panel-structured designs and time-based methods

Many quasi-experimental designs also exploit time-structured data to estimate causal effects, but require strong assumptions about temporal patterns. Panel data structures provide repeated measurements of the same units over timewhether individuals, firms, hospitals, or regions-and are a foundational data structure for many quasi-experimental designs. While panel data are not a design in themselves, they enable analytic strategies that control for time-invariant confounding through within-unit comparisons. Designs such as difference-in-differences, fixed effects regression, and synthetic control methods all rely on panel data to estimate counterfactual trends under specific assumptions about group comparability and time dynamics. The structure itself, however, does not guarantee causal identification; it must be paired with a valid design logic that supports meaningful contrasts. The following methods rely on panel or longitudinal data structures that enable within-unit comparisons over time.

- Time-structured designs, including interrupted time series (ITSA), multiple time-series, and difference-in-differences (DiD), are widely used in public health, economics, and policy evaluation. However, their validity depends not simply on having repeated outcome data, but on design features that support meaningful counterfactual contrasts. Without a valid comparison group and credible assumptions about underlying trends, observed changes may reflect secular trends, regression to the mean, seasonality, concurrent interventions, or other time-varying confounders rather than causal effects of the intervention.
- Difference-in-Differences (DiD) designs are a cornerstone of quasi-experimental inference, but their validity hinges on critical assumptions that are often underappreciated. At its core, DiD compares outcome changes over time between treated and untreated groups. The parallel trends assumption is central: treated and control groups must follow similar outcome trajectories in the absence of the intervention. While visual checks of pre-intervention trends are common, they are not definitive. Researchers should also ask conceptually: What unmeasured factors might drive outcome trends? Are these factors likely to be time-invariant across groups or group-invariant over time-as DiD requires? Naming these factors can clarify when fixed effects may suffice and when they won't.

Modern multi-period DiD methods go well beyond traditional two-way fixed effects (TWFE) models. In settings with multiple time periods, staggered treatment adoption, or treatment effect heterogeneity, TWFE can yield misleading estimates. Recent estimators address these issues by recovering group-time-specific treatment effects under more flexible
assumptions, including conditional parallel trends (Callaway and Sant'Anna 2021; Sun and Abraham 2021). These designs allow for covariate adjustment and accommodate heterogeneity across units and over time. Yet even these advances do not eliminate DiD's dependence of assumptions. The strict exogeneity assumption demands that treatment timing is unrelated to unobserved outcome determinants-even in prior periods. This can be violated when policies respond to trends, or when actors anticipate future interventions. Granger-style causality tests and lead indicators can help detect such anticipatory behavior. When parallel trends are questionable, triple-difference (DDD) designs offer a remedy by adding a third group to help difference out time-varying confounders. Group-specific trends can also be added as a sensitivity analysis; if results shift substantially, DiD assumptions may not hold. Finally, statistical inference in DiD requires care. Serial correlation and clustering can lead to underestimated standard errors and false positives. Reliable options include clustering at the group level, clustered bootstrapping, or aggregating data to group-time units (Wing et al. 2018; Roth et al. 2023). Thus, DiD remains powerfulbut only when its assumptions are made explicit, justified, and tested.

- Interrupted Time Series Analysis (ITSA) and Multiple Time-Series Designs. Interrupted time series analysis (ITSA) designs assume that, in the absence of the intervention, the pre-intervention trend or data-generating process would have continued. This requires both sufficient pre-intervention data to establish a stable trend and confidence that no other concurrent changes occurred that could explain a shift in level or slope. Multiple time-series designs enhance ITSA by adding a comparison group, but validity still depends on the assumption that the comparison group would have followed a similar trajectory if it had been exposed. In both cases, failure to justify these assumptions through design features-such as long pre-trends, careful selection of comparison units, or documentation of external eventscan undermine causal attribution.
- Time-Varying Confounding in Longitudinal Designs. Timebased designs are especially vulnerable to confounding from co-occurring events-such as changes in diagnostic criteria, economic shocks, or policy spillovers-that coincide with the intervention but are unrelated to it. These sources of bias are often difficult to detect post hoc and must be anticipated through design-level protections: including unaffected comparison groups, incorporating time-varying covariates such as seasonality or surveillance intensity, and testing for spurious changes in outcomes unlikely to be affected. Without such safeguards, apparent treatment effects may reflect unrelated dynamics. To support valid causal conclusions, time-based observational designs must satisfy several conditions:
- A meaningful comparison: either across groups (e.g. treated vs. untreated) or over time (e.g. pre/post), with clear temporal anchoring;
- Stability of pre-intervention trends: or appropriate modeling of departures from trend;
- Absence of major shocks at the intervention point: unless they can be accounted for or shown to be irrelevant;
- Justified comparability of groups: especially in multiple time-series designs;
- Clear definition of time zero and follow-up periods: to avoid immortal time bias and preserve temporal logic.
These are not merely analytic refinements-they are demands placed on the design. Without them, observed changes over time may be descriptive but not causal.
- Case-Crossover and Case-Time-Control Designs are specialized within-subject methods for studying transient exposures and acute events, commonly used in pharmacoepidemiology and injury research. These designs treat each individual as their own control-comparing outcomes during exposed and unexposed periods within the same person-to eliminate between-subject confounding under the assumption of time-invariant confounding. The validity of these designs depends critically on appropriate time window selection and the absence of time-varying confounders.

Even sophisticated time-based methods-such as fixedeffects panel regression or event history analysis-require strong assumptions to yield causal interpretations. These include the absence of unmeasured time-varying confounders, correct temporal alignment of exposure and outcome, and non-informative censoring. Without these, such models may produce precise estimates that are causally uninterpretable.
4.1.2.1. Example: A difference-in-differences analysis of automotive plant closures and opioid deaths. The key ideas of DiD modeling are illustrated well by a study comparing how opioid-overdose mortality rates changed over time in counties where automotive-assembly plants shut down versus how they changed in similar counties that did not lose a plant (Venkataramani et al. 2020). Because the two groups exhibited nearly parallel mortality trends in the years before any closures (Figure 6), the divergence after closure likely reflects the causal impact of the plant exit. The estimated effect was large: by five years post-closure, treated counties had roughly 8.6 more overdose deaths per 100,000 than would have been expected had they followed the control counties' trajectory. This design controls for unchanging county characteristics (e.g. long-standing health access, culture) and for nationwide trends in opioid mortality. Yet the inference stands on the assumption that no other shock coincided with the plant shutdowns, and that control counties indeed represented the counterfactual for treated counties. In short, it embodies the DiD logic: compare changes over time in the treated group to changes in a credible comparison group.

Other quasi-experimental variants-such as double-pretest, switching replications, and repeated-treatment designs-can also strengthen inference under specific threats

![](https://cdn.mathpix.com/cropped/9cf8a422-856b-41dc-8da7-b268614f2772-043.jpg?height=892&width=1511&top_left_y=176&top_left_x=305)
Figure 6. Difference-in-differences analysis: Mortality rates increase faster in counties with closed automotive plants than in counties where automotive plants did not close. Source: Figure redrawn from Venkataramani et al. 2020.

when embedded within a strong design logic (Harris 2006; Bärnighausen et al. 2017). As discussed next, their use should be motivated by a clearly stated causal question and a design logic that supports it.

### 4.2. Five foundational requirements for defining causal contrasts

The suitability of any study design for causal inference begins with a fundamental question: Does the study actually pose a causal question? Research aims may be descriptive (e.g. measuring prevalence), predictive (e.g. forecasting outcomes), or causal (e.g. estimating the effect of an intervention) (Ito et al. 2025). Only causal aims require the design prerequisites discussed here. Misalignment-such as using causal language to report predictive findings-can lead to invalid conclusions and policy missteps.

Causal effects are contrasts between what would happen under different conditions. To estimate such effects from observational data, five logically necessary design conditions must be met. These prerequisites apply whether the data are prospective or retrospective, and whether treatment assignment is randomized or not.

1. Valid Comparison Group. A study must enable meaningful contrasts across exposure levels-whether binary, categorical, or continuous. This requires that the data include sufficient numbers of units in each exposure category, and that those units are conditionally comparable on relevant covariates. Without a well-defined compari-son-created by design or through adjustment-counterfactual inferences are unsupported. The comparison
must also satisfy the positivity condition (also called overlap): for every combination of covariates used in adjustment, there must be a non-zero probability of receiving each exposure level under study. Without positivity, causal effects are undefined in regions of the covariate space, and identification fails. Violations of positivity often arise when exposure is deterministically linked to covariates-such as when certain subgroups (e.g. children, the uninsured, or high-income individuals) are never observed under specific exposure levels. In such cases, observed contrasts cannot support valid counterfactual comparisons, and model-based extrapolations may be unsupported by data. Analysts should diagnose positivity violations using covariate support plots, propensity score diagnostics, or examination of extreme weights. If violations are found, inference should be limited to the region of common support, and interpretations should reflect the restricted population for which causal effects are identified.
2. Temporal Ordering. The exposure must precede the outcome, and the timing of both must be consistently defined across participants (Barker et al. 2024). Ambiguities in causal ordering-such as retrospective assignment of exposures after outcomes or delayed documentation-can undermine internal validity of causal interpretation even in otherwise well-structured studies. Mediation analysis imposes an even stronger requirement: the mediator must come after the exposure but before the outcome. Cross-sectional data rarely satisfy these conditions. In longitudinal or retrospective studies, a clearly defined time zero-the point at which exposure begins and follow-up starts-is essential to prevent misclassification and biases such as immortal
time bias. Misaligned timelines can create spurious associations or obscure true causal effects. Valid causal inference requires temporal anchoring of exposures, outcomes, and covariates to preserve the logic of the causal contrast.
3. Conditional Exchangeability. To support valid causal inference, treated and comparison groups must be conditionally exchangeable: that is, potential outcomes must be independent of treatment assignment, given a set of observed covariates. In randomized experiments, this condition is achieved by design. In observational studies, conditional exchangeability must be justified by showing that treatment assignment is not confounded after adjusting for relevant covariates-ideally secured through study design features such as matching, eligibility restrictions, or quasi-random assignment mechanisms, not merely assumed.

- In the Potential Outcomes (PO) framework, conditional exchangeability is a required assumption but cannot be directly tested, since counterfactuals are unobservable.
- In the Structural Causal Model (SCM) framework, conditional exchangeability is a consequence of the causal structure: if a valid adjustment set (e.g. one that blocks all backdoor paths) can be identified from a directed acyclic graph (DAG), then causal effects are identifiable via conditioning. Moreover, SCM-based methods such as Invariant Causal Prediction (ICP) allow partial empirical evaluation of these conditions by testing whether conditional distributions remain stable across environments or interventions. When ICP is found to hold, it provides indirect support for exchangeability assumptionssomething unavailable in the PO framework.
- Conditional exchangeability is not limited to binary exposures. For continuous exposures-such as drug dose, pollutant level, or income-it must hold across the entire range of the exposure. That is, after conditioning on covariates, the assignment mechanism must not depend on unobserved determinants of the potential outcome, regardless of exposure level. This assumption enables unbiased estimation of dose-response functions or marginal structural models, provided that other conditions such as positivity and correct functional form are also met.
- Conditional exchangeability also assumes that each unit's outcome depends only on its own treatment and that treatment conditions are consistently defined (i.e. no interference and no hidden variation, as formalized by the Stable Unit Treatment Value Assumption, or SUTVA).
- Even when design-level exchangeability is not fully plausible, analytic methods such as instrumental variables, front-door adjustment, latent variable modeling, or causal discovery algorithms (e.g. FCI) may offer partial remedies. However, each method rests on its own structural or independence assumptions, which must be explicitly stated and justified. Without a credible rationale for conditional
exchangeability-whether design-based, structural, or empirical-observed outcome differences may reflect confounding rather than causal effects.
- After any adjustment intended to secure conditional exchangeability-such as matching, stratification, or propensity score weighting-researchers must evaluate whether measured covariates are indeed adequately balanced across exposure groups. If substantial imbalance remains, the assumption of exchangeability is not credible. Balance checks (e.g. standardized mean differences, overlap plots, or covariate distributions by propensity score strata) serve as a necessary diagnostic (Zhang et al. 2019). Without them, claims of confounding control rest on untested assumptions.

4. Sufficient variation and stability. Causal effects cannot be identified if all units are similarly exposed or if outcomes are too unstable to detect differences. In time-series and longitudinal designs, both pre- and post-intervention data must be available in sufficient quantity to establish trends and detect change. Lack of variation or instability undermines both identifiability and inference.
5. Transportability. To generalize findings beyond the study setting, additional conditions must be justified. These include similarity in the distribution of effect modifiers, structural stability of causal mechanisms, and-when applicable-the use of formal transport formulas. In the SCM framework, such assumptions can be explicitly characterized using graphical methods and do-calculus (Pearl and Bareinboim 2014). Transportability assumptions are distinct from exchangeability: the latter concerns valid comparisons within the study; the former concerns whether those comparisons remain valid in new populations or contexts.

These requirements provide the foundation for interpreting the study designs most relevant for observational causal inference (Tables B. 1 and B.2). Designs that meet these crite-ria-when implemented with appropriate safeguards-can support valid interventional inference. Those that fail to meet them cannot, regardless of the analysis methods applied.

### 4.3. Design justification and fit-for-purpose alignment

Even if a causal contrast is identifiable in principle-for example, based on a DAG showing that the necessary conditions for identifiability are met-actually estimating it from observed data requires a study design that is not only logically sound but also fit for purpose: it must generate data that allow for practical identification, estimation, and interpretation of the effect in question. A design that is internally valid for answering one question may be ill-suited for another. For example, a regression discontinuity design may support estimation of a local average treatment effect (LATE) near a threshold, but not of an average treatment effect (ATE) across the broader policy-relevant population. Similarly, difference-in-differences designs can estimate changes over time between groups, but only under strong assumptions
such as parallel trends and the absence of time-varying confounding.

To answer the causal question that a study is intended to address, it is necessary to align

- the causal estimand (e.g. average treatment effect of an intervention in a defined population),
- the intervention (what is being manipulated or compared), and
- the target population or setting (who or what the results aim to inform).

For example, if the goal is to evaluate the impact (e.g. the ATE) of a policy change on hospitalizations in a particular state, then the design must compare outcomes under that policy to outcomes under an alternative (e.g. pre-policy or no-policy) condition in a comparable group. A national crosssectional survey lacking a control group, a pre-policy baseline, or temporal structure will not suffice-even if it includes the right variables.

Misalignment between study design and causal question can wholly preclude valid inference. For example, suppose a researcher uses a convenience sample of patients receiving a new drug and observes improved outcomes. Even if baseline covariates are adjusted for in analysis, this design cannot identify the effect of the drug in the population for which regulatory approval is being sought because there is no untreated comparison group, the treatment is not randomly or externally assigned, and the patients studied may differ systematically from the target population. The issue here is not merely bias or omitted variables, but design misalignment: the structure of the study does not correspond to the causal question being asked. The choice of a study designwhether prospective cohort, retrospective case-control, nested case-cohort, or another structure-that is adequate for answering a specified causal question must, therefore, be explicitly justified in light of this alignment.

An adequate study design must not only support the estimation of the intended causal contrast, but also address threats to validity (see Section 4.4). For example, retrospective designs may obscure temporal ordering; inappropriate inclusion or exclusion criteria may misrepresent the target population; and generalizing from a convenience sample without assessing representativeness risks serious extrapolation error. These threats may be addressed through design-based safe-guards-such as eligibility restrictions, use of pre-intervention covariates, or matched comparison groups-or via analytic strategies such as pre-trend testing, covariate matching, falsification checks, or sensitivity analyses. Section 4.4 summarizes standard threats to internal validity and potential remedies for QE designs.

### 4.3.1. Example: Prior antibiotic exposure and patient-level risk of resistance: case-control/cohort logic in practice

To illustrate how study design can support causal inference, consider the challenge of determining whether observed antimicrobial resistance (AMR) in patients with bacterial infections is caused by specific other uses of antimicrobial agents.

Studies of AMR often ask whether antibiotic exposure increases a patient's risk of subsequent infection with a resistant organism. A common design samples "cases" with resistant isolates and "controls" with susceptible isolates drawn from the same laboratories and time window, and compares prior antibiotic exposure histories over a fixed look-back period (e.g. 30 days). Properly interpreted, the estimand of interest is interventional: What would be the difference in the probability of a resistant isolate at culture time if, over the previous 30 days, a specified class of antibiotics had been withheld rather than administered? To support that interpretation, the design must (i) define the intervention precisely (drug class, window, dose/route, and whether inhospital and outpatient orders both count), (ii) fix time zero at specimen collection so that all exposure precedes outcome ascertainment, and (iii) achieve conditional exchangeability so that exposed and unexposed patients are comparable on determinants of both treatment and resistance (e.g. prior colonization, device use, comorbidity burden, ward exposure, and recent length of stay).

A tutorial review of AMR study designs (Schechner et al. 2013) emphasizes both the appeal and the pitfalls of this "risk-factor" approach: it is efficient for rare outcomes and allows detailed measurement at the patient level, but is highly vulnerable to confounding by indication (patients clinically judged to need antibiotics are also those at higher baseline risk of resistance), control-selection bias (if controls are sampled in ways related to exposure), and temporal ambiguity (if exposure windows bleed into post-culture orders). The review's classification of designs (patient-level case-control and cohort studies vs unit-level ecological and time-series comparisons) and discussion of strengths and limitations map naturally onto the necessary conditions discussed in this section. The authors fnd that without a welldefined intervention, a common time zero, overlap/positivity across covariate strata, and credible control of confounding (by design, not only modeling), the same odds ratios that are often reported as if they implied an intervention's effect are in fact only associational contrasts susceptible to multiple biases. In practice, strengthening moves include incident-user restrictions (to avoid "immortal-time" artifacts from improper treatment of time zero), matching on ward and calendar week (to control history effects), pre-specification of a biologically plausible exposure window, and adjudication of prior colonization status. When these elements are present and documented, patient-level estimates can be read as evidence about an actionable policy (withhold vs administer a class over a defined interval); when they are absent, reported associations should be interpreted, at most, as descriptive risk markers rather than as estimates of the effect of changing prescribing.

### 4.3.2. Example: Hospital stewardship policy and resistance trajectories (interrupted time-series and DiD)

Following up on the previous example, at the hospital level, a common causal question about AMR is: How did implementing a formulary restriction or prospective audit-andfeedback policy at a known start date $t_{0}$ change the
trajectory of resistant infections over the next year, relative to what would have happened without the policy? The appropriate estimand is typically based on changes in the level and trend of AMR rates after $t_{0}$ over prespecified horizons (e.g. 12 months), in rates standardized to exposure (e.g. per 10,000 patient-days). Interrupted time-series (ITS) with segmented regression addresses such estimands directly by modeling pre-policy trends and identifying breaks in level and/or slope at $t_{0}$. Augmenting ITS with difference-in-differences (DiD) modeling with a contemporaneous comparator unit (hospital/ward/drug) strengthens protection against secular "history" effects (Campbell and Stanley 1963), provided parallel trends are demonstrated in the pre-period. Achieving sound and useful comparisons requires following the principles already discussed (Campbell and Stanley 1963): clearly defining the intervention (policy content, enforcement, scope), aligning measurement to a sharp $t_{0}$; ensuring adequate pre-intervetion data collection to characterize trend and seasonality; documenting co-interventions (e.g. laboratory platform changes, infection-prevention campaigns) that could mimic an effect; checking for spillovers to the comparator; and keeping susceptibility testing methods and case definitions stable across the series.

Recent methodological summaries in stewardship research reiterate these points as best practice-treat segmented regression as the default, include a control series when feasible, test for and display pre-period parallel trends, and incorporate falsification checks (placebo dates, negative-control organisms unlikely to respond to the targeted policy). When those elements are present, ITS/DiD estimates are interpretable as interventional effects on trajectories rather than as cross-sectional differences. When they are not-e.g. when a rising pre-trend continues unchanged after $t_{0}$ or
when a comparator shows the same break-the necessary conditions for identifying the policy's effect are not met, and conclusions should be restricted accordingly. In short, unitlevel stewardship evaluations can answer the intervention question directly, but only if the design enforces the temporal and comparability requirements that create a credible counterfactual path (Langford et al. 2024). In longitudinal studies of AMR and other health risks, a risk set is the group of units (people, wards, time points, etc.) that are eligible to experience the outcome at a given time and can therefore serve as valid comparators-provided they share the same time origin and eligibility at that moment, have exposure and covariates measured beforehand under identical definitions, satisfy exchangeability with adequate overlap (positivity), are subject to the same censoring/competing-risk rules, and are not affected by spillovers. In a patient-level casecontrol or nested case-control study, the risk set at the moment a case occurs consists of all patients still under observation who have not (yet) had the outcome; controls should be sampled from this set (incidence-density "density" sampling) so that exposure odds compare exchangeable candidates for becoming a case at that time. In time-to-event analyses, the risk set updates continuously as individuals enter or exit follow-up; hazard models (e.g. Cox proportional hazards regression) condition on the current risk set at each event time. Using controls (or comparisons) from the same properly defined risk set helps avoid bias from immortal time, inappropriate look-back windows, or population mismatch.

Table 11 summarizes common causal-inference challenges in AMR and links each to appropriate design responses that help satisfy the necessary conditions for valid interventional

Table 11. Examples of causal-inference challenges and design responses in antimicrobial Resistance studies.
| Causal-inference challenge | Why it threatens identification | Design safeguards and analysis checks |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
