| Temporal ambiguity/ no common time-zero | Exposure window overlaps or follows outcome ascertainment; post-outcome treatment contaminates exposure. | Fix time zero at culture/implementation date; define exposure strictly as days before time 0 ; exclude post-time-zero orders; prespecify biologically plausible windows. |
| Confounding by indication (patient-level) | Clinical factors drive both treatment and resistance risk. | Design-stage control: incident-user restriction; restrict on prior multidrugresistant organism (MDRO) colonization; match on ward and calendar week; prespecify covariates (devices, prior length of stay (LOS), comorbidity); document overlap. |
| Selection/control-sampling bias (case-control) | Controls sampled in ways related to exposure; source-population mismatch. | Sample controls independently of exposure from the same risk set; use density (incidence-density) sampling; verify that control eligibility mirrors case eligibility at the same time zero. |
| Immortal-time and alignment bias | Exposure requires survival/time at risk unavailable to comparators. | Align eligibility, exposure assessment, and outcome risk windows; use newuser (incident-exposure) designs; apply time-varying treatment methods where needed. |
| History/secular trends (unit-level) | Non-policy changes at $t_{0}$ (lab platforms, campaigns) explain observed break. | ITS with adequate pre-period; add DiD/synthetic control; document cointerventions; placebo dates; negative-control outcomes; show pre-trend diagnostics. |
| Seasonality/autocorrelation | Cyclic patterns or serial correlation masquerade as policy effects. | Model seasonality; use segmented regression with autocorrelation adjustments; prespecify frequency (e.g. monthly per 10,000 patient-days). |
| Measurement drift/ instrumentation | AST methods, case definitions, or coding change across periods/groups. | Lock definitions; audit for mid-series changes; include indicator terms if unavoidable; sensitivity analysis excluding change points. |
| Spillovers/interference | Policy in treated unit affects comparator; patient-level exposure affects others. | Choose comparators with minimal contamination; test for spillovers; consider cluster boundaries; report sensitivity to alternative comparators. |
| Positivity/overlap failures | Some covariate strata almost never receive one exposure/policy. | Check overlap visually and numerically; restrict to common support; avoid extrapolative models; report who is in-scope for inference. |
| Ecological aggregation/ transportability | Unit-level associations misread as individual effects; context-specific effects overgeneralized. | State estimand (unit-level vs. individual); avoid cross-level claims; justify transportability; include contextual modifiers or limit scope. |
| Outcome misclassification/ competing risks | Mixed colonization/infection endpoints; changes in testing intensity. | Use consistent, clinically meaningful endpoints; adjust or stratify by testing rates; sensitivity analyses to alternative definitions. |


causal inference. Where needed, complementary data-analysis checks are also noted, with details deferred to Section 5.
4.3.2.1. Measurement accuracy. QE designs that approximate randomized trials-such as difference-in-differences, regression discontinuity, synthetic controls, or target trial emulation-can strengthen causal inference by better approximating counterfactual contrasts if conditions such as positivity and conditional exchangeability hold (Hernán and Robins 2016; Igelström et al. 2022). However, the validity of these designs in practice also depends critically on how well exposures, outcomes, and covariates are measured. If they rely on poorly specified proxies or surrogate indicators, even structurally sound designs may yield effect estimates with limited causal or policy relevance. For example, if the outcome is mortality but the exposure is a vague or indirect measure-such as production volume or time-averaged pol-lution-then the estimated effect may not reflect the impact of a real-world intervention. Designs that use unvalidated surrogate measures-such as aggregated exposures or composite scores-require validation, triangulation, and crossdesign comparison to ensure their conclusions remain meaningful for decision-making (Lawlor et al. 2016; Hammerton and Munafò, 2021).

Even well-structured designs with adequate measurements can be undermined by biased or flawed data collection. To achieve practical validity, the sources of data and the sampling strategy must be clearly described and justified, reproducible, and aligned with the causal estimand and inferential goals. This includes demonstrating how sampling frames, inclusion/exclusion criteria, and data linkages preserve representativeness or comparability. Uncorrected sampling biases, selective record linkage, or undocumented data provenance can introduce biases that no analytic correction can fully overcome.

A necessary condition for valid estimation of a causal contrast is that measurement procedures must either be comparable across exposure groups or that any differences must be explicitly modeled, corrected, or shown to be inconsequential through sensitivity analysis. Measurement quality must also be sufficient to reliably detect the causal contrast of interest. Without such safeguards, differential misclassification-particularly when proxy measures differ in validity across groups-can produce spurious effects or obscure real ones. This includes misuse of surrogate endpoints (e.g. biomarkers not linked to meaningful outcomes) or proxy exposures or covariates (e.g. area-level socioeconomic status used in place of individual-level data). Recent work on risk of bias for QEs (Barker et al. 2024) has emphasized the importance of assessing whether outcomes are measured in the same way across groups ("assessment and detection bias") and whether the measurement process is reliable as implemented in the study. In studies that include surveys, these threats go beyond instrument validity to include inter-rater consistency, training, and comparability of measurement contexts. Designs should anticipate and document potential detection biases-such as greater clinical surveillance of treated units-which may lead to apparent outcome differences that reflect observation
intensity rather than true effects. Recent tools for risk-of-bias appraisal-such as the revised JBI instrument for quasiexperimental studies (Barker et al. 2024)-highlight the need to distinguish between three sources of measurement-related bias: differences in measurement timing or frequency (e.g. pre/post assessments), inconsistency in how outcomes are measured across groups, and unreliability of the measurement procedures themselves. Constructive design strategies to address these threats include ensuring consistent outcome definitions and timing across groups, employing validated and standardized measurement tools, training and, where possible, blinding outcome assessors, and conducting sensitivity analyses to assess the impact of potential measurement error or detection bias on estimated effects.

Even when a study's structure aligns with the causal question, valid interventional inference depends on measurements that preserve the meaning of the causal contrast. Inconsistent definitions, timing, or collection procedures across exposure groups can distort this contrast, leading to invalid conclusions-even if the broader design appears sound. Causal inference does not require perfect measurement, but it does require sufficient measurement quality and comparability to support a meaningful comparison. In observational studies, where group assignment is not randomized, such comparability is part of what makes groups analytically exchangeable.

Two related design threats merit particular attention. First, intervention administration bias arises when comparison groups differ not only in the primary exposure but also in ancillary treatments, protocols, or levels of oversight (Barker et al. 2024). For example, if one group receives additional monitoring, training, or regulatory scrutiny, observed outcome differences may reflect those co-interventions rather than the exposure of interest. Recent guidance from the JBI risk-of-bias tool (Barker et al. 2024) emphasizes that such cointerventions can undermine internal validity if comparison groups differ systematically in treatments or care unrelated to the primary exposure. Ensuring comparability across groups in all aspects except the exposure of interest is a necessary safeguard against biased causal attribution. Second, detection and reporting biases may emerge in administrative, policy, or surveillance data when changes in documentation, coding, or measurement coincide with exposure assignment. For instance, regulatory rollouts may trigger changes in inspection intensity, data reporting requirements, or performance incentives-obscuring or amplifying true effects.

What matters is not measurement precision per se, but preservation of the counterfactual logic implied by the estimand. If measurement timing or quality breaks that logice.g. by systematically differing across groups or pre/post peri-ods-no statistical adjustment can fully restore it. This is a design concern, not merely an analytic one, and should be addressed at the level of data collection protocols, not only model specification.
4.3.2.1. Measurement error and misclassification. When precise individual-level measurements are unavailable,
researchers must employ design-based solutions if valid causal inference is to be achieved. These include multilevel designs (which account for nested data structures and allow separate modeling of individual and group-level variation), validation subsamples (in which a subset of observations is measured more accurately to calibrate or adjust less precise data), repeated measures (multiple observations per unit to average out random error or identify patterns), and calibration models (which formally relate error-prone proxies to gold-standard measures). Measurement error should be modeled explicitly, and aggregate measures should be validated against individual-level benchmarks where feasible. Without such safeguards, effect estimates may reflect data artifacts rather than causal relationships. Technical methods to correct for measurement error-such as regression calibration, simulation extrapolation (SIMEX), or Bayesian measurement mod-els-are available, but they require additional data on the structure and extent of measurement error (Keogh et al. 2020; Shaw et al. 2020; Tao et al. 2021). There is a great need and opportunity to incorporate such data collection into study designs and to strengthen analytic implementation of these correction strategies (Shaw et al. 2018).
4.3.2.3. Heterogeneous treatment effects. Finally, a necessary condition for decision-relevant causal inference is that the study design allow for-and not obscure-heterogeneity in causal effects. Treatment effects often vary across individuals, subgroups, or contexts, and ignoring this variation can mask real benefits or harms and mislead policy or clinical decisions. To preserve and detect such variation, researchers should employ design-based strategies such as stratified sampling (oversampling key subgroups), blocked randomization (in trials), or purposeful inclusion of effect modifier strata in observational designs. In analysis, methods such as conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimation, Bayesian hierarchical models, and machine learning algorithms for heterogeneous treatment effects (e.g. causal trees or forests) can help quantify and communicate subgroup-specific effects (Athey and Imbens 2016; Künzel et al. 2019; Kallus and Oprescu 2024). Even simple pre-specified subgroup con-trasts-when supported by adequate sample sizes and theoretical justification-can help avoid misleadingly uniform conclusions. Designs that fail to accommodate meaningful effect heterogeneity risk producing averaged estimates that are irrelevant, misleading, or inequitable in practice.

A variety of models for heterogeneous trealnt effect (HTE) estimation are now available to support individualized or subgroup-specific causal inference across diverse causal questions and data structures. These include causal trees and forests, Bayesian Additive Regression Trees (BART), meta-learners (e.g. T-, S-, and X-learners), and heterogeneous IV models (Salditt et al. 2024; Abécassis et al. 2025). Each method requires careful justification of assumptions, including positivity, overlap, and covariate sufficiency for stratification or adjustment. While these models flexibly capture effect variation across observed covariates, they may miss important sources of heterogeneity that arise from unobserved factors, limiting the reliability of purely covariate-based approaches.

To address this, recent metholhave extended HTE estimation to instrumental variable (IV) settings. IV models with unobserved heterogeneity in treatment effects (UHTE) can, under strong and restrictive assumptions-such as binary instruments and treatments, monotonicity, and no covariatesidentify local average treatment effects (LATEs) (Mogstad and Torgovitsky, 2024). However, LATE interpretations are fragile: modest violations of these conditions can invalidate the estimand or render it difficult to interpret. Recognizing these limitations, recent approaches aim to improve robustness and interpretability by combining even weak instruments with rich observational covariates (Kallus \& Oprescu, 2024). These hybrid methods use machine learning to flexibly model treatment effect heterogeneity while retaining some of the bias-reducing advantages of IV logic. Although identification remains challenging, such designs expand thleasible scope of HTE estimation-especially in policy-relevant settings where both unmeasured confounding and heterogeneity are expected.

### 4.4. Internal validity and threat diagnosis

A valid design must not only support causal identification but also rule out rival explanations for the observed effect. These include the classic threats to internal validity in QEs summarized in Table 12 (Campbell and Stanley 1963). While threats such as confounding, selection bias, and attrition are often addressed analytically, their root causes are typically structural. That is, they arise from how the comparison groups were formed, not merely from how they are modeled. Without a study design that ensures exposed and unexposed units are exchangeable in expectation-through randomization, natural assignment, matching, or restriction-no statistical adjustment can fully restore valid causal interpretation. This distinction underscores a core principle: valid causal inference depends first on the soundness of study design and data collection, not simply on the ingenuity of analysis.

This principle is especially important in the context of selection bias and attrition, which stem not from statistical anomalies but from how study groups are formed and maintained. If participants self-select into exposure groups based on characteristics related to outcomes-or if loss to followup differs systematically by exposure-then even advanced adjustment methods can fail to restore valid causal interpretation unless the study design has ensured adequate measurement and identifiability. As Campbell and Stanley (1963) emphasized, selection is not merely a nuisance variable; it is often the most pernicious threat to internal validity. Selection bias can threaten internal validity by distorting causal estimates even for the study population, and it can separately limit external validity when the selected sample does not represent the target population. Analytic solutions such as propensity score weighting or g-methods can only succeed if design conditions-such as exchangeability, positivity, and complete measurement of confounders-are already satisfied.

More broadly, even when a causal contrast is present-for example, between exposed and unexposed groups-valid

Table 12. Quasi-experimental threats to internal validity.
| Threat to internal validity | Description | Proposed solutions |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| History | Unrelated events occurring during the study period that affect the outcome. These external influences may confound the observed effect. | - Use control groups exposed to the same external events but not the intervention <br> - Implement staggered time series designs to help detect and adjust for concurrent changes <br> - Document and statistically adjust for significant external events |
| Maturation | Natural changes within participants over time that influence the outcome. Maturation effects may mimic or obscure true intervention effects, especially in longitudinal studies. | - Use control groups that match the intervention group in age, time, or other maturation-related factors <br> - Use shorter study durations when possible <br> - Analyze differences-in-differences between groups to isolate the intervention effect |
| Testing effects | The effect of taking a pretest on posttest outcomes, potentially influencing results. Pretesting can sensitize participants and inflate apparent effects. | - Use non-reactive measures or avoid pretests when feasible <br> - Use equivalent control groups that also take the pretest <br> - Counterbalance testing order or implement alternate forms of the test |
| Instrumentation; changes in measurement procedures | Changes in measurement tools, procedures, or definitions over time that affect the outcome. These can obscure true effects or create spurious ones. | - Standardize outcome measurement procedures across groups and time points, using consistent tools, definitions, and data collection intervals <br> - Use calibration and inter-rater reliability checks. Document inter-rater or inter-instrument agreement, training procedures, and calibration protocols. <br> - Document and statistically adjust for any known instrumentation changes <br> - Analytic adjustment may involve modeling measurement drift or incorporating inter-rater reliability into uncertainty estimates. |
| Regression to the mean | Tendency of extreme scores to move closer to the mean on subsequent measurement, creating illusion of intervention effect. | - Include multiple pre-intervention measures (e.g. double pretests) <br> - Use randomized control groups to distinguish regression effects from true changes <br> - Avoid selecting participants based on extreme scores <br> - Use control groups with similar score distributions <br> - Analyze pretest distributions and control accordingly. Use time-series modeling or simulation to estimate expected regression-to-the-mean effects. |
| Selection Bias | Pre-existing differences between groups bias the results. Without randomization, selection into groups can confound causal conclusions. | - Use random assignment when feasible <br> - Use matching or statistical adjustments (e.g. propensity scores) to reduce baseline differences and control for observed confounders <br> - Ensure baseline equivalence through covariate analysis <br> - Use causal discovery techniques (e.g. Fast Causal Inference, FCI) to explore whether latent confounding or selection mechanisms could explain observed effects <br> - Use sensitivity analysis and nonequivalent dependent variables to help diagnose selection bias |
| Experimental mortality (attrition) | Loss of participants over time, leading to biased comparisons if dropout is nonrandom. High attrition reduces statistical power and may distort effects. | - Track reasons for attrition and compare dropouts to completers <br> - Use intention-to-treat analysis to preserve original group assignments <br> - Minimize participant burden to reduce dropout <br> - Assess whether attrition is differential across groups or related to exposure and/or outcome |
| Selection-Maturation Interaction | Groups that differ at baseline may also mature at different rates, producing effects not attributable to the intervention. | - Match groups on key baseline characteristics that influence maturation <br> - Use pretest comparisons and statistical controls to adjust for maturation-related differences <br> - Designs using double pretests help differentiate selection and maturation effects |
| Ambiguous temporal precedence | Difficulty determining whether the intervention caused the outcome or vice versa. Cross-sectional studies are especially vulnerable. | - Use longitudinal, randomized, staged, or staggered designs with clearly defined "time zero" to establish temporal precedence. <br> - Implement randomized or staged interventions <br> - Cross-lagged or mediation analysis models may explore directionality, but cannot compensate for design flaws. |
| Diffusion or imitation of treatments | Control group participants inadvertently receive elements of the intervention. This contamination reduces contrast between groups. | - Use geographically or temporally separated groups to minimize interaction <br> - Implement blinding when feasible <br> - Monitor and analyze for contamination effects |
| Interactive effects | The effect of the intervention may depend on the presence or level of another intervention or condition (e.g. policy, patient population, software version). | - Design to isolate key interactions (e.g. factorial designs) <br> - Pre-specify subgroup analyses <br> - Avoid over-interpretation of pooled effects |


causal inference is not possible unless the study design also addresses other plausible alternative explanations for observed differences. These include threats such as history, maturation, regression to the mean, instrumentation changes, selection bias, and differential attrition. These threats can be mitigated through placebo or falsification tests, robustness checks, and, where feasible, triangulation with other designs or datasets (Lawlor et al. 2016; Hammerton and Munafò 2021). However, no adjustment technique can rescue inferences from a study that fails to rule out such non-causal explanations. These classic threats have been systematized in recent critical appraisal tools, such as the 2024 revision of the JBI tool for quasi-experimental studies (Barker et al. 2024), which organizes risks into six domains: temporal precedence, selection and allocation bias, confounding, intervention administration, outcome measurement, and participant retention. This taxonomy complements the traditional typologies listed in Table 12 and can aid in operationalizing risk-ofbias assessments at the study, outcome, or result level.

### 4.5. Discussion: mapping classical designs to causal logic

Observational studies are frequently categorized by their design labels-such as cohort, case-control, time-series, or ecological-rather than by the logical properties that determine whether valid interventional inferences can be drawn. These labels are familiar, convenient, and often useful for orienting readers. But as emphasized throughout this section, they are not sufficient to judge whether a study's design supports valid causal interpretation. The same label may be applied to studies that differ substantially in how well they satisfy the necessary conditions for internal validity and causal inference.

The design logic and necessary conditions for valid causal inference discussed throughout this section seek to provide a more principled way to assess whether a study supports valid interventional conclusions. Instead of asking what a study design is called, we focus on whether it defines clear time zero and exposure contrast, ensures exchangeability through design features rather than analytic assumptions, and avoids threats such as confounding, selection bias, and temporal ambiguity. These conditions are not specific to any one design label; rather, they form a cross-cutting logic that applies across diverse observational frameworks.

To illustrate this, Table 13 maps common observational study types to the design-level conditions outlined above. It highlights the circumstances under which each design type can support interventional causal inference, as well as the common limitations that can undermine such claims. In doing so, it underscores the point that classical designs can be useful, but only when implemented in a way that satisfies the logical prerequisites for valid causal inference. This reframing is intended to help users move beyond relying on design labels as proxies for validity. A study described as a "prospective cohort" or a "case-control" design may or may not support causal conclusions, depending on whether it meets the necessary design conditions. The goal is not to
promote or dismiss any particular design class, but to reorient evaluation toward the structure of the causal contrast and the safeguards built into the study's architecture.

This principle-driven perspective also sets the stage for the structured checklist at the end of Section 4, which operationalizes these necessary conditions as diagnostic questions. Rather than treating causal validity as a judgment of overall study type, it enables a more fine-grained and transparent evaluation of whether a given design supports the causal claims it intends to make.

### 4.6. Summary: why study design cannot be an afterthought

When causal claims are made from observational data, attention often turns first to statistical models, adjustment methods, and sensitivity analyses. But these analytic tools, no matter how sophisticated, cannot rescue a study from a foundational flaw in logic: if the data were not collected in a way that allows for meaningful causal contrasts, then no model can make the resulting conclusions valid. Causal inference is not just an exercise in curve fitting or statistical control; it is a matter of whether the structure of the study itself permits one to answer the causal question being posed.

This section has argued that certain features of study design are not merely helpful, but logically necessary, if observational studies are to support valid interventional conclusions. These include having a valid comparison group, establishing temporal ordering, ensuring that exposure and outcome are defined with respect to a shared causal framework, and protecting against confounding and selection bias through the architecture of the study rather than post hoc statistical remedies.

The logic of causal inference demands that we ask: What would have happened to this unit at this time had it been exposed differently? A study that cannot support this con-trast-because it lacks comparability, temporal structure, or credible control for alternative explanations-cannot justify an interventional interpretation of its findings. Such a study may yield useful descriptive or predictive insights, but its results cannot answer causal questions.

Even in time-structured designs-where multiple pre- and post-intervention observations are available-the presence of longitudinal data does not, by itself, confer internal validity. Interrupted time-series, difference-in-differences, and panel designs can all support causal inference, but only if their underlying assumptions (e.g. stability of trends, comparability across units, exogeneity of timing) are justified by design rather than assumed in analysis. The same holds for threats to validity identified decades ago by Campbell and Stanley (1963): history, maturation, selection, instrumentation, and attrition. These threats remain as relevant as ever, and they are no less dangerous for being well-known. What undermined internal validity then continues to do so now-especially when observational designs are pressed into service to support interventional conclusions without sufficient structural safeguards.

Table 13. Classical observational designs assessed against design-level conditions for causal inference.
| Study design | Typical use case | Can support interventional inference when ... | Common limitations |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Prospective cohort | Estimating risk over time following exposure | Exposure precedes outcome; groups are exchangeable or rendered so by design (e.g. restriction, matching); loss to follow-up is minimal and non-differential | Confounding by indication; timevarying confounding; differential attrition |
| Retrospective cohort | Reconstructing exposures and outcomes from records | Same as above, plus: exposure and outcome measurements are accurate and temporally anchored; data capture aligns with causal time ordering | Selection on survival; measurement error; incomplete or inconsistent historical records |
| Case-control | Studying rare outcomes retrospectively | Cases and controls are selected independently of exposure; exposure assessment precedes outcome; matching or adjustment removes confounding | Recall bias; control selection bias; odds ratio may not approximate risk ratio when outcome is common |
| Case-crossover | Transient exposures affecting acute events | Exposure status varies over time within individuals; no carryover or time trends; exposure timing is unrelated to outcome risk outside target window | Time-varying confounding; carryover effects; assumption of no withinsubject exposure trend |
| Ecological/Aggregate-level | Population-level exposures and outcomes | Group-level exposure accurately reflects individual-level exposure; outcome differences are not confounded by other between-group differences | Ecological fallacy; uncontrolled grouplevel confounding; weak causal interpretation |
| Cross-sectional | Snapshot of exposure and outcome at one time | Exposure is plausibly exogenous; temporality can be inferred from design logic or known lags; confounders are measured and adjusted | Temporal ambiguity; simultaneity; inability to establish directionality of effect |
| Time-series (no comparison group) | Evaluating population interventions over time | Intervention is clearly timed; pre-intervention trend is stable; no other concurrent changes plausibly explain the outcome shift | History bias; secular trends; no control group to isolate effect |
| Difference-in-differences | Pre/post comparisons between groups | Parallel trends assumption holds; groups are exchangeable in trend trajectories absent intervention; no time-varying confounders introduced at intervention | Violation of parallel trends; anticipation effects; policy spillovers; dynamic confounding |
| Interrupted time series (ITSA) | Single group pre/post design with multiple time points | Pre-intervention trend is stable; intervention timing is sharp; no other events explain post-intervention trend break | Maturation, history effects, instrumentation changes; need for enough data points pre- and postintervention |
| Synthetic control | Estimating intervention effect with weighted comparison | Weighted control group reproduces pretreatment trajectory; no unmeasured shocks affect treated unit uniquely; treated unit lies within convex hull of controls | Extrapolation risk; sensitivity to donor pool; unobserved shocks; limited generalizability |
| Regression discontinuity | Exploiting threshold-based assignment | Running variable is not manipulable; units just above and below threshold are exchangeable; outcome model is smooth except at threshold | Local average treatment effect (LATE) only; functional form misspecification; limited generalizability |


In short, study design creates the logical conditions under which causal claims can or cannot be defended. It sets the stage, defines the contrasts, and limits what can be learned. Once the data have been collected, this stage is largely set. What remains is to carry out an analysis that respects the constraints imposed by the design. But if those constraints preclude causal inference-because the design does not allow for valid causal contrasts-then no analytic ingenuity can overcome that fact.

This is why design must be the first focus of any serious attempt to draw interventional conclusions from observational data. It is not simply a matter of good practice or better science. It is a matter of logical necessity.

The next section, Section 5, turns to data analysis: how estimation, modeling, and inference must proceed in ways that build on, rather than try to replace, the foundations laid by design. When the design establishes a defensible causal contrast, analytic methods can refine, adjust, and probe the robustness of effect estimates. But first, we summarize the necessary conditions and the corresponding checklist questions for study design to support valid causal inference.

### 4.7. Summary table and checklist for Section 4

The conditions presented throughout this Section 4 have sought to articulate logically necessary design-level prerequisites for valid interventional causal inference from observational data. These are intended to be not simply methodological preferences or best practices, but essential requirements that, logically, must be satisfied to support a valid interpretation of observed contrasts as causal effects of defined interventions in specified populations. They are also not merely theoretical; the conditions can be operationalized via a structured evaluation of study design features. The table and checklist that follow translate each logically necessary condition into a set of concrete considerations that practitioners, reviewers, and decision-makers can use to assess whether a given observational study design is equipped to support valid interventional causal inference.

inference, highlights its practical implications, lists supporting design features, clarifies its intended purpose, cautions against common pitfalls, and gives a concrete example. For illustrations of how these conditions map to classical study types, see Table 13; for examples of how they relate to standard validity threats, see Table 12. The table is organized into four thematic domains that follow the natural logic of causal study design:

1. Defining the Question and Data Foundations: clarifying the estimand, source population, and data provenance (B1-B4);
2. Providing Structural Foundations for Causal Contrast: ensuring group comparability, exchangeability, and identifiability (B5-B8);
3. Clarifying Temporal Logic and Causal Direction: establishing time order and avoiding simultaneity (B9-B11);
4. Addressing Internal Validity Threats: addressing sources of bias such as history, regression to the mean, measurement inconsistency, and attrition (B12-B16).

While Checklist A addressed conceptual and identificationlevel requirements (see Section 3), the Checklist B that follows focuses specifically on whether a study's design makes it possible to validly estimate the intended causal contrast. To support transparent reporting and structured evaluation of these conditions, Checklist B reformulates each designlevel requirement into a diagnostic question that can guide study design, peer review, or critical appraisal-similar in spirit to tools like STROBE (Cuschieri 2019) or ROBINS-I (Barker et al. 2024), but grounded explicitly in the logic of causal design.

Together, the summary Table 14 and Checklist B seek to create a bridge between conceptual rigor and applied assessment. They are intended to guide practical study design, promote clarity in reporting, and inform causal claims based on observational data and the designs of the studies that generated the data.

## 5. Necessary conditions for valid causal modeling and data analysis

After a clear causal question has been posed (Section 3) and a logically valid study design has been implemented and data collected to answer it (Section 4), the next step is to analyze the data in a way that yields valid and empirically defensible causal conclusions. This stage is sometimes viewed as a purely technical matter of choosing an estimator or fitting a regression model. But data analysis is, or should be, much more. It is the critical phase in which theoretical causal models are tested against empirical evidence, model assumptions are evaluated, and causal conclusions are either supported, revised, or refuted based on their observable implications. In this sense, it is "where the science happens."

This section treats data analysis not as an algorithmic pipeline, but as a scientific process. Causal hypothesesexpressed through structural causal models (SCMs) or DAGs-must be translated into statistically testable implications, linked to observable variables, and subjected to empirical scrutiny. This includes ensuring that the desired causal contrast is identifiable under the assumed model and the available data, that valid estimation methods are used to obtain causal estimates despite realistic challenges such as missing data or error-prone measurements, and that the

extent to which conclusions are warranted by the data, not just by modeling assumptions, is made clear.

This section is both lengthy and methodologically diverse. Table 15 provides an overview of the necessary conditions developed in this section, why they matter, quick diagnostics, default remedies, and "where to find it" cross-references to subsections and checklists. It is intended as a reader's map and running checklist-experienced readers can use it to jump to the parts they need, while newcomers can keep it at hand as they work through the section.

Valid causal inference from observational data depends on satisfying a sequence of logically necessary conditions during the data analysis. These include the following steps and technical methods, which are explained further in the following subsections:

- Specify a causal model to use in analyzing the data: Define a plausible causal structure-whether grounded in prior theory, elicited from experts, or informed by data-using DAGs or SCMs that explicitly distinguish causes, confounders, colliders, mediators, and outcomes. The model must be capable of identifying the causal contrast of interest and generating empirically testable implications.
- Identify the causal effect (estimand): Determine whether the causal effect of interest is identifiable from the
observed data under the assumed model and available data.
- Check that necessary conditions for causal effect identification hold (or at least are not falsified) in the data. This typically requires that the model satisfies conditions such as conditional exchangeability, positivity, and the absence of inappropriate conditioning (e.g. on colliders). Technical methods such as DAG-based criteria, do-calculus, and falsification tests support this step, given a DAG model (Textor et al. 2016). Section 4 addressed these requirements at the design stage; data analysis now tests how well they are supported in the observed data.
- Select and justify a valid adjustment set to control for confounders: A valid adjustment set blocks all noncausal backdoor paths without opening new sources of bias. Adjustment decisions must follow from the causal model-not from statistical associations-and must be empirically verified. Failure to appropriately select and implement an adjustment set undermines causal interpretation. This step ensures that subsequent statistical estimation that conditions on the variables in a valid adjustment set thereby targets the causal contrast of interest and avoids spurious associations due to uncontrolled confounders, overcontrol, or conditioning on colliders.
- Estimate causal effects from data: Translate the identified causal estimand into a statistical estimand and estimate it using valid methods-whether regression, inverse probability weighting, g-computation, or doubly robust machine learning approaches. Estimators must respect causal temporality, avoid conditioning on post-treatment variables, and be robust to empirical limitations such as limited overlap, measurement errors, missing data, latent (unmeasured) variables or model misspecification.
- Empirically test and validate the causal model and conclusions: Valid causal inference requires more than estimation. Analysts must also evaluate how well the assumed model is empirically supported by the data and whether rival causal models might be true instead. This includes:
- Use model diagnostics to test whether the assumptions and implications of the model are supported by the data. As discussed later, methods for carrying out this step include conditional independence tests, overlap and balance checks, residual diagnostics, invariance across environments (invariant causal prediction, ICP), and robustness metrics such as E-values or sensitivity analysis tools (e.g. Sensemakr, multiverse analysis, invariant causal prediction). Models that make incorrect predictions must be revised or rejected.
- Diagnose threats to validity, including any threats to internal and external validity arising from the study design and data (Cambell and Stanley, 1963) as well as statistical threats such as measurement errors in exposure and covariates, omitted variables, missing data, model misspecification, and violations of positivity or exchangeability.
- Apply adversarial strategies such as falsification tests and the use of negative controls to probe for residual bias, model misspecification, or violations of identifying assumptions (see Section 5.5.3). Negative controls are auxiliary exposures/outcomes chosen to share confounding or bias structure with the primary analysis while being causally null, providing targeted evidence about residual bias. Placebo tests are broader falsification checks (e.g. placebo timing, outcomes, or groups) designed to detect assumption violations by asking whether the design finds effects when none should exist.
- Use corroborative strategies such as triangulation across methods with distinct bias structures to assess whether different approaches converge on similar conclusions (also covered in Section 5.5.3).
- Characterize remaining uncertainties and test robustness of causal conclusions by examining whether conclusions hold under alternative plausible models and assumptions. Where appropriate, explore rival explanations and quantify the degree to which unmeasured factors could alter conclusions. Sections 6 and 7 expand on these strategies, including multiverse analysis, triangulation across study designs, sensitivity analysis, and value-of-information methods for prioritizing further work.
- Promote transparency and reproducibility throughout by following open-science principles such as preregistering
hypotheses and blinding analyses where feasible, documenting modeling decisions, disclosing assumptions, sharing code and data, and supporting independent replication. Where results are sensitive to key modeling choices, report that sensitivity and explain its implications for the strength and generalizability of the causal conclusion. Section 6 covers open-science principles and replication in greater depth.

In this view, causal inference from observational data is not a single statistical operation, but a multi-stage process that involves (i) satisfying logically necessary conditions for causal identification; (ii) constructing, estimating, testing, and refining causal models; and (iii) confronting those models with data through diagnostic and robustness checks. This section develops that process step by step. The goal is not to assume model validity but to test it-and to construct, revise, or defer causal conclusions in light of what the data can and cannot support.

### 5.1. Causal model specification

The first task of data analysis for valid causal inference is not simply to estimate a causal effect, but to fit and evaluate a causal model-one capable of expressing the hypothesized structure, identifying the causal contrast of interest, estimating it from data, and confronting the model's testable implications with data. Whether this model is supplied by prior theory, learned via causal discovery algorithms, or constructed from expert knowledge, it functions as a scientific hypothesis: a claim about how the data were generated, with testable implications and empirical consequences.

The essence of constructing a causal model is to (a) identify the direct causal parents of each non-exogenous variable, i.e. $\operatorname{Pa}(x)$ for each node $x$, where $\operatorname{Pa}(x)$ denotes the parents of $x$, e.g. the set of variables (nodes) with arrows pointing into $x$ in a DAG representing an SCM; and (b) quantify how the value (or conditional probability distribution) of $x$ depends on the values of its parents, $\mathrm{Pa}(x)$. Causal discovery software such as the bnlearn package in $R$ and Python (Scutari 2010) can help with both tasks, especially if human knowledge (or LLM knowledge) can be used to constrain the search for possible causal DAG models describing the data-generating process. In more complex settings-where relationships among variables may be nonlinear, high-dimensional, or subject to latent confounding-newer methods such as Doubly Robust Causal Feature Selection (DRCFS) (Quinzan et al. 2023) can be used to identify the set of causally relevant features (i.e. the parents) for a given target variable. A variety of non-parametric machine-learning methods for quantifying how $x$ depends on $\operatorname{Pa}(x)$ are now available to help to avoid biases due to parametric model specification errors (Moccia et al. 2024).

In observational studies, key identification conditionsespecially, conditional exchangeability, positivity, and avoidance of conditioning on colliders-must be operationalized in the data analysis and confronted with data using causal models. These conditions are logical prerequisites for expressing a causal estimand as a function of the observed data distribution. If they fail, no statistical method-regardless of
complexity-can restore validity. The following paragraphs examine how each of these foundational conditions can be empirically assessed, supported, or challenged, and what can be done when they appear to be violated.

### 5.1.1. Conditional exchangeability: comparing like with like

As previously discussed, conditional exchangeability requires that, conditional on a set of observed covariates, the potential outcomes are independent of treatment assignment. In DAG terms, this corresponds to blocking all backdoor paths from treatment to outcome via appropriate adjustment (Pearl, 2009). In principle, the DAG itself encodes the causal structure that justifies this assumption; in practice, it must be defended with both subject-matter knowledge and empirical evaluation using diagnostic tools.

Minimal sufficient adjustment sets-identified using software such as DAGitty (Textor et al. 2016)-offer a principled starting point. If a valid adjustment set exists for a causal contrast (effect), then the effect is identifiable in principle, based on the DAG structure. Analysts should also assess whether the quantitative value of the causal effect estimate is stable across alternative valid adjustment sets; instability may suggest unmeasured confounding or model misspecification. Invariant Causal Prediction (ICP) methods (Peters et al. 2016) offer a complementary approach by testing whether conditional distributions of the outcome, given proposed parent sets, remain stable across different environments, interventions, and subpopulations. Such invariance supports the plausibility that the adjusted variables are causally sufficient.

Flexible machine learning models-such as random forests, gradient-boosted trees, or neural networks-can support diagnostics of exchangeability and model robustness by improving covariate balance or predictive accuracy in propensity score and outcome models (for example, by helping ensure that treated and untreated groups are similar in their observed characteristics, and by more accurately predicting who receives treatment or what outcomes occur, given those characteristics) (Moccia et al. 2024). While these models cannot justify causal assumptions on their own, their use in cross-fitting or sample-splitting frameworks (Chernozhukov et al. 2018) can reduce overfitting and improve finite-sample stability, particularly when estimating treatment effects in high-dimensional settings.

While exchangeability is often treated as a purely assumed condition-especially within the Potential Outcomes (PO) framework, where the so-called Fundamental Problem of Causal Inference precludes empirical verification, as explained in Section 3-this is not the whole story. In Structural Causal Models (SCMs) and DAG-based frameworks, exchangeability violations may be empirically detectable. Conditional independence constraints implied by a DAG can be tested against data; and causal discovery algorithms such as Fast Causal Inference (FCI) can identify patterns in the data (including unshielded colliders or Y-structures) that reveal the presence of latent confounding (Spirtes et al. 2001). Although not all violations are testable, the SCM framework supports partial empirical confrontation of causal assumptions, which can
falsify incorrect models even when they cannot be fully confirmed.

Because the assumption of no unmeasured confounding is both critical and often only partially testable, best-practice guidance now recommends incorporating quantitative sensitivity analysis (QSA) into routine causal inference from observational data (Groenwold et al. 2010; Faries et al. 2025). Even when exchangeability cannot be fully verified, its fragility can-and should-be systematically assessed. Recent advances in doubly robust (DR) learners-such as the DR-learner (Shin and Antonelli 2023) and R-learner (Nie and Wager 2021)-offer promising strategies for estimation under uncertainty. These methods combine modeling of both the outcome and the treatment assignment process, and can yield valid causal effect estimates if either model is correctly specified (hence "doubly robust"). Related approaches-including targeted maximum likelihood estimation (TMLE), augmented inverse probability weighting (AIPW), and double/debiased machine learning (DML)-share this property, offering a safeguard against moderate misspecification of either the outcome model or the treatment assignment (propensity score) model (Moccia et al. 2024).

These techniques often benefit from cross-fitting and sample splitting, in which different subsets of the data are used for nuisance parameter estimation (e.g. propensity scores or outcome regressions) and for causal effect estimation. This procedure helps prevent overfitting-particularly important in high-dimensional (many-variable) settings-and improves performance in finite samples (Chernozhukov et al. 2018; Moccia et al. 2024). While these methods can enhance robustness and efficiency, their validity still fundamentally depends on satisfying core identification conditions such as exchangeability (no unmeasured confounding) and positivity (adequate overlap in treatment assignment). In other words, no algorithm, however flexible, can overcome structural violations of causal assumptions.

When exchangeability remains uncertain, quantitative bias analysis (QBA) offers a complementary strategy for evaluating whether observed effects are robust to plausible levels of unmeasured confounding-beginning with study design considerations and extending through post hoc sensitivity analysis. Tools such as E-values-which quantify the minimum strength of association that an unmeasured confounder would need to fully explain away an observed effect (VanderWeele and Ding 2017)-and the omitted variable bias (OVB) framework developed by Cinelli and Hazlett (2020)which benchmarks the potential impact of unmeasured confounding relative to observed covariates using partial $\mathrm{R}^{2}$ measures-support systematic robustness assessment.

These tools encourage analysts to ask: (a) What strength of unmeasured confounding would be required to overturn my finding? (b) Is such confounding plausible, given known covariate effects? and (c) How would my conclusions change if such bias were present? Importantly, they do not require specification of a particular unmeasured confounder, allowing for generalized sensitivity assessments that are broadly applicable. Open-source implementations-such as the EValue and Sensemakr R packages-enhance transparency, reproducibility, and accessibility for applied researchers. While these tools
cannot restore validity when assumptions fail, they provide essential insight into whether observed effects are likely robust or fragile to plausible levels of unmeasured or incompletely controlled confounding (Faries et al. 2025).

### 5.1.2. Positivity: ensuring meaningful comparison across combinations of covariates

As explained in Section 4, positivity requires that all levels of treatment have non-zero probability of occurring for every stratum of covariates used in adjustment. Without this, causal effects become undefined in certain regions of covariate space, and estimates may be driven by extrapolation rather than empirical comparison.

This condition is rarely satisfied perfectly in practice, especially in high-dimensional settings. Its plausibility can be assessed by examining the distribution of estimated propensity scores-plots of their densities by treatment group (e.g. kernel density estimate [KDE] plots, which show smoothed distributions) can reveal regions of sparse overlap or sharp cutoffs. Distributions of inverse probability weights also serve as diagnostics: large or unstable weights signal positivity violations. Machine learning models such as gradient boosted trees or random forests can flexibly estimate propensity scores and improve overlap diagnostics-especially when combined with cross-fitting to mitigate overfitting by using separate folds for model training and treatment effect estimation (Chernozhuko et al. 2018).

When positivity fails, possible remedies include trimming (i.e. dropping) units in regions of poor overlap (Crump et al. 2009), using overlap weighting (Zhu et al. 2023), or redefining the estimand to focus on the region of common support. However, these techniques cannot solve a fundamental absence of data: positivity violations limit external validity and should be transparently reported.

### 5.1.3. Avoiding collider bias: when adjusting does harm

Unlike confounding, where adjustment is essential, conditioning on a collider-a variable influenced by (a child or a common descendent of) both the exposure and the outcome, such as hospitalization-can open a spurious path and introduce bias. In some cases, analysts unknowingly introduce collider bias by adjusting for variables that lie downstream of both treatment and outcome, or for proxies that capture post-treatment information.

Detecting and avoiding collider bias requires careful use of causal diagrams and restraint in model specification. Analysts should avoid conditioning on variables that are children of both treatment and outcome or that plausibly serve as common effects of unmeasured causes. Sensitivity analyses that compare models with and without suspect variables, along with counterfactual reasoning, can help identify whether such variables are acting as colliders or confounders.

### 5.1.4. Bringing the conditions together

Exchangeability, positivity, and the avoidance of collider bias are central to the validity of any causal analysis, defining the boundaries of causal contrasts that can be validly estimated
from the data. They are sometimes treated as formalities, but in practice these conditions are often fragile and easily violated. Even when the study design is sound, analytic mis-steps-such as uncritical adjustment, undiagnosed overlap violations, or unverified assumptions such as that there are no hidden confounders-can undermine valid inference.

Fortunately, a growing suite of tools-graphical, algorithmic, and diagnostic (e.g. specification tests such as the classic Ramsey RESET test (Ramsey 1969), which detects model misspecification by checking for neglected nonlinearities or omitted interactions in regression models)-make it possible to confront causal models with data, evaluate their assumptions, and diagnose potential violations. To assess robustness across analytic decisions (e.g. different adjustment sets or model specifications), multiverse analysis can help identify how conclusions vary across plausible modeling choices (Steegen et al. 2016; Götz et al. 2024). While no empirical test can prove a model's truth, many tools can falsify models that are incompatible with the observed data, helping to narrow attention to those structures that remain viable.

Table 16 summarizes key conditions that must be satisfied for valid causal inference from observational data, includ-ing-but not limited to-the core identifiability requirements of exchangeability, positivity, and collider control. It extends beyond identifiability to cover threats such as model misspecification, mediator bias, measurement error, proxy variables, and sensitivity to unmeasured confounding. For each condition, the table outlines why it matters, how violations can be diagnosed using empirical or graphical tools, what analytic strategies may mitigate violations, and what limitations or caveats remain. Rather than assuming ideal conditions, the table supports a constructive workflow: one in which analysts identify where assumptions may fail, confront models with data, transparently assess robustness, and refine or delimit their inferences accordingly. When identifiability conditions cannot be plausibly met or verified, the appropriate response is not to force estimation anyway, but to acknowledge uncertainty, delimit the scope of inference, and identify what further data or design modifications would be needed to enable valid conclusions.

### 5.2. Clarifying the roles of variables: confounders, colliders, and mediators

A critical part of causal model construction and validation during the data analysis phase involves ensuring that variables are correctly classified according to their structural roles in the causal model. Misclassification leads to incorrect testable implications and biased effect estimates. One of the most common-and consequential-errors in observational causal analysis is misclassifying the roles of variables:

- Confounders should be adjusted for to block non-causal backdoor paths. A variable qualifies as a confounder if it (i) is a cause of the outcome, (ii) is associated with the exposure, and (iii) is not on the causal pathway from exposure to outcome-that is, not a mediator (Lederer

| Condition | Why it matters | Diagnostic tools/Indicators | Constructive strategies/ Remedies | Caveats and limitations |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Conditional exchangeability | Ensures that, given covariates, potential outcomes are independent of exposure. Without it, effect estimates reflect confounding. | Causal DAGs; domain knowledge; Stability of effect estimates across valid adjustment sets; Invariant Causal Prediction (ICP); residual diagnostics; falsification/placebo tests; outcome comparisons among pre-exposure groups; multiverse analysis. | Include sufficient adjustment set (via DAG); use causal discovery algorithms; use expert elicitation; conduct quantitative bias analysis (e.g. E-values, OVB formulas). | Multiverse analysis to assess robustness across adjustment choices; use QBA to assess sensitivity to unmeasured confounding. Exchangeability is untestable directly; bias analysis only informs robustness, not validity. |
| Overlap/Positivity | Ensures that every covariate pattern has both exposed and unexposed units. Violations lead to unstable or unidentified estimates. | Propensity score histograms; overlap plots; minimum/ maximum e(x); region-ofoverlap checks; KDE plots. Use flexible machine learning (e.g. random forests, GBMs) to better model propensity scores and reveal overlap limitations. | Trimming/extreme-weight exclusion (e.g. Crump rule); redefining target population; matching on covariates; coarsening; targeted estimation in supported regions. | Remedies may limit generalizability; inference conditional on trimmed population. |
| No conditioning on colliders | Conditioning on common effects opens bias-inducing backdoor paths. | Causal DAGs; unexpected associations between exposure and outcome after adjustment; sensitivity to collider inclusion. | Use DAG-based criteria to exclude colliders; test for sensitivity to including/ excluding suspect variables. | Collider status may not be empirically testable without strong causal knowledge. |
| No adjustment for mediators (when estimating total effects) | Conditioning on mediators can block part of the causal path and bias total effect estimates. | DAG inspection; time-ordering; sensitivity to inclusion of post-treatment variables. | Restrict adjustment to pretreatment covariates when estimating total effects; conduct mediation analysis only when assumptions are justified. | Mediation estimates rely on strong, untestable assumptions (e.g. no $\mathrm{M} \rightarrow$ Y confounding). |
| Measurement alignment and proxy variables | Misclassified or aggregated variables can introduce residual confounding and mislead modeling. | Compare original vs. coarsened variables; test proxy sufficiency via conditional independence; inspect residuals; discretization diagnostics. | Use validated or continuous measures where possible; conduct robustness checks; sensitivity analysis for misclassification. | Proxy sufficiency is rarely tested coarse variables may mask heterogeneity. |
| Model misspecification | Incorrect functional form or parametric assumptions distort estimates. | Ramsey RESET test; link tests; residual plots; comparison across specifications. Use sample splitting and crossfitting to improve estimator robustness. | Use flexible, nonparametric estimators (e.g. ML-based); apply cross-fitting and doubly robust methods; ensemble models. | Flexible models may overfit without regularization or sample splitting. |
| Model uncertainty | Multiple causal models may fit the data equally well. | Invariant Causal Prediction (ICP); variation across equivalent DAGs; instability of effect estimates across plausible models. | Test over multiple models (multiverse); triangulate across DAGs; report bounds or ranges. | No unique model may be identified; conclusions must be conditional on model space. |
| Sensitivity to Unmeasured Confounding | Even small unmeasured confounders can bias estimates. | E-values; OVB framework (Cinelli \& Hazlett); benchmarking vs. known covariates. | Use bias formulas; report robustness bounds; explore design-based alternatives (e.g. IVs). | Cannot restore validity; methods indicate fragility, not correction. |


et al. 2019). Such variables must be adjusted for to satisfy conditional exchangeability.

- Mediators should generally not be adjusted for when estimating total effects, but should be adjusted for when estimating controlled direct effects of exposure on outcome. Mediators transmit the causal effect from exposure to outcome. Adjusting for a mediator blocks part of the total effect of interest and will lead to underestimation of the total effect. In mediation analysis, mediators are central to decomposing effects into direct and indirect components, but this requires strong identification assumptions and a clearly defined estimand.
- Effect modifiers change the size or direction of the causal effect of exposure on outcome and give rise to heterogeneous treatment effects (VanderWeele 2009; Corraini et al. 2017). Effect modification is not a nuisance to be
controlled away but a feature of the causal data-generating process. Decisions about whether and how to model effect modifiers depend on the causal estimand: average effects implicitly average over heterogeneity, whereas conditional or subgroup effects require explicit modeling via interactions, stratification, or heterogeneous-effects methods.
- Post-treatment variables-any variable causally affected by the exposure-should usually not be adjusted for. They are particularly likely to give misleading effects estimates when incorrectly treated as confounders and conditioned on. Because they lie downstream of treatment, adjusting for them can introduce selection bias or block genuine causal pathways. Unless the analysis explicitly aims to estimate mediation or path-specific effects, posttreatment variables should not be adjusted for. This

Glossary for Table 16 and Section 5.1.
| Term/Tool | Definition/Purpose |
| :--- | :--- |
| AIPW (augmented inverse probability weighting) | A doubly robust estimator that combines outcome regression with inverse probability weighting to adjust for confounding. Produces valid estimates if either the treatment or outcome model is correctly specified. |
| Causal DAG | A directed acyclic graph that represents hypothesized causal relationships among variables. Arrows encode causal influence, and the graph supports identification and testable implications. |
| Collider | A variable that is influenced by two other variables. Conditioning on a collider can introduce spurious associations and bias estimates. |
| Confounder | A variable that influences both the treatment and the outcome, creating a non-causal association between them unless adjusted for appropriately. |
| Conditional exchangeability | The assumption that, after adjusting for a set of observed covariates, the potential outcomes are independent of treatment assignment. |
| Cross-fitting/Sample splitting | A technique used in causal inference and machine learning to reduce overfitting and restore efficiency. The sample is split into folds; nuisance models are trained on one fold and predictions are applied to another. Estimates are then averaged across folds. Often used with doubly robust estimators like DML and TMLE. |
| DML (Double/Debiased machine learning) | A doubly robust framework that uses sample splitting and cross-fitting to combine machine learning predictions of treatment and outcome models, enabling consistent and efficient causal effect estimation in high-dimensional settings. |
| Doubly robust | A property of certain estimators that remain consistent if either the treatment model or the outcome model is correctly specified (but not necessarily both). Methods like AIPW, DML, and TMLE achieve this property by combining separate models for treatment and outcome. |
| E-value | A sensitivity metric that quantifies the minimum strength of association an unmeasured confounder would need to have with both the treatment and outcome to fully explain away an observed effect. |
| Falsification logic | The principle that valid causal models must yield testable implications that can be confronted-and potentially refuted-by data. |
| FCI (fast causal inference) | A constraint-based causal discovery algorithm that infers causal structure from conditional independence tests, allowing for latent confounding and selection bias. Helps detect colliders and assess model plausibility. |
| Invariant causal prediction (ICP) | A method that identifies causal relationships by finding associations that remain stable (invariant) across different environments, datasets, or subgroups. |
| Inverse probability weighting (IPW) | A singly robust method for adjusting for confounding by weighting each observation by the inverse (reciprocal) of its estimated probability of receiving the treatment actually received. Creates a pseudopopulation in which treatment is independent of confounders, enabling estimation of marginal causal effects (usually the average treatment effect, ATE). Assumes no unmeasured confounding, positivity, and correct specification of the treatment model. |
| KDE plot (kernel density estimate plot) | A smoothed estimate of the probability distribution of a variable (e.g. propensity score), used to visualize overlap between groups. Poor overlap may signal violations of the positivity assumption. |
| Multiverse analysis | A robustness strategy that systematically explores how causal conclusions vary across a wide range of reasonable modeling and adjustment choices. |
| Negative Control | A variable, outcome, or exposure expected not to be causally affected by the treatment (or not to affect the outcome). If an association is observed where none should exist, this suggests unmeasured confounding, measurement error, or model misspecification. Used to detect threats to validity by identifying spurious associations. For example, testing whether a treatment affects a biologically implausible outcome or an outcome measured before exposure. |
| OVB framework (omitted variable bias analysis) | A method developed by Cinelli and Hazlett (2020) that assesses how sensitive estimated causal effects are to omitted confounders, using benchmarks based on the explanatory power (partial $\mathrm{R}^{2}$ ) of observed covariates. |
| Placebo Test | A variable or outcome expected to show no causal effect. Observing an association where none should exist suggests unmeasured confounding, bias, or model misspecification-e.g. if a treatment is identified as affecting a pre-treatment or biologically implausible outcome. |
| Positivity (overlap) | The requirement that all units have a positive probability of receiving each treatment level, given their covariates. Violations prevent estimation of causal effects in some regions of the covariate space. |
| Propensity score | The probability of receiving the treatment, conditional on covariates. Used to assess and adjust for confounding. |
| Quantitative bias analysis (QBA | A set of methods that quantify how unmeasured confounding or other biases (e.g. misclassification) could alter study conclusions. Tools include E-values, the OVB framework, and simulation-based sensitivity analyses. |
| Red teaming | A process for adversarially evaluating the robustness of causal conclusions by actively seeking alternative explanations, modeling choices, or unrecognized biases that could plausibly invalidate the primary findings. |
| RESET test (Regression Specification Error Test) | A diagnostic that tests for incorrect model functional form, such as missing nonlinearities or interactions, by adding powers of fitted values to the regression model. |
| Sensitivity analysis | A suite of methods that evaluate how robust causal conclusions are to potential violations of assumptions, such as unmeasured confounding or model misspecification. |
| Singly robust | A property of certain estimators that produce valid (consistent) estimates only if one component-typically the treatment model or the outcome model-is correctly specified. |
| TMLE (targeted maximum likelihood estimation) | A doubly robust estimation method that combines machine learning-based nuisance estimation with a targeting step to improve bias-variance trade-offs for estimating causal effects (e.g. ATE). Supports valid inference under flexible model specifications. |
| Trimming/Restriction | The practice of excluding observations with extreme or unsupported values of the propensity score, to avoid extrapolation beyond the region of covariate overlap. |


includes variables such as adherence, treatment modifications, or downstream biomarkers, which are often tempting to include for statistical control but causally inappropriate.

- Colliders (i.e. common children or descendants of exposure and outcome, such as hospitalization or inclusion in a retrospective study of exposed workers who are now deceased) must not be adjusted for, as doing so opens a non-causal path and induces spurious associations-a phenomenon known as collider bias. This form of bias can arise silently, especially when standard variable selection methods (e.g. stepwise regression) include colliders based on statistical associations rather than causal reasoning.

Accurately distinguishing among these roles is a prerequisite to sound causal modeling. Incorrect classification of variables leads to incorrect testable implications, invalidates adjustment sets, and produces effect estimates that cannot be given causal interpretations. Analysts must rely not on statistical intuition alone but on causal logic-ideally encoded in directed acyclic graphs (DAGs)-to classify and justify variable roles. As a basic safeguard, no variable that occurs after treatment initiation in the DAG should be included in the adjustment set unless the causal goal explicitly requires it. Adjusting for them can bias estimates by controlling away part of the causal effect, unless the goal is explicitly to estimate mediation or path-specific effects. Which variables belong in an adjustment set depends on the causal estimand being targeted (e.g. total effect, direct effect, mediated effect, or heterogeneous effects); variable inclusion decisions that are appropriate for one causal question may bias estimates for another.

Mediation analysis seeks to decompose total causal effects into direct and indirect components, as discussed in Section 3. However, this decomposition depends on strong and generally untestable assumptions, such as no unmeasured confounding between the mediator and outcome, and the absence of exposure-mediator interaction (unless explicitly modeled). As Igelström et al. (2022) caution, "These effect estimands can only be estimated given additional assumptions that are difficult to verify and may lack applicability." Moreover, as mentioned in Section 3.2, in the Potential Outcomes framework, formal definitions of natural direct and indirect effects (NDE, NIE) require so-called cross-world assumptions, such as $\mathrm{Y}(1, \mathrm{M}(0))$-what would have happened under treatment if the mediator had taken its untreated value. These counterfactuals mix incompatible worlds and cannot be empirically observed or verified (Andrews and Didelez 2021). Analysts should therefore approach mediation analysis with skepticism and transparency, and clearly communicate any causal conclusions as contingent on unverifiable assumptions. In empirical science, such models should be regarded as hypotheses-tentative and subject to robustness checks, rather than definitive causal decompositions.

In practice, variable roles are often ambiguous. Some variables may plausibly act as confounders in one model and mediators or moderators in another. Socioeconomic status, for example, may influence both treatment access and health
outcomes, or it may be partially influenced by prior exposures. In such cases, multiple models should be explicitly considered. Multiverse analysis-running parallel analyses under different plausible assumptions-can help determine which conclusions are robust and which depend heavily on specific causal role assignments (Steegen et al. 2016; Götz et al. 2024). Sensitivity analyses can also probe how effect estimates change under alternative inclusion criteria.

Many real-world causal relationships are not additive or linear. Ignoring potential interactions among variables when aggregating risks can result in misestimated effects and misleading policy implications. Conversely, testing for and interpreting interaction effects can sharpen insight into heterogeneous treatment effects (HTEs). Ignoring interactions among variables-especially those that moderate treatment effects-can yield misleading average estimates and obscure important heterogeneity. Analysts should test for and interpret interaction effects where plausible, particularly in stratified populations or policy-relevant subgroups.

Finally, variable roles can be undermined by data coding choices. Discretizing, averaging, or aggregating continuous confounders (e.g. using average income in a zip code as a proxy for individual incomes) invites residual confoundingthat is, confounding that remains even after conditioning on the proxy variable. Coding that misaligns temporal information, or includes proxy variables without showing (e.g. via conditional independence tests) that they are sufficient statistics for the original variables that they replace can all introduce bias. Ensuring that data structure and measurement align with causal assumptions is an indispensable part of this process.

In sum, distinguishing among confounders, mediators, post-treatment variables, and colliders is a necessary condition for credible causal inference. Doing so requires explicit causal modeling, careful alignment of variable definitions with temporal and structural assumptions, and thorough sensitivity analyses and conditional independence testing. When these practices are followed, variable selection becomes not a statistical convenience, but a scientific act of causal reasoning. To support this process, Table 17 summarizes key variable types, their causal roles, and the practical analytic strategies available for handling them. It extends beyond classifying variables to include practical challenges such as residual confounding from discretized variables, reliance on proxies for unmeasured causes, effect modification, and ambiguous variable status across competing DAGs. For each type, the table outlines the risks of misclassification, recommended analytic approaches, and diagnostic tools to test or mitigate those risks. By bringing together classification logic, empirical diagnostics, and robustness strategies, the table highlights that assigning and handling variable roles is a central part of confronting causal claims with data.

### 5.3. Identification of the causal effect: selecting and justifying an adjustment set

Valid causal inference from observational data requires that differences in outcomes between exposed and unexposed

| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Confounder | Affects both exposure and outcome; lies on a backdoor path | Identify via DAGs; adjust using stratification, regression, matching, or inverse probability weighting (IPW) | Check covariate balance; justify inclusion using subject-matter knowledge and adjustment set criteria (e.g. DAGitty) |
| Proxy for confounder | Imperfectly measured stand-in for a true confounder | Use IV methods, sensitivity analysis (e.g. E-values, Sensemakr), or proxy adjustment strategies | Evaluate residual confounding; benchmark against observed covariates; calibrate partial $\mathrm{R}^{2}$; check proxy validity |
