Glossary for Table 17: Key terms and tools in variable classification and confounder adjustment.

| Term/Tool | Definition/Purpose |
| :--- | :--- |
| Causal DAG | A directed acyclic graph representing hypothesized causal relationships among variables. Used for causal modeling and to determine valid adjustment sets. |
| Causal forests | A nonparametric ensemble method for estimating conditional average treatment effects (CATEs), allowing flexible modeling of effect heterogeneity. |
| CATE (conditional average treatment effect) | The average causal effect of a treatment for units with a specific covariate profile. Often estimated using flexible models like causal forests or meta-learners. |
| CDE/NDE/NIE | Controlled Direct Effect, Natural Direct Effect, and Natural Indirect Effect-effect estimands used in mediation analysis to decompose total effects. |
| Collider | A variable that is causally influenced by both treatment and outcome (or their causes). Conditioning on it induces spurious associations. |
| Confounder | A variable that causally influences both exposure and outcome; must be adjusted for to block non-causal backdoor paths. |
| DAGitty | A web-based software tool for constructing DAGs and identifying minimal sufficient adjustment sets for causal inference. |
| E-value | A sensitivity metric quantifying the minimum strength of association an unmeasured confounder would need to fully explain away an observed effect. |
| GAM (generalized additive model) | A flexible regression model that allows non-linear relationships between covariates and outcome via smooth functions. |
| g-Methods | A family of causal inference methods (e.g. g-computation, marginal structural models) that adjust for timevarying confounding and pl-treatment variables. |
| HTE (Heterogeneous treatment effects) | Variation in treatment effects across individuals or subgroups defined by covariates. Identifying HTEs supports tailored decision-making and fairness analysis. |
| IPW (inverse probability weighting) | A weighting method that adjusts for confounding by reweighting observations based on estimated treatment probabilities. |
| IV (instrumental variable) | A variable that affects treatment but has no direct effect on the outcome, used to estimate causal effects in the presence of unmeasured confounding. |
| Mediator | A variable that transmits part of the causal effect from exposure to outcome. Adjusting for it can block the total effect. |
| Meta-learners | A class of machine learning estimators (e.g. T-learner, X-learner, DR-learner) that estimate heterogeneous treatment effects by combining outcome and treatment models. |
| Multiverse analysis | A robustness approach that systematically explores how conclusions change across many plausible modeling choices (e.g. alternative DAGs or adjustment sets). |
| Partial $R^{2}$ | The proportion of outcome variance explained by a covariate, conditional on other covariates. Used in OVB analysis to benchmark potential unmeasured confounding. |
| Post-treatment variable | A variable affected by treatment. Conditioning on it can introduce selection bias or block causal pathways. |
| Proxy variable | An imperfect stand-in for an unmeasured variable. May lead to residual confounding unless shown to be a sufficient surrogate. |
| Sensemaker | An R package that implements the OVB sensitivity analysis framework, benchmarking unmeasured confounding using partial $\mathrm{R}^{2}$ and robustness values. |

groups can be attributed to the exposure itself-not to other variables that differ between groups and also influence the outcome. In randomized experiments, this comparability is ensured by design. In observational studies, where treatment assignment is not controlled by the investigator, it must be reconstructed analytically. The primary tool for doing so is statistical adjustment: conditioning on a set of covariatescalled an adjustment set-that blocks all non-causal backdoor paths from exposure to outcome and renders the groups exchangeable, given the assumed causal structure (Pearl 2009; Textor et al. 2016; Huntington-Klein 2021).

When such a valid adjustment set exists, the causal effect of interest can be identified using standard covariate adjustment methods. However, not all identifiable effects require adjustment sets: some may be identified via front-door criteria (Huntington-Klein 2021; Pearl 2009), instrumental variables, or other structural features of the model. Nonetheless, in many applied settings, identifying and conditioning on a valid adjustment set remains the most transparent and commonly used strategy.

It is common for DAG models to have many (sometimes dozens of) valid adjustment sets for the same causal contrast. This redundancy enables robustness checks: the same effect can be estimated using alternative conditioning strategies, and stability across these estimates supports the internal consistency of the DAG. Modern DAG software such as DAGitty (Textor et al. 2016) automates the identification of valid adjustment sets, enabling analysts to explore these alternatives efficiently.

Selecting a valid adjustment set for estimating a causal contrast is not simply a matter of plugging variables into a statistical model - e.g. choosing independent variables to include in a regression model. Rather, it requires articulating and testing scientific claims about the structure of the datagenerating process: specifically, about dependency relationships between variables, e.g. as represented in a causal DAG model. To justifiably claim that a causal effect has been estimated, it is necessary to do more than include covariates in a model: it is also necessary to show that adjustment (i.e. conditioning on the selected covariates) has succeeded in removing bias due to confounding, making the exposed and unexposed groups meaningfully comparable in terms of their potential outcomes. This requires a defensible causal model, appropriate estimation methods, and empirical diagnostics capable of detecting when these conditions are not met.

The process begins by identifying confounders structurally, not statistically, using a causal model. Confounders lie on non-causal backdoor paths between exposure and outcome and must be adjusted for to block spurious associations (Pearl 2009; Huntington-Klein 2021). On the other hand, as noted previously, conditioning on mediators, colliders, or their descendants can open bias-inducing paths and distort effect estimates. DAG structure-not statistical associationgoverns whether a variable should be included in an adjustment set in order to estimate a causal contrast. Tools like DAGitty (Textor et al. 2016) can be used to identify minimal sufficient adjustment sets, given a specified causal DAG. However, the responsibility remains with the analyst to justify
both the structure of the DAG and the selection of an adjustment set that aligns with the causal estimand, avoids unnecessary adjustment, and performs adequately in the observed data. DAG-based software supports adjustment decisions, but cannot replace the scientific judgment required to defend them.

Empirical evaluation of the adjustment procedure is essential, even when a valid adjustment set has been identified from a causal model. In many common estimation strat-egies-such as matching, stratification, or inverse probability weighting-successful adjustment is expected to yield improved balance in the distribution of observed confounders between treatment groups. As discussed in Section 5.1, diagnostics such as standardized mean differences, overlap plots, and propensity score distributions can reveal poor covariate overlap or implementation issues that may compromise valid estimation. In such cases, sensitivity analysis tools-such as E-values or omitted variable bias benchmark-ing-can help assess the robustness of causal conclusions to plausible violations of conditional exchangeability. While these tools cannot repair invalid assumptions, they provide essential insight into whether the adjustment procedure has worked as intended in the observed data and whether the resulting estimates are likely to be reliable.

Sometimes, bias is introduced not by omission but by overcontrol. M-bias arises when analysts adjust for a variable that is a common effect-or a descendant-of two unmeasured causes of exposure and outcome. Conditioning on such a collider opens a spurious (non-causal path) between exposure and outcome, inducing spurious association between them. These structures are not always obvious, especially when variable selection is driven by p -values, stepwise regression, or model fit statistics. As Lederer et al. (2019) emphasize, adjusting for more variables than necessary can amplify bias rather than reduce it. Confounder selection must be theory-driven, anchored in the assumed causal structure, and justified accordingly.

Confounding cannot always be eliminated by adjustment alone, since valid adjustment sets for effects of interest do not necessarily exist. Instrumental variable (IV) methods offer a potential solution, provided their strong assumptions are met. In settings where unmeasured confounding cannot be ruled out, instrumental variable (IV) methods may offer a partial solution. A valid instrument must satisfy three assumptions: (1) it must be associated with the exposure (relevance), (2) it must affect the outcome only through the exposure (exclusion restriction), and (3) it must be independent of all unmeasured causes of the outcome (independence). These assumptions are strong and generally untestable, especially the exclusion restriction. Moreover, IV methods typically estimate a local average treatment effect (LATE) among com-pliers-those whose treatment status is affected by the instrument-limiting generalizability (Igelström et al. 2022). Analysts must interpret such estimates with care and avoid extrapolating to populations not directly supported by the data.

Ultimately, confounder control is not a checkbox in a modeling workflow. It is a scientific claim about the comparability of groups, the validity of structural assumptions, and

Table 18. Justification and implementation of adjustment set and confounder control.
| Challenge | Why it matters | Recommended approach | Diagnostic tools | Remedies if not met |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Identifying correct adjustment set | Prevents confounding bias without inducing collider or mediator bias | Use DAGs to apply backdoor criterion; identify minimal sufficient adjustment set | DAGitty; domain knowledge; d-separation checks | Revise DAG; use multiverse analysis |
| Empirical evaluation of adjustment (see Section 5.4) | Estimation validity depends on correct functional form and model specification | Matching, IPW, stratification, regression, TMLE, DML | Balance diagnostics; model fit checks | Use cross-fitting; switch to flexible estimators |
| Avoiding overcontrol (e.g. M-bias) | Adjusting for colliders can create spurious associations | Use causal logic to exclude colliders; test via DAGs | Model instability when including vs. excluding suspect variables | Remove suspect variables; document rationale |
| Assessing unmeasured confounding | Even small unmeasured confounders can bias estimates | E-values; OVB sensitivity analysis (e.g. Sensemakr) | Robustness metrics; benchmark against known covariates | Report bounds; interpret cautiously; consider IVs |
| Justifying adjustment set selection | Transparency supports credibility and replication | Explain variable roles; publish DAG; avoid data-driven variable selection | Pre-specification; consistency with causal theory | Clarify assumptions; document revisions |
| Using IVs appropriately | IVs can address unmeasured confounding but require strong, often untestable assumptions | Two-stage least squares; LATE interpretation | Falsification tests; domain justification for exclusion restriction | Report assumptions clearly; test for IV relevance and independence |


the empirical support for those assumptions in the data. Claims of causal inference rest on this foundation. When variable selection is justified by theory, implemented with appropriate methods, and supported by diagnostics and sensitivity analyses, causal estimates can be scientifically credible. When these conditions are not met, statistical adjustments may produce only the illusion of validity. Table 18 summarizes methods and diagnostic tools for addressing the challenges of confounder control using adjustment sets.

### 5.4. Estimating structural causal models and causal effects from data

Once a causal model has been specified and a valid adjustment set has been identified for the causal estimand of interest, the next step is to fit that model to data and estimate the causal effect. This is where theory meets observationwhere the structural causal model (SCM), understood as a set of hypotheses about how variables generate one another, is confronted with empirical reality. The goal is not merely to compute an effect size, but to do so in a way that is logically consistent with the causal model, empirically supported by the data, and robust to plausible deviations from ideal assumptions.

### 5.4.1. Estimating effects in the SCM framework

In the SCM framework, causal claims are encoded in a directed acyclic graph (DAG) and a corresponding set of structural equations. Each variable is modeled as a function of its direct causes and a noise term-for example, $x=f(P a(x), u)$ where $P a(x)$ are the parents of $x$ in the DAG and $u$ is an exogenous error random variable. Estimating such a model from data involves two core tasks: fitting these structural equations to data and using them to derive causal quantities such as interventional or counterfactual effects. Depending on the analytic goal, this may involve fully specifying the SCM or directly estimating a causal contrast without fitting every component of the model.

A necessary precondition for valid estimation is identification, as discussed in the previous section. The analyst must
determine whether the causal quantity of interest-such as an average treatment effect (ATE), conditional effect (CATE), or local effect among compliers (LATE)-is identifiable from the observed data under the assumed model. This typically requires satisfying the backdoor criterion or applying do-calculus to derive an equivalent statistical estimand. Identification also depends on empirical conditions: sufficient overlap in treatment assignment (positivity), variation in key covariates, and adequate support across subgroups. Without these, estimation either becomes unstable or depends on extrapolation to unsupported regions of the data.

Once a causal effect has been identified-whether via a valid adjustment set (i.e. satisfying the backdoor criterion), the front-door criterion, an instrumental variable, or more general applications of do-calculus-estimation can proceed using methods appropriate to the identification strategy and the structural assumptions of the model. When a causal effect is identified via a valid adjustment set-meaning the backdoor criterion is satisfied and all non-causal paths from exposure to outcome are blocked by conditioning on observed variables (Pearl 2009)-estimation can typically proceed using standard statistical tools. A valid adjustment set implies that the observed conditional distribution of the outcome given exposure and covariates, $P(y \mid x, z)$, equals the causal distribution $P(y \mid d o(x), z$ ), where the covariate vector $z$ consists of variables in the adjustment set. In such cases, causal estimands-such as the conditional average treatment effect (CATE), defin-d as CATE $=E(Y \mid d o(x), z)-E\left(Y \mid d o\left(x^{*}\right)\right.$, $z$-can be expressed as a functional of the observed data distribution. Standard estimation techniques such as regression, inverse probability weighting (IPW), and doubly robust estimators (such as TMLE or DML) can then be applied (Moccia et al. 2024). As previously mentioned, these methods allow analysts to estimate the causal effect using plug-in estimators (that is, methods that estimate causal effects by plugging in predicted values from a fitted model into a formula for the effect) that rely on models for observed quantities such as $P(y \mid x, z)$, corresponding to an outcome regression model, and $P(x \mid z)$, corresponding to a propensity score model.

However, if identification is achieved via alternative routes-such as the front-door criterion, instrumental variables, or general applications of do-calculus-standard statistical estimation approaches may no longer apply. In these settings, the causal estimand typically depends on more complex combinations of conditional distributions or on structural assumptions that are not directly estimable through covariate adjustment alone. Estimating such effects may require modeling multiple intermediate relationships (e.g. mediator and outcome models in the front-door case), solving systems of equations, or using specialized methods such as two-stage least squares or G-estimation. The identification strategy therefore has direct implications for which estimation procedures are valid and feasible-and whether standard tools suffice or more specialized methods are required.

In all cases, once identification is secured, estimation proceeds by statistically fitting the structural equations to the available data. In parametric SCMs, this is often done using linear or generalized linear models (GLMs). In more complex or high-dimensional settings, flexible nonparametric toolssuch as random forests, splines, or neural networks-can be used to estimate the functions $f$ without imposing strong functional form assumptions (Moccia et al. 2024). These estimated equations can then be used to simulate interventions: for example, by replacing the equation for treatment with a fixed value and propagating its downstream effects through the system, thereby estimating the distribution $P(Y \mid d o(x))$. This procedure, known as graph "surgery" in SCMs (Pearl 2009) and supported by g -computation in the Potential Outcomes framework, allows for estimation of both marginal (meaning average, not incremental) and conditional causal effects, as well as individual-level counterfactuals when noise terms are appropriately modeled.

In many cases, however, analysts focus not on estimating the full model but on estimating specific causal effects using efficient estimators. Traditional approaches include outcome regression and inverse probability weighting (IPW), the latter of which reweights the sample to emulate a randomized experiment. More recent methods combine both strategies into doubly robust estimators that offer consistent estimates when either the outcome or treatment model is correctly specified (Moccia et al. 2024).

While plug-in estimators-such as outcome regression or inverse probability weighting-are straightforward to implement when a valid adjustment set is available, they can perform poorly when combined with flexible machine learning methods. In particular, when nuisance functions such as the outcome model $P(y \mid x, z)$ or the treatment (propensity score) model $P(x \mid z)$ are estimated using nonparametric or highcapacity learners (e.g. random forests or neural networks), there is a risk of overfitting. This occurs when the same data are used both to fit the nuisance models and to estimate the causal effect using them, potentially leading to biased or unstable estimates for realistic sample sizes. To address this problem, modern causal inference techniques rely on sample splitting and cross-fitting. In sample splitting, one part of the data is used to estimate the nuisance models, and a separate part is used to estimate the treatment effect. Cross-fitting
extends this idea by repeating the procedure across multiple folds and averaging the results, thereby improving efficiency and reducing sensitivity to any single split. These procedures are integral to doubly robust estimation frameworks such as Targeted Maximum Likelihood Estimation (TMLE), Double Machine Learning (DML), and the DR-learner. By decoupling model fitting from effect estimation, they restore valid inference even in high-dimensional or flexible modeling settings, and help ensure that machine learning tools enhance rather than undermine causal validity.

When treatment effect heterogeneity is of interest, models such as causal forests and meta-learners (e.g. T-learner, Xlearner, DR-learner) can be used to estimate conditional average treatment effects (CATEs) across covariate strata (Künzel et al. 2019). The term meta-learner in this context refers to a class of algorithmic strategies that estimate heterogeneous treatment effects (HTEs) by combining multiple supervised learning models into modular, multi-stage procedures. Unlike stacked ensemble methods in classical machine learning, these metalearners serve as frameworks for decomposing the CATE estimation problem into sub-tasks-typically involving models for potential outcomes, propensity scores, or imputed treatment effects-that can each be estimated using flexible regression or classification algorithms. For example, the $T$ learner estimates two separate models (T stands for "two") for the treated and untreated groups and computes the difference in predicted outcomes. The X-learner improves on this by imputing individual-level treatment effects and modeling them directly, often achieving greater precision when treatment assignment is imbalanced. The DR-learner incorporates doubly robust estimation principles, combining outcome regression and propensity scores to adjust for confounding while maintaining consistency under model misspecification. These learners offer accessible, modular frameworks for high-dimensional (i.e. many-variable) causal modeling, and can accommodate diverse machine learning algorithms as internal engines (Künzel et al. 2019; Nie and Wager 2021; Shin and Antonelli 2023). These methods enable effect estimation tailored to individuals or subgroups and support applications in personalized medicine and policy targeting. However, they are sensitive to sparsity and require careful validation to avoid misleading inferences. Their interpretability depends not just on algorithmic performance, but on the adequacy of the assumed causal structure.

### 5.4.2. Addressing practical statistical challenges: measurement error, missing data, latent variables, and model specification errors and uncertainties

Causal estimation rarely proceeds under ideal conditions. Key variables may be measured imprecisely, have missing values for some or all units, or be entirely unobserved. Models may be misspecified, either in functional form (e.g. incorrect regression model) or in causal structure (e.g. omitted variables or misspecified DAGs). Both forms of misspecification can bias estimates or mislead variable selection (Quinzan et al. 2023). Even flexible, machine learning-based estimators can be fragile when key confounders are unmeasured, mismeasured, or poorly represented in high-dimensional feature
sets. Without careful model specification and regularization, overfitting or underadjustment may distort causal claims. These problems differ in degree, but share a common feature: each introduces gaps between the data we have and the data our causal models assume.

Missing data can be seen as a special case of measurement error-where the error is infinite. Latent (unmeasured, unobserved) variables represent the extreme where a variable has missing values everywhere. Model uncertainty reflects a different kind of incompleteness: what's missing is not a value, but knowledge of the correct way to represent the data-generating process. All of these threaten valid estimation by distorting the relationships among variables, violating assumptions, or weakening identification.

Addressing such pragmatic obstacles is often a prerequisite for credible causal claims. These challenges require principled methods that can yield valid estimates even when full model knowledge and data are unavailable. Recent advances in doubly robust estimation, orthogonalization, and causal feature selection offer promising paths forward. By identifying causally relevant variables rather than merely predictive ones, and by accommodating latent structure, cycles, or nonlinear relationships, such methods provide tools for estimation that are not only flexible but also theoretically grounded and robust to structural uncertainties (Quinzan et al. 2023).

The subsections below examine how each issue arises, how it threatens validity, and what strategies-statistical, computational, and conceptual-can be used to mitigate their impact. Robust approaches such as doubly robust estimation, cross-fitting, and orthogonalization play a key role when full model knowledge is unavailable (Quinzan et al. 2023).

### 5.4.2.1. Measurement error: accounting for mismeasured variables in causal estimation

Even when identification conditions are met and appropriate adjustment variables are selected, causal effect estimates can be severely biased if key variables-particularly exposures, confounders, or outcomes-are measured with error. Measurement error in covariates used for adjustment can induce residual confounding, while misclassification of exposures or outcomes can attenuate (or, in some multivariate models, exaggerate) estimated effects or even reverse their sign (Carroll et al. 2006). These distortions are particularly problematic in environmental, occupational, and epidemiologic studies, where exposures are often proxied by coarsely aggregated or imperfectly recalled data. When error-prone variables are treated as if observed without error, standard estimation methods yield biased results, even under otherwise valid identification strategies. Moreover, even non-differential misclassification of confounders or instrumental variables-often assumed to be innocuous-can severely bias estimates and compromise identification (Hernán and Cole 2009; Shaw et al. 2020).

A range of methods has been developed to address measurement error in causal inference. Regression calibration, one of the most widely used approaches, replaces mismeasured variables with their expected values conditional on observed
proxies and other covariates. While intuitive and easy to implement, this method assumes classical (non-differential, mean-zero) error and linear relationships. Simulation-extrapolation (SIMEX) methods improve upon this by simulating additional noise and modeling the trend of parameter estimates as measurement error increases, then extrapolating back to the error-free case (Carroll et al. 2006). SIMEX can be applied to a variety of estimators, including propensity scores and structural models, but requires knowledge or assumptions about the variance of the measurement error. Bayesian errors-in-variables models offer a more flexible and principled framework, explicitly modeling the joint distribution of latent true values and observed proxies while incorporating prior knowledge and uncertainty (Richardson and Gilks 1993). These methods can be embedded in structural causal models, enabling posterior inference on causal effects despite uncertain measurements (Gustafson 2004). Recent extensions allow consistent estimation in the presence of measurement error without external validation data, using constructed instrumental variables derived solely from observed covariates under specific structural conditions (Miles et al. 2024).

Measurement error also poses challenges for causal discovery algorithms and graphical modeling approaches. In particular, it can distort conditional independence relations, which are central to structure learning in many causal discovery algorithms. For example, in a chain-structured DAG model $X \rightarrow Y \rightarrow Z$, the true variables satisfy the conditional independence relationship that $Z$ is conditionally independent of $X$ given $Y$. However, when all variables are measured with error, the measured value of $X$ may remain informative about $Z$ even after conditioning on the measured value of $Y$, because it helps infer the true (latent) value of $Y$. As a result, measurement error can create spurious dependencies or mask true independencies, undermining the validity of causal discovery methods (Zhang et al. 2017). For example, observed variables may fail to satisfy the d-separation criteria implied by the true causal graph, leading to biased or incorrect inferences. Even in theory-driven DAG analysis, mismeasured proxies may introduce unblocked backdoor paths or create new sources of bias.

Recent guidance from the STRATOS Measurement Error Working Group (Shaw et al. 2018, Shi et al. 2020) emphasizes that measurement error should be treated as a fundamental threat to internal validity, not as a technical nuisance that can safely be ignored-as it has been in the vast majority of applied work surveyed by the group (Shaw et al. 2018). Analysts are urged to examine error structures (e.g. differential vs. non-differential), explore robustness using sensitivity analysis, and transparently report the assumptions underlying any correction. Differential measurement error-when the probability of misclassification depends on the outcome or exposure-can lead to overestimation or underestimation of causal effects in unpredictable directions (Imai and Yamamoto 2010). Nonparametric sensitivity analyses allow researchers to evaluate the extent to which such errors could overturn their conclusions.

Graphical tools such as extended DAGs or "measurement DAGs" (mDAGs) have recently been proposed to formally depict mismeasurement processes and their implications for
identification (Wardle et al. 2024; Rudolph and Ross 2025). By including both latent variables and their imperfect proxies in the graph, these diagrams can clarify which biases arise from measurement error and how they affect the recoverability of causal effects. This approach also helps map traditional epidemiologic concepts-such as information bias-onto explicit structural representations (Hernán and Cole 2009).

When external validation data are available-e.g. biomarker substudy data or gold-standard measurements for a subset of observations-they can support calibration or validation modeling. In the absence of such data, analysts may benchmark their estimates using plausible ranges of measurement error severity and assess the fragility of causal conclusions across those scenarios.

Emerging approaches to measurement error in causal inference also leverage machine learning to address measurement error, including the use of random forest models to construct instrumental variables for mismeasured predictors (Yang et al. 2022), or kernel-based nonparametric estimators that generalize IV approaches to continuous, error-prone exposures (Zhu et al. 2022). While still under development, these methods offer promising avenues for bias reduction in high-dimensional or data-mined settings.

Table 19 summarizes the logically necessary conditions for valid interventional causal inference when key variables may be subject to measurement error, and Checklist C. 1 provides a corresponding checklist for practitioners. These conditions highlight that causal identification strategies-such as backdoor adjustment or instrumental variable analysis-can be invalidated if they rely on mismeasured variables without proper correction or justification. In particular, failure to assess whether measurement error compromises exchangeability, blocks or opens causal paths, or violates instrumental variable assumptions can result in biased or misleading effect estimates. Importantly, these threats apply even when standard identification criteria appear to be satisfied on a causal diagram involving idealized, error-free variables.

While Table 19 focuses on necessary conditions, several additional practices-such as leveraging external validation data, explicitly modeling latent variables, applying sensitivity analysis, or using machine learning-based correction meth-ods-can strengthen inference when measurement error is present. These strategies, though not required for identifiability, may improve robustness, transparency, and interpretability in applied settings and are discussed briefly in the final

Table 19. Necessary conditions for valid causal inference with measurement error.
| Necessary condition | Why this matters | Implications for practice | Supporting methods/Tools | Use case/Context | Common pitfalls | Example |

| Determine whether key variables are measured with error | If key variables (e.g. exposures, outcomes, confounders, instruments) are mismeasured, the assumptions required for identification may not hold. | Assess which variables are proxies and whether their use could bias estimation or invalidate identification. | Subject-matter knowledge; DAGs with latent variables; STRATOS recommendations | All observational studies relying on observed variables for identification | Ignoring error in an instrumental variable or outcome | Using a noisy surrogate for treatment without checking whether it satisfies the exclusion restriction |

1. Have key variables used in identification (e.g. exposure, outcome, confounders, instruments) been assessed for possible measurement error?
2. Has the impact of measurement error on causal identification assumptions (e.g. exchangeability, backdoor closure, instrument validity) been explicitly considered?
3. If identification relies on proxy or error-prone variables, have the implications for bias or misidentification been evaluated using causal diagrams or formal reasoning?
4. Have any assumptions of ignorable, classical, or non-differential measurement error been explicitly justified (e.g. based on subject-matter knowledge or empirical evidence)?
column of the table and throughout the broader measurement error literature.
5.4.2.2. Missing data: addressing incomplete observations in causal estimation. A stark but useful view from the potential outcomes framework reframes all causal inference as a missing data problem-not only due to traditional concerns like item nonresponse or loss to follow-up, but because the potential outcomes that define causal effects are, by their nature, unobserved. From this perspective, "even with no explicit missing data, all of the information needed to identify the average treatment effect is always missing" (Edwards et al. 2015). Estimating causal effects requires a series of implicit imputations based on assumptions-about the absence of measurement error, the exchangeability of observed and unobserved units, the ignorability of missingness, and more. When these assumptions are incorrect, the resulting imputations are flawed and bias can arise, even when the dataset appears complete.

Traditionally, missing data arise frequently in observational studies due to loss to follow-up, incomplete records, or nonresponse on key variables. If not handled appropriately, missing values can bias causal effect estimates, degrade precision, and obscure whether the necessary conditions for valid inference have been met (Sterne et al. 2009). However, the impact of missing data depends critically on the mechanism generating it. A standard taxonomy of types of "missingness" distinguishes among missing completely at random (MCAR), missing at random (MAR), or missing not at random (MNAR) (Sterne et al. 2009; Little and Rubin 2019). MCAR implies that missingness is unrelated to both observed and unobserved data; MAR allows dependence on observed variables but not the missing values themselves; MNAR allows dependence on unobserved quantities, including the missing value. Most standard statistical methods-including the multiple imputation and inverse probability weighting methods discussed next-yield unbiased causal estimates only under MCAR or MAR assumptions, and only if the imputation or weighting models are correctly specified.

The naïve approach of restricting analysis to complete cases is valid only under MCAR, and even then can result in considerable loss of efficiency-that is, increased standard errors and wider confidence intervals. More principled methods include multiple imputation (MI), in which the analyst fills in missing values multiple times using a probabilistic model, then pools estimates across completed datasets to account
for imputation uncertainty (Sterne et al. 2009). When the imputation model includes all variables related to both missingness and the outcome, MI can yield consistent effect estimates under MAR. Computationally, this is often implemented using iterative techniques such as expectationmaximization (EM) algorithms (Dempster et al. 1977) or Bayesian data augmentation (Tanner and Wong 1987; Enders et al. 2013), which alternate between imputing missing values and estimating parameters conditional on them. Alternatively, inverse probability weighting for missing data (IPW-MD) constructs weights that account for the probability that each observation is fully observed, reweighting the sample to restore representativeness (Seaman and White 2013). Both methods can be extended to high-dimensional (i.e. involving many covariates or predictors) settings using flexible machine learning models for imputation or propensity estimation, provided steps are taken to avoid overfitting or post-treatment conditioning.

Doubly robust estimators-such as augmented inverse probability weighting (AIPW), targeted maximum likelihood estimation (TMLE), or double/debiased machine learning (DML)-can also be adapted to missing data contexts. As explained previously, these estimators remain consistent if either the outcome model or the missingness model is correctly specified, but not necessarily both (Bang and Robins 2005; Rotnitzky et al. 2012). Recent extensions of DML accommodate missing covariates by integrating imputation into the estimation process via sample splitting and orthogonalization, helping to reduce bias due to model misspecification. In more challenging settings, where data are plausibly MNAR, additional tools such as negative control variables (variables known not to be causally related to the outcome, used to detect unmeasured confounding or bias), proxy variables (imperfect substitutes for unmeasured variables), or shadow variables (variables associated with the missingness process but independent of the missing value conditional on observed data) can sometimes support partial identification or bounding of causal effects when point identification is impossible (Manski 2003; Miao and Tchetgen Tchetgen 2018). These approaches can be combined with graphical models, sensitivity analyses, and expert judgment to evaluate whether plausible missingness mechanisms invalidate or merely attenuate or otherwise bias the causal conclusions (Mohan and Pearl 2021).

Because the mechanism of missingness is often not directly observable, its implications must be carefully inferred
from context, subject-matter knowledge, and the structure of the causal model. Analysts must ask not only whether data are missing, but whether the process that led to missingness plausibly preserves the assumptions required for identifiability. In particular, one must consider whether a complete-case analysis (i.e. analyzing only cases without missing data) is likely to be biased. This depends on both the missingness mechanism and the causal role of the missing variable. For example, if missingness in a confounder depends on the outcome, conditioning only on complete cases can open spurious backdoor paths and introduce selection bias. Conversely, if missingness occurs only in covariates and is plausibly unrelated to the outcome, complete-case analysis may remain valid, though potentially inefficient (Lee et al. 2021).

When bias is likely, a central question becomes whether multiple imputation (MI) or another principled method can reduce it. MI replaces missing values with draws from their predictive distribution, conditioned on observed data, and then combines results across multiple completed datasets. For this approach to yield consistent estimates under MAR, the imputation model must include all variables associated with both the missingness process and the outcome-including the outcome itself, if predictors are partially missing (Sterne et al. 2009). This requirement, though sometimes overlooked, is essential: failure to condition on the outcome when imputing missing predictors can attenuate exposureoutcome associations and bias causal estimates. Similarly, variables with skewed distributions may be transformed prior to imputation to avoid generating implausible values or distorting associations.

Recent studies have highlighted additional requirements for valid imputation in causal analysis. When combining MI with semiparametric estimators such as TMLE or DML, the imputation model must reflect the structure of the substantive model-including nonlinearities and interaction termslest incompatibility induce bias (Dashti et al. 2024). Substantive-model-compatible imputation strategies, which ensure alignment between the imputation and analysis models, are increasingly recommended as the default. Moreover, in high-dimensional settings, machine learning-based imputation tools (e.g. random forests, gradient boosting) can offer flexible alternatives, though they require careful regularization and may still falter if key predictors of missingness are omitted.

The decision to use MI should also weigh whether it improves not just bias but statistical efficiency (i.e. the precision of the estimate). When data are MCAR or when missingness affects variables that are weakly associated with the outcome, the gains from MI may be modest. In such cases, a complete-case analysis may remain acceptable, particularly if it preserves the structural assumptions needed for identifiability. But when auxiliary variables are available-predictors of missingness that are not part of the main causal modelMI can substantially increase precision by leveraging additional information (Lee et al. 2021; Holovchak et al. 2024).

Regardless of method, a sensitivity analysis is essential when missingness may be MNAR or when the structure of the causal DAG is uncertain. MNAR violations are not testable from the data and can lead even sophisticated methods
astray. Techniques such as delta-adjustment (which shifts imputed values by a fixed or scenario-dependent amount to assess sensitivity; Leacy et al. 2017), pattern-mixture models (which model the distribution of the outcome separately for observed and missing data patterns; Little and Rubin 2019; Little and Wang 1996), and selection models (which explicitly model the probability of missingness as a function of observed and unobserved variables; Little and Rubin 2019; Miao et al. 2024) allow analysts to explore how conclusions change under alternative assumptions about the missingness mechanism. Multiverse analysis (which systematically varies analytic decisions across plausible specifications; Steegen et al. 2016) and tipping point analysis (which identifies the smallest violation of an assumption that would change the study's conclusions) can further illuminate whether a given causal estimate is robust or fragile to such departures. In some cases, graphical models for missing data-such as miss-ingness-directed acyclic graphs (m-DAGs)-can reveal whether causal effects are theoretically recoverable under the assumed structure of dependencies between missingness, observed variables, and unobserved quantities, or whether bounds rather than point estimates are the most honest representations of uncertainty Mohan and Pearl 2021; Holovchak et al. 2024; Daniel et al. 2012).

It is also essential to ensure that imputation methods are aligned with the purpose of the analysis. Methods appropriate for prediction may exploit patterns in missingness that violate assumptions required for causal interpretation (Sperrin et al. 2020). A strategy that maximizes predictive accuracy-such as treating missingness itself as inform-ative-may yield biased or uninterpretable results if applied uncritically to estimate causal effects. Thus, the method used to handle missing data must be selected based not only on statistical fit, but on its compatibility with the causal estimand of interest.

In specialized settings such as mediation analysis or transportability studies, the stakes are even higher. Missingness in mediators, intermediate confounders, or effect modifiers can break the identification conditions for indirect effects or transportable treatment effects, even under MAR (Barker et al. 2023; Dashti et al. 2024). In such cases, the imputation model must not only preserve associations among observed variables, but also respect the temporal and structural roles of each variable in the causal pathway. Substantive-modelcompatible MI, structural equation modeling, or DAGinformed bounds may be needed to obtain valid inferences. In such settings, Bayesian estimation methods offer an appealing alternative to traditional approaches. For example, Bayesian data augmentation combined with mediation models allows analysts to incorporate prior information, avoid problematic normality assumptions, and obtain credible intervals even under missingness in mediators or outcomes (Enders et al. 2013). Simulation studies show that such methods can outperform multiple imputation or bootstrap proce-dures-especially when indirect effects are weak or samples are small-while accommodating flexible model structures.

In line with established guidelines (Sterne et al. 2009), analysts should report the number and pattern of missing values, compare individuals with complete and incomplete
data on key covariates, describe the variables and assumptions used in imputation models, and-where feasible-compare results across complete-case and multiply-imputed datasets. When a large fraction of data is imputed, comparing the distribution of imputed values to that of observed values for the same variable can help assess the plausibility of the imputation process. Sensitivity analyses under plausible MNAR scenarios should also be reported to gauge robustness of conclusions. This view is consistent with the applied framework proposed by Carpenter and Smuk (2021), which recommends prespecifying your approach to missing data (including whether complete-case analysis is reasonable), exploring observed vs missing data patterns empirically, and reporting diagnostic checks of imputed vs observed distributions as part of transparent reporting.

A practical decision framework-such as that proposed by Carpenter and Smuk (2021)-emphasizes prespecifying whether complete-case analysis is likely valid, exploring differences between individuals with and without missing data, and using auxiliary variables to improve MAR plausibility. When the proportion of missing data is large, or when conclusions are likely to be controversial or policy-relevant, sensitivity analysis becomes essential to assess how robust results are to plausible MNAR mechanisms. These steps help ensure that missing data handling is not only statistically defensible, but also transparent and aligned with the study's causal aims. These recommendations also align with the decision framework proposed by Lee et al. (2021) through the STRATOS initiative. Their guidance emphasizes three central questions: whether complete-case analysis is likely to introduce bias; whether multiple imputation is likely to reduce bias or improve efficiency; and whether sensitivity analysis is warranted due to potential MNAR mechanisms or uncertainty in the assumed causal structure. By following such diagnostics, analysts can more transparently justify their handling of missing data and evaluate its potential impact on causal conclusions.

In sum, handling missing data in causal analysis is not merely a technical preprocessing step-rather, it is an integral part of the causal effect identification and estimation process. The necessary conditions for valid causal inference include specifying a plausible model for missingness; inclusion of outcome, exposure, and auxiliary variables in any imputation model; compatibility between imputation and analysis models; and sensitivity analyses when uncertainty about the missingness mechanism remains. These requirements must be justified not only statistically, but causallybased on the assumed data-generating structure and the nature of the causal question at hand.
5.4.2.3. Latent variables and unmeasured confounding: estimating causal effects under partial observability. Much of the foundational theoretical research on causal inference from observational data in the SCM framework in recent decades has assumed that all relevant confounders are observed and appropriately adjusted for. This assumption, sometimes called causal sufficiency, states that all common causes of all pairs of variables are observed (Spirtes 2010). (Conditional
exchangeability, by contrast, is a weaker, target-specific assumption requiring only that-given observed covariatesthe treatment is statistically independent of potential outcomes. In the SCM framework, this corresponds to the condition that all non-causal (i.e. "back-door") paths from the treatment to the outcome are blocked by the observed covariates. While causal sufficiency implies the possibility of conditional exchangeability, conditional exchangeability may also be achieved without it if adjustment strategies exist.) But in practice, it is often-and perhaps even usually-the case that not all direct causes of exposure and outcome are measured. Some may be unrecorded, imperfectly measured, or fundamentally unobservable. These latent variables-also called unmeasured, unobserved, or hidden confounders-can induce bias by violating conditional exchangeability, thereby threatening the validity of causal effect estimates.

Yet the presence of latent variables does not necessarily preclude valid causal inference. Many classical and modern methods provide constructive strategies for detecting, modeling, and sometimes adjusting for latent structure-provided certain assumptions or patterns are present in the data. Rather than treating latent confounding as a fatal flaw, analysts can approach it as a modeling challenge to be confronted using a combination of theory, structure, and empirical tests. Classical statistical methods-including finite mixture models, factor analysis, and structural equation models (SEMs)-have long included latent variables as integral components. SEMs, in particular, can represent latent confounders using measurement models and testable constraints (e.g. over-identifying restrictions), allowing for partial identification or model comparison even in the presence of unmeasured causes. Though typically parametric, SEMs support formal testing of path coefficients, structural constraints, and fit indices, offering a well-developed framework for latent variable modeling (Bollen 1989).

Recent work has extended classical statistical frameworks to accommodate latent subgroups in mediation analysis, allowing for subgroup-specific indirect effects that reflect heterogeneous mechanisms across individuals (Kim et al. 2018; Wang et al. 2021). These methods use mixture modeling or nonparametric Bayesian clustering to identify and estimate distinct mediating pathways, even when subgroup membership is latent.

Machine learning-based methods have recently greatly expanded the toolkit for dealing with latent confounding, especially when researchers suspect that key unmeasured causes are indirectly reflected in available data. For example, methods like the Causal Effect Variational Autoencoder (CEVAE; Louizos et al. 2017) use flexible, data-driven models to infer hidden causes from high-dimensional proxies-such as lab measurements, behavioral logs, or demographic profiles. These approaches rely on a machine learning architecture known as the variational autoencoder (VAE), which learns a latent-variable model that jointly estimates the hidden confounders and their influence on treatment and outcomeusing proxy variables without treating them as direct substitutes. (A VAE uses an encoder-decoder architecture trained via variational inference, a method that approximates complex posterior distributions by optimizing over a simpler
family of distributions, enabling efficient latent-variable modeling even when exact inference is intractable.) Under assumptions about the relationship between observed proxies, latent causes, and the causal graph structure, the VAE's latent space can be interpreted as capturing unmeasured confounding.

More recent advances seek to disentangle different kinds of hidden structure-for example, separating latent variables that causally influence the outcome from those that represent irrelevant noise or instrumental effects. Methods such as Treatment-Effect Deconfounding VAE (TEDVAE; Zhang et al. 2021) aim to improve robustness by isolating only the causally relevant dimensions of latent variation. A recent extension, InfoCEVAE (Harada and Kashima 2024), augments this approach by explicitly encouraging the latent representation to be both informative about potential outcomes and balanced across treatment groups. This is achieved by optimizing mutual information criteria and enforcing latent matching, thereby reducing bias from hidden confounders while improving interpretability and robustness under weaker assumptions. A complementary approach, MIM-DRCFR (Cheng et al. 2022), minimizes mutual information between latent factors to encourage independence among confounding, instrumental, and adjustment components. This disentanglement supports more reliable effect estimation, particularly in high-dimensional settings where overlapping causal mechanisms may otherwise obscure identification. In biomedical applications where training data (e.g. cell-line screens) differ systematically from target domains (e.g. patient data), context-aware autoencoders like CODE-AE (He et al. 2022) extend the VAE framework by disentangling intrinsic biological signals from context-induced confound-ers-substantially improving generalization and robustness. While these models offer flexibility and scalability, their causal validity depends on assumptions about how the latent space encodes confounding and whether it is sufficient to block all back-door paths from treatment to outcome.

Graphical models provide complementary, nonparametric tools for discovering causal relationships, even when not all variables are observed. One influential example is the Fast Causal Inference (FCI) algorithm (Spirtes et al. 2001), which can infer aspects of the underlying causal structure even in the presence of latent (unmeasured) variables. FCI outputs a structure known as a partial ancestral graph (PAG)-a type of graphical model that encodes both observed associations and uncertainty about possible hidden causes. PAGs can be interpreted as projections of the full (but unobserved) causal graph onto the space of observed variables, preserving key ancestral relationships even when some variables are missing. In favorable cases-such as when a Y-structure is present (i.e. two independent causes jointly influence a common effect)FCI can distinguish genuine causal paths from spurious associations due to latent confounding. This makes it a useful diagnostic tool in high-dimensional or observational studies where full adjustment is impossible.

More recent developments extend this framework in several ways. In particular, score-based causal discovery approaches-such as those developed by Ng et al. (2024)use objective functions specifically designed to account for
latent structure. An additional hybrid method, GFCI (Greedy Fast Causal Inference), combines elements of score-based search and constraint-based refinement to handle latent confounding more flexibly (Ogarrio et al. 2016). GFCl uses a score-guided search to generate candidate graphs, then applies constraint-based checks to refine the structure-making it both computationally efficient and more robust to unmeasured variables in practice. These methods offer formal identifiability guarantees under certain conditions and tend to be more robust than traditional constraint-based methods when conditional independence tests are unreliable or noisy.

While these graphical and score-based algorithms aim to reconstruct the broader causal structure, another promising line of work focuses more narrowly on identifying direct causes of specific outcomes. For example, doubly robust causal feature selection (e.g. DRCFS; Quinzan et al. 2023) aims to identify the direct causal parents of a target variable, even in nonlinear, high-dimensional settings where some confounders may be unobserved. Rather than attempting to recover the full causal graph or relying solely on conditional independence tests, DRCFS assesses whether intervening on a candidate variable changes the expected value of the outcome, controlling for others. It does so using a doubly robust estimation strategy-built on the double machine learning framework-which combines two models: one that predicts the outcome given covariates, and another that models the treatment (or input) assignment. This approach improves robustness by yielding consistent estimates if either model is correctly specified (but not necessarily both), and can help uncover causally relevant features even when latent confounding is present. Under specific assumptions-such as no feedback from outcome to predictors and independence between error terms and predictors-this approach can deliver statistically efficient estimates: that is, estimates that converge quickly to the truth as the sample size increases (at the optimal $\sqrt{ } s$ rate), and remain valid even if one of the models is misspecified. This allows practitioners to pinpoint causally relevant features without needing to observe or adjust for every confounding factor directly.

In practice, practitioners have two broad strategic options when confronting latent confounding, as synthesized by Zhu et al. (2024). Circumvention-based methods aim to sidestep direct modeling of latent variables by leveraging identification strategies such as instrumental variables, front-door adjustment, proxy variables with known structure, or limited experimental data. These are effective when strong auxiliary information or quasi-experiments are available. Inferencebased methods, by contrast, attempt to reconstruct latent variables or their effects from observed data, using techniques such as structural equation models (SEMs), variational autoencoders (VAEs), double machine learning, or score-based causal discovery. Each class of methods carries distinct assumptions and tradeoffs, depending on the setting, identifiability conditions, and available covariates.

Sensitivity analysis tools-such as the E-value, sensemakr, and Rosenbaum bounds (Rosenbaum and Silber 2009; Heng and Kang 2024)-quantify how strongly an unmeasured confounder would need to be associated with both exposure and outcome to explain away an observed effect
(VanderWeele and Ding 2017; Cinelli and Hazlett 2020). While such analyses do not correct for bias, they help assess the robustness of inferences under plausible violations of conditional exchangeability.

Finally, negative control variables offer an empirically testable strategy: if a variable known not to be causally affected by treatment (or not to affect the outcome) nonetheless shows a strong association, it may indicate unmeasured confounding or model misspecification (Lipsitch et al. 2010). In many fields, including environmental health, economics, and pharmacoepidemiology, such falsification tests have become a key component of causal model validation.

Although many of these methods assume acyclic causal structure, recent advances have extended structural causal models to include latent variables and feedback loops. For example, Bongers et al. (2021) propose a general framework for SCMs with cycles and latent confounders, showing that many of the desirable properties of acyclic models can be recovered under solvability conditions. In particular, they show that interventional distributions remain well-defined in systems with feedback loops as long as the structural equations are solvable. This is especially relevant in settings such as ecology, macroeconomics, neuroscience, and other domains where equilibrium dynamics, regulation, or reciprocal causation are common.

In short, latent variables complicate-but need not pro-hibit-valid causal inference. Recent advances have shown that analysts are not limited to either naïve assumptions or defeatist conclusions. Instead, a growing toolkit of creative strategiesranging from structural learning (causal discovery of DAG or PAG graph structures) with partial observability, to disentangled deep latent-variable models, to doubly robust causal feature selection-offers practical paths forward even under severe hidden confounding. Whether through design-based circumvention or inference-based methods, the key is to combine structural assumptions, testable implications, auxiliary data, and modern computational tools to identify and estimate causal impacts despite the effects of latent variables. Rather than assuming unmeasured confounding away, analysts should test for its presence, model its possible impact explicitly, and transparently communicate how conclusions might change under alternative plausible assumptions. The growing synthesis of causal theory, machine learning, and sensitivity analysis makes this both more feasible and more essential than ever-especially in complex, high-stakes domains where latent variables are more often the rule than the exception.

Table 21 summarizes necessary conditions for valid causal inference in the presence of latent variables, along with common pitfalls, methodological tools, and example contexts. Checklist C. 3 provides a checklist for assessing whether these conditions have been adequately addressed in a given study. Together, they are intended to guide analysts in designing, diagnosing, and transparently reporting causal inferences where unmeasured confounding or other latent structures may be present.

### 5.4.2.4. Model misspecification and estimator robustness.

Even when a causal estimand is identified and relevant variables are correctly selected, estimation procedures must still
model how the outcome depends on the exposure and covariates. If these models are incorrectly specified-e.g. by assuming linearity when the true relationship is nonlinearthen causal estimates may be biased, confidence intervals may be invalid, and conclusions may be misleading. Model misspecification is often addressable-but only if it is recognized and treated explicitly.

At its core, model misspecification arises when the statistical estimand used in estimation diverges from the identified causal estimand due to incorrect assumptions about functional form, distributional structure, effect heterogeneity, or interactions. This divergence can occur in both parametric and nonparametric settings, and may affect any component of the estimation pipeline: outcome models, treatment models, or variance estimation. This issue is especially relevant in doubly robust estimation frameworks, which rely on at least one correctly specified model to yield consistent estimates.

Causal estimators differ in their sensitivity to model assumptions. Simple outcome regression methods rely heavily on correct model specification, especially when covariate overlap is weak. In contrast, doubly robust estimators-such as targeted maximum likelihood estimation (TMLE), augmented inverse probability weighting (AIPW), and debiased machine learning (DML)-offer protection against some forms of misspecification, provided at least one nuisance model is correctly specified (Goetghebeur et al. 2020). However, this protection against misspecification holds only when all confounders are observed; in the presence of hidden confounding, even doubly robust methods may yield biased estimates (Narita et al. 2023). Moreover, these estimators remain vulnerable to other forms of structural misalignment, such as omitted interactions, poorly captured nonlinearity, or incorrect causal graphs. Modern approaches-especially those incorporating cross-fitting, sample splitting, and nonparametric or machine learning meth-ods-can mitigate misspecification risk by allowing the data to inform the shape of functional relationships. Crucially, these tools must still respect the underlying causal structure; flexibility alone does not guarantee causal validity.

Recent work emphasizes that even among doubly robust estimators, performance can vary substantially depending on model selection strategy and how well covariate-balancing properties are preserved (i.e. how well the estimator ensures that treated and control groups are similar on observed confounders) (Ning et al. 2020). Penalized M-estimators (an Mestimator is any estimator obtained by minimizing a loss function measuring the mismatch between model predictions and the observed data, such as least squares or maximum likelihood) with covariate balancing steps (i.e. regularized methods that directly enforce covariate balance between treatment groups) help mitigate the impact of model misspe-cification-especially in high-dimensional settings where correct model specification is unlikely and overfitting is a major risk. Matching estimators (i.e. methods that compare treated and untreated units with similar covariate profiles, typically based on a distance metric or propensity score) exhibit a different form of robustness: when the misspecified model still serves as a covariate score-that is, it balances the distribution of observed covariates across treatment groups-

| Explicitly consider the possibility of latent confounding | Unmeasured common causes can bias effect estimates even when observed covariates are well-adjusted | Use subject matter expertise and causal diagrams to assess plausibility of latent confounders | DAGs, partial ancestral graphs (PAGs), sensitivity analysis frameworks, negative controls | Observational studies without full covariate data | Assuming ignorability or exchangeability without justification | Failing to adjust for parental mental health in a study of childhood trauma and adult depression |
| Distinguish between circumventable and noncircumventable latent confounding | Some latent structures can be addressed using identification strategies; others require modeling or sensitivity analysis | Classify latent variables as addressable via design (e.g. IVs) vs. needing latent reconstruction | Instrumental variable analysis, front-door adjustment, negative controls, Zhu et al. (2024) framework | Studies with potential instruments, mediators, or proxies | Overrelying on weak or invalid instruments | Using geographic proximity as an IV without checking exclusion restrictions |
| Test for the presence or impact of latent confounding | Causal conclusions should not rest on untestable assumptions when diagnostics or proxy indicators are available | Use statistical tests, proxy comparisons, or model fit diagnostics to detect unmeasured confounding | Partial ancestral graphs, overidentification tests, negative controls | High-stakes inference from nonrandomized data | Treating absence of evidence as evidence of absence | Assuming confounding is minimal in an analysis with known measurement limitations |
| If using latent variable models for adjustment, justify their assumptions clearly | Misuse or misinterpretation of latent variable models can lead to greater bias than no adjustment | Articulate and defend assumptions such as latent ignorability, completeness, and factorization | SEMs, VAEs, TEDVAE, InfoCEVAE, DRCFR, CODE-AE | High-dimensional settings with proxy variables | Treating VAE embeddings as valid confounder adjustments without validation | Using VAE output as covariates without confirming balance or causal structure |
| Evaluate robustness to unmeasured confounding | Even with latent adjustment, estimates may be sensitive to misspecification or unobserved structure | Conduct formal sensitivity analysis or test alternative plausible models | Rosenbaum bounds, E-value, sensemakr | Biomedical or policy applications where unmeasured variables are likely | Reporting point estimates without uncertainty from latent bias | Reporting a causal effect of treatment without noting sensitivity to hidden severity indicators |
| If identification is partial, limit causal claims to effect components that are identifiable under latent structure assumptions | Only certain causal effects may be identifiable in the presence of latent variables | Focus on estimands that can be bounded, partially identified, or robustly estimated | Partial identification, bounding methods, frontdoor estimators | Studies where full adjustment is impossible but partial mediation is known | Overstating identifiability when only bounds are available | Claiming total effect identification in mediation analysis without confounder control |
| Transparently communicate assumptions and limitations | Scientific integrity and replicability require clarity about latent structure assumptions | Make explicit which variables are unobserved, what assumptions were made, and how conclusions might change | Study preregistration, DAG annotations, sensitivity analysis reports | All nonrandomized studies with model-based inference | Obscuring key assumptions or implying causal certainty | Omitting limitations of a latent confounder adjustment in policy recommendations |


Checklist C.3. Conditions for valid causal inference with latent variables.

| Checklist question | Response (Yes/No/Partly/N/A) | Explanation/evidence/Caveats |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1. Has the possibility of latent confounding been explicitly assessed, and ruled out only with strong justification (e.g. via subject matter knowledge, design features, or causal graphs)? |  |  |
| 2. If latent confounding is plausible, has it been classified as circumventable (e.g. via IVs, frontdoor, negative controls) or requiring latent reconstruction (e.g. SEMs, VAEs, proxy models)? |  |  |
| 3. If no adjustment is made for unmeasured confounding, have diagnostic tests or proxy-based falsification checks been conducted to assess its likely impact? |  |  |
| 4. If latent variable models (e.g. SEMs, VAEs, TEDVAE) are used for adjustment, have their core identification assumptions (e.g. latent ignorability, completeness) been clearly stated and justified? |  |  |
| 5. Has a formal sensitivity analysis (e.g. Rosenbaum bounds, E -value, sensemakr) been conducted to evaluate whether estimated effects could plausibly be explained by unmeasured confounding? |  |  |
| 6. If full identification is not possible, are causal claims limited to estimands that are identifiable or bounded under latent structure assumptions? |  |  |
| 7. Are the study's assumptions, limitations, and conclusions clearly conditioned on the presence and treatment of latent variables-including how results might change under plausible alternative assumptions? |  |  |

matching can yield consistent estimates even when the model is incorrect in form (Waernbaum 2012). Bayesian approaches can also accommodate misspecification if constructed around decision-theoretic loss functions rather than fully specified parametric models (Stephens et al. 2023). This allows valid inference under exchangeability assumptions without relying on fully joint likelihoods.

Vansteelandt et al. (2012) caution that variable selection procedures developed for prediction-such as stepwise regression or regularization methods-may fail to identify weak confounders and thus exacerbate bias. Confounder selection procedures should prioritize reducing bias in the exposure effect estimator rather than optimizing predictive performance. Moreover, some doubly robust estimators yield approximately valid confidence intervals even when the confounder selection process is ignored, and some estimation strategies retain valid inference even under partial misspecification of the relationships between confounders and treatment or outcome (Vansteelandt et al. 2012). If covariates are highly correlated with the exposure-whether as intermediates, confounders, or colliders—even correctly specified models may yield inflated variance in adjusted estimates, sometimes exceeding that in unadjusted models (Schisterman et al. 2017).

But even these estimators are not immune to structural misalignment, such as omitted interactions, poorly captured nonlinearity, or incorrect causal graphs. To address these risks, analysts should prioritize estimators that balance flexibility with robustness. Modern approaches-especially those incorporating cross-fitting, sample splitting, and nonparametric or machine learning methods-can mitigate misspecification risk by allowing the data to inform the shape of functional relationships.

Robustness can also be evaluated empirically. Residual diagnostics, goodness-of-fit tests, out-of-sample predictive performance, and comparison across alternative model specifications can help reveal misfit. More formally, multiverse analysis and specification curve analysis (which plots causal effect estimates across a wide range of reasonable model specifications to visualize robustness) can expose how results vary under defensible modeling choices, highlighting which assumptions materially affect conclusions. Open-source tools such as the $R$ packages specr and multiverse facilitate these analyses. Where models yield unstable or fragile estimates across plausible alternatives, substantive interpretation should be cautious (Simonsohn et al. 2020). When structural assumptions about exposure are misspecified-e.g. due to interference or network structure-causal conclusions may still be recovered under weaker identification frameworks that separate exposure definition from modeling assumptions (Sävje 2024).

In high-dimensional or complex settings, model misspecification may be unavoidable. In such cases, transparency is essential. Rather than reporting a single point estimate from a possibly misspecified model, it may be more informative to present bounded estimates, sensitivity analyses, or a range of plausible values across specifications. Where modeling choices are particularly consequential, they should be disclosed and justified as part of the analytic workflow.

Ultimately, robust estimation depends not only on sophisticated methods but also on an attitude of scientific accountability: testing models, disclosing uncertainty, and confronting the limitations of inference.

Table 22 summarizes the key necessary conditions discussed in this section for valid causal inference in the presence of potential model misspecification, along with recommended strategies, tools, and common pitfalls. These conditions serve as a complement to identification assumptions by emphasizing the robustness and diagnostic evaluation of estimation procedures. Checklist C. 4 provides the corresponding checklist for use by practitioners in assessing whether these conditions have been adequately addressed in applied work.

### 5.4.3. Causal inference from longitudinal and time-series data

Many exposures, treatments, and outcomes of interest in health risk analysis and public health and policy analyses unfold over time. Whether studying the long-term impact of pollution, the dynamics of disease progression, or policy interventions on social systems, analysts must confront temporal structure directly. Time introduces complexity-via feedback, adaptation, lagged dependencies, and censoringbut also opportunity. When modeled appropriately, the temporal ordering of events can help distinguish causes from effects, identify mediators, and diagnose violations of causal assumptions.

However, standard methods of causal inference developed for static or cross-sectional data often fail in dynamic contexts. In particular, naively applying regression adjustment to longitudinal data can yield biased or misleading estimates, especially in the presence of time-varying confounding, dynamic treatment regimes, or feedback. To draw valid interventional conclusions from time-indexed data, one must account not only for causal structure but for the temporal structure of the data and the underlying causal processes. Key challenges include the following:

- Time-varying confounding: A covariate may affect both future treatment and the outcome of interest, but itself be influenced by earlier treatment. In such cases, standard regression adjustment can induce bias by conditioning on variables that are both mediators and confounders. For instance, adjusting for post-treatment covariates may block part of the treatment effect or open backdoor paths via collider bias (Rosenbaum 1984; Robins et al. 1994, 1999).
- Dynamic feedback and reverse causation: Variables may influence one another recursively over time. If $Y(t)$ affects $X(t+1)$, and we attempt to estimate the effect of $X$ on $Y$, failure to account for feedback can confound causal interpretation. A valid model must preclude the use of future information to predict or estimate past or contemporaneous effects.
- Censoring and competing risks: In survival or event-history data, units may drop out or experience alternative events that preclude observation of the outcome. If censoring is
