informative (i.e. related to both treatment and outcome), then standard estimators may be biased. Methods like inverse probability of censoring weighting (IPCW) (Robins et al. 1994) or competing risks regression (Fine and Gray 1999) are required.

- Interference and violations of consistency: Treatments administered to one unit may affect outcomes in others (interference), or the same treatment label may correspond to different intervention protocols across units or time (violating consistency). Both undermine the welldefinedness of potential outcomes.
- Latent structure and under-measurement: In many applications, especially in medicine or policy, the true state of
the system is only partially observed. Hidden confounders or latent (unobserved) temporal dynamics can induce spurious associations or mask true causal effects, particularly when treatment assignment depends on unobserved variables.

These challenges imply that standard identification strategies must be carefully reconsidered and extended to give valid results in temporal settings.

If variables are measured repeatedly over time, then accurate causal modeling requires that measurements of exposures, confounders, and outcomes be aligned at the correct (causally relevant) lags; treatment variation must exist at each
time point of interest; and time-varying confounding must either be absent or adequately controlled. Outcomes must also be aligned at lags that reflect the true causal delays and measured frequently enough to resolve them. For example, if short-term air pollution spikes increase hospital admissions after a delay of $1-3$ days, then aligning exposure and outcome on the same day will underestimate or miss the effect entirely, while using only monthly averages may wash out the daily variation needed to detect it. (This is analogous to the Nyquist theorem in signal processing: the measurement interval should be at least twice as fine as the shortest causal lag of interest to avoid obscuring or misattributing effects.)

A similar challenge arises in evaluating responses to public policy interventions. In the opioid epidemic, policy changes such as prescription monitoring programs, naloxone distribution, or prescribing restrictions may produce effects on multiple time scales: some impacts (e.g. immediate reductions in fatal overdoses after large-scale naloxone distribution) may appear within days or weeks, while others (e.g. shifts from prescription opioids to illicit fentanyl) may unfold over months or years. Data must therefore be collected and analyzed at resolutions that can capture both short-term and long-term dynamics. Follow-up periods should be long enough to observe effects of policy changes or interventions at the longest relevant lag, but not so long that unrelated processes dominate.

Observing a sharp change immediately after an intervention whose primary effects are expected to emerge only slowly-such as through behavioral adaptation or popula-tion-level spread akin to SIR epidemic dynamics-should raise caution: the change may be coincidental or due to other, faster-acting causes. For example, if banning a source of air pollution (such as smoking in bars) is immediately followed by a significant drop in heart attack rates within a few days, analysts should consider the possibility that the drop was not caused by the ban. Ignoring mismatched time scales between hypothesized causal mechanisms and observed changes can lead to false attribution or missed effects.
5.4.3.1. Marginal structural models (MSMs). Several methods have been developed to estimate causal effects from longitudinal observational data under explicit assumptions that allow the effects of an exposure to be untangled from the effects of confounders-including time-varying confounders affected by prior exposure. In the potential outcomes framework, under the assumptions of consistency, exchangeability (no unmeasured confounding), and positivity, the G-formula estimates counterfactual means by modeling the full joint distribution of time-varying covariates, treatments, and outcomes across time (Robins 1986). This approach requires correct specification of that joint model. Marginal Structural Models (MSMs) use inverse probability of treatment weighting (IPTW) to reweight the observed data so that, in the weighted pseudo-population, treatment assignment is independent of measured confounders-reducing bias from confounding when estimating causal effects. This method can handle time-varying confounding that is itself affected by prior treatment, provided the treatment model is correctly
specified and all relevant confounders are measured (Hernán and Robins 2020). G-estimation of Structural Nested Models estimates causal effects by positing a model for the outcome that includes a parameter for the treatment effect and then finding the parameter value that makes the residuals uncorrelated with treatment history-the value expected if the treatment had no effect. This allows sequential bias correction in settings with dynamic treatment regimes and feedback. Necessary conditions for the validity of these approaches typically include no unmeasured confounding (or sufficient adjustment via proxies), correct model specification (especially for time-varying covariates), no interference and well-defined interventions, and sufficient overlap (positivity) across treatment groups and time. When these conditions are not met, standard methods can fail to recover valid interventional effects-even if temporal ordering is respected.
5.4.3.2. Deconfounding time series variables. Recent machine learning approaches attempt to relax the strong assumptions of these previous methods by learning latent representations of hidden confounders from observed data (i.e. using neural networks to compress patterns in the observed variables into low-dimensional hidden factorsbased in part on weighted combinations of the inputs-that approximate the effects of unmeasured confounders). These models are especially promising when unmeasured confounding or complex time dynamics render classical identification infeasible. For example, the Variational Temporal Deconfounder (VTD) combines a variational recurrent autoencoder with a treatment-outcome network (i.e. it uses a type of neural network that learns hidden (latent) patterns over time-based on observed proxy variables-to estimate both the treatments individuals likely received and the outcomes they would have experienced under different treatments (Feng et al. 2023; Dai et al. 2025). Such "deconfounding" approaches do not require or assume that that there are no hidden confounders, but they do require the presence of informative proxy variables.

An alternative to deep neural network-based deconfounding is the Sequential Deconfounder (Hatt and Feuerriegel 2024), which estimates hidden confounders using a statistical model based on Gaussian Markov latent variables. Unlike earlier deconfounding methods that require multiple concurrent treatments, this approach can be applied when only a single treatment is administered at each time step. It models the latent confounder as a stochastic process that evolves over time, conditioned on the history of observed treatments. Under a sequential ignorability (i.e. sequential conditional exchangeability) assumption given the latent variables, the method enables unbiased estimation of individualized treatment effects. It also supports empirical diagnostics: if a model incorporating the inferred latent substitutes improves out-ofsample outcome prediction compared to a model without them, this suggests that confounding has been at least partially addressed.

Typical necessary conditions for valid inference using these models are that the latent structure must be learnable from proxies (proxy sufficiency); the model must reflect the
correct temporal dependencies; treatments must be conditionally independent given latent state (a strong and often untestable assumption); and the latent variables must capture all relevant sources of confounding and effect heterogeneity. When these conditions are violated-e.g. when proxies are weak, temporal dependencies are misspecified, or latent confounding is multi-scale-estimates may still be biased despite model flexibility.

Very recent research has also explored how attentionbased neural networks-the same core technology behind Large Language Models-can help uncover causal relationships in complex time series data. For example, Zhu et al. (2025) developed a two-stage approach using a modified Transformer model, called the Temporal Attention Forecasting Network (TAFNet), to detect evolving patterns in multivariate time series. In the first stage, the model produces attention matrices that highlight which time points and variables to "pay attention to," revealing potential causal dependencies. In the second stage, these attention patterns are translated into a graphical model that represents which variables are likely to cause changes in others, even as those relationships shift over time. This method is particularly well-suited for identifying Granger-type causal relationships in systems with non-stationary, nonlinear, or high-dimensional dynamics (Zhu et al. 2025).
5.4.3.3. Inferring causal structure from longitudinal observational data. When causal relationships among variables are not fully specified a priori, researchers may attempt to infer them from time-indexed observational data. A growing suite of causal discovery methods for longitudinal has emerged to tackle this problem, often under restrictive assumptions. While such approaches can detect candidate dependencies or rule out implausible causal models, they generally do not, on their own, establish identifiability of interventional effects. They are perhaps best viewed as falsification tools-useful for testing the compatibility of data with assumed or hypothesized causal structures, particularly in dynamic systems with feedback, lags, or latent variables. Causal discovery methods from time-indexed data can suggest plausible structural dependencies, but rarely suffice to establish identifiability of interventional effects without additional assumptions such as causal sufficiency, faithfulness, or proxy recoverability (Gong et al. 2024).

As discussed in Section 3, statistical tests for discovering predictive causality based on directed information flows between time series originated in the late 1960s with Granger causality tests (Granger 1969) and were later generalized to non-parametric methods for estimating transfer entropy between time series (Schreiber 2000). These methods are valuable for screening predictive dependencies, but they do not imply interventional causation unless strong conditions such as no unmeasured confounding, correct model specification, and no contemporaneous feedback (Granger 1969). Violations of these assumptions are common in realworld systems, especially in high-dimensional or nonlinear settings (Gong et al. 2024).

A complementary approach to causal discovery of dependence relationships between time series variables generalizes the PC (Peter-Clark) algorithm (named after its creators, Peter Spirtes and Clark Glymour), a well-known method for learning causal structures from observational data, based on testing conditional independencies between variables. Constraint-based algorithms, such as PCMCI+ (Runge 2020; Rehak et al. 2024), extend the PC algorithm (Spirtes et al. 2001) to time-series data by combining conditional independence testing with temporal constraints. PCMCI+ adapts the PC algorithm to handle time-series by incorporating "momentary conditional independence" (MCI) constraints to identify both lagged (time-delayed) and contemporaneous (simultaneous) dependence or independence relationships between variables. Such methods give provably correct answers under certain assumptions-typically, causal sufficiency (all common causes of the measured variables are either observed or adequately adjusted for), the Causal Markov condition (each variable is independent of its noneffects, given its direct causes), and faithfulness (all and only those independencies implied by the causal graph appear in the data)-but their validity can break down if time resolution is coarse relative to causal delays or if relevant variables are omitted. Coarse sampling can also obscure true causal lags, conflating lagged and contemporaneous effects in a way that creates apparent simultaneity and masks the actual temporal ordering. Moreover, even with adequate time resolution, multiple dynamic DAGs can be statistically indistinguishable from one another unless additional con-straints-such as experimental interventions, temporal ordering restrictions, or strong substantive knowledge-are available to break what is known as Markov equivalence. Still, these algorithms are among the few methods with provable asymptotic correctness (i.e. for sufficiently large samples) under specified assumptions and can detect violations of hypothesized dynamic DAG (directed acyclic graph) structures. They have recently been extended to allow for causal discovery in multiple time series with the same variables observed in different locations, and with some unobserved (latent or hidden) variables allowed (Günther et al. 2023).

By contrast, other temporal causal discovery algorithmsboth score-based (which search over possible graphs to find the one that maximizes a goodness-of-fit score, often penalized for complexity) and noise-based (which infer directionality by making assumptions about the form of unexplained variation)-take a different approach from conditional independence testing. One innovative score-based method is NOTEARS ("Non-combinatorial Optimization via Trace Exponential and Augmented lagRangian for Structure learning"), which reformulates the requirement that a causal graph be acyclic into a smooth algebraic constraint on a function of its weighted adjacency matrix. This turns the otherwise combinatorially challenging task of graph search into a fast, continuous optimization problem that modern solvers can handle efficiently (Zheng et al. 2018). DYNOTEARS (Pamfil et al. 2020) generalizes NOTEARS from static data to vector autoregressive (VAR) settings, simultaneously estimating both lagged and contemporaneous causal edges by combining time-series regression with an acyclicity constraint.

This makes it applicable to longitudinal and time-series data where variables may influence each other both across and within time steps.

Another family of noise-based methods uses statistical properties of model residuals to orient causal edges. VarLiNGAM ("Vector Autoregression Linear Non-Gaussian Acyclic Model") (Hyvärinen et al. 2010; Jiao et al. 2024) extends VAR models by applying the LiNGAM framework (Shimizu et al. 2006; Harada and Fujisawa 2021), which assumes that relationships are linear and that the innovations (residuals) are non-Gaussian and mutually independent. Under these conditions, the causal ordering and directionality can be uniquely identified without prior knowledge of temporal ordering beyond the VAR structure. The basic idea of LiNGAM is that, in a correctly causally ordered system, the unexplained variance in each variable (its residuals) is statistically independent of the values of other variables, whereas incorrect orderings leave systematic dependencies in those residuals. VarLiNGAM applies this principle to the residuals from vector autoregressive models, enabling the identification of contemporaneous causal directions in multivariate time series. Additive Noise Models (ANMs) generalize the LiNGAM idea by allowing each effect variable to be generated as a possibly nonlinear function of its cause plus an independent noise term (Peters et al. 2014). Unlike LiNGAM, ANMs do not require the noise to be non-Gaussian, and they are often applied in a pairwise fashion: for two variables, the correct causal direction is the one in which the noise is statistically independent of the cause. Extensions of ANMs to multiple variables also exist. In both LiNGAM and ANM approaches, correct model specification and satisfaction of the independence and distributional assumptions are necessary conditions for valid causal inference. These conditions can be fragile in real-world dynamic systems, where feedback loops violate acyclicity and hidden confounders induce dependence in residuals, leading to biased or spurious orientations (Herdeanu et al. 2025).
5.4.3.4. Dynamic causal structural models. Dynamical Structural Causal Models (DSCMs) provide a mathematically rigorous framework for representing and reasoning about causal mechanisms in continuous-time systems with feedback, stochasticity, and latent confounding (Boeken and Mooij 2024). Unlike traditional vector autoregressive (VAR) models-which model discrete-time dependencies among time-indexed processes for forecasting-DSCMs extend the structural causal modeling framework to continuous time and encode explicit interventional semantics. In a DSCM, variables are represented as full stochastic processes, with structural equations mapping exogenous trajectories to endogenous ones (i.e. rules that determine how the timeevolving behavior of observed variables is generated from external inputs or noise processes over time). This allows DSCMs to represent complex dynamics-including those governed by stochastic differential equations (SDEs)-while preserving causal interpretability under intervention.

For specialists, a key technical insight is that, under certain regularity conditions, solutions to SDE systems can be reformulated as DSCMs via Itô maps-that is, measurable functions that map realizations of driving noise processes (e.g. Brownian motion) to full trajectories of system variables. This formulation yields well-defined counterfactual distributions and enables the valid application of tools like the do-calculus in continuous-time settings. DSCMs distinguish between predictable dependencies (non-instantaneous, e.g. lagged influences) and adapted dependencies (which may include instantaneous effects), and encode these graphically. They support both perfect interventions (e.g. forcing a process to follow a specified trajectory) and subsampling operations, which allow mapping continuous-time causal structure to dis-crete-time observations-though not without information loss. Importantly, DSCMs satisfy a Markov property based on $\sigma$-separation, which generalizes d-separation to cyclic, latentvariable systems. This enables sound application of con-straint-based causal discovery algorithms like Fast Causal Inference (FCI), provided certain assumptions (e.g. faithfulness, exogeneity of integrators) are met.

Despite these advances, estimation from observational data remains challenging. Valid inference requires assumptions such as unique solvability of the underlying SDE system, sufficient time resolution to detect causal lags, and either full observability or recoverability of latent confounders. Current estimation techniques are largely theoretical, and methods for functional conditional independence testing in highdimensional time series are still emerging. Nonetheless, DSCMs provide a principled foundation for bridging continu-ous-time dynamical modeling with modern causal inference.
5.4.3.5. Cautions and opportunities for learning dynamic causal models from observational data. A variety of deep learning and attention-based models have been developed in recent years offering varying tradeoffs among interpretability, scalability, and robustness. However, these methods still require strong assumptions and often lack identifiability guarantees in real-world settings.

Neural network architectures-including recurrent nets, attention mechanisms, and graph neural networks-for causal discovery in temporal data can detect nonlinear, highorder dependencies and perform well on synthetic benchmarks, but they often lack theoretical guarantees and require extensive tuning (Gong et al. 2024; Herdeanu et al. 2025). Performance may degrade in the presence of unobserved confounding, time-varying causal structure, or irregular sam-pling-common features of real-world longitudinal data. Necessary conditions for valid application often include: availability of sufficient and relevant proxy variables for unobserved causes; stability of learned attention or feature patterns over time and across environments; adequate sample size and coverage to train accurate models; and approximate correctness of the model class for the underlying temporal dependencies.

No single method consistently outperforms others across diverse causal challenges such as hidden confounding, time-
lags, feedback, and non-stationarity (Herdeanu et al. 2025). The large-scale CausalDynamics benchmark shows that methods that work well in one setting may perform poorly in another, especially when data are high-dimensional or confounded. The choice of evaluation metric can also influence which methods appear "best." For example, the Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic curve (AUROC)-which measures how well a method distinguishes true from false links overall-can appear high in sparse networks simply because most pairs of variables are not connected. In such cases, the Area Under the Precision-Recall Curve (AUPRC)which focuses on the accuracy of predicted links among those declared as causal-may give a more realistic picture of performance.

In practice, these findings reinforce that dynamic causal inference methods are most useful when applied not as "black boxes," but as tools to test and refine hypothesized models, with careful attention to both their assumptions and to appropriate performance metrics-e.g. by checking for violations of faithfulness (the assumption that all and only the statistical independencies implied by the causal structure appear in the data), unexplained dependencies, or implausible feedback paths.

While deep learning and other structure-learning methods can identify candidate causal relationships, estimating the quantitative impact of exposures on outcomes in temporal settings typically requires different techniques. In panel or longitudinal data, econometric approaches-such as fixed effects, difference-in-differences, dynamic panel GMM, and synthetic controls-offer frameworks for controlling unobserved heterogeneity and estimating dynamic causal effects. Causal inference methods like marginal structural models, longitudinal TMLE, and Bayesian structural time series adapt to time-varying confounding and feedback. Recent machine learning approaches combine these principles with flexible function approximation to estimate heterogeneous treatment effects over time. These methods complement structurelearning by enabling policy-relevant effect size estimation once a plausible causal model is specified.

Table 23 summarizes the major classes of methods for estimating or discovering dynamic causal relationships from longitudinal observational data, along with their key identifying assumptions and their main strengths and limitations in applied settings such as public health applications.
5.4.3.6. Estimating causal effects in dynamic, longitudinal, and panel data. Estimating causal effects from data that evolve over time presents challenges absent in static settings. Outcomes may depend on their own past values, exposures may change in response to prior outcomes, and confounders can themselves be time-varying and affected by earlier treatment. In addition, unobserved factors-some constant over tilthers evolving-can bias naive estimates. Effective estimation strategies must therefore account for temporal dependence, feedback, and evolving confounding, while making explicit and testable identifying assumptions. To turn a learned or hypothesized temporal causal structure into valid quantitative estimates of dynamic exposure-outcome effects,
investigators typically draw on econometric panel-data methods, causal inference techniques adapted to time-varying treatments, modern machine-learning effect estimators, or specialized event history models.

Econometric panel-data methods were originally developed to address unobserved heterogeneity between units observed repeatedly over time (Wooldridge, 2010). Fixed effects and random effects models control for time-invariant unobserved heterogeneity-that is, for stable characteristics of each unit (such as a person, region, or company) that are not directly measured but could bias results if correlated with exposure. Fixed effects do this by allowing each unit to have its own intercept in a regression model; random effects instead treat these differences as coming from a common distribution, which can be more efficient (yielding more precise estimates with narrower confidence intervals for the same sample size) if the random-effects assumption holds

Dynamic panel GMM (Generalized Method of Moments) estimators are designed for situations where the outcome depends on its own past values (lagged dependent variables) and where conventional regression could be biased by endogeneity (correlation between explanatory variables and the error term, violating the exogeneity assumption) (Blundell and Bond 1998). These estimators use internal instrumentsvariables derived from the panel's own lagged values-that are correlated with the problematic predictors but uncorrelated with the current error term, enabling consistent estimation. They are especially useful for panels with many units but relatively short time series, a common setting in epidemiology, public health, and policy evaluation.

From a different but complementary perspective, the dif-ference-in-differences (DiD) designs introduced earlier support estimation of causal effects by comparing how outcomes change from before to after treatment in a treated group versus an untreated group. The key assumption is parallel trends: that without the treatment, both groups would have experienced the same changes (Huntington-Klein 2021). While DiD is often implemented in a panel-data regression framework (with fixed effects for units and time), its identifying power comes from this counterfactual trend assumption rather than solely from controlling for stable unit characteristics. Event-study extensions, which estimate differences at multiple time points before and after exposure, provide visual and statistical diagnostics for the parallel-trends assumption. Synthetic control methods (Abadie et al. 2010) generalize this logic by constructing a counterfactual for a treated unit from a weighted combination of untreated units that best matches its pretreatment history; the post-treatment difference between the treated unit and its synthetic control is then attributed to the intervention.

While these econometric approaches address time dependence and some forms of confounding, causal inference methods from epidemiology and biostatistics explicitly target bias from time-varying confounding affected by prior treatment-a scenario where conventional regression often fails. Marginal Structural Models (MSMs) estimated with inverse probability of treatment weighting (IPTW) reweight the data to create a pseudo-population in which treatment assignment is independent of measured confounders,

Table 23. Methods for estimating or discovering dynamic causal relationships from longitudinal observational data.
| Method/Family | Key identifying assumptions | Strengths (public/occupational health applications) | Limitations/Risks |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Effect-estimation methods |  |  |  |
| G-formula (Robins 1986) | Consistency; exchangeability (no unmeasured confounding); positivity; correct specification of joint distribution over time | Fully parametric adjustment for timevarying confounding; interpretable effect estimates | Sensitive to model misspecification; high-dimensional covariates may require regularization or ML (machine learning) |
| Marginal structural models (MSMs) with IPTW (Hernán et al. 2000) | Consistency; exchangeability; positivity; correct treatment model | Handles time-varying confounding affected by prior exposure; welldeveloped diagnostics | Weight instability with rare exposures or positivity violations; relies on measured confounders |
| Structural nested models/G-estimation (Robins et al. 1994, 1999) | Consistency; sequential exchangeability; correct model for counterfactual outcomes | Accommodates dynamic treatment regimes; sequential bias correction | Requires strong modelling assumptions; computationally intensive |
| Double-robust estimators (e.g. TMLE, AIPW, DML) (Bang and Robins, 2005; Chernozhukov et al. 2018) | Either exposure or outcome model correctly specified; exchangeability; positivity | Protection against model misspecification; accommodates ML for nuisance estimation | Requires careful diagnostics; sensitive to extreme weights |
| Latent-variable \& deconfounding approaches |  |  |  |
| Latent-variable deconfounding (e.g. VTD, Sequential Deconfounder) (Hatt and Feuerriegel, 2024) | Proxy sufficiency; learnable latent structure; conditional independence of treatment given latent state | Addresses unmeasured confounding when valid proxies exist; applicable to complex temporal patterns | Assumptions often untestable; biased if proxies weak or dynamics misspecified |
| Latent-variable tolerant constraintbased causal discovery (e.g. FCI) (Spirtes et al. 2001; Boeken and Mooij 2024) | Allows hidden confounding; requires faithfulness; correct conditional independence tests | Identifies potential latent confounding; outputs partiallyoriented graphs (PAGs) | May leave many edges unoriented; computationally heavy for large graphs |

| Modern ML-based causal discovery |  |  |  |

enabling unbiased effect estimation if all relevant confounders are measured and the treatment model is correctly specified (Hernán and Robins 2020). G-computation (the gformula) models the full joint distribution of covariates, treatments, and outcomes over time, and uses it to simulate counterfactual scenarios. G-estimation of Structural Nested Models estimates counterfactual outcomes conditional on treatment and covariates, enabling sequential bias correction in dynamic treatment regimes (Hernán and Robins 2020). Targeted maximum likelihood estimation (TMLE) extends these approaches by combining outcome modeling and propensity score modeling in a doubly robust framework, allowing integration of machine learning for flexible nuisance parameter estimation while preserving valid statistical inference. Collectively, these methods provide a principled bridge
between design conditions and effect estimation, enabling analysts to move from a credible causal structure to quantitative effect estimates that remain valid under complex temporal confounding.

Recent advances in machine learning-based causal estimators allow causal effects to be estimated from longitudinal or panel data even in high-dimensional, nonlinear, and heterogeneous settings. Some methods-such as double/debiased machine learning (DML) (Chernozhukov et al. 2018)-were originally developed for cross-sectional data but can be adapted to time-indexed settings by incorporating lagged variables, time indicators, and block cross-fitting to respect temporal dependence, enabling valid inference for Average Treatment Effects (ATE) and Conditional Average Treatment Effects (CATE). Other semiparametric, doubly robust
learners-such as augmented inverse probability weighting (AIPW) (Kurz 2022) (which combines outcome regression and propensity score weighting, remaining consistent if either is correctly specified), targeted maximum likelihood estimation (TMLE) (Petersen et al. 2014) (which updates an initial machine learning prediction to optimize a causal parameter while preserving double robustness), and the DR learner (Kennedy 2025) (which uses flexible machine learning for nuisance models and combines them to directly target CATE estimation)-can incorporate machine learning to flexibly model covariates, treatments, and outcomes across time. Their longitudinal extensions allow consistent estimation of ATE, CATE, and more granular heterogeneous treatment effects (HTEs) (Nie and Wager 2021) even in the presence of time-varying confounding (Hernán and Robins 2020). Specialized neural network and deep learning architectures such as Recurrent Marginal Structural Networks (Lim et al. 2018) and Recurrent Continuous Adversarial Balancing (Zhu et al. 2023) are explicitly designed for longitudinal observational data. These models adjust for time-varying confounding using recurrent sequence-to-sequence structures, enabling estimation of dynamic treatment effects at the individual level and over multiple decision points, as well as ATEs by averaging over individuals. As with all complex, flexible machine learning models, large datasets, careful regularization, and rigorous validation are critical to avoid overfitting in high-dimensional temporal contexts. Also, as with other longitudinal causal estimators, their validity depends on key identification conditions such as sequential ignorability (no unmeasured time-varying confounding), positivity (nonzero probability of each treatment history within covariate strata), and consistency/SUTVA (well-defined, noninterfering treatments).

When the primary outcome of interest is the timing or occurrence of events (such as death, onset of addiction or diseases, infection, or changes in health), event-history, survival data analysis, and transition models are often more appropriate than standard regression approaches. The Cox Proportional Hazards (CPH) model estimates hazard ratios under the proportional hazards assumption, meaning that the ratio of hazards between groups is assumed to be constant over time. In its standard form, the Cox model is an associational tool; causal interpretation of hazard ratios requires that confounding (including time-varying confounding) be appropriately addressed and that other causal assumptions hold. Unless one assumes no treatment effect or makes an additional untestable assumption, hazard ratios from the CPH model generally do not have a causal inter-pretation-even in randomized or correctly specified settings (Martinussen 2022). Parametric survival models (e.g. exponential, Weibull, Gompertz) impose specific functional forms for the hazard; if correctly specified, these can improve statistical efficiency but, like the Cox proportional hazards model, they require correct adjustment for confounding and satisfaction of causal assumptions to support causal claims. Discrete-time hazard models estimate event probabilities in fixed intervals, making them particularly useful for data collected in regular waves. Their causal validity also depends on proper adjustment for confounding and correct model specification. Multi-
state models capture transitions between multiple states (e.g. healthy → diseased → recovered), while competing risks models account for situations where one event type precludes another. Causal inference extensions-such as marginal structural hazard models, structural nested failure time models, and targeted maximum likelihood estimation for survival data-adapt survival analysis to handle time-varying treatments and confounding, often integrating machine learning to improve robustness and flexibility (Hernán and Robins 2020).

In sum, valid causal inference from longitudinal and timeseries data requires more than simply choosing a sophisticated estimator. Across econometric, epidemiologic, and machine learning approaches, certain conditions recur. Measured (or well-proxied) confounders must be sufficient to block all non-causal backdoor paths; models for treatment, outcome, and censoring must either be correctly specified or replaced with robust, semiparametric alternatives; and there must be adequate overlap in treatment assignments across relevant covariate histories. Treatment variation must also exist at each time point of interest, and time-varying confounding must either be absent or adequately controlled.

Diagnostics tailored to the method at hand-such as parallel trends checks in difference-in-differences, pre-intervention balance for synthetic controls, proportional hazards tests for Cox models, or overlap plots for inverse-probabilityweighted MSMs-are essential for assessing whether key assumptions hold in the observed data. Stability or invariance of estimated effects across subgroups or time periods can provide additional reassurance that results reflect genuine causal structure rather than transient patterns or structural breaks.

When these conditions fail-due to dynamic selection, unmeasured time-varying confounders, coarse measurement intervals, or structural changes-effect estimates may be biased even if the assumed causal graph is correct. Complementary strategies such as doubly robust estimation with temporal cross-fitting, falsification analyses (e.g. placebo treatments or outcomes), rolling-window estimation, or landmark survival analysis can strengthen the evidentiary basis for causal claims by probing the sensitivity of results to violations of assumptions.

These constraints reinforce that methodological choice must be guided not only by statistical fit, but by the plausibility and testability of the assumptions needed for causal identification in dynamic settings. Table 24 summarizes necessary conditions for valid causal inference from longitudinal data, why they matter, their practical implications, and common pitfalls. Checklist C. 5 helps systematically verify that each requirement has been addressed before interpreting results as causal.

### 5.5. Validation of causal models and estimates

Estimation of causal effects is not the conclusion of causal inference but rather it is - or should be - another crucial point at which the causal models and assumptions used to derive the estimates are confronted with data. A causal claim
is credible only if the estimated effect is (i) consistent with observed evidence under plausible models, (ii) robust to reasonable perturbations in assumptions, and (iii) applicable to the populations and contexts for which it is intended. Meeting these conditions requires more than reporting a point estimate with a confidence interval. It involves testing the model and its predictions against observations, probing the stability of results, and communicating the remaining uncertainty with clarity.

### 5.5.1. Model diagnostics: confronting the model with data

The first task is to evaluate whether the data meaningfully support the model's identifying assumptions. Many of the required checks that should be carried out during the data analysis have already been discussed. These include the following:

- Overlap and positivity checks-using propensity score distributions, kernel density plots, or inverse probability weight diagnostics-reveal whether treated and untreated groups are comparable in their distributions of observed covariates.
- Covariate balance checks confirm that adjustment has succeeded in removing systematic differences between groups. Standardized mean differences and graphical diagnostics are commonly used to verify the balance of observed covariates.
- Model adequacy checks-including residual plots, crossvalidation, and formal specification tests (e.g. Ramsey's RESET)-assess whether the chosen functional forms and error structures plausibly represent the data-generating process. Use of flexible machine-learning methods and double-robust methods reduces dependence on parametric modeling assumptions.
- Cross-environment falsification tests-e.g. invariant causal prediction (ICP)-test whether estimated causal relationships remain stable across environments and interventions that share the same underlying mechanisms.
- Sensitivity analyses-such as E-values, omitted variable bias (OVB) frameworks (Cinelli and Hazlett 2020), and quantitative bias analysis-quantify the magnitude of unmeasured confounding that would be required to overturn the conclusions. Sensitivity analyses and bounding calculations can also be used to explore how robust the conclusions are to measurement error, missing data, omitted or latent variables, and model specification errors.

In addition to these foundational diagnostics, it is essential to test the internal coherence of the causal model itself. If a causal graph admits multiple valid backdoor adjustment sets, a robust analysis should estimate the causal effect separately for each set and compare results. For example, in an observational exposure-response study (e.g. for chemical exposure and cancer risk, or for opioid exposure variables and overdose mortality rates), one adjustment set might adjust for demographic and dietary factors, while another adjusts for biomarker and comorbidity factors. Large, unexplained
discrepancies in effect estimates between the results calculated from alternative adjustment sets would raise concerns about residual confounding, model misspecification, or violations of assumptions: different defensible adjustment sets should yield consistent conclusions if the model is well specified.

When diagnostics indicate violations of necessary conditions for valid causal inference-such as non-positivity, inconsistencies across estimates using different adjustment sets, or implausibly large implied confounding-the scientific response is to revise the causal model, adopt alternative estimators or estimands, or apply bounding and partial identification methods to report plausible ranges or sets of possible effects rather than potentially misleading point estimates.

### 5.5.2. Diagnosing threats to internal and external validity in quasi-experiments

As noted by Campbell and Stanley (1963) and summarized in Table 12, the internal validity of causal inferences from quasiexperiments can be undermined by historical events concurrent with treatment, maturation processes unrelated to treatment, testing effects, changes in measurement instruments, regression toward the mean, preexisting selection differences, attrition, and interactions between selection and maturation. External validity can be compromised by what they termed "interaction of pretesting and treatment," by selection-treatment interactions that produce population-specific effects, by reactive or "Hawthorne" arrangements in which study participation alters behavior, and by interference between multiple treatments. In exposure-response applications in public health, the "pretest" concept is rarely literal; the analogous threat arises when baseline measurement or disclosure of baseline results influences subsequent exposures, behaviors, or reporting, thereby modifying the apparent exposureresponse relationship.

These threats can be addressed both through study design and post hoc analysis. History effects can be probed by replicating in settings where the suspected historical event is absent; maturation can be examined by including groups matched on their maturation trajectory; testing or measurement-reactivity effects can be assessed by comparing subgroups with and without potentially reactive baseline measurements or by minimizing reactivity in baseline ascertainment; instrumentation threats can be addressed through stability checks of measurement properties, use of alternate forms, or use of blinded raters; regression artifacts can be tested by examining subgroups not selected for extreme baseline scores; and selection bias can be probed by falsification tests on pretreatment outcomes. Attrition bias can be at least partly addressed by comparing observed characteristics of dropouts and completers, while selection-maturation interactions can be detected through multiple follow-up waves that reveal divergent natural trajectories. For external validity, population-specific effects can be evaluated through replication in diverse samples, and reactive arrangements can be tested by comparing unobtrusive and reactive measurement modes. A combination of appropriate study design and data analysis steps can often refute or adjust for these

1. Temporal ordering of events is known and used appropriately. Analyses respect causal direction and do not condition on future variables when estimating past or contemporaneous effects
2. Exposures, covariates, and outcomes are measured and aligned at causally relevant lags
3. Measurement frequency is fine enough to resolve the shortest hypothesized causal delay
4. Follow-up period is sufficient to capture the full causal effect, including delayed or sustained impacts
5. Treatments and covariates are consistently defined over time. Definitions and coding should remain stable across time points.
6. All potential confounders, including time-varying ones, are identified
7. The presence or impact of unmeasured confounding is assessed (e.g. via proxy-variable tests, sensitivity analysis, or causal discovery)
8. Treatment variation (positivity) is confirmed within covariate histories at each time point; analysis is restricted to common support if needed
9. Model assumptions and structure match the data-generating process; robustness to misspecification is tested (e.g. with doubly robust estimators or alternative specifications and lags)
10. Censoring, missing data, and competing risks are addressed using appropriate methods, with rationale documented
11. Method-specific diagnostics are reported for tests of identifying assumptions (e.g. pre-trend checks for DiD, proportional hazards tests for CPH, overlap diagnostics for MSM/IPW, and, for causal discovery, justification of assumptions such as faithfulness, no instantaneous feedback, and adequate sample size))
12. Effect stability or invariance is checked across time periods and subgroups (e.g. via rolling-window estimates, subgroup analyses)
13. Identifiability is verified when latent structure is suspected (e.g. by confirming proxy sufficiency, using experimental variation, or performing sensitivity analysis)
threats; when they cannot be eliminated, explicitly documenting plausible alternative (non-causal) explanations strengthens the transparency and credibility of the resulting causal claims.

While these design- and analysis-based strategies address specific, well-known threats to validity, a complementary step is to actively challenge the causal model itself. This is done through systematic "stress tests" that look for patterns the model should not produce if its assumptions are correct. Such adversarial checks-ranging from placebo and nega-tive-control tests to cross-method triangulation-are discussed next.

### 5.5.3. Logic of validation: falsification, triangulation, and negative controls

The logic of model validation is fundamentally adversarial: a credible model should survive serious attempts to refute it. Falsification tests examine whether the model produces "effects" where none should exist (Keele and Small 2019). Placebo interventions-such as creating a "pseudo-exposure" variable for a time period before the exposure could have occurred, or for a population not yet exposed-should yield null effects if the model is unbiased. Negative controls provide a way to do this (Lipsitch et al. 2010; Arnold et al. 2016; Shi et al. 2022). A negative control exposure is one with no plausible causal link to the outcome but sharing confounding structure with the primary exposure. A negative control outcome (NCO) is one that should not be affected by the exposure but could share unmeasured causes with it. Significant associations in such tests often point to residual
confounding, selection bias, or correlated measurement error. Negative controls can also be used to detect bias from conditioning on colliders during enrollment or follow-up (selection bias) and from differential misclassification or other measurement error (Arnold et al. 2016; Shi et al. 2022). Prespecifying negative controls at the study design stage can improve transparency and avoid selective reporting. However, while negative controls can reveal the presence of bias, they often cannot determine its direction or magnitude, and their interpretation must remain grounded in plausible causal structures (Arnold et al. 2016; Shi et al. 2022).

Negative controls are particularly important in instrumental variable (IV) designs (Branson and Keele 2020). Keele et al. (2019) distinguish between negative control outcomes (NCOs)-variables the IV should not affect if the exclusion restriction holds-and negative control instruments (NCIs), which should be unrelated to the outcome conditional on the original IV. They caution against common errors such as failing to condition on the original IV in negative control instrument tests, and note that many apparent rejections of IV validity reflect violations of auxiliary modeling assumptions (e.g. linearity in two-stage least squares) rather than the core exclusion restriction. They also propose a falsification framework tailored to IV designs: identify subpopulations in which nearly all units receive (or do not receive) the treatment. If the instrument is valid, it should have no effect on the outcome in those subpopulations-i.e. the intention-to-treat (ITT) effect must be null. A non-zero ITT effect provides strong evidence against the IV assumptions. This approach is straightforward, implementable via standard regressions, and the estimated effect size helps gauge bias magnitude. It
complements negative control strategies by offering an orthogonal test of IV plausibility.

Recent work has clarified the scope and implementation of negative-control tests in IV designs. Danieli et al. (2026) formalize common negative-control falsification tests for IVs as conditional-independence tests and show they are sensitive not only to the usual IV assumptions of independence and exclusion but also to functional form; consequently, valid IV designs can fail these tests due to model misspecification. Practical guidance includes (i) stating the conditions that candidate NCOs/NCls must satisfy in the IV setting, (ii) conditioning on the original IV when testing NCIs, (iii) employing joint tests and multiple-testing adjustments across a systematically assembled set of NC variables (rather than one-off choices), and (iv) considering upstream causes of the instrument as potential NCIs. Used this way, negative-control tests complement randomization-based checks and placebo designs without over-penalizing correct designs for modeling artifacts.

A complementary approach is provided by randomizationbased falsification tests (Branson and Keele 2020), designed to evaluate the as-if randomization assumption for natural experiments and IVs. Rather than assessing covariate balance one variable at a time, these tests compare global measures of covariate balance (or bias) for the instrument against the balance distribution that would occur under simulated random assignment. This allows investigators to judge whether the instrument is materially closer to random assignment than the exposure itself, avoiding reliance on parametric assumptions and yielding easily interpretable graphical diagnostics. This method also addresses the common situation where some covariates are balanced while others are not, providing a single summary test of plausibility for the IV's asif randomization property. Multiple negative controls, used with awareness of statistical power and multiple testing, can strengthen inferences. When possible, functional-form-robust tests should be employed to separate failures of structural assumptions from auxiliary modeling assumptions.

Other falsification strategies target reverse causation: temporal reversals, for example, test whether future exposures predict past outcomes. Split-sample validation can assess the stability of effects across regions, institutions, or time periods. Cross-method triangulation-comparing estimates from different identification strategies such as difference-in-differences, IV, g-computation, and marginal structural models-can test whether different approaches converge on similar conclusions. Lawlor et al. (2016) proposed the following criteria for triangulation in observational causal inference: the use of at least two approaches with distinct bias structures addressing the same causal question; explicit consideration of exposure timing and duration; transparent acknowledgment of each approach's dominant bias; and articulation of the expected direction of bias, including methods expected to bias effect estimates in opposite directions. Converging results across such approaches bolster causal claims, while divergence signals the need for further investigation.

Finally, outcome-wide epidemiology (VanderWeele and Ding 2017) broadens the scope of assessment by examining the effects of a single exposure across multiple outcomes simultaneously-including those where effects may be
beneficial, harmful, or null. This comprehensive framing, while not designed explicitly as falsification tests, can still inform assessments of model coherence by revealing unexpected associations (e.g. harmful effects where benefits are expected, or vice versa) and helping policymakers weigh tradeoffs among outcomes.

These falsification and triangulation strategies illustrate how causal models can be stress-tested for hidden bias, violated assumptions, or incoherent effect patterns. They differ in scope and implementation but share the goal of challenging the model's credibility before results are interpreted or used for decision-making. Taken together, they emphasize that valid causal conclusions require:

1. Empirical support for model assumptions - demonstrated via overlap, balance, model adequacy, and sensitivity analyses.
2. Internal coherence of estimates - agreement across alternative valid adjustment sets, robustness across environments, and absence of implausible effect patterns.
3. Refutation resilience - survival of falsification attempts, negative-control tests, and adversarial probes.
4. Cross-method consistency - triangulation with other credible identification strategies.
5. Transparency about remaining uncertainty - explicit quantification of how plausible violations of assumptions could alter conclusions.

Table 25 summarizes selected approaches, the threats they address, their key assumptions, and their practical strengths and limitations. Embedding these strategies into the analytic workflow serves two purposes. First, it identifies weaknesses and potential failure modes before interpretation and policy use, prompting model revision, reframing of conclusions, or adoption of bounding approaches when necessary. Second, it produces a transparent record of how the model was stress-tested, which threats were probed and how, and which results proved resilient. The combination of modern causal diagnostics, classical validity threats and refutation tests, negative controls (including in IV settings), and outcome-wide approaches provides a comprehensive and scientifically credible foundation for interpreting and communicating causal estimates.

Table 26 translates the five conditions above into more operational guidance, linking each to its rationale, practical implications, supporting methods, and common pitfalls. Checklist C. 5 offers a template for documenting whether each requirement has been addressed, the evidence supporting it, and any remaining caveats.

## 6. Interpretation and result integrity

Drawing valid causal conclusions from observational data does not end with estimation of causal contrasts. Even when statistical models are carefully specified and confounding appears to be controlled, the interpretation of those results must meet additional necessary conditions to avoid overstating or misrepresenting causal inferences. The need for this

| Cross-method triangulation (Lawlor et al. 2016) | Strengthen causal claims by comparing results from methods with distinct, unrelated bias structures; increases robustness when findings align and makes bias sources explicit when they differ. | Model misspecification; method-specific bias; unmeasured confounding. | At least two identification strategies with unrelated dominant biases; clear articulation of expected bias direction; comparable target estimands. | Requires data amenable to multiple valid approaches; interpretation difficult if methods disagree; potential residual shared biases. | Studies with multiple analytic strategies available (e.g. IV, DiD, g-computation, MSMs); complex questions with no single fully credible method; complementary data sources or settings. |
| Temporal reversal tests | Test for reverse causation by checking whether future exposures "predict" past outcomes; simple, direct probe of causal directionality. | Reverse causation; misspecified temporal ordering. | Accurate timing of exposure/outcome; absence of timedependent confounding that could induce spurious reversal effects. | Fails if exposure/ outcome timing is mismeasured; may be confounded by anticipatory behavior. | Time-series or longitudinal settings where reverse causation is plausible. |
| Split-sample validation | Assess stability of effect estimates across subgroups (regions, institutions, time periods); highlights instability and setting-specific moderators. | Lack of generalizability; effect heterogeneity; model overfitting. | Comparable measurement across subgroups; sufficient power in each subset. | Low power if subgroups small; differences may reflect noise rather than bias. | Multi-site or multiperiod studies; policy evaluations across jurisdictions. |
| Outcome-wide epidemiology (VanderWeele and Ding 2017) | Evaluate exposure effects across many outcomes to assess coherence and detect unexpected patterns; broad view of exposure's impact and possible tradeoffs. | Model incoherence; unexpected tradeoffs; residual confounding if bias patterns inconsistent. | Correct classification and measurement of multiple outcomes; adequate control for multiple testing. | Not a direct falsification test; requires multiple-outcome data; susceptible to false positives without correction. | Public health exposures with wide-ranging possible effects; policy impact assessments. |


discipline is underscored by meta-research. For example, Gerrits et al. (2019) found that a large majority of published health services research papers examined exhibited multiple questionable reporting practices, many of them directly relevant to causal interpretation. Among the most frequent were stating conclusions that do not reflect findings, including stating implications for policy and practice that do not adequately reflect the results in the context of the referenced
literature; not mentioning contradictory evidence; and drawing conclusions and generalizations that are not allowed by the sampling and outcome measures used. Less frequent reporting practices included generalizing findings to populations, settings, or time periods not supported by the data; claiming causation without appropriate design or justification; and failing to address the clinical relevance of non-significant results. In most cases, several of these interpretive

Table 26. Necessary conditions for validating causal models and estimates.
| Necessary condition | Why this matters | Implications for practice | Supporting methods/ Tools | Use case/Context | Common pitfalls | Example |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Empirical support for model assumptions (overlap, balance, model adequacy, sensitivity) | Without empirical evidence that key assumptions are approximately satisfied, effect estimates may be driven by model artifacts rather than causal relationships. | Diagnose overlap and balance before and after adjustment; assess model specification; quantify robustness to unmeasured confounding, measurement error, and missing data. | Overlap plots, standardized mean differences, residual diagnostics, RESET test, crossvalidation, Evalues, OVB frameworks, quantitative bias analysis. | Observational exposure-response studies; quasiexperiments. | Ignoring poor overlap or imbalance; relying solely on p -values; omitting sensitivity analysis. | Air pollution study shows adequate covariate balance but sensitivity analysis reveals a small unmeasured confounder could reverse conclusions. |
| Internal coherence of estimates (agreement across valid adjustment sets for an effect, absence of implausible effect patterns) | If different valid adjustment sets yield divergent estimates, or effects vary implausibly across contexts, residual confounding, model misspecification, or assumption violations may be present. | Identify multiple valid backdoor sets; estimate effects separately; investigate unexplained discrepancies; test robustness across environments; revise model if needed. | DAG-based adjustment set identification; covariate set comparison; invariant causal prediction (ICP); bounding approaches; specification curve analysis. | Epidemiologic studies with rich covariate data; multienvironment studies. | Selecting only one "preferred" adjustment set; failing to report discrepancies; ignoring instability across contexts. | BPA-cancer risk estimates differ substantially between biomarker-adjusted and diet-adjusted models. |
| Refutation resilience (survival of falsification attempts, negative-control tests, and adversarial probes) | Models that fail falsification or negative-control tests are unlikely to yield credible causal estimates. | Pre-specify placebo and negative controls; conduct tests for null effects where none are expected; use orthogonal falsification frameworks in IVs; interpret failures cautiously. | Negative control exposures/ outcomes/ instruments; placebo interventions; intention-to-treat tests in subpopulations; Danieli et al. (2026) IV falsification framework. | IV studies; quasiexperiments; longitudinal observational analyses. | Post-hoc selection of negative controls; misclassifying variables as valid NCs; overinterpreting failures due to auxiliary model misspecification. | In an IV analysis of education and health, a negative control outcome (eye color) shows a spurious association with the instrument. |
| Cross-method consistency (triangulation with other credible identification strategies for the same data) | Agreement among methods with distinct bias structures strengthens credibility; disagreement signals need for further scrutiny. | Identify at least two credible strategies with different dominant biases; compare effect directions and magnitudes; explain discrepancies. | Difference-indifferences, IV, gcomputation, MSMs, structural nested models; Lawlor et al. triangulation criteria. | Policy evaluation; observational epidemiology. | Using methods with shared unmeasured confounding; failing to consider bias direction. | Smoking-lung cancer association replicated in both case-control and cohort studies with differing bias profiles. |
| Transparency about remaining uncertainty | Decision-makers require clarity about how violations of assumptions could alter conclusions; lack of transparency undermines trust. | Quantify and report robustness to plausible violations; document threats probed, methods used, and results; clearly communicate residual uncertainty. | Sensitivity and bias analyses; uncertainty visualization; structured reporting checklists; Evalues, OVB, Sensemakr | Risk assessment; health policy recommendations. | Omitting documentation of tested threats; presenting point estimates without uncertainty context. | Reporting that conclusions are robust unless an unmeasured confounder increases risk by $\geq 2$-fold. |


lapses co-occurred in the same article, amplifying the risk of misleading readers and policymakers.

### 6.1. Example: Vitamin E supplements-causal language from a non-causal design

A 1996 cohort analysis of older U.S. adults (Losonczy et al. 1996) reported that "use of vitamin E reduced the risk of allcause mortality $[\mathrm{RR}=0.66 ; 95 \% \mathrm{Cl}: 0.53,0.83]$ and risk of coronary disease mortality $(\mathrm{RR}=0.53 ; 95 \% \mathrm{Cl}: 0.34,0.84)$."

Exposure was based on self-reported non-prescription supplement use at study visits, without random assignment, and the authors adjusted for recorded covariates. The abstract's phrasing as a risk reduction caused by use of vitamin E reads as an interventional claim even though the study design was observational.

Such interventional causal language is not warranted without a design that supports counterfactual inference. Differences between supplement users and non-users may reflect healthier behaviors, differential access to care, or other

unmeasured factors that affect mortality; the authors themselves noted in their Discussion section-although not in their abstract-that "It is plausible that our results are due to confounding by other factors associated with vitamin supplement use, such as practice of better health habits and access to better health care by users compared with nonusers." Consequently, the necessary identifiability condition of (conditional) exchangeability-that, after adjusting for measured covariates, supplement users and non-users would have had the same distribution of outcomes had they received the same treatment (i.e. no unmeasured confounding)-has not been established here. Without exchangeability (along with positivity and consistency), these adjusted associations cannot be interpreted as effects of assigning vitamin E , and the subsequent randomized evidence is consistent with that reading.

When the corresponding intervention was tested in randomized trials, the benefits did not appear (Heart Protection Study Collaborative Group 2002). In the MRC/BHF Heart Protection Study ( 20,536 high-risk adults randomized to antioxidant vitamins vs placebo), antioxidant vitamin supplementation did not reduce all-cause mortality, coronary events, stroke, or major vascular events. Meta-analyses of randomized trials likewise found no mortality or cardiovascular benefit from vitamin E; high-dose vitamin E ( $\geq 400 \mathrm{IU} / \mathrm{d}$ ) was associated with increased all-cause mortality in one widely cited dose-response meta-analysis (Vivekananthan et al. 2003).

An appropriate interpretation of the cohort findings would have been to state that vitamin E use was associated with lower observed mortality in that population, while
emphasizing that causality cannot be inferred from those data. Treating adjusted relative risks from a non-causal design as if they were intervention effects risks overstatement; subsequent randomized evidence contradicting the purported benefits. This illustrates why meeting the design-level necessary condition is essential before drawing interventional conclusions.

For causal inference in particular, the danger of misinterpreting or misreporting findings is acute: overstatement can transform an exploratory association into a seemingly definitive claim of intervention effect, while unwarranted generalization can imply broad applicability from a narrow or context-specific finding. Causal interpretation is strengthened when the exposure is framed as a specific, potentially actionable intervention whose counterfactual contrast is well defined, rather than as a vague risk factor (Glass et al. 2013). This section outlines core interpretive principles and tests for interpreting findings responsibly-especially when results may influence public health, litigation, or policy-and highlights necessary conditions for avoiding inferential error and overstatement, while increasing the credibility, transparency, and practical value of reported results.

### 6.1.1. Do not infer causality from non-causal designs and analyses

Causal interpretation is not valid unless the underlying study design supports it. Cross-sectional surveys, ecological analyses, and uncontrolled before-after comparisons generally lack the structural assumptions needed for valid interventional conclusions. Interventional causal claims-those asserting

Table 27. Necessary conditions for valid interpretation of causal estimates from observational data.

| Necessary condition | Why this matters | Implications for practice | Supporting methods/Tools | Use case/Context | Common pitfalls | Example |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Study design supports causal interpretation | Non-causal designs cannot justify interventional claims | Restrict causal language to designs with temporal ordering and confounding control | DAGs, quasi-experimental designs, longitudinal analysis | Evaluating policy impacts | Inferring causality from cross-sectional surveys | Treating ecological correlations as causal |
| Assumptions for causal identification are met | Association $\neq$ causation; misinterpretation can mislead policy | State, test, and justify identification assumptions; avoid causal terms when unjustified | DAG-based criteria, falsification tests, placebo outcomes, conditional independence tests, sensitivity analyses | Environmental epidemiology | "Table 2 fallacy"; overreliance on $p$-values | Misinterpreting covariate coefficients as effects |
| Hypotheses and analytic plans are pre-specified | Prevents p -hacking and selective reporting | Register protocols; distinguish confirmatory from exploratory analyses | OSF, ClinicalTrials.gov, Registered Reports; blinded analysis; specification curves | Occupational health | HARKing; undisclosed multiple testing | Changing covariates post hoc to reach significance |
| Measurements are valid and reliable | Poor measurement biases estimates and interpretation | Use validated tools; quantify error rates; adjust for misclassification | SIMEX, regression calibration, m-DAGs; test-retest reliability, validation substudies | Self-reported exposure data | Differential measurement error | Biomarker calibration of dietary recall data |
| Exposure still predicts outcome after adjustment | Necessary condition for causal interpretation | Test post-adjustment explanatory power | Conditional independence tests, residualized regressions, AIPW | Drug safety studies | Overcontrol; collider bias | Effect disappears after adjusting for mediator |
| Alternative causal explanations are evaluated | Rival models or causal structures may fit the data equally well; ignoring them risks overconfidence in one explanation | Compare proposed model to plausible alternatives; document differences and why the preferred model is more credible | Red-teaming, rival-model analysis, causal discovery (e.g. FCI, GES), theory-driven and data-driven model comparison | Policy evaluation, epidemiology | Assuming the chosen DAG is the only plausible structure | Comparing multiple DAGs for air pollution-asthma relationship and retaining the one most consistent with evidence |
| Heterogeneity is evaluated | Effects often differ across subgroups and contexts | Pre-specify modifiers; test interactions; report on additive \& multiplicative scales | Causal trees, forests, stratified models, RERI | Public health interventions | Ignoring effect modification | Vaccine benefit greater in elderly than young adults |
| Results are presented transparently | Clear, complete reporting aids interpretation and replication | Report subgroup results, uncertainty, interaction measures and study limitations explicitly | STROBE checklist, visualizations | Health disparities research | Opaque aggregation of results; poorly discussed limitations | Not reporting stratumspecific effects |
| Data \& code are shared | Transparency enables replication and credibility | Archive analysis scripts, data, documentation | GitHub, OSF, Zenodo | Air pollution studies | Incomplete documentation | Missing code for key steps in analysis |
| Interpretive claims are appropriately qualified | Overstated or causal language or unjustified policy advice can mislead readers and overstate evidence | Use hedged language; explicitly tie causal framing to where assumptions and supporting evidence hold | Language guidelines, redteaming, pre-registered interpretation frameworks | Observational studies in healthcare | Spin, causal overstatement, conflating association with causation; | Framing results as "consistent with causal effect" rather than "causes" |

1. Does the study design permit causal interpretation under stated assumptions? Is it free of fundamental design flaws (e.g. no control group, misaligned times for variables) that cannot be remedied post hoc by modeling?
2. Are all identification assumptions stated, plausibly satisfied, and (where possible) tested and verified using tools such as falsification tests, placebo outcomes, and conditional independence tests?
3. Were the hypotheses and analytic plans pre-specified, and are all deviations transparently reported?
4. Are exposure, outcome, and covariates measured with validated and reliable tools, with error rates quantified through methods such as test-retest reliability studies or validation sub-studies?
5. Has it been verified that the exposure still significantly predicts the outcome after full adjustment for all confounders (including latent and residual confounding) and other threats (measurement error, missing data, latent variables, model misspecification, coincident historical trends, etc.)?
6. Have plausible alternative causal models or rival explanations and effects estimates been identified, compared, and documented?
7. Is heterogeneity of effects evaluated and reported on both additive and multiplicative scales, using stratum-specific estimates with a consistent reference group, and reporting interaction measures (e.g. RERI) with confidence intervals or P-values where appropriate?
8. Are results presented clearly, with appropriate measures of uncertainty and interaction?
9. Are all major study limitations (in design, conduct, measurements, analysis, modeling assumptions, and validation) explicitly and thoroughly reported and discussed?
10. Are data, code, and full documentation publicly shared to enable replication?
11. Is causal or strong interpretive language appropriately hedged, and limited to contexts supported by the analysis?
what would happen under hypothetical actions-require a design that enables counterfactual reasoning, such as quasiexperimental or longitudinal frameworks with clear temporal ordering and controlled confounding.

### 6.2. Example: Moderate alcohol consumption-causal language from non-causal designs

For decades, observational cohort studies reported that light-to-moderate drinking was "protective" for coronary outcomes, often with hazard ratios suggesting a $20-30 \%$ lower risk compared with abstainers. A widely cited meta-analysis of observational studies concluded that "Light to moderate alcohol consumption is associated with a reduced risk of multiple cardiovascular outcome" even after adjusting for factors such as the number of confounders included in a study (Ronksley et al. 2011). Such associations have been widely (mis)reported in interventional causal terms in the scientific literature as well as in news media, despite the non-causal designs and analyses on which they rest. This tendency is evident even in the titles and conclusions of influential observational studies-for example, "Moderate alcohol consumption reduces risk of ischemic stroke: the Northern Manhattan Study" (Elkind et al. 2006). This study explicitly states that "Cox proportional hazard regression modeling was used to assess hazard ratios and their $95 \% \mathrm{Cls}$ for the association of drinking with risk of stroke and vascular events" i.e. it used a non-causal, associational model (Aalen et al. 2015; Martinussen 2022) to quantify statistical associations-but it then nonetheless drew the striking interventional causal conclusion that "Our results demonstrate a strong protective effect of recent moderate alcohol consumption on risk of IS in a multiethnic, elderly population. Moderate consumption, defined as $\leq 2$ drinks of liquor, cans of beer, or glasses of wine, reduced the risk of IS by $\approx 50 \%$."

In practice, drinking is self-selected and entangled with health behaviors, socioeconomic status, and clinical factors that are difficult to measure and adjust; many studies also compared current drinkers to a pooled "non-drinker" group that included former drinkers who had quit because of illness ("sick-quitter" bias), inflating the appearance of benefit. When former drinkers are separated from lifetime abstainers and other design limitations are addressed, the apparent advantage for moderate drinkers largely attenuates or disappears (Stockwell et al. 2016).

These features make the key identifiability requirement of (conditional) exchangeability-that, after adjusting for measured covariates, drinkers and nondrinkers would have had the same distribution of outcomes under the same expos-ure-implausible. Without credible exchangeability (as well as positivity and consistency), adjusted associations from non-causal designs do not warrant interventional claims about what would happen if people were assigned to drink moderately. Consistent with this reading, Mendelian randomization analyses that use genetic proxies for lower alcohol consumption (e.g. variants in ADH1B/ALDH2) have failed to support cardioprotective effects of moderate alcohol and instead show higher blood pressure and stroke risk with greater intake (Holmes et al. 2014). Attempts to obtain direct randomized evidence on clinical cardiovascular outcomes have not established benefit. Re-analyses of prospective cohorts that correct key biases (e.g. separating lifetime abstainers from former drinkers and improving control for confounding) do not find a mortality benefit for low-volume drinkers compared with lifetime abstainers (Zhao et al. 2023).

In short, treating adjusted hazard ratios from self-selected drinking patterns as if they were effects of assigning moderate alcohol intake is a fallacy that has produced dramaticseeming, policy-relevant conclusions that do not stand up to careful investigation. Evidence from stronger causal designs

| E1 | Population validity | Study sample may not represent the target population. | Estimates may not apply to different demographic or cultural groups. | Stratified analyses; representativeness checks; reweighting | Adjust for sample differences; test subgroup effects | Convenience samples reduce generalizability |
| E2 | Ecological validity | Study setting may not reflect real-world conditions. | Controlled environments may exaggerate or distort effects. | Natural experiments; field studies; contextual reporting | Enhances realism and interpretability | Tradeoff with internal control |
| E3 | Temporal validity | Causal relationships may shift over time. | Findings may not hold under new conditions or future periods. | Longitudinal replication; historical sensitivity analysis | Tests durability and temporal invariance | Data may not span enough time |
| E4 | Treatment × Setting interaction | Effects vary by environment (e.g. hospitals vs. clinics). | Cannot generalize effects across sites without testing for variation. | Multi-site studies; causal transport formulas; metaanalysis | Capture heterogeneity across settings | Transportability requires strong assumptions |
| E5 | Treatment × Population interaction | Effects differ across demographic groups. | One-size-fits-all claims can mislead policy. | Subgroup analyses; CATE estimation; Invariant Causal Prediction (ICP) | Identifies effect modifiers and stable relationships | Requires sufficient subgroup sample sizes |
