| E9 | Over-control of confounding | Controlling for too many variables or for colliders may distort real-world relationships. | Adjusted models may no longer reflect naturalistic dynamics. | DAG-guided adjustment using minimal sufficient adjustment sets; model comparison with/without controls | Avoids removing meaningful causal pathways | Easy to misclassify variables as confounders |
| E10 | Publication bias | Studies with null or negative findings often go unpublished. | Inflates perceived generalizability and effect size. | Funnel plots; preregistration; trim-and-fill; trial registries | Improves evidence base scope | Bias adjustment methods have low power |
| E11 | Unjustified extrapolation across contexts | Applying findings across populations, conditions, or interventions without validation. | External claims may rest on unverified assumptions. | Transportability theory; ICP; meta-regression; sensitivity analysis | Provides formal extrapolation conditions | Requires comparable variables and models |

| 11. Review external-validity for quasiexperiments (QEs) | QE designs depend on natural variation and are vulnerable to multiple threats in E1-E10. | Use checklist of externalvalidity threats; document tests and mitigations. | Campbell \& Stanley threat table, design-specific diagnostics, replication in varied settings | Difference-in-differences or IV policy evaluations | Treating review as formality; ignoring flagged issues | QE study replicates across multiple jurisdictions before policy adoption. |

1. Have alternative explanations been tested through falsification methods (e.g. negative controls, placebo outcomes, simulation-based checks) under plausible violations of assumptions such as unmeasured confounding or selection bias?
2. Have findings been compared across methods or designs with unrelated bias structures (triangulation) to assess consistency?
3. Has the generalizability of the findings been explicitly assessed (e.g. via transport formulas, reweighting, subgroup analyses, Invariant Causal Prediction) to the intended target population or setting?
4. Have heterogeneous treatment effects (CATEs) been estimated, and their stability across relevant subgroups or datasets evaluated?
5. Have treatment × setting interactions (e.g. site-level heterogeneity, multi-site models) been tested to identify location-specific effects?
6. Have treatment × population interactions (e.g. effect modification by demographics or baseline risk) been tested?
7. Has the representativeness of the study sample been verified, or has reweighting been applied to match the target population?
8. Does the study setting plausibly reflect real-world conditions for application, or has artificiality in design been mitigated (e.g. via hybrid or field designs)?
9. Has the temporal validity of the effect been assessed, including replication across periods and explicit consideration of historical context that may influence effects?
10. Has the study evaluated its vulnerability to publication bias or selective reporting, including searching for contradictory or null results (e.g. registries, preprints, bias diagnostics)?
11. Are any extrapolations across jurisdictions, sectors, species, or other contexts justified with explicit assumptions, formal transportability tools, or bounding analyses?
12. For quasi-experimental designs, has a structured external-validity review covering all applicable items above been conducted and documented?
and corrected analyses is consistent with the view that necessary design-level conditions-especially credible exchangeability-were not met, so interventional claims of cardiovascular benefit are not supported.

### 6.3. Example: PM 2.5 reductions-interventional claims from associational models

In its 2022 policy assessment for the $\mathrm{PM}_{2.5}$ National Ambient Air Quality Standards, the U.S. EPA stated that meeting lower annual standards would reduce $\mathrm{PM}_{2.5}$-associated health risks by roughly $7-9 \%$ at $11.0 \mu \mathrm{~g} / \mathrm{m}^{3}$ and $30-37 \%$ at $8.0 \mu \mathrm{~g} / \mathrm{m}^{3}$ across 30 study areas. These are explicit interventional predictions: they forecast percentage reductions in mortality risks caused by counterfactual reductions in ambient $\mathrm{PM}_{2.5}$.

A subsequent methodological critique examined two long-term mortality studies selected by EPA to generate these forecasts and found that both relied on Cox propor-tional-hazards models fit to observational cohort data and that none of four minimum conditions for valid interventional prediction was satisfied: (1) a design supporting causal effects of interventions (e.g. quasi-experiments with appropriate controls); (2) causal models and methods appropriate to the intervention question; (3) at least approximately satisfied model assumptions; and (4) explicit modeling and adjustment of non-causal sources of association (e.g. confounding, exposure-measurement error, misspecification) (Cox 2023a). Without these, associational hazard ratios are not warranted as effects of assigning lower $\mathrm{PM}_{2.5}$ levels.

As in the Vitamin E and alcohol examples, interpreting these associational estimates as interventional causal effects also fails the necessary identifiability requirement of
conditional exchangeability. Even after adjustment, areas and persons experiencing different long-term $\mathrm{PM}_{2.5}$ levels may differ systematically in factors that influence mortality (e.g. socioeconomic, meteorologic, healthcare, behavioral, or compositional differences), and exposure measurement error and model misspecification can further distort hazard-based contrasts. Absent credible exchangeability (together with positivity and consistency), adjusted associations from noncausal designs should not be presented as the mortality changes that would follow if air quality were assigned to tighter standards.

An appropriate, technically sound, framing would emphasize that the underlying long-term cohort analyses estimate associations between $\mathrm{PM}_{2.5}$ and mortality under observed conditions, while reserving interventional conclusions for analyses that satisfy the above conditions (for example, welldesigned quasi-experimental evaluations of regulatory changes). It is difficult or impossible to interpret or validate causal predictions derived from the selected associational hazard-ratio models (Aalen et al. 2015; Martinussen 2022), so policy-impact statements should acknowledge these limitations rather than present them as established effects of lowering $\mathrm{PM}_{2.5}$.

A foundational principle is that association is not causation. Observational studies often report statistically significant associations between exposures and outcomes, but these do not warrant causal interpretation unless the assumptions needed for valid causal identification-such as temporal ordering, sufficient control of confounding, and appropriate model specification-are explicitly stated, empirically tested, and critically assessed for plausibility and consistency with the data. Depending on the methods used, this
may require strong assumptions (e.g. no unmeasured confounding), or weaker but more conservative assumptions that support sensitivity analyses (see Section 5.5). DAGs can clarify the assumed causal structure, reveal possible sources of bias, and help define appropriate adjustment sets.

Analysts should avoid overreliance on p-values, as statistical significance does not equal clinical importance, nor does it imply causality. They should report interval estimates (e.g. confidence or credible intervals) and interpret them clearly, being careful not to equate nonsignificant findings with no effect. Causal conclusions should be based on stated (and, ideally, tested) theoretical assumptions, design features, and effect size estimates-not threshold-based significance testing alone. It is good practice to avoid language such as "significant" and "not significant," as this oversimplifies inference and may obscure the true range of uncertainty. Analysts should instead discuss practical clinical, mechanistic, or biological interpretations of effect sizes (Lederer et al. 2019).

In addition, analysts should avoid the Table 2 Fallacymisinterpreting covariate-outcome associations in adjustment models as causal effects (Westreich and Greenland 2013). These coefficients are often included for control, not causal interpretation, and typically lack adjustment for their own confounders (Lederer et al. 2019), making causal interpretation inappropriate.

Subjective interpretive criteria (e.g. plausibility, consistency, analogy) must be used with caution. As noted by Hill (1965) and successors, such inductive criteria may reflect judgment rather than testable causal structure. When invoked, they should be supplemented with formal modeling, falsification testing, or mechanistic validation (Section 5.5.3). These tools can help rule out certain forms of bias and reduce overinterpretation, but success in falsification tests should not be confused with positive evidence of a causal effect; such tests help detect inconsistency with causal explanations but do not confirm them.

Analysts should also use caution in the language they apply to describe findings (Hernán 2016). Terms such as "effect," "impact," or "caused by" should be used only when the assumptions required for causal identification are clearly stated and plausibly satisfied. Misuse of causal language can mislead readers and policymakers. Avoiding causal implications when reporting purely associative results is essential to prevent misinterpretation. Even well-intentioned phrasing can create an illusion of causal certainty where none is warranted. We recommend explicitly qualifying language and avoiding interventional causal terminology unless all necessary conditions for its validity are satisfied and disclosed.

### 6.3.1. Pre-specify hypotheses and analytic plans

Analysts have substantial flexibility in modeling choices, which can lead-intentionally or not-to questionable research practices such as p-hacking, HARKing (Hypothesizing After Results are Known), and selective reporting. To guard against these risks, hypotheses and analytic strategies should be pre-specified before outcome data are examined. This includes defining exposures, outcomes, covariates, modeling
approaches, alternative causal hypotheses or theories (e.g. alternative causal DAG models) and subgroup analyses. Prespecification should also extend to sample size planning and stopping rules. Adding data after reviewing preliminary results-especially with the goal of achieving statistical sig-nificance-can capitalize on chance and distort effect estimates. Justifications for deviations from the original analytic plan should be transparently reported.

### 6.4. Example: Alternative causal explanations compatible with the same observed pattern-opioid mortality and prescription supply

Observed associations and temporal patterns are often compatible with multiple, substantively different causal explanations. This example illustrates how causal interpretationdistinct from statistical association or prediction-plays a critical role in evaluating which explanations are most supportable, and in clarifying what conclusions can and cannot be drawn from available evidence.

Several widely cited studies report that individuals who later initiate heroin or fentanyl use are disproportionately likely to have previously used prescription opioids, often with a clear temporal ordering in which prescription exposure precedes illicit opioid use (Jones 2013; Hartung et al. 2021; Dashti et al. 2024). At a descriptive level, this pattern is real and often statistically robust. However, it does not, by itself, identify a unique causal explanation. One possible causal interpretation is a gateway model, in which exposure to prescription opioids causally increases the probability of later heroin or fentanyl use, for example by inducing tolerance, dependence, or preferences for opioid effects that subsequently generalize to illicit opioids. Under this interpretation, reducing exposure to prescription opioids-especially highdose, long-duration, or nonmedical exposure-should causally reduce later initiation of heroin or fentanyl, holding other factors constant.

An alternative causal interpretation, equally compatible with the same observed temporal pattern, is a common-liability model (Dashti et al. 2024). On this view, a relatively small subpopulation has elevated vulnerability to substance use disorders due to stable individual, social, or environmental factors. Members of this subpopulation tend to seek psychoactive drugs, using prescription opioids when they are easily available and substituting toward heroin or fentanyl when access, price, or enforcement constraints change. In this model, prescription opioid use does not cause later illicit opioid use; instead, both are downstream consequences of shared vulnerability (called a "common liability") interacting with changing availability conditions. The observed sequencing arises because prescription opioids are often more accessible earlier in time, not because they causally initiate a progression. Consistent with this interpretation, Dash et al. argue that although prescription opioid use often temporally precedes heroin or fentanyl initiation, individual-level evidence does not clearly support a causal gateway mechanism and is at least as consistent with a common-liability explanation.

Crucially, both models predict the same descriptive facts: higher rates of prior prescription opioid use among those who later use heroin or fentanyl, and a consistent temporal ordering between the two. Yet the policy implications differ sharply. Under a gateway interpretation, reducing prescription opioid exposure should reduce later illicit opioid use. Under a common-liability interpretation, supply-side restrictions on prescription opioids may have little effect on, or may even increase, illicit opioid harms if vulnerable individuals substitute toward more dangerous drugs in the presence of unchanged demand.

This example illustrates a broader interpretive point: observational evidence such as consistent, significant associations and temporal ordering, even when statistically robust, may not uniquely determine causal structure. The same data can be generated by multiple causal models with materially different implications for intervention and policy, and distinguishing among them requires evidence that goes beyond the observed association itself, such as evidence bearing on underlying vulnerability or liability (e.g. behavioral, genetic, phenotypic, or developmental evidence), changes in availability or incentives, or patterns of response to specific interventions (Dashti et al. 2024). For this reason, alternative causal explanations that are plausibly compatible with the available data should be articulated in advance, along with the qualitative and quantitative patterns that would tend to support or undermine each. Pre-specification of competing causal hypotheses-and of the analyses intended to discriminate among them-helps prevent post hoc selection of a favored narrative once a particular association has been observed. As discussed further in the Discussion and Conclusions section, this can be framed in decision-analytic terms as explicitly characterizing an ambiguity set of observationally compatible causal models and then asking which models can be ruled out, or meaningfully constrained, by additional assumptions, data, or necessary-condition arguments. This example also illustrates how the same observational evidence can satisfy some necessary conditions for causal inference (e.g. temporal ordering and statistical dependence) while failing to satisfy others (e.g. unique identification of a causal model or explanation), a distinction that recurs across the different causal frameworks discussed in the paper.

Pre-registration of hypotheses and planned methods, including covariate adjustments, modeling choices, and subgroup analyses, helps distinguish between confirmatory and exploratory inferences. Analysts should also clearly distinguish pre-specified hypotheses from post hoc interpretations. When exploratory analyses are conducted, these should be transparently labeled as such in the text. Moreover, all major outcomes and analyses-regardless of whether they yielded statistically significant results-should be reported to avoid selective disclosure and inflated estimates of effect. When results conform to a pre-registered protocol, the credibility of causal interpretations improves. Platforms such as OSF, ClinicalTrials.gov, and the Registered Reports format offer infrastructure for such transparency. Pre-specification is not meant to suppress exploration but to ensure it is clearly marked as such in interpretation and reporting.

As discussed in Section 5.4, pre-specification can be complemented by internal validation tools such as leave-one-out sensitivity checks, blinded analysis, multiverse analysis, and specification curve analysis (Simonsohn et al. 2020; Baldwin et al. 2022). In blinded analysis, trial analyses are conducted on scrambled, masked, or outcome-excluded datasets before unblinding, allowing verification of analytic workflows while reducing confirmation bias. Such approaches, already in use in genomics and clinical trials, can be adapted to observational studies to preserve objectivity. These limit the influence of researcher degrees of freedom and reduce the risk of confirmation bias in interpretation.

### 6.4.1. Use valid and reliable measurement tools

As discussed in Sections 4 and 5, valid causal inference and causal interpretation of estimated contrasts are constrained by the data on which they rely. Poor measurement of exposures, outcomes, or covariates can lead to biased estimates, attenuated effect sizes, or misclassification. To maintain interpretive integrity, researchers should use validated instruments and report the reliability and validity of measurement tools. Misclassification and measurement error degrade interpretability. Unreliable or biased measurement of exposures, outcomes, or covariates can mask true effects, create spurious associations, or attenuate effect estimates. Tools should be validated, error rates quantified, and measurement processes described. Where measurement error is suspected, analysts should apply errors-in-variables corrections (Section 5), calibrate self-reports using biomarker data, or use instrument validation studies to estimate misclassification rates. Additional corrective methods include regression calibration, simulation-extrapolation (SIMEX), Bayesian errors-in-variables models, and measurement-DAG ("m-DAG") approaches, each suited to particular error structures (see Section 5.4.2.1.). Testretest reliability and inter-rater agreement should be reported when applicable. Crucially, differential measurement errorwhere error rates differ by exposure or outcome group-can induce spurious associations and is especially damaging to causal interpretation.

### 6.4.2. Confirm that the outcome still depends on the exposure after adjustment

A necessary condition for interpreting any observed association as causal is that changes or differences in exposures must continue to predict corresponding variations in the outcome even after appropriate adjustment for confounding and other threats discussed in Section 5 - measurement error, missing data, latent variables, model misspecification, collider bias, selection bias, and standard threats to internal validity in quasi-experimental studies (Huntington-Klein 2021). In other words, effects or responses are not conditionally independent of their direct causes after conditioning on other variables (Pearl 2009. Conversely, if, after adjustment, the exposure and outcome are conditionally independent, then the data do not suggest that the outcome depends directly on exposure, or that exposure is a direct cause of the outcome. This principle, while perhaps obvious in principle, is
often ignored in practice. Regression coefficients and other indicators of association are often presented without conditional independence tests or evidence of full adjustment for threats to internal validity.

This requirement follows from a basic logic of causation: If setting the exposure to different values would genuinely alter the distribution of the outcome, then the exposure must retain explanatory relevance after accounting for other relevant variables. Conversely, if the exposure no longer contributes to explaining outcome variation once confounders are controlled, then the data no longer support a claim of interventional impact. Analysts must therefore verify that the exposure still matters in the adjusted model-not merely as a statistical correlate, but as a persisting contributor to variation in the outcome under the assumed causal structure.

Several analytic tools can help assess this condition (Duong et al. 2024). Statistical conditional independence tests, for example, probe whether the exposure and outcome remain statistically significantly associated given the set of adjusted covariates (Huntington-Klein 2021). Residualized regressions offer a related strategy: analysts first regress the outcome on covariates (excluding the exposure), and then test whether the residual variation is still predicted by the exposure. Doubly robust estimators (see Section 5) can also be used here: they allow estimation of causal effects when either the outcome model or the treatment model is correctly specified, offering some protection against moderate model misspecification. In this interpretive context, their role is to help assess whether the exposure retains explanatory power after adjustment. When applied, both model components and diagnostic checks should be reported, and the underlying assumptions-such as no unmeasured confounding, correct specification of at least one model, and sufficient covariate overlap-should be stated and justified.

It is not uncommon for a causal signal to be attenuated or even eliminated after adjustment, especially in studies with many covariates or limited outcome variation. This attenuation does not necessarily indicate absence of causal-ity-it may also result from model overfitting, inappropriate covariate inclusion (e.g. mediators or colliders), or insufficient statistical power. Analysts must therefore interpret null or diminished adjusted associations cautiously and consider plausible alternative explanations, including specification error and overcontrol.

To support valid causal interpretation, reports should clearly document whether the exposure continues to predict the outcome after adjustments for the threats discussed in Section 5, how this was tested, and what assumptions underlie the conclusions. Absence of explanatory power postadjustment weakens the case for a causal effect, but presence alone does not prove it. Still, confirming that the exposure matters in adjusted models is a necessary-not sufficient-condition for any interventional causal interpretation. Analysts can test this using conditional independence tests, residualized regressions, or doubly robust estimators such as augmented inverse probability weighting (AIPW) (Duong et al. 2024). If the exposure no longer predicts the outcome after adjustment, this may suggest overcontrol or collider bias, model misspecification, or the absence of a true
causal effect. In many studies, especially those with many covariates or low signal-to-noise ratio, overadjustment or poor model fit can mask any remaining causal signal.

As discussed in Section 5, doubly robust estimators such as augmented inverse probability weighting provide valid estimates of the average treatment effect (ATE) when either the propensity score model or the outcome regression model is correctly specified. These estimators combine outcome regression and inverse probability weighting to protect against model misspecification. When used, both model components and diagnostics should be reported, and assumptions such as no unmeasured confounding and correct model specification must be justified. Robust interpretation also benefits from testing alternative functional forms, variable codings, and estimation strategies. Where feasible, design-based methods such as instrumental variables, regression discontinuity, or synthetic controls can be applied as complementary identification strategies. Consistent conclusions across such specifications increase interpretive credibility (Fu 2023).

A sound and useful causal estimate should hold not only within the sample and model from which it was derived but also across relevant populations and settings. External validation examines whether the study population represents the intended target population and whether differences can be addressed through reweighting or formal transportability methods. Where effect heterogeneity is expected, subgroup analyses and effect modification assessments (see D.5) are essential. Failure to assess generalizability can lead to incorrect policy inferences when causal effects vary across contexts.

Before drawing strong causal conclusions, investigators should systematically compare the proposed causal model to plausible alternatives and rival hypotheses-including theorydriven, data-driven, or causal-discovery-informed variantsand document the features that distinguish them. Red-teaming and rival-model analysis can help identify whether other causal structures might explain the data equally well or better.

### 6.4.3. . Evaluate and interpret heterogeneity and effect modification

Valid interpretation of estimated causal effects should consider that causal effects are often heterogeneous-they may vary across individuals, contexts, or subgroups (see Sections 4.3.2.3 and 5.4.1). The effect of an exposure on the outcome is often not the same for everyone. It may change, or be modified, depending on the levels of other variables (e.g. age, sex, income, genetic status, policy context). For example, a public health intervention might benefit high-risk groups while having little or no effect on others. Recognizing this heterogeneity is essential for policy relevance and for tailoring recommendations. Modeling heterogeneity in individuallevel responses helps to shed light on the key interpretive issues of to whom estimated causal effects apply, and under what conditions. Conversely, failure to account for heterogeneity can lead to overgeneralization or poor policy targeting.

Effect heterogeneity can be explored across pre-specified subgroups (e.g. age, sex) or identified through data-driven methods such as causal trees (Künzel et al. 2019). While prespecified analyses align with confirmatory hypotheses and ease interpretability, data-driven subgroup discovery-especially when validated via sample splitting or cross-valid-ation-can reveal novel heterogeneity patterns. Both approaches should be transparently reported, with caution about multiple testing and overfitting.

In addition to interaction terms and stratified models, analysts may use causal trees to discover subgroups with systematically different treatment effects (Duong et al. 2024). Causal trees, particularly "honest" trees that split data into separate estimation and partitioning sets, support interpretable subgroup analysis while reducing overfitting (Athey and Imbens 2016; Duong et al. 2024). These models yield clear ifthen rules for targeting interventions. However, results depend on tuning parameters such as tree depth, and bootstrapping may be needed to assess stability. Causal effect estimates can be operationalized to inform treatment allocation (or exposure modification) strategies that maximize population-level benefit. These strategies can be derived via policy learning techniques. Policy evaluation should account for uncertainty, fairness, and interpretability

Causal trees are particularly valuable when interpretability is prioritized, as they can transparently partition populations by effect modifiers and support targeted policy design. To avoid overfitting, cross-validation and pruning methods should be applied, and subgroup results should be interpreted with appropriate uncertainty quantification. Causal forests extend causal trees by averaging across multiple trees to reduce variance and improve stability of CATE estimates (Künzel et al. 2019). They support individualized predictions and offer a flexible alternative to linear models. However, their implementation requires careful tuning, large sample sizes, and often more computational resources.

Importantly, both the presence and the magnitude of effect modifications often depend on whether relative or absolute changes in risk are considered. An exposure may appear to have constant effects on an additive scale (e.g. risk differences) but variable effects on a multiplicative scale (e.g. risk ratios or odds ratios), or vice versa. This scale dependence means that conclusions about heterogeneity-and their policy implications-depend on whether additive or multiplicative effects are being considered. Moreover, effect modification refers to causal variation in the effect of a single exposure variable across levels of one or more other variables, whereas interaction describes joint effects and often captures statistical departure from linear additivity in regression models. As Igelström et al. (2022) note, "The presence and extent of EMM [Effect Measure Modification] mathematically depends on the choice of an additive or multiplicative scale. or both .... If both the exposure and effect modifier are causes of the outcome, then EMM will always be present on at least one scale." Analysts must therefore pre-specify the measurement scale(s) and effect modifiers of interest and interpret interactions in light of both statistical modeling choices and substantive causal questions.

To interpret estimated causal contrasts accurately, analysts should investigate effect modification using interaction terms, stratified analyses, or more advanced tools such as causal forests or quantile-based effect summaries (Künzel et al. 2019). These techniques aim to estimate Conditional Average Treatment Effects (CATEs) or distributions of individual-level effects. Effect heterogeneity is often substantial and substantively important (Huntington-Klein 2021, Chapters 10 and 22). However, interpretation must account for statistical uncertainty, especially in the tails of the effect distribution, often requiring bootstrapping or other resampling methods to quantify the reliability of subgroup-specific effects. Visualizations (e.g. sorted effects plots) and bootstrapped uncertainty intervals are recommended for transparency. Moreover, policy recommendations should be commensurate with the strength and limitations of the study. If causal conclusions depend on unverifiable assumptions, these should be disclosed, and any recommendations should be explicitly qualified as contingent on those assumptions.

To assess the significance of treatment heterogeneity across subgroups, analysts should also apply formal hypothesis testing frameworks with multiple testing correction to control for family-wise error rates. This guards against false positives due to multiple comparisons and supports more reliable subgroup inferences. Beyond estimating heterogeneous effects, analysts may use Conditional Average Treatment Effects (CATEs) to help optimize intervention strategies. These can involve individualized treatment rules or subgroup-based allocations (or exposure reduction priorities and schedules). The design and evaluation of such strategies should be transparent, and their assumptions-including model generalizability and stability-should be explicitly stated.

Beyond sampling variability and heterogeneity, causal interpretations are also subject to uncertainty about causal structures and assumptions. As explained in Section 5, multiverse (Rijnhart et al. 2022; Götz et al. 2024), specification curve analysis (Simonsohn et al. 2020), and vibration-ofeffects analyses (Patel et al. 2015) can map the stability of results across defensible analytic choices, while value-of-information analysis can identify which assumptions or data elements most affect decision-relevant conclusions. These tools help decision-makers gauge the robustness of causal claims and prioritize further research.

### 6.4.4. Present results transparently and accessibly

Interpretation suffers when results are aggregated, overcomplicated, or poorly visualized. Researchers should use clear tables, plain-language summaries, and accessible graphics (e.g. using color-blind-friendly palettes). Whenever possible, include individual-level effect distributions, subgroup results, and visual uncertainty (e.g. confidence bands). The STROBE guidelines (Cuschieri 2019) provide practical recommendations for reporting observational study results clearly and completely (Lederer et al. 2019).

When interaction is of substantive interest, authors should report measures on both the additive and multiplicative scales. In particular, the Relative Excess Risk due to

Interaction is often recommended for public health interpretation, as it captures the degree of synergy between exposures under the sufficient component cause framework (Knol and VanderWeele 2012). Confidence intervals and P-values for these interaction measures should be reported to aid interpretation. Effect estimates (e.g. risk ratios or risk differences) should be presented for each joint exposure stratum using a single, clearly defined reference group-typically the stratum with the lowest baseline risk-to facilitate interpretation and reconstruction of interaction measures.

To support interpretability, studies reporting effect modification or interaction should:

1. Report stratum-specific effect estimates;
2. Report the effect of one exposure within strata of the other;
3. Include additive and multiplicative interaction measures with confidence intervals and P-values; and
4. List the confounders adjusted for each exposure (Knol and VanderWeele 2012).

Tools and spreadsheets are publicly available to compute RERI and related interaction measures using standard output from SAS, Stata, or R (Knol and VanderWeele, 2012, Appendix).

### 6.4.5. Share data and code for interpretive transparency

Open-science practices-including pre-registration, thorough documentation of modeling decisions, and encouraging independent replication-reduce the risk of selective reporting and strengthen causal claims (Lakens et al. 2024). Emphasis should remain on effect sizes, uncertainty intervals, robustness to alternative explanations, and substantive importance rather than on statistical significance alone. Transparency and replicability are integral to credible causal interpretation of observational data. Authors should deposit raw data, analysis scripts, and derived outputs in open repositories (e.g. the Open Science Framework repository, OSF, at www.cos.io/ products/osf) and provide sufficient documentation to reproduce results. Studies lacking reproducibility infrastructure face interpretive limits, especially when causal claims are made.

Clearly referenced data and code also allow for errorchecking, sensitivity analysis, and future meta-analytic use. Transparent sharing also requires accurate and complete documentation of all major research decisions. This includes describing how data were cleaned and processed, what analytic choices were made (and when), and whether any deviations from planned protocols occurred. Detailed documentation enhances trust, facilitates replication, and allows for more meaningful peer evaluation. Interpretive integrity requires engaging with evidence that contradicts or complicates one's findings. Failure to discuss alternative explanations, null results, or prior studies reaching different conclusions can distort scientific understanding. Responsible interpretation includes balanced presentation of the
literature, limitations of one's own findings, and plausible alternative interpretations supported by data or theory.

These interpretive constraints underscore that valid causal conclusions require more than technically sound estimation. Even when study design and analytic methods are appropriate, results must be interpreted within the limits imposed by the data, the assumptions, and the analytic plan. Table 27 summarizes interpretive conditions for responsible causal interpretation of results from observational data, explaining why each matters, its implications for practice, supporting tools, and common pitfalls. Checklist D presents a checklist to help systematically verify whether each requirement has been addressed before framing results as causal. Together, these tools are intended to help ensure that reported conclusions and causal interpretations are credible, reproducible, and appropriately qualified. Our hope is that the checklist can guide authors, reviewers, and readers in evaluating whether a study's interpretation and conclusions meet the minimum standards for valid interventional causal inference and interpretation. Items should be answered based on explicit information in the manuscript, and responses should be supported by evidence or citations where applicable.

## 7. Robustness, generalizability, and external validity

Causal estimates should be conceptually well-defined and empirically grounded (Section 3), derived from sound study designs (Section 4), competently analyzed (Section 5), and carefully interpreted (Section 6). Even when causal estimates are internally valid and carefully interpreted, however, they remain susceptible to external threats arising from changes in population, setting, time, or underlying assumptions. This section focuses on the robustness, stability, and generalizability of causal conclusions when applied beyond a study's original context. Sections 5 and 6, considered the robustness and trustworthiness of a single study's conclusions in the face of plausible variations in model specifications, analytic choices, and interpretive framing. This section considers external robustness-whether causal conclusions and predicted effects of interventions estimated from a study persist when conditions, populations, settings, or time periods change. These are threats to external validity, and they determine whether study findings can reliably inform real-world decisions.

A conclusion that is fragile under slight perturbations or plausible contextual changes, or that is valid only under narrow conditions, may be insufficiently reliable to guide policy interventions or actions (Schwartz and Prins 2024). Some studies also face ecological validity threats-where the conditions under which the data were generated differ meaningfully from real-world application contexts-and others may lose relevance over time. Highly controlled study environments may also suffer from artificiality, limiting the relevance of results for practical implementation (Schwartz and Prins 2024). Hybrid designs and naturalistic settings can help mitigate these concerns. This section outlines necessary conditions for ensuring that causal findings are not only internally credible but also reliable for guiding actions and causally
effective interventions in real-world conditions beyond the original study.

### 7.1. Challenge conclusions with alternative explanations and falsification tests

Many of the methods used to probe internal validity, discussed in Section 5, can be adapted to test external robustness by asking whether conclusions survive plausible variations in assumptions, modeling choices, and contextual changes. A foundational tenet of scientific inference is that valid causal conclusions must survive attempts to refute them or explain them away. Robust causal claims should not be artifacts of untested or fragile assumptions or unacknowledged confounding.

One approach is to assess how conclusions behave under plausible violations of key assumptions in contexts different from the original study-such as in the presence of unmeasured confounding, model misspecification, selection bias, or changes in population composition or covariates. While falsification tools such as negative controls and simulation-based checks were introduced in Section 5 as robustness diagnostics, they also serve a broader role: testing the coherence of causal claims under adversarial scenarios or plausible alternative structures. They are increasingly widely used use as stress tests for fragility in modern causal analysis and modeling software. For example, the authors of the DoWhy opensource Python library for causal analysis (Sharma and Kiciman 2020; Blöbaum et al. 2024) describe the use of several falsification tests in the fourth and final step of their "end-to-end library for causal inference." They explain that this causal inference software library is
> ...based on the formal framework of causal graphs to specify and test causal assumptions. DoWhy presents an API for the four steps common to any causal analysis-(1) modeling the data using a causal graph and structural assumptions, (2) identifying whether the desired effect is estimable under the causal model, (3) estimating the effect using statistical estimators, and finally (4) refuting the obtained estimate through robustness checks and sensitivity analyses. In particular, DoWhy implements a number of robustness checks including placebo tests, bootstrap tests, and tests for unobserved confounding. (Sharma and Kiciman 2020)

Falsification tests can take several forms. As discussed in Section 5, negative control variables-including exposures that should have no causal effect on the outcome (NCEs) or outcomes not plausibly impacted by the exposure (NCOs)can help detect residual confounding or bias (Shi et al. 2020); they can also help to correct or place bounds on the effects of unmeasured confounders under certain conditions (Arnold et al. 2016; Flanders 2023), although they are not a panacea for unmeasured confounding (Penning de Vries and Groenwold, 2023). Placebo outcomes-outcomes assumed to be unaffected by the exposure-help identify spurious correlations (Arnold et al. 2016). Simulation-based falsification methods such as the bootstrap tests in DoWhy create counterfactual datasets under known null or non-causal structures to evaluate whether the original methods would falsely detect effects under those conditions. If similar patterns emerge in these simulated or placebo settings, the original
causal inference may be suspect. Applied to external robustness, these tools can also be used in parallel datasets, replications in different populations, or historical replays to test whether the observed pattern is context-specific. Such falsification techniques are most informative when grounded in substantive knowledge and clearly documented. However, failure to falsify is not equivalent to validation: passing a falsification test supports-but does not prove-causal validity. Thus, these tests function best as stress diagnostics for fragility in real-world applications, not as certifiers.

Triangulation offers a complementary route to external robustness: converging evidence from diverse study designs or populations, each subject to different biases, strengthens the case for a stable causal relationship (Lawlor et al. 2016; Matthay et al. 2019; Hammerton and Munafò, 2021). For example, a policy effect estimated in one state can be tested against data from another state with similar baseline conditions but different implementation rules. If both yield similar conclusions despite different bias structures, external robustness is supported. However, selective reporting and publication bias can skew the apparent weight of support. Analysts and readers should consider whether null, contradictory, or unpublished findings exist that could alter the interpretation.

### 7.2. Evaluate generalizability and transportability

Even correct and robust findings from a single study may not generalize beyond the study boundaries to other target populations, conditions, and interventions. Internal validity is a necessary but not sufficient condition for broader applicability. Generalizability (i.e. external validity) asks: will the effect estimated here still hold there? Transportability extends this question to populations with different covariate distributions or effect-modifying factors (Pearl and Bareinboim 2014). Results obtained in one context-such as a specific geographic region, time period, population subgroup, or institutional setting-may not extend to others. As mentioned earlier, seminal work by Campbell and Stanley (1963) cataloged multiple potential threats to external validity, including interactions between treatment (exposure) and testing, subject selection, experimental setting, and historical timing. These threats commonly arise in quasi-experimental and observational settings where treatment assignment mechanisms differ across contexts. Modern methods formalize these concerns. For example, causal transport formulas estimate how an effect derived from a "source" population would translate to a "target" population by reweighting the source sample to match the target on observed covariates (Levy et al. 2024). This approach typically requires strong assumptions, such as the absence of unmeasured effect modifiers.

To illustrate the basic idea, suppose a study estimates the causal effect of an intervention in a source population in which $70 \%$ of participants are male, while the target population of interest is evenly split by sex. If treatment effects differ by sex, then the average causal effect estimated in the source population will generally not apply directly to the target population. A transportability analysis addresses this mismatch by explicitly modeling effect heterogeneity and
reweighting or adjusting the source-population estimates to reflect the covariate distribution in the target population. Intuitively, this asks not whether the same effect holds everywhere, but how the effect would change if the study population were "recomposed" to resemble the target population with respect to effect-modifying variables.

More formally, the transportability framework developed by Pearl and Bareinboim (2014) uses causal DAGs to represent which mechanisms are shared across populations and which differ. Differences between source and target popula-tions-such as sex ratios, baseline risk factors, health system characteristics, or exposure mechanisms-are encoded as selection variables in a causal graph. Graphical criteria can then be used to determine whether a causal effect is transportable in principle and, if so, to derive a transport formula that combines data from the source population with covariate information from the target population. When these criteria are not satisfied-such as when unmeasured effect modifiers differ across populations-transportability is not achievable without additional assumptions or data. Thus, transportability analyses make explicit both how causal conclusions are adjusted for new contexts and why such adjustments may or may not be warranted.

Another empirically grounded approach is Invariant Causal Prediction (ICP) (Peters et al. 2016; Heinze-Deml et al. 2018; Pfister et al. 2019). As introduced in Section 5, ICP tests whether causal relationships remain stable across different populations or settings. If a presumed causal law or relationship (such as the SCM formula or conditional probability distribution for a variable given the values of its parents) holds in multiple settings with varying background variables, it gains credibility as a stable (and hence generalizable) causal structure. Conversely, instability across settings can signal context-specific mechanisms or unmeasured effect modification.

Mechanistic reasoning can strengthen generalizability claims: if the underlying biological, behavioral, or policy mechanisms plausibly operate the same way in the target context, external validity is more defensible. Conversely, when mechanisms depend on context-specific resources, infrastructure, or cultural factors, direct transport may be risky (Schwartz and Prins 2024). Examples of such interactions are common:

- Suppose that a patient education program increases medication adherence in a tightly managed clinical trial. When implemented in routine care, adherence rates drop sharply due to lower follow-up intensity, weakening the observed treatment effect. This illustrates how ecological validity and "treatment × setting interaction" can erode generalizability despite strong internal validity.
- In a large Department of Veterans Affairs study of substance use disorder treatment (Tiet et al. 2007), the relative benefit of more intense (in-patient/residential) versus out-patient care depended strongly on patients' baseline severity. Regression modeling suggested that those with more severe disorders improved more in in-patient settings, while those with less severe problems had similar
outcomes in either setting. This illustrates how differences in the composition of the target population-here, severity mix-can limit the generalizability of an observed treatment effect to new populations.
- Identifiable subpopulations-defined by observed charac-teristics-within clustered datasets (e.g. patients within clinics, residents in different communities) may respond very differently to different exposures or treatments, highlighting the importance of detecting subgroup-specific effects to assess external validity in real-world contexts (Fokkema et al. 2018). For example, in a large-scale U.S. cohort study Fokkema and Zeileis (2024) used tree-based methods to detect subgroups of children with divergent academic growth trajectories in reading, math, and sci-ence-subgroups defined by baseline characteristics (e.g. SES, motor skills, behaviors) and embedded in clustered structures (children within schools). These subgroup-specific patterns reveal how population composition and institutional clustering (e.g. schools) can constrain the generalizability of findings to other contexts or systems

Ultimately, generalizability claims require both empirical support and conceptual justification. Assumptions of effect homogeneity, stable unit treatment value, or external similarity must be made explicit and, where possible, testedideally with replication across diverse settings. Otherwise, even a perfectly executed study may have limited policy relevance beyond its original sample.

### 7.3. Causal tree-based methods for identifying generalizable heterogeneity

Average treatment effects (ATEs)-even when robust and internally valid-can obscure substantial variation across subpopulations or settings. Identifying heterogeneous treatment effects (HTEs) through causal tree-based methods (Athey and Imbens 2016) offers a strategy to assess which findings may generalize or fail to transport across contexts. Causal forests, a leading machine-learning tool, can uncover such HTEs (Künzel et al. 2019). For example, in a reanalysis of two randomized trials of intensive vs. standard glycemic control in type 2 diabetes, researchers applied causal forests to pooled individ-ual-level data $(n=12,042)$. They identified eight subgroups, defined by variables including hemoglobin glycation index, eGFR, fasting glucose, age, and BMI, in which the risk differences for major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) ranged from $-5.1 \%$ to $+3.1 \%$ (Edward et al. 2022). Importantly, HTEs identified in one study did not replicate across similar subgroups in the other study-despite high concordance in variable importance-but pooling individual-level data revealed seven subgroups with consistent treatment effects across studies (Raghavan et al. 2022). This illustrates both the fragility and recoverability of subgroup findings across studies.

More generally, model-based forests and related techniques extend HTE detection to contexts with clustered data and between-study heterogeneity (Fokkema and Zeileis 2024). These methods allow researchers to test whether subgroup effects observed in one setting persist in others.

By applying these methods across multiple datasets, trials, or environments, researchers can distinguish stable HTEs (more likely to generalize) from context-specific effects (less portable). This approach enriches assessments of external validity by linking effect heterogeneity to observable covariates and contextual moderators.

### 7.4. Evidence base integrity

External robustness is meaningless if the underlying evidence base is distorted. Findings that appear consistent across a few studies may still reflect selective reporting or biased publication patterns, especially when replication is rare, preregistration is uncommon, or sponsorship shapes dissemination. Failure to present contradictory findings or to acknowledge credible null results can create a misleading appearance of consensus and robustness and inflate confidence in estimated effects. To assess the integrity of the evidence base, analysts can conduct systematic searches for unpublished, null, or contradictory findings in trial registries, preprint archives, and conference proceedings; utilize statistical diagnostics in meta-analyses-such as funnel plot asymmetry, Egger's test, and trim-and-fill methods-to detect small-study effects and publication bias; and perform sensitivity or robustness analyses to evaluate how pooled estimates might change if plausible numbers of missing null studies were included (Egger et al. 1997; Sterne et al. 2011; Mathur and VanderWeele, 2020). These statistical strategies help assess evidentiary robustness beyond a single study, ensuring that generalizability claims are grounded in a comprehensive and unbiased evidence base (Lin \& Chu, 2018).

Beyond statistical diagnostics for publication bias, it is also essential to consider the institutional drivers that shape research dissemination. For example, Gerrits et al. (2020) found that a higher "pressure to create societal impact" was significantly associated with a greater number of questionable research practices (QRPs) in reporting Health Services Research (HSR) results. They explain that

> In this study we focus on the field of Health Services Research (HSR). HSR has a direct link to policy and practice, where stakeholders and funders may contribute considerably to the interpretation of results. ..

> Recent work has suggested that scientific HSR publications may include a median of six QRPs [questionable research practices] in the reporting of messages and conclusions. QRPs were primarily found in reported implications and recommendations for policy and practice, in a lack of mentioning of contradictory evidence, and in the conclusions of the scientific publication. The occurrence of these QRPs is concerning as messages and conclusions reported in scientific HSR literature are often transferred to policy makers, managers, and the general public... [and] may be accepted as established evidence and become part of the decision-making process on health and healthcare.

Specific training and attention to coauthor conflicts of interest were tied to fewer such practices. This underscores that even when quantitative tests (e.g. funnel plots or Egger's regression) suggest no bias, the broader research environment may still skew the integrity of the evidence base. Thus, effective assessments of external robustness
should pair statistical tools with scrutiny of questionable research practices s and inclusion and reporting biases-such as exclusion of contradictory evidence from the literature reviews, discussions, and conclusions of paper-to ensure that generalized causal claims rest on a complete, balanced, and transparently reported body of evidence.

### 7.5. Anticipating and addressing threats to external validity in quasi-experimental designs

Quasi-experimental (QE) designs are indispensable in many policy-relevant settings where randomization is infeasible (see Section 4). Their reliance on naturally occurring variation, however, makes them especially susceptible to the threats to external validity outlined earlier in Sections 7.1-7.4. Table 28 outlines common threats to external validity (Campbell and Stanley 1963), and identifies strategies for detecting, mitigating, or explicitly modeling these challenges. The main threats are as follows:

- Population validity (E1) is compromised when the study sample differs systematically from the target population. For example, an intervention tested only in urban hospitals may not perform similarly in rural clinics. Representativeness checks, stratified analyses, and poststratification reweighting can help quantify and, in some cases, correct for such differences.
- Ecological validity (E2) refers to the realism of study conditions. Effects observed in highly controlled or atypical settings may fail to carry over to more variable real-world environments. Natural experiments, field studies, and detailed contextual reporting can help bridge this gap.
- Temporal validity (E3) is threatened when causal relationships shift over time due to evolving technology, demographics, or societal norms.
- Treatment × setting interaction (E4) occurs when institutional factors-such as resource availability, staffing, or policy norms-alter the effectiveness of an intervention. Multi-site evaluations, site-by-treatment interaction models, and transportability formulas can identify and quantify these differences.
- Treatment × population interaction (E5) arises when an intervention benefits certain demographic or risk groups but not others, while sample-treatment interaction (E8) indicates that effectiveness depends on unmeasured participant characteristics. Conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimation, causal forests, and subgroupspecific analyses can detect and characterize such heterogeneity.
- Treatment × history interaction (E6) is a related concern, arising when results are tied to unique historical events-such as pandemics or major regulatory changes-that may not recur. Replication across multiple time periods, historical falsification tests, and explicit modeling of temporal covariates can help separate transient effects from durable causal relationships.
- Artificiality of experimental design (E7) can limit applicability if eligibility criteria, implementation protocols, or
exposure conditions are contrived in ways that suppress real-world complexity. Hybrid trials, field RCTs, and qualitative triangulation can improve ecological relevance.
- Over-control of confounding (E9)-especially when colliders or intermediates are mistakenly adjusted for-can distort effect estimates and reduce transportability. Directed acyclic graph (DAG)-guided variable selection and the use of minimal sufficient adjustment sets can help preserve relevant causal pathways.
- Publication bias (E10) and selective reporting of litera-ture-as discussed in detail in Section 7.4-can also compromise external validity in QE contexts. Selective publication of "positive" findings and under-reporting of null or contradictory results can create a misleading sense that an observed effect is consistent or generalizable. In quasi-experimental designs-where replications are often limited and results may be context-specificpublication bias can disproportionately inflate perceived robustness. While the detection and mitigation strategies in E. 4 (e.g. funnel plot asymmetry, Egger's test, trim-andfill, registry searches) apply here, QE studies benefit from incorporating such checks proactively, before generalizing findings to new settings or populations.
- Finally, unjustified extrapolation across contexts (E11)such as applying results from one sector, jurisdiction, or species to another without formal testing-can mislead policy. Meta-regression, Invariant Causal Prediction, and transportability theory provide formal conditions and empirical checks for such extrapolation.

These threats arise not from estimation error or analytic fragility but from mismatches between the study context and the contexts to which causal conclusions are applied. Anticipating, detecting, and addressing these threats is essential for ensuring that study findings are not only internally credible but also broadly informative for decision-making in diverse real-world environments (Campbell and Stanley 1963).

Addressing threats to external validity requires an explicit, documented process. For each QE study, analysts should identify which threats are plausible, apply relevant diagnostics or mitigation strategies, and evaluate whether the findings remain stable under plausible changes in population, setting, time, and analytic specification.

The following tables present this guidance in two complementary formats. Table 29 outlines necessary conditions for robustness and generalizability, explaining why each matters, its implications for practice, relevant methods, common pitfalls, and illustrative examples. Checklist E translates these conditions into a set of practical, yes/no-style questions for systematically evaluating whether a study's internally valid causal estimates are likely to transport to other populations, settings, time periods, or designs. These tools are intended for use once internal validity requirements have been satisfied (see Section 5), to assess whether causal claims remain plausible when extended to real-world conditions, target populations, or new contexts. Checklist responses can be marked Yes/No/Partly/N/A, with optional comments or evidence supporting each judgment.

## 8. Discussion and conclusions

This paper has proposed a framework for systematically evaluating whether interventional causal inferences and effect estimates drawn from observational data satisfy necessary conditions for validity. It is grounded in explicit, empirically testable, and independently verifiable criteria-conditions that the "causal revolution" of recent decades (Schwartz and Prins 2024) suggests must be met for drawing valid conclusions about causal effects from non-experimental data. They are intended to move the process of drawing valid and defensible causal inferences from observational data beyond opinion, judgment, or convention alone, toward a more transparent, reproducible, and decision-relevant science. Organized into five logically distinct domains-(A) conceptual and definitional clarity, (B) study design prerequisites, (C) analytic requirements for valid modeling and effect estimation, (D) interpretation and result integrity, and (E) robustness, generalizability, and external validity-this framework seeks to clarify the minimum standards that any defensible interventional causal claim from observational evidence should meet.

While the traditional Bradford Hill viewpoints remain influential in public health, Hill himself emphasized that he believed that no fixed checklist of necessary and sufficient conditions could establish causation (Hill 1965). Our approach departs from that stance by proposing empirically testable necessary conditions for valid interventional effect estimation, recognizing that failure to meet any of them undermines the credibility of a causal claim. In doing so, we respond constructively to calls-such as those by Glass et al. (2013)-to focus on decision-relevant, interventional causation. The Glass et al. framework for "causal inference in public health" highlights the conceptual importance of interventional causal thinking for policy and practice. Our work extends it by providing operational tools, structured criteria, and falsificationoriented diagnostics to evaluate whether observational evidence meets modern standards for interventional claims.

The stakes are high. Invalid causal claims are not merely academic missteps; as illustrated in previous examples in Sections 3 and 4, they can mislead public policy, distort regulatory decisions, undermine litigation outcomes, and impair occupational and public health protections. The history of environmental, pharmaceutical, and public health controversies shows that inadequate control of confounding, unjustified extrapolation across contexts, or failure to consider alternative explanations can all create a dangerous illusion of causal certainty (D'Amico et al. 2025). By contrast, more trustworthy causal inferences can inform life-saving decisions, targeted regulations, and cost-effective interventions (Munafò et al. 2017). This framework therefore speaks to both scientific integrity and the ethical responsibility to avoid preventable harm.

Our motivation stems from widespread concern-echoing loannidis and many others-that many published observational studies contain implicit or explicit causal conclusions not well supported by the data or methods adduced as evidence to support them (Munafò et al. 2017; D'Amico et al. 2025). Too often, causal language is applied without
corresponding attention to identification, robustness, falsification, or alternative explanations (Hernán 2016). This misleads readers and decision-makers who depend on scientific evidence for action. Our goal is not to discourage causal learning from observational data, but to provide a path for doing it soundly.

The tables, criteria, and checklists developed in previous sections are designed for practical use-not only by researchers conducting studies, but also by peer reviewers, regulators, litigators, expert panels, and informed stakeholders assessing whether a causal claim holds up under scrutiny. They are meant to serve as a checklist, a map, and an invitation to improved reasoning and communication. While this framework aims to be comprehensive and rigorous, it remains open to revision, expansion, and empirical testing. We welcome contributions from researchers in diverse fields, including applications to time-series data, adaptive trials, and complex systems, as well as empirical evaluations of how published causal claims fare when assessed against the checklist.

For practitioners-whether scientists, legal experts, regulators, or corporate risk managers-our conclusions are as follows:

1. All necessary conditions matter. Causal conclusions from observational data are only as strong as the weakest domain they violate-conceptual and definitional clarity, study design, data analysis, interpretation and integrity of results, and external validity. Internal consistency is not enough; every foundational and design-based prerequisite must be met to support valid causal inferences and conclusions.
2. Identifiability and falsifiability are essential. Confident claims of causal effect must rest on identifiable contrasts, tested assumptions, and falsifiable structures. Without these, the risk of bias, spuriousness, or irreproducibility remains high.
3. Methods do not guarantee validity. Statistical tools such as regression, matching, or stratification can control for confounding only if key assumptions (e.g. exchangeability) are satisfied and explicitly justified.
4. Generalizability must be tested, not assumed. Effects must be evaluated for transportability to other populations, settings, and time periods-including the target populations, conditions, and interventions which they are applied.
5. Transparency strengthens trust. Clear reporting of assumptions, limitations, and alternative explanations improves both science and decision-making.

For decision-makers and policy analysts, it will often be necessary to supplement the necessary conditions for establishing interventional causation reviewed here with normative principles for deciding what to do when those conditions are not met. For example, suppose that one or more studies report statistically significant positive associations between a mother's use of Tylenol during pregnancy and subsequent diagnoses of autism in offspring; or between hormone replacement therapy (HRT) and cancer risk in post-
menopausal women; or between consumption of ultra-processed foods and a variety of adverse human health outcomes.

Now suppose that the necessary conditions for establishing an interventional causal relationship between these exposures and adverse responses are not (yet) met-perhaps because the associations do not adequately control for all likely confounders, or because they are sensitive to modeling assumptions that have not been tested or verified. How should a regulator or public health official decide what to do in such circumstances?

We do not advocate that decision-makers should do nothing until interventional causation has been fully clarified. Rather, we propose that interventional causation in such cases should be regarded as not yet established, and that deliberative decision-making should therefore explicitly consider the possibility that reducing exposure would not reduce harm in the manner that the observed statistical associations might suggest if interpreted causally.

Decision-making under uncertainty is more often the rule than the exception in health risk management and regulatory policymaking. If interventional causation has not been established at the time a decision must be made, then the bestinformed decisions are those that acknowledge this fact as a source of uncertainty about the consequences of alternative interventions. In practice, deliberation about what to do may also draw on complementary evidence streams-including mechanistic studies, animal experiments, and toxicological data-which can inform judgments about biological plausibility and potential harms even when interventional causal effects in humans have not been definitively identified. Normative decision-analytic methods (such as maximum expected utility decision-making) hold that decision-making should be informed by, but not stymied by, such uncertainties, and that decisions are improved-not undermined-by acknowledging them when they exist. In short, we believe that the checklists and conditions discussed here for assessing evidence of interventional causation should inform, but not preclude, timely action; and that decisions can and should be made using appropriate normative methods of decision analysis even when their consequences are uncertain (Cox 2015).

A final clarification is warranted regarding the interpretation and use of the framework developed here. If observed associations do not, by themselves, establish interventional causation, this does not imply that the absence of such evidence constitutes evidence of no risk, nor that concerns about potential harms are unwarranted. It implies only that the causal status of the association remains uncertain. Methodological rigor in causal inference is value-neutral: causal statements that lack a rigorous basis can be misused to overstate harms just as readily as to understate them. The purpose of articulating necessary conditions for causal inference is, therefore, not to provide a rhetorical basis for dismissing potential health risks, but to reduce both types of error-false attribution of causation and unwarranted dismissal of plausible causal hypotheses-by making assumptions explicit and clarifying what conclusions are and are not supported by available evidence. When used appropriately,
rigorous causal analysis can support precautionary decisionmaking by sharpening understanding of uncertainties and tradeoffs, rather than serving as a justification for either reflexive action or inaction.

More quantitatively, causal analytics and decision analysis intersect in their shared use of sensitivity analyses to examine the robustness of conclusions to remaining uncertainties. Classical decision analysis, which represents preferences over decisions with probabilistic outcomes via expected utilities, cannot be directly applied when causal models linking decisions to outcome probabilities are unavailable. However, expected-utility theory can be extended to such cases using distributionally robust optimization (Kuhn et al. 2025). The central result is that normative axioms imply that decisions should be chosen to maximize the minimum expected utility over an "ambiguity set" of plausible probability distributions over outcomes implied by alternative causal models (Gilboa and Schmeidler 1989; Kuhn et al. 2025). From this perspective, necessary conditions that help to eliminate some potential causal models reduce the ambiguity set and, thereby, potentially increase the maximized minimum expected utility, providing positive value of causal information even if it is only partial. Waiting for complete causal understanding where the ambiguity set shrinks to a single model - is neither necessary nor desirable for many risk management decisions under (model) uncertainty. Finally, translating evidence into standards and policy requires keeping the boundary between scientific inference and policy judgment explicit. For example, under the Clean Air Act in the United States, the U.S. EPA Administrator must set NAAQS using the "latest scientific knowledge," yet the final level reflects an explicit judgment about acceptable risk and an "adequate margin of safety" (McClellan 2012). Courts have likewise underscored that scientific evidence informs-but does not determinethe Administrator's policy choices (Whitman v. American Trucking Associations, 531 US 457 2001). Our framework is designed to support this division of labor between science and policy by providing necessary conditions and technical methods for producing decision-relevant, interventional effect estimates-with assumptions, uncertainty, and sensitivity analyses made transparent-while leaving value-laden choices about acceptable risk to policymakers (National Research Council (US) 2009; National Academies of Sciences, Engineering, and Medicine; Division on Earth and Life Studies; Board on Environmental Studies and Toxicology 2017).

Throughout, this paper has drawn on ideas and methods from philosophy of causation, epidemiology, statistics, econometrics, and computer science. While these traditions often use different terminology and tools, the necessary-conditions perspective provides a common lens for understanding how they relate: each approach proposes mechanisms, assumptions, or designs intended to satisfy particular requirements for valid interventional inference, and each fails when those requirements are not met. Making these connections explicit is intended to help readers translate insights across domains rather than treat them as competing or incompatible frameworks.

Following this framework does not guarantee truth, since uncertainties typically remain, but it does provide a systematic way to ask the right questions, avoid predictable errors, test consistency of causal beliefs with data, and strengthen the foundations of causal reasoning in real-world applied science. Meeting all necessary conditions across the five domains offers the best chance of producing trustworthy, decision-relevant causal claims-whether for research, regulation, litigation, or public health action. By embedding necessary conditions into practical tools and checklists, we hope to make modern causal inference from observational data not only more transparent, but also more trustworthy and impactful for science, policy, and public health.
