| Glossary |  |
| :--- | :--- |
| Term or tool | Definition or purpose |
| Augmented Inverse Probability Weighting (AIPW) | A doubly robust estimator that combines outcome regression with inverse probability weighting to estimate a specified causal estimand while adjusting for confounding. Under standard regularity conditions, it yields consistent estimates if either the treatment assignment model or the outcome model is correctly specified (Hernán and Robins, 2020). |
| Average treatment effect (ATE) or average causal effect (ACE) | A causal effect defined as the difference between the average outcome that would be observed if the entire population were exposed to the treatment and the average outcome that would be observed if the same population were unexposed. Also referred to as the average causal effect. |
| Back door path | A path between two variables (typically a cause X and an outcome Y ) that induces a spurious, non-causal association due to common causes or other structural dependencies. In a directed acyclic graph (DAG), a backdoor path from $X$ to $Y$ is any path that begins with an arrow pointing into $X$ and connects to $Y$ through other variables. Such paths represent non-causal pathways that can bias estimation of the causal effect of $X$ on $Y$ unless appropriately blocked (Pearl, 2009). |
| Conditional average treatment effect (CATE) | The average causal effect of a treatment for units with a specific covariate profile. Often estimated using flexible models like causal forests or meta-learners. |
| Causal contrast | An expression of the causal effect of interest, defined as a comparison between potential outcomes under different exposure or intervention conditions. For example, the causal effect in the exposed compares the observed disease experience of exposed individuals with the disease experience those same individuals would have had if unexposed. |
| Causal effect | In the context of the Potential Outcomes framework, a causal effect is the difference between two potential outcomes-what would happen under one treatment or intervention versus what would happen under a different treatment or intervention. |
| Causal explanation | Elaboration of how causes work, distinct from causal identification. Causal explanation comprises construct validity (identification of the "active ingredients" of the exposure and the mechanisms through which it operates, i.e. mediation) and external validity (identification of the causal partners of the exposure under study, i.e. interaction), following the classification of Shadish, Cook, and Campbell (2002). |
| Causal field | A term developed by Mackie (1974). The causal field expresses the boundaries within which we ask a causal question. For example, when we talk about the causes of stroke, we often take for granted the fact that the human brain requires oxygen to function. We consider that part of the taken-for-granted context in which all strokes happen (i.e. we do not consider the human brain requiring oxygen to be a cause of interest because it is taken as given). Causal fields are often left unarticulated, which can lead to miscommunication or disagreement about what is being claimed as causal. |
| Causal forests | A nonparametric ensemble method for estimating Conditional Average Treatment Effects (CATEs), allowing flexible modeling of treatment effect heterogeneity using tree-based partitioning. |
| Causal identification | Causal identification refers to the conditions under which a specified causal effect of an exposure on an outcome can be uniquely inferred from data as the explanation for an observed association. Identification requires ruling out alternative explanations such as chance, confounding, selection bias, and measurement error, and depends on assumptions including a well-defined intervention (consistency) and positivity. Identification concerns whether a causal effect is, in principle, learnable from the available data and assumptions, distinct from how it is estimated in practice. |
| Cause (Mackie, 1974) | X is a cause of Y if X and Y occurred within a causal field and, in the circumstances, Y would not have occurred if X had not occurred. It is an INUS condition-Insufficient but Necessary part of a condition which is Unnecessary but Sufficient for the result Mackie, 1974). The INUS condition is a way to understand causationin complex systems, especially when outcomes have multiple contributing factors. |
| Cause (Rothman, 1976) | A cause of a disease event is an event, condition, or characteristic that preceded the disease event and without which the disease event would not have occurred at all or would not have occurred until some later time. |
| Controlled direct effect (CDE), Natural Direct effect (NDE), and Natural indirect effect (NIE) | Controlled Direct Effect, Natural Direct Effect, and Natural Indirect Effect are effect estimands used in mediation analysis to decompose total effects of a treatment or intervention into direct and indirect pathways. They help to understand how a treatment or exposure affects an outcome both directly and indirectly through a mediator variable. The CDE examines a direct effect with a fixed mediator. The NDE examines a direct effect with a mediator set to its natural value under control. The NIE examines indirect effect through a mediator. |
| Collider | A variable that is influenced by two other variables-the treatment and outcome or their causes. A collider is a node in a DAG where two or more arrows converge from different variables (e.g. a common child). For example, $\mathrm{A} \rightarrow \mathrm{C} \leftarrow \mathrm{B}$, where C is a collider because it depends on both A and B . Conditioning on a collider can create spurious associations and bias estimates. |
| Compound construct | An exposure or treatment comprising multiple parts that may have different effects on an outcome for the same individual. |
| Conditional exchangeability | The assumption that, after adjusting for a set of observed covariates, the potential outcomes are independent of treatment assignment. |


| Term or tool | Definition or purpose |
| :--- | :--- |
| Confounder | A variable that is associated with both the treatment and the outcome. It can create a spurious association or mask a real causal effect. A confounder influences both the treatment and the outcome, creating a non-causal association between them unless adjusted for appropriately. Confounders must be adjusted for to block noncausal backdoor paths. |
| Confounding | It is where the relationship between a treatment or exposure and an outcome is distorted by the presence of another variable, a confounder. It is the non-exchangeability that arises from common causes of the exposure and the disease. |
| Consistency | When the outcome under an observed exposure is the same as what would happen if that exposure were assigned. The actual outcome under the exposure is the potential outcome under that exposure |
| Correlated | A synonym for associated. It means two variables are related, but it does not mean one causes the other. In causal inference, the goal is to distinguish correlation from causation, using methods such as randomized controlled trials, natural experiments, or statistical controls to rule out confounding factors. Correlation tells us that two variables move together. Properly theorized and executed counterfactual investigation can tell us if changing one variable would change the other, which introduces the possibility of identifying, isolating, and understanding causation. |
| Counterfactual | What would have occurred under circumstances that differ in particular ways from what did occur. A counterfactual is a hypothetical scenario describing what would have happened to an individual, group, or system if a different action or treatment had been taken, while all other conditions remained the same. Counterfactuals are often used in epidemiology to refer to the disease experience of an exposed cohort under conditions of non-exposure. The goal is to estimate what would have happened under different circumstances (the counterfactual), which is essential for understanding cause and effect. |
| Cross-fitting/Sample splitting | A technique used in causal inference and machine learning to reduce overfitting and restore efficiency. The sample is split into folds; nuisance models are trained on one fold and predictions are applied to another. Estimates are then averaged across folds. Often used with doubly robust estimators like Double/Debiased Machine Learning (DML) and Targeted Maximum Likelihood Estimation (TMLE). |
| Directed acyclic graph (DAG), family relationships in | Parent: A variable that is a direct cause of another variable in a DAG. <br> Child: A variable that is the direct consequence of another variable in a DAG. |
| Directed acyclic graph (DAG), Paths in | A sequence of lines connecting two variables. <br> Blocked path: A path that is intercepted by a controlled variable or an uncontrolled collider. <br> Unblocked path: A path that is not intercepted by a controlled variable or an uncontrolled collider. <br> Back door path: A path that connects two variables, where an arrow points into the treatment/exposure. <br> Front door path: A path that connects two variables, where the arrow emanates from the treatment/exposure. |
| DAGitty | A web-based software tool for constructing DAGs and identifying minimal sufficient adjustment sets for causal inference. |
| Difference-in-differences (DiD) | A statistical technique commonly used in causal inference and econometrics to estimate the causal effect of a treatment, policy, or intervention when randomized experiments are not feasible. DiD compares the changes in outcomes over time between a group that is exposed to a treatment or intervention (the "treatment group") and a group that is not (the "control group"). DiD estimates the causal effect of a treatment or policy by comparing the changes in outcomes over time between a treatment group and a control group, accounting for time trends common to both. |
| Direct effect | In the context of DAGs, an arrow connecting two variables that is not intercepted by another variable on the path; a causal relationship with no articulated mediator. |
| Directed acyclic graph (DAG) | A graphical method for depicting hypothesized causal relationships between variables and deducing the statistical associations implied by these causal relationships. "Directed" means that the arrows point in only one direction because they indicate causation. "Acyclic" means that no variable can cause itself directly or through any number of other variables. |
| Double/Debiased machine learning (DML) | A doubly robust framework that uses sample splitting and cross-fitting to combine machine learning predictions of treatment and outcome models, enabling consistent and efficient causal effect estimation in high-dimensional settings. |
| Do-calculus | Judea Pearl's mathematical rules for manipulating expressions involving the do-operator. It provides a formal way to transform and simplify causal queries, especially when trying to express interventional probabilities in terms of observable data. The do-calculus helps determine whether and how causal effects expressed with the dooperator can be identified from observational data, given a DAG. |
| Do-operator | Judea Pearl's mathematical representation of an intervention on a graph-setting the value of a variable to a particular value generally described as setting (X) to (x). It allows a researcher to define and reason about causal effects or what happens to other variables when one intervenes. The do-operator allows a researcher mathematically express interventions, while do-calculus provides the formal tools to analyze and identify causal effects involving those interventions. |
| Doubly robust | A property of certain estimators that remain consistent if either the treatment model or the outcome model is correctly specified (but not necessarily both). Methods like AIPW, DML, and TMLE achieve this property by combining separate models for treatment and outcome. |
| Ecological fallacy | The mistaken inference that associations observed at the group or aggregate level also apply at the individual level. It occurs when group-level correlations (e.g. between air pollution and mortality by city) are interpreted as evidence of individual-level causal effects, despite potential differences in within-group relationships. The ecological fallacy occurs when conclusions about individual-level relationships are incorrectly drawn from grouplevel or aggregate data. |
| Effect measure modification | When the association between an exposure and an outcome varies across categories or levels of another variable, when using a particular measure of association (e.g. risk difference, risk ratio). |
| Effectiveness | The effect of a treatment or intervention in real-world conditions in the population of interest. |


| Term or tool | Definition or purpose |
| :--- | :--- |
| Epistemology | Branch of philosophy concerned with theories of knowledge, or how we know things. |
| Estimand | The specific quantity or parameter that a study aims to estimate. Represents the precise target of inference. It is what you ultimately want to learn about from the data. Typically defined using the potential outcomes framework (e.g. [(1)]-[(0)]) or through structural causal models. |
| E-value | A sensitivity metric that quantifies the minimum strength of association an unmeasured confounder would need to have with both the treatment and outcome to fully explain away an observed effect. |
| Exchangeability | The condition that, conditional on measured covariates, the distribution of potential outcomes is the same across treatment groups. Thus, treated and untreated groups are comparable after adjusting for covariates. This condition is required to identify causal effects from observational data. Some methods assume it; others attempt to test or adjust for violations (e.g. using instrumental variables, sensitivity analysis, or graphical algorithms that allow latent confounding). |
| Exclusion restriction | The exclusion restriction is a key assumption required for the validity of instrumental variable (IV) analysis in causal inference. It states that the instrumental variable affects the outcome only through its effect on the treatment or exposure, and not through any other pathway. |
| External validity | The extent to which the effect of an exposure found in a particular population or time period would also be found in another population or time period. The term can be used more specifically to mean extrapolation to the target population, that is the population in which the researcher wants to estimate the causal effect. Extrapolation beyond the target population is often referred to as "transportability." |
| Falsification logic | The principle that valid causal models must yield testable implications that can be confronted, and potentially refuted, by data. |
| Fast causal inference (FCI) | A constraint-based causal discovery algorithm that infers causal structure from conditional independence tests, allowing for latent confounding and selection bias. FCI helps detect colliders and assess model plausibility. |
| Generalized additive model (GAM) | A flexible regression model that allows non-linear relationships between covariates and outcome via smooth functions. |
| G-methods | A family of causal inference methods such as g -computation and marginal structural models that adjust for timevarying confounding and post-treatment variables. |
| Heterogeneous treatment effects (HTE) | Variation in treatment effects across individuals or subgroups defined by covariates. Identifying HTEs support tailored decision-making and fairness analyses. |
| Idealized randomized trial | A perfectly executed randomized trial. A trial with an infinite sample size, no post-randomization nonexchangeability (e.g. perfect adherence, no loss to follow-up) and perfect measurement. |
| Identifiability | The causal effect of interest is said to be identifiable (or causally identifiable) if it is uniquely determined by the joint distribution of the observed variables under stated assumptions |
| Identifiability assumptions | The assumptions needed to translate a causal effect into an observable quantity under the Potential Outcomes framework-exchangeability, positivity, and consistency. They are conditions or assumptions that allow researchers to uniquely estimate or identify a causal effect from observed data. |
| Immortal time bias | A bias that arises when periods during which an outcome could not have occurred are misclassified or excluded, typically inflating survival time in exposed groups. |
| Indirect effect | The effect of an exposure, treatment, or intervention on an outcome that works through an articulated mediator. |
| Information bias | Errors in an estimate deriving from the way in which variables are measured. Also known as "measurement error." |
| Instrumental variable (IV) | A variable that affects exposure but has no direct effect on the outcome and is not related to unmeasured confounders. IVs are used to identify causal effects when randomization is not possible. IVs must satisfy exclusion and independence conditions. The relationship between the IV and the outcome is used to assess effects of the exposure on the outcome when that relationship is confounded. |
| Intent-to-treat analysis Interaction | The effect of treatment assignment on the outcome in a trial rather than the effect of the actual treatment itself. A synonym for effect modification. "Effect modification" is used more frequently for categorical variables whereas "interaction" is used more frequently for continuous variables. |
| Internal validity | Whether the study design supports a valid causal interpretation by ruling out alternative explanations for the observed effect such as coincidental historical trends, confounding, bias, and reverse causation. Internal validity refers to the degree to which a study's design, conduct, and analysis allows for a trustworthy and unbiased estimate of the causal effect within the specific study population. A study has high internal validity if the observed association between the treatment or exposure and the outcome can be confidently attributed to a causal relationship, rather than to confounding, bias, or other methodological flaws. |
| INUS | An acronym developed by J.L. Mackie in The Cement of the Universe: A Study of Causation that describes causes of interest as insufficient but necessary components of unnecessary but sufficient causes (INUS causes). The INUS condition is a way to understand how causes work in complex systems, especially when outcomes have multiple contributing factors. In earlier publications, Mackie uses "non-redundant components" rather than "necessary components." |
| Invariant causal prediction (ICP) | A method that identifies causal relationships by finding associations that remain stable (invariant) across different environments, datasets, or subgroups. ICP leverages the idea that causal relationships remain stable (invariant) across different environments or settings, while non-causal associations may change when the environment changes. |
| Inverse probability weighting (IPW) | A singly robust method for adjusting for confounding by weighting each observation by the inverse (reciprocal) of its estimated probability of receiving the treatment actually received. Creates a pseudo-population in which treatment is independent of confounders, enabling estimation of marginal causal effects (usually the average treatment effect,). IPW assumes no unmeasured confounding, positivity, and correct specification of the treatment model. It is a weighting method that adjusts for confounding by reweighting observations based on estimated treatment probabilities. |
| Kernel density estimate (KDE) plot | A smoothed estimate of the probability distribution of a variable (e.g. propensity score), used to visualize overlap between groups. Poor overlap may signal violations of the positivity assumption. |
| Mediator | A variable that transmits part of the causal effect from exposure to outcome. It is a variable that explains the mechanism or pathway through which a causal effect occurs. Specifically, a mediator is an intermediate variable that is affected by the exposure or treatment (often called the independent variable) that affects the outcome (dependent variable). A mediator is a variable that transmits part or all the effect from the exposure or treatment to the outcome, helping a researcher understand how and why a causal effects occur. |


| Term or tool | Definition or purpose |
| :--- | :--- |
| Meta-learners | A class of machine learning estimators (e.g. T-learner, X-learner, DR-learner) that estimate heterogeneous treatment effects by combining outcome and treatment models. |
| Multiverse analysis | A robustness strategy that systematically explores how causal conclusions vary across a wide range of plausible modeling and adjustment choices (e.g. alternative DAGs or adjustment sets). |
| Negative control | A variable, outcome, or exposure expected not to be causally affected by the treatment (or not to affect the outcome). If an association is observed where none should exist, this suggests unmeasured confounding, measurement error, or model misspecification. Negative control is used to detect threats to validity by identifying spurious associations. For example, testing whether a treatment affects a biologically implausible outcome or an outcome measured before exposure. |
| Non-exchangeability | When the risk of disease in the exposed and unexposed differs for reasons other than the exposure effect itself. |
| Omitted variable bias analysis (OVB) framework | A method developed by Cinelli and Hazlett (2020) that assesses how sensitive estimated causal effects are to omitted confounders, using benchmarks based on the explanatory power (partial $\mathrm{R}^{2}$ ) of observed covariates. |
| Parallel trends | Assumption that, in absence of treatment, treated and control groups would follow similar trends. Required for traditional difference-in-differences analysis. |
| Partial $R^{2}$ | The proportion of outcome variance explained by a covariate, conditional on other covariates. Used in OVB analysis to benchmark potential unmeasured confounding. |
| Placebo test | A variable or outcome expected to show no causal effect. Observing an association where none should exist suggests unmeasured confounding, bias, or model misspecification-e.g. if a treatment is identified as affecting a pre-treatment or biologically implausible outcome. A placebo test is a falsification or robustness check on a research method in a situation where no effect is expected. Finding no effect in the placebo test supports the researcher's original causal claims. Finding an effect suggests possible problems with the researcher's analysis. |
| Positivity (overlap) | The requirement that for every combination of covariates, there is a non-zero probability of receiving each exposure level. It is the requirement that all units have a positive probability of receiving each treatment level, given their covariates. Violations make causal effects undefined in some subgroups. |
| Positivity | An identifiability assumption requiring that there are both exposed and unexposed people in all strata of the confounders (aka experimental treatment assignment). |
| Post-treatment variable | A variable affected by treatment. Conditioning on it can introduce selection bias or block causal pathways. |
| Potential outcome (PO) | The outcome that would be observed for a unit under a specific treatment or condition, whether or not that treatment occurred. It is central to defining and estimating causal effects. It is an outcome that would happen under specified exposure conditions. |
| Pretest reactivity | Change in behavior or outcomes caused by taking the pretest, independent of the intervention. May bias results in pretest-posttest designs. |
| Propensity score Proxy variable |  |
|  | The probability of receiving the treatment, conditional on covariates. Used to assess and adjust for confounding. An imperfect stand-in for an unmeasured variable. May lead to residual confounding unless shown to be a sufficient surrogate. |
| Quantitative bias analysis (QBA) | A set of methods that quantify how unmeasured confounding or other biases (e.g. misclassification) could alter study conclusions. Tools include E-values, the OVB framework, and simulation-based sensitivity analyses. |
| Quasi-experimental (QE) design | A study design used to estimate causal effects without using random assignment. |
| Randomized controlled trial (RCT) | A type of scientific experiment used to evaluate the causal effect of an intervention, treatment, or policy. In an RCT, participants are randomly assigned to either a treatment group (receiving the intervention) or a control group (not receiving the intervention). |
| Red teaming | A process for adversarially evaluating the robustness of causal conclusions by actively seeking alternative explanations, modeling choices, or unrecognized biases that could plausibly invalidate the primary findings. |
| Regression to the mean | Tendency of extreme measurements to move closer to the average on retesting. |
| Regression Specification Error Test (RESET) | A diagnostic that tests for incorrect model functional form, such as missing nonlinearities or interactions, by adding powers of fitted values to the regression model. |
| Response type | Introduced by Sander Greenland and James Robins, response type refers to an individual's set of potential outcomes for a particular exposure and disease. For binary exposures and outcomes, each individual can be categorized into one of four possible response types: doomed, causal, preventive, and immune. |
| Selection bias | Non-exchangeability that arises from the processes through which individuals select into or are selected into the study or final analytic sample. Selection bias is a type of systematic error that occurs when the individuals or units included in a study or analysis are not representative of the target population, often because of the way they were selected. Selection bias can distort estimates of associations or causal effects, making results misleading or invalid. |
| Sensemakr | An R package that implements the OVB sensitivity analysis framework, benchmarking unmeasured confounding using partial $R^{2}$ and robustness values. |
| Sensitivity analysis | A suite of methods that evaluate how robust causal conclusions are to potential violations of assumptions, such as unmeasured confounding or model misspecification. |
| Singly robust | A property of certain estimators that produce valid (i.e. consistent) estimates only if one component, typically the treatment model or the outcome model, is correctly specified. |
| Social phenomena | Phenomena that emerge from human interactions but are distinct from, and cannot be reduced to, the characteristics of individuals. They are real things that act causally in the world but often cannot be directly observed as they are concept-dependent and relational. They have stability but are contingent and changeable. |
| Spillover effects | When treatment received by one unit affects outcomes for another unit, for example, by contagion, displacement, or herd effects. |
| Stable unit treatment value assumption (SUTVA) | A requirement to estimate treatment effects. The assumption has two parts: (1) there are no unrepresented versions of treatment (each individual has only one potential outcome for each treatment) and (2) there is no interference between units (each individual has only one potential outcome for each treatment regardless of the treatment other individuals receive). |
| Statistics | Statistics is the science of drawing reasoned conclusions from data to understand and quantify uncertainty, variation, and relationships. A statistic is any quantity calculated from data, and a test statistic is one with a known probability distribution when a stated null hypothesis-usually that no difference or effect exists-is true. Inferential statistics has two main branches: estimation, which measures the size of unknown quantities, and hypothesis testing, which evaluates whether data are consistent with a null hypothesis. The two are closely connected, since a $95 \%$ confidence interval includes all parameter values that would not be rejected under that null hypothesis. Because we usually study samples to make inferences about populations, valid inference |


| Term or tool | Definition or purpose |
| :--- | :--- |
|  | depends on design: random or stratified sampling allows probability theory to define the behavior of test statistics, while observational studies require explicit, testable assumptions. Sound statistical work distinguishes exploratory analysis, which seeks patterns and generates hypotheses, from confirmatory analysis, which tests them with defined methods and controls. Above all, statistics begins with thoughtful study design-deciding what data to collect, how to define and code variables, and how to ensure questions are answerable. Properly used, statistics provides an objective, transparent, and disciplined way to convert data into reliable knowledge. |
| SUTVA (stable unit treatment value assumption) | The assumption that (1) the potential outcome for any unit depends only on that unit's own treatment, not on the treatment of other units (no interference), and (2) each treatment condition is consistently defined across units (no hidden variations). Violations can arise from spillover effects, contagion, or treatment non-uniformity. |
| Target trial | The randomized trial that you would ideally conduct to address the causal question of interest. Used as a guide to develop observational studies. |
| Time zero or index date | The starting point for defining exposure and tracking outcomes. It must be clear and consistent to avoid biases like immortal time bias or misalignment of exposure and outcome. |
| Targeted maximum likelihood estimation (TMLE) | A doubly robust estimation method that combines machine learning-based nuisance estimation with a targeting step to improve bias-variance trade-offs for estimating causal effects (e.g. ATE). TMLE supports valid inference under flexible model specifications. |
| Total effect | The effect of an exposure that works through all pathways, both direct and indirect that emanate from the exposure. |
| Treatment | An exposure that is assignable. |
| Triangulation | Integrating results from studies or analyses using approaches that vary in the key sources of bias. |
| Well-defined intervention assumption | Exposures can be conceptualized as treatments in a randomized controlled trial. Sometimes used as a synonym for SUTVA and consistency. |
